The path a change takes to production

Everything between a commit and a running system

Git from the object database up, GitHub as a platform rather than a website, and then the whole operational toolchain: Linux, networking, containers, Kubernetes, infrastructure as code, pipelines, deployment strategies, secrets, observability, incident response and cost. Every command that matters, with what it does, when it is dangerous, and how to undo it.

00

The path of a change

A DevOps engineer's job is to make this path fast, safe and boring. Every chapter in this book is one segment of it, and every outage you will ever handle happens where two segments meet.

The three questions this book answers

What does this command do?

Part I and the reference tables. Not just the flag, but the model underneath it — most Git confusion is a wrong mental model, not a forgotten option.

How do I undo it?

Every destructive operation in this book carries a recovery path. destructive markers flag the commands that lose work, and the reflog chapter exists because almost nothing in Git is actually lost.

How does this work at 200 engineers?

The practices that survive a small team and the ones that do not: branching strategy, monorepo tooling, pipeline caching, environment promotion, and who is allowed to press what.

the whole loop, in one screen
$ git switch -c fix/checkout-timeout
$ git add -p                      # stage hunks, not files
$ git commit -m "fix: raise upstream timeout to 15s"
$ git push -u origin HEAD
$ gh pr create --fill --reviewer @platform
  → CI: lint ✓  unit ✓  integration ✓  image build ✓  trivy ✓
$ gh pr merge --squash --auto
  → tag v2.14.3 → image ghcr.io/acme/api@sha256:9f3c… → argocd sync staging
$ kubectl -n staging rollout status deploy/api --timeout=120s
  → canary 10% for 10m … error rate 0.02% … promote 100%
$ kubectl -n prod rollout undo deploy/api    # the command you must never hesitate to run
The interview frame

The most revealing DevOps interview question is not “how does Kubernetes work” but “walk me through what happens when you merge a pull request”. A strong answer names every stage above, the artefact that flows between them, the gate at each boundary, who or what approves it, and how long a rollback takes. If you can also say what happens when each stage fails, you are describing a platform rather than a set of tools.

How to read the command tables

Commands are marked safe (local, reversible), care (changes shared state or history you may have pushed) or destructive (can discard uncommitted or unreferenced work). Learn the recovery for every command in the last two categories before you need it at 2am.

G01

How Git actually works

Git is a content-addressable object database with a thin layer of naming on top; every behaviour that feels arbitrary — detached HEAD, the difference between reset and restore, why a rebase changes every hash downstream — falls out of that one fact. Learn the storage model and you stop memorising flags, which matters most at 03:00 when you are recovering a branch someone force-pushed over.

A key–value store that happens to track files

Underneath the porcelain, Git stores everything in .git/objects as immutable values keyed by the hash of their own content. You hand it bytes, it returns a 40-hex-character SHA-1 (or 64-hex SHA-256 in a repository initialised with git init --object-format=sha256); you hand back the hash, it returns the bytes. There is no update operation and no delete that ordinary commands reach for. The database only grows, and history is rewritten by writing new objects and repointing a name at them.

The hash is not taken over the content alone. Git prefixes a header — object type, space, content length in bytes, NUL — hashes header plus content, and stores the result zlib-compressed at .git/objects/ab/cdef…, where ab is the first byte of the hash in hex. That header is why sha1sum file and git hash-object file disagree, and why an empty tree always hashes to 4b825dc642cb6eb9a060e54bf8d69288fbee4904 in every SHA-1 repository on earth.

plaintext · the serialised form Git hashes
blob 6\0hello\n
tree 67\0100644 README.md\0[20 raw bytes]040000 src\0[20 raw bytes]
commit 139\0tree ff54940...\nauthor ...\ncommitter ...\n\nInitial import\n
python · reproduce a blob hash without Git
import hashlib

content = b"hello\n"
store = b"blob %d\0" % len(content) + content
print(hashlib.sha1(store).hexdigest())
# ce013625030ba8dba906f756967f9e9ca394464a  -- matches git hash-object
print(hashlib.sha256(store).hexdigest())
# 2cf8d83d9ee29543b34a87727421fdecb7e3f3a183d337639025de576db9ebb4

Two consequences follow immediately. Identical content is stored once no matter how many paths or commits reference it, so copying a 200 MB fixture into a second directory costs nothing. And because an object's identity is its content, you cannot alter one: altering it produces a different object at a different address, and everything pointing at the old address still points there.

Four object types, and nothing else

blob
Raw file bytes. No filename, no permissions, no timestamp, no history. A blob does not know it is a file.
tree
A directory listing: a sorted set of entries, each carrying a mode, a name, and the hash of a blob (a file) or another tree (a subdirectory). Names and modes live here, not in blobs.
commit
A pointer to exactly one tree — the whole project snapshot at that moment — plus zero or more parent hashes, author and committer identities with timestamps, an optional GPG/SSH signature, and the message.
tag
An annotated tag object: the hash and type of the object it names, the tag name, a tagger identity, a message, an optional signature. Lightweight tags are not objects at all — just a ref file.

That is the whole vocabulary. There is no "diff" or "changeset" object: Git stores full snapshots and computes diffs on demand, which is why git log -p is slower than git log and why renames are detected heuristically rather than recorded.

Tree entry modes — the only modes Git records
ModePoints toMeaning
100644blobRegular file
100755blobExecutable file — the only permission bit Git tracks
120000blobSymlink; the blob content is the link target string
040000treeSubdirectory
160000commitGitlink — a submodule pinned at a commit in another repository

Note what is absent: owner, group, mtime, and every permission bit but the executable flag. Git also cannot track an empty directory, because there is no blob to put in a tree entry — hence the .gitkeep convention.

See it for yourself

Two plumbing commands make the database visible. git hash-object computes (and with -w, writes) an object; git cat-file reads one back, with -t for type, -s for size and -p for a pretty-printed dump.

a repository with two files, walked from the top down
$ git init -q -b main objdemo && cd objdemo
$ printf 'hello\n' > README.md && mkdir src && printf 'print("hi")\n' > src/app.py
$ git add . && git commit -q -m 'Initial import'

$ printf 'hello\n' | git hash-object --stdin
ce013625030ba8dba906f756967f9e9ca394464a
# the same hash the commit's tree will reference -- content, not context

$ git cat-file -t HEAD && git cat-file -s HEAD
commit
139

$ git cat-file -p HEAD
tree ff54940a083e4b8f4c6940af45d0ff22255c2029
author Demo <a@b.c> 1770801240 +0000
committer Demo <a@b.c> 1770801240 +0000

Initial import

$ git cat-file -p HEAD^{tree}
100644 blob ce013625030ba8dba906f756967f9e9ca394464a    README.md
040000 tree 212c4fdae26c1d9b9ecee80ce328176151064663    src

$ git cat-file -p 212c4fda
100644 blob b80e3222ab264bd7cafb376749bd18814fd66776    app.py

$ git cat-file -p ce013625
hello

Five objects for two files: two blobs, two trees, one commit. Walk that chain once by hand and the model is yours permanently. git ls-tree -r HEAD does the recursion for you; git rev-parse HEAD:src/app.py resolves a path straight to its blob hash.

Commits are an immutable DAG

Each commit names its parents, so commits form a directed acyclic graph pointing backwards in time. A normal commit has one parent, a merge has two or more, a root commit has none. Nothing points forwards — there is no list of children anywhere in the database. Every "what came after this?" question is answered by walking from the tips inwards, which is why git log takes a starting ref and why finding a commit's children requires a scan.

Because the hash covers the tree, the parents and both timestamps, changing anything about a commit changes its hash — and therefore the hash of every descendant, whose parent pointer now differs. That is the whole story of rebase and amend: Git never edits a commit, it builds new ones and moves a branch label to the new tip. The originals stay in the database, orphaned.

amend does not modify anything — it writes a replacement
$ git rev-parse HEAD
5f691f4b6ac71b7d04d1d76afba5e6a193ca0860

$ git commit --amend -m 'Add world to the greeting'
$ git rev-parse HEAD
31c1788c354c984050655c8ed180294eb684b759
# different object; same tree, same parent, new committer date and message

$ git fsck --unreachable --no-reflogs
unreachable commit 5f691f4b6ac71b7d04d1d76afba5e6a193ca0860

$ git cat-file -p 5f691f4
tree 44a79e40cee52bc62f179e17b5512d669f1daafb
parent f310472995d9e956bec48db24b1e7a81f2c15f46
# still there, still readable, still recoverable

The original did not disappear when the branch label moved. It became unreferenced — a completely different state, and one Git is in no hurry to resolve.

Refs are files that contain a hash

A branch is not a container of commits. It is a 41-byte file whose entire content is a hash and a newline; tags and remote-tracking branches are the same. The namespace is a directory tree under .git/refs, optionally flattened into .git/packed-refs, or held in the newer binary reftable backend.

refs/heads/main
A local branch. git commit advances whichever one HEAD currently names.
refs/tags/v1.4.0
A tag. Contains a commit hash (lightweight) or a tag object's hash (annotated). Nothing advances it.
refs/remotes/origin/main
Your last-observed position of the remote's branch. Written by fetch and push, never by commit. It is a cache, not a live view.
HEAD
Normally a symbolic ref: the file contains the text ref: refs/heads/main, not a hash. It answers "which branch am I on?".
.git/HEAD contains "ref: refs/heads/main" (a symbolic ref) * 5f691f4 Add world unreachable -- orphaned by --amend | | * 31c1788 Add world to the greeting refs/heads/main <== HEAD | | | | * 4d1b7c2 Bound the retry queue refs/heads/feat/queue | | | | | | +-+-+ | * f310472 Initial import refs/tags/v0.1.0 refs/remotes/origin/main

Every label above is a file containing one of those hashes. Deleting refs/heads/feat/queue deletes a file; the commits it named are untouched. Creating a branch writes 41 bytes — that is why branching is free, and why git branch -D prints the hash it is abandoning. That printed hash is your recovery handle.

Detached HEAD, properly

When HEAD contains a raw hash instead of ref: refs/heads/…, you are in detached HEAD state. Nothing is broken: you can inspect, build, test and commit as normal. But your commits have no branch label following them, because git commit updates whatever HEAD names and HEAD is naming a commit, not a branch — so each commit simply moves HEAD forward. The moment you git switch main, HEAD points elsewhere and that work is unreachable from any ref.

Interview · the question behind the question

"You committed on a detached HEAD and switched away. Is the work gone?" The answer they want is the mechanism, not the incantation: the commits are in the object database and are unreachable from refs, but HEAD's own reflog recorded every position it held, so git reflog yields the hash and git branch rescue <hash> writes a ref that makes it reachable again. Candidates who answer "you'd lose it" are describing a mental model in which branches contain commits. They will make the same error under pressure with reset --hard.

CI systems detach deliberately, checking out the exact commit under test rather than a branch that could move mid-pipeline. A build script that calls git rev-parse --abbrev-ref HEAD then gets the literal string HEAD — the classic reason a pipeline tags an image myapp:HEAD. Read the CI provider's ref variable instead.

The three trees

The everyday commands all move data between three snapshots. HEAD is the tree of the commit you are on. The index (staging area, cache) is a binary file at .git/index holding a flat list of every path with its blob hash, mode and cached stat data — a complete proposed tree, not a diff. The working tree is the files on disk. git status is two comparisons: HEAD against index ("Changes to be committed") and index against working tree ("Changes not staged").

Which of the three trees each command writes to
CommandHEADIndexWorking tree
git add—written—
git commitwritten (branch ref moves)——
git reset --softwritten——
git reset (mixed, the default)writtenwritten—
git reset --hardwrittenwrittenoverwritten
git restore --staged—written—
git restore——overwritten
git switch / git checkout <branch>HEAD repointedwrittenwritten (merged)
git stash—reset to HEADreset to HEAD
Moving between the three trees deliberately
CommandWhat it doesNotes
git add -pStage selected hunks: working tree to indexsafe nothing is lost; unstaged hunks stay on disk
git restore --staged pathCopy HEAD's version of a path into the indexsafe unstages without touching disk (the old reset HEAD path)
git restore pathCopy the index's version over the file on diskdestructive discards uncommitted edits with no recovery — they were never objects
git reset --soft HEAD~1Move the branch back one commit, keep index and diskcare the classic "recompose the last commit"; recover with git reset --soft ORIG_HEAD
git reset --hard origin/mainForce branch, index and disk to a refdestructive commits recoverable via reflog; uncommitted work is not
git checkout <commit> -- pathCopy one path from any commit into index and diskcare overwrites that path; prefer git restore --source
git ls-files -sPrint the index: mode, blob hash, stage, pathsafe stage numbers 1/2/3 appear only during a conflict
The one thing Git cannot give back

Everything committed is recoverable, and everything staged is recoverable — git add writes a real blob into the object database, so a badly-timed reset --hard after an add can be undone with git fsck --lost-found. Edits that were never staged have no object anywhere. git restore file, git checkout -- file and git clean -fd destroy them permanently. Editor undo history is your only remaining option, so stage early and stage often.

Packfiles, delta compression and gc

New objects land as individual zlib-compressed loose objects — fine for hundreds, disastrous for millions. Git periodically rewrites them into packfiles: .git/objects/pack/pack-*.pack with a .idx companion for random access. Inside a pack, an object may be stored as a delta against a similar object instead of in full; Git picks bases by a size-and-name heuristic across a sliding window (pack.window, default 10) and chains them up to pack.depth (default 50). Bases are chosen freely, so a newer object is frequently the base and an older one the delta.

This is why a decade of history over a 40 MB working tree fits in a 90 MB .git: a text file edited a thousand times costs one full copy plus a thousand small deltas. It is equally why binaries behave badly — a 5 MB PNG re-exported thirty times will not delta-compress and costs 150 MB forever. That is the entire argument for Git LFS.

counting objects before and after packing
$ git count-objects -vH
count: 5
size: 20.00 KiB
in-pack: 0

$ git gc
$ git count-objects -vH
count: 0
in-pack: 9
packs: 2
size-pack: 3.04 KiB

$ git rev-list --objects --all | cut -d' ' -f1 \
    | git cat-file --batch-check='%(objectname) %(objecttype) %(objectsize) %(objectsize:disk)'
31c1788c354c984050655c8ed180294eb684b759 commit 198 151
94954abda49de8615a048f8d2e64b5de848e27a1 blob 12 21
Find what is actually making the repo big

%(objectsize:disk) reports the on-disk cost inside the pack. Pipe the batch-check output through sort -k4 -n -r | head -20, then map the hashes back to paths with git rev-list --objects --all. This finds the accidentally-committed 400 MB core dump that no longer exists in any current tree.

git gc packs loose objects and refs, expires reflog entries, and prunes unreachable objects past their grace period. It fires automatically as git gc --auto, and large repositories are better served by the incremental git maintenance run. Ignore the folklore around git gc --aggressive: it recomputes every delta with a much larger window, takes hours on a big repository, and buys almost nothing on one that is already packed routinely.

Reachability, and why almost nothing is truly lost

An object is reachable if you can arrive at it from any ref — branch, tag, remote-tracking ref, stash or reflog entry — by following parent, commit-to-tree and tree-to-blob edges. Everything else is unreachable, which is not the same as deleted: prune removes only objects that are unreachable and older than gc.pruneExpire, default 2.weeks.ago. Since Git 2.44 those objects move into a cruft pack (recognisable by its .mtimes file) that records per-object timestamps, so the two-week clock survives repacking.

The reflog is the second and more useful safety net. Every ref update — commit, reset, rebase, merge, checkout, branch delete — appends a line to .git/logs/HEAD or .git/logs/refs/heads/<branch> recording the old hash, the new hash and the cause. Reflog entries count as refs for reachability, so anything the reflog names is safe from pruning. Reachable entries expire after 90 days (gc.reflogExpire), unreachable ones after 30 (gc.reflogExpireUnreachable).

recovering a branch after a bad reset
$ git reflog -n 5
31c1788 HEAD@{0}: commit (amend): Add world to the greeting
5f691f4 HEAD@{1}: commit: Add world
f310472 HEAD@{2}: commit (initial): Initial import

$ git branch rescue 5f691f4
$ git fsck --lost-found --no-progress
# dangling objects with no reflog entry are written to .git/lost-found/
Two commands that make loss permanent

git reflog expire --expire-unreachable=now --all followed by git gc --prune=now deletes the safety net and then the objects, in that order, with no confirmation. This pair circulates as a "shrink your repo" recipe and it is how teams turn a recoverable bad rebase into a real one. Related and more dangerous in production: a force-push does not delete anything on the server. Objects pushed to GitHub, GitLab or Bitbucket stay reachable by hash — often indefinitely, and visible through the pull-request and API endpoints — long after the branch is rewritten. A committed secret that was force-pushed away is still a leaked secret. Rotate the credential; do not rely on rewriting history, and open a support request to have the objects purged.

PLUMBING WORTH KNOWING
Inspecting the database directly
CommandWhat it doesNotes
git cat-file -p HEADPretty-print any object by hash or revisionsafe -t type, -s size, -e existence check for scripts
git cat-file --batch-checkStream type/size/disk-size for many objectssafe the tool for repository size forensics
git hash-object -w fileCompute a hash and write the blob into the databasesafe omit -w to compute only; --stdin reads a pipe
git rev-parse HEAD^{tree}Resolve any revision expression to a hashsafe also --git-dir, --show-toplevel, --abbrev-ref
git ls-tree -r -l HEADList a tree with modes, hashes and blob sizessafe --name-only for paths; reads history, not disk
git ls-files -sDump the index, including conflict stagessafe the only direct view of the middle tree
git show-refList every ref with its hashsafe git for-each-ref --format for scripting
git symbolic-ref HEADRead or set what HEAD points atcare setting it moves the branch pointer without touching disk
git update-ref refs/heads/x shaWrite a ref directly, bypassing porcelaincare records a reflog entry; -d deletes the ref
git fsck --unreachableVerify object integrity and list orphanssafe add --no-reflogs to see what only the reflog is holding
git count-objects -vHLoose vs packed object counts and sizessafe a rising count means gc has not run
git gc --prune=nowRepack and immediately delete unreachable objectsdestructive removes the grace period; plain git gc is what you want

Three sentences carry the rest of Part I. The object database is append-only and content-addressed, so nothing is edited and identical content is stored once. Branches, tags and HEAD are labels — small files holding a hash — so moving them is cheap and reversible. Reachability, not deletion, decides what survives, which is why the reflog and the two-week prune window make almost every accident recoverable, and why the few commands that clear the reflog deserve real caution.

G02

The everyday commands

Ninety per cent of production Git is a dozen commands used well. The engineers who never lose an afternoon to Git are not the ones who memorised more flags — they are the ones who know, at every moment, which of the three trees a command is about to write, and who leave behind a history that can be searched under incident pressure.

The working set, and the question each command answers

Treat the daily commands as answers to questions, not as verbs. status asks where am I and what have I touched. diff asks what changed, between which two trees. add moves content working tree → index; restore moves it backwards, index → working tree or HEAD → index. commit turns the index into a permanent object, switch moves HEAD, and log and show read without writing anything. Once you can name the source and destination tree of every command you type, the surprises stop.

Modern Git splits these cleanly: git switch (Git 2.23, August 2019) only ever moves between branches, git restore only ever writes files. Both were carved out of git checkout, which does both jobs and disambiguates by guessing. You still need the old spellings — every answer written before 2020 uses them, and RHEL 7 and Debian buster images ship Git older than 2.23, where git switch does not exist at all.

The split of checkout, with the legacy spelling you will still meet
IntentModern (Git 2.23+)Legacy equivalentTrees written
Move to an existing branchgit switch maingit checkout mainHEAD, index, working tree
Create and movegit switch -c feat/xgit checkout -b feat/xHEAD (index and tree unchanged)
Create from a remote branchgit switch --track origin/xgit checkout --track origin/xHEAD, index, working tree
Deliberately detachgit switch --detach v2.1.0git checkout v2.1.0HEAD, index, working tree
Return to previous branchgit switch -git checkout -HEAD, index, working tree
Discard unstaged edits to a filegit restore src/app.pygit checkout -- src/app.pyWorking tree only
Unstage a file, keep the editgit restore --staged src/app.pygit reset HEAD src/app.pyIndex only
Reset a file to HEAD entirelygit restore --staged --worktree src/app.pygit checkout HEAD -- src/app.pyIndex and working tree
Take one file from another commitgit restore --source=v2.1.0 conf/app.inigit checkout v2.1.0 -- conf/app.iniWorking tree (legacy also stages)
The ambiguity that eats a day's work

The right-hand column is why the split happened. git checkout config is genuinely ambiguous when the repository holds both a branch and a directory called config: Git prefers the branch, and where no such branch exists it silently overwrites your uncommitted edits under config/ instead. There is no recovery — working-tree content was never hashed into the object database, so there is no reflog entry, no dangling blob, nothing for git fsck to find. The same applies to git restore . and git checkout -- .. Before discarding anything wholesale, run git stash push -u; two seconds turns an unrecoverable mistake into git stash pop.

status, read in one line per file

Plain git status is a tutorial that prints hints. In a working session you want git status --short --branch (-sb): one dense line per path, plus a header showing divergence from upstream. The short format uses two columns — the first is the index state, the second the working tree state — and reading them as a pair is the entire skill. MM means you staged one version and have since edited it again, which is the classic cause of "I committed but my fix isn't in there".

Short-format status codes
CodeIndex columnWorking-tree columnWhat it means in practice
M modifiedcleanStaged and ready; a commit now captures it exactly
 McleanmodifiedEdited but not staged; a commit ignores it
MMmodifiedmodifiedStaged, then edited again — only the staged version commits
A addedcleanNew file, staged. It was untracked until git add
D  /  DdeleteddeletedRemoved from the index / removed from disk only
R renamedcleanRename inferred from content similarity, not recorded
??untrackedGit has never hashed this path; not covered by any ignore rule
!!ignoredOnly shown with --ignored; matched an ignore rule
UUboth modifiedUnresolved conflict; index holds three stages for the path

For scripts and hooks, never parse the human output. --porcelain=v1 is a stable, documented format; --porcelain=v2 --branch also reports object IDs, file modes and ahead/behind counts. Add -z for NUL-terminated records, so paths containing spaces or newlines cannot corrupt the parse.

add, and the case for staging hunks

git add -p walks each change hunk by hunk and asks whether to stage it. It is the single habit that most improves a history, because it decouples how you worked from what you ship. Real work is messy: you fix the bug, rename a badly named variable two functions up, delete a stale comment, add a log line you needed while debugging. Committed together that is a change nobody can review, revert or bisect cleanly. Staged as hunks it becomes three commits — the fix, the rename, the cleanup — and reverting the fix later does not undo the rename.

The prompt keys that matter: y stage, n skip, s split into smaller hunks, e edit the hunk by hand, d skip the rest of this file, a stage the rest of this file, q quit. When s refuses to split further, use e: delete the lines you do not want, and turn an unwanted - removal line into context by replacing the leading minus with a space. Do not touch the @@ counts; Git recalculates them. The same -p mode exists on git restore -p, git stash push -p and git commit -p.

Verify before you commit

Hunk staging without verification is worse than no staging: it is easy to stage half a change and leave that commit non-compiling, which silently breaks git bisect months later. The habit is git add -p, then git diff --staged to read exactly what will be committed, then — for anything going near a release branch — git stash push --keep-index to hide the unstaged remainder and run the tests against precisely the staged tree.

diff: three questions, three invocations

Every git diff confusion comes from not knowing which two trees are being compared. There are only three you need daily.

git diff
Working tree vs index — "what have I changed that is not staged?" Prints nothing after git add -A, even having edited a hundred files.
git diff --staged
Index vs HEAD — "what will the next commit contain?" --cached is the older synonym, identical in behaviour.
git diff HEAD
Working tree vs HEAD — "what have I changed since the last commit, staged or not?" The total, and what people mean by "the diff".

Beyond that, diff takes arbitrary endpoints. git diff main..feat/x compares the two tips; git diff main...feat/x — three dots — compares the merge base against feat/x, which is what a pull request shows and almost always what you want when reviewing a branch that has fallen behind. Getting this wrong makes a stale branch look as though it reverts everything merged into main since it forked.

Four modifiers earn their keep. --stat gives the per-file churn summary and is the right first look at any large change. --word-diff diffs by token rather than line, which turns an unreadable reflowed-prose or minified-config diff into a legible one; --word-diff-regex='[A-Za-z0-9_]+' tunes what counts as a word. -w strips whitespace-only noise after a reformat. And the separator -- limits the diff to paths: everything after it is a path, never a revision, which is how you disambiguate a file whose name matches a branch. Pathspecs take magic prefixes worth memorising — ':!*.lock' excludes generated lockfiles, ':(glob)**/*.tf' forces glob semantics across directory boundaries, ':(icase)readme*' matches case-insensitively.

commit: amend, messages, and the seven rules

git commit -m is for one-line commits and nothing else. The moment a change needs justification, drop the -m and let the editor open — a message written in an editor gets a body, a message written as a shell argument does not. Set git config --global core.editor explicitly; the default on a bare Linux host is often vi, which is how people end up committing the message :wq.

git commit --amend replaces the tip commit with a new one built from the current index. It is not an edit: as the chapter on the object model explains, the old commit still exists, unchanged, reachable through HEAD@{1} for ninety days. Use --amend --no-edit to fold a forgotten file into the previous commit, and --amend --reset-author after fixing a wrong user.email, so the author identity is rewritten and not just the committer.

Amend after push is a force-push in disguise

Amending a commit already on the remote gives it a new object ID, so your branch and origin have diverged and the next push is rejected with ! [rejected] main -> main (non-fast-forward). The fix is git push --force-with-lease, never plain --force: the lease refuses if the remote moved since your last fetch, and it is the only thing standing between you and deleting a colleague's commits. On a shared branch do not amend at all — add a new commit. The recurring incident is an engineer amending a tagged release commit, force-pushing, and leaving CI, the deploy pipeline and every other clone pointing at an object that belongs to no branch.

A commit message is documentation written at the only moment you still have the full context. The seven conventions below are near-universal in professional repositories; the first five are enforced mechanically by most linters.

01 · Subject
Under 50 charsimperative, capitalised, no full stop
02 · Blank line
Mandatorytools parse subject as everything before it
03 · Body
Why, not whatwrapped at 72 columns
04 · Trailers
Refs and sign-offFixes: PROJ-412, Co-authored-by:
  1. Separate subject from body with a blank line. Without it, git log --oneline, git shortlog, the forge UI and every changelog generator treat the whole message as one subject.
  2. Limit the subject to 50 characters. Not arbitrary: --oneline adds a hash and decorations, and GitHub truncates at 72. Target 50, treat 72 as the ceiling.
  3. Capitalise the subject line. Skipped by teams using Conventional Commits, which requires a lower-case type prefix — pick one convention and lint it.
  4. Do not end the subject with a full stop. It is a title, and the character is wasted.
  5. Use the imperative mood. "Fix null deref in the session cache", not "Fixed". The test: the subject completes "If applied, this commit will…". Git's own generated messages — Merge branch, Revert — are imperative, so yours read consistently beside them.
  6. Wrap the body at 72 columns. Git does not wrap for you and indents the body four spaces when displaying, so 72 keeps it inside 80.
  7. Explain why, not what. The diff states what changed. The body records the intent, the alternatives rejected, the constraint that forced an ugly solution, and the ticket. This is the paragraph the on-call engineer reads at 03:00 through git blame.

Conventional Commits: the trade

Conventional Commits standardises the subject as type(scope): description — feat, fix, docs, refactor, perf, test, build, ci, chore — with a breaking change marked by a ! before the colon or a BREAKING CHANGE: trailer.

What you buy is mechanisation. semantic-release, release-please and git-cliff read those prefixes and derive the next version automatically — fix bumps the patch, feat the minor, a breaking marker the major — so the changelog stops being a file somebody forgets to edit and becomes a build artefact. You also get a filterable history: git log --oneline --grep '^fix' is a genuine incident tool.

What you pay is friction and honesty. The type is a guess made at authoring time, and mislabelling a breaking change as feat ships a silently incompatible minor release to every consumer. Contributors bikeshed over chore versus build. Enforcement needs commitlint in a commit-msg hook and a CI check, because a hook alone is bypassed with --no-verify. Adopt it if you publish a versioned artefact other teams consume; skip it if you deploy one service from main and nobody reads the changelog.

A realistic session

one bug fix, split into two honest commits
$ git switch -c fix/session-null-deref

$ git status -sb

$ git diff --stat

    Guard against evicted cache entries

    resolve() assumed CACHE.get() always returns an entry, but the LRU
    evicts under memory pressure, so a hot-path request could raise
    AttributeError and 500. Returning None re-runs the DB lookup, which
    is the pre-cache behaviour.

Would remove scratch.py
$ git clean -fd

log as a forensic tool

During an incident, git log stops being a list and becomes a search engine over every line of code your team has ever written. Four capabilities do the heavy lifting.

Shape. git log --oneline --graph --decorate --all is the canonical topology view: every ref, drawn as a graph, with branch and tag labels. Add --simplify-by-decoration on a huge repository to show only commits carrying a ref.

Content search. -S is the pickaxe: git log -S'DEFAULT_TIMEOUT' --oneline lists only commits where the number of occurrences of that string changed — where it was introduced or removed — skipping every commit that merely moved the line around. -G takes a regular expression and matches any commit whose diff text contains it, moves included. Use -S for "when did this constant appear", -G for "which commits ever touched a line matching this". Add --pickaxe-regex to make -S take a regex too, and --all to search every ref rather than the current branch.

Scope. --since='2 weeks ago' and --until filter on commit date, which a rebase resets — Git 2.37+ adds --since-as-filter when history-order truncation bites. --author='priya' matches a substring of the author field. --grep searches messages, and multiple --grep terms are OR-ed unless you add --all-match. Anything after -- is a pathspec, so git log --oneline --since='1 month ago' -- infra/ ':!infra/generated' is a precise question. --follow traces one file across renames: exactly one path, and a similarity heuristic, so a rename plus a heavy rewrite still ends the trail.

Format. --pretty=format: makes log scriptable — %h abbreviated hash, %an author, %ad author date (with --date=short or --date=relative), %s subject, %d decorations, %C(auto) context-sensitive colour. A permanent alias pays for itself:

ini · ~/.gitconfig
[alias]
    lg  = log --graph --decorate --date=short \
          --pretty=format:'%C(auto)%h %C(blue)%ad %C(green)%an%C(auto)%d %Creset%s'
    st  = status --short --branch
    last = show --stat --oneline HEAD
    # who last touched every line of a file, oldest change first
    churn = !git log --format=%an -- "$1" | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn #
Interview: "a config value changed and broke prod — find the commit"

The weak answer is git blame, which shows only the last commit to touch each line, so a later reformat, lint autofix or file move buries the real change. The strong answer is a ladder. Start with git log -S'<the value>' --oneline --all -- path/to/config to find where the string entered or left the tree, across every ref. If it was moved rather than introduced, switch to -G; if the file was renamed, add --follow or drop the pathspec. When blame really is the right tool, use git blame -w -C -C -L 40,60 path — -w ignores whitespace and repeated -C detects lines copied from other files. And when the bad commit is somewhere in a range but not identifiable by content, stop searching and run git bisect run ./scripts/check.sh, which finds it in log₂(n) builds. Naming that escalation — pickaxe, then blame with copy detection, then bisect — separates someone who has debugged production history from someone who has read the manual.

rm, mv, and clean

git rm file deletes from both the working tree and the index. git rm --cached file removes it from the index only, leaving it on disk as untracked — the way to stop tracking a file that should never have been committed. git mv old new is exactly mv plus git rm plus git add: no rename is recorded, it is inferred later from content similarity, so a plain mv and git add -A produce an identical commit.

git clean deletes untracked files, and it is the one command in the daily set with no recovery path at all — those files were never hashed, so no reflog, no fsck --lost-found, nothing. Always dry-run: git clean -nd lists what -fd would delete. -x additionally removes ignored files (node_modules, .env, local TLS keys) and -X removes only ignored files. Git refuses to clean without -f or -i unless clean.requireForce is false; leave that default alone.

Ignoring: tracked is not the same as untracked

The rule that trips everyone: ignore patterns apply only to untracked files. Once a path is in the index, .gitignore has no effect on it — Git keeps reporting and committing changes forever, so adding .env to .gitignore after committing it does precisely nothing. The fix is git rm --cached .env && git commit, and that removes it going forward only: the secret remains in every historical commit, every clone, and the forge's reflogs and pull-request refs. Rotate the credential. Rewriting history with git filter-repo is cleanup, not remediation.

Rules are read in increasing precedence from core.excludesFile (a personal global list — editor swap files and OS cruft, not project rules), .git/info/exclude (repository-local, uncommitted), and the .gitignore files in the tree, where a deeper file beats a shallower one and a later line beats an earlier one. A trailing / matches directories only; a pattern with a slash anywhere but the end is anchored to the directory holding the .gitignore; ! negates; **/ matches across levels. The hard limit: you cannot re-include a file whose parent directory is excluded, because Git never descends into an excluded directory — build/ followed by !build/keep.txt silently fails, so exclude the contents instead with build/*. When you cannot see why a file is ignored, git check-ignore -v path prints the file, line number and pattern responsible.

.gitattributes: line endings, diffs and merges

.gitattributes is the committed, per-path counterpart to configuration. Unlike core.autocrlf — a per-machine setting every contributor must remember to set — it applies to everyone who clones. Any cross-platform repository should have one.

plaintext · .gitattributes
# Normalise: store LF in the repository, check out native line endings
*               text=auto

# Files that must keep one specific ending regardless of platform
*.sh            text eol=lf
*.bat           text eol=crlf
Dockerfile      text eol=lf

# Never touch these, never try to diff them as text
*.png           binary
*.pdf           binary
*.woff2         binary

# Generated files: collapse in review, exclude from language stats
package-lock.json  -diff linguist-generated=true
*.tfstate          -diff -merge

# Better hunk headers: show the enclosing function/class in @@ lines
*.py            diff=python
*.md            diff=markdown
*.go            diff=golang

# Append-only files: keep both sides instead of conflicting
CHANGELOG.md    merge=union

# Binary project files no human can merge: take one side, then re-generate
*.pbxproj       merge=binary -diff

text=auto tells Git to detect text files and store them with LF internally, converting on checkout. Introducing it does not retroactively fix files already committed with CRLF — run git add --renormalize . once and commit the result, or the next Windows contributor produces a diff touching every line of every file. -diff marks a path uninteresting, so git diff prints only Binary files differ: exactly what you want for lockfiles. diff=python selects a built-in hunk-header driver so the @@ line names the enclosing function, and a custom textconv driver — git config diff.exif.textconv 'exiftool' — diffs binary formats through a converter. merge=union resolves conflicts by concatenating both sides: correct for changelogs, catastrophic for source code. A merge=ours driver must be registered once per machine with git config merge.ours.driver true, or Git falls back silently.

The daily command table

The working set, with risk markers

Three habits carry the rest of Part I. Read git status -sb as two columns, so you always know what will actually be committed. Stage in hunks and read git diff --staged before every commit, so the history describes the change rather than the afternoon. And write the body: a subject tells the reviewer what you did, but only the body tells the on-call engineer, eighteen months later, why you were right to.

G03

Branching & merging

A branch is forty-one bytes on disk, which is why creating one costs nothing and why deleting one can cost you an afternoon. Merging is not magic either: it is a three-way comparison against a common ancestor, and once you can name that ancestor, conflicts stop being random and start being predictable.

A branch is a movable pointer

In most version control systems a branch is a copy: of a directory, of a server-side path, of the whole tree. In Git a branch is a file under .git/refs/heads/ containing a forty-character hash and a newline. That is the entire data structure. HEAD is a second file containing the text ref: refs/heads/main — a symbolic ref that points at a branch rather than at a commit.

Three consequences follow, and they explain nearly everything about how Git feels to use.

  • Creating a branch is instant and free. No files are copied, no history is duplicated. You wrote forty-one bytes. A repository with two hundred branches is not meaningfully larger than one with two.
  • Committing moves the pointer. git commit writes a new commit object whose parent is the current tip, then rewrites the branch file to hold the new hash. The branch does not "contain" commits; it points at one, and reachability does the rest.
  • Deleting a branch deletes only the pointer. The commits remain in the object database until garbage collection decides they are unreachable — by default thirty days for objects that were never reachable from any ref, ninety days for anything still in a reflog.

Refs get packed into .git/packed-refs when there are many of them or after git gc, so a missing file under refs/heads/ does not mean a missing branch. Never read these files in scripts; use git rev-parse, git symbolic-ref and git for-each-ref, which read both locations.

what a branch actually is
$ cat .git/HEAD
ref: refs/heads/main
$ cat .git/refs/heads/main
e42f0091a7c3b5d84f2ce7719b0a4d6f8c11ae23
$ wc -c .git/refs/heads/main
      41 .git/refs/heads/main
$ git switch -c feat/idempotent-orders
Switched to a new branch 'feat/idempotent-orders'
$ git rev-parse HEAD feat/idempotent-orders main
e42f0091a7c3b5d84f2ce7719b0a4d6f8c11ae23
e42f0091a7c3b5d84f2ce7719b0a4d6f8c11ae23
e42f0091a7c3b5d84f2ce7719b0a4d6f8c11ae23
# three names, one commit, nothing copied

switch, list, rename, delete

git switch (Git 2.23, 2019) moves HEAD; git restore writes files. The older git checkout did both, which is why git checkout foo silently discarded your edits to a file named foo whenever no branch called foo existed. Use switch for anything that moves a pointer.

git switch -c feat/x creates at HEAD and moves there; add a start point — git switch -c hotfix/tls origin/main — to branch from somewhere else, which is the correct way to start a hotfix while you happen to be sitting on a feature branch. git switch -C name resets an existing branch to the start point instead of refusing, so it is care, not safe. git switch - returns to the previous branch, exactly like cd -, and is the single highest-value keystroke in this chapter. git switch --detach abc123 makes detached HEAD an explicit request rather than an accident.

Listing deserves flags. Bare git branch gives you names in alphabetical order, which is useless once you have thirty. git branch -vv gives you name, tip hash, upstream and ahead/behind in one line. Sort by recency permanently with git config --global branch.sort -committerdate (Git 2.19+), so the branch you touched this morning is at the top rather than buried under archive/.

Renaming is git branch -m old new, or git branch -m new to rename the branch you are on. Renaming is local only: the remote still has the old name, so a rename is followed by git push origin -u new and git push origin --delete old, and if it is the default branch you must also change the default on the hosting side and run git remote set-head origin -a so origin/HEAD stops pointing at a branch that no longer exists.

What -d refuses and what -D risks

git branch -d name deletes the branch only if its tip is reachable from its upstream or from the current HEAD — that is, only if the work is already somewhere else. git branch -D name skips that check. The commits are not destroyed: they become unreachable objects, and the deletion message prints the hash you need.

The bookkeeping that matters: deleting a branch also deletes its reflog at .git/logs/refs/heads/name. Recovery is therefore not git reflog show name — that file is gone — but either the hash printed at deletion time, the HEAD reflog if you were ever on the branch (git reflog then git branch name abc123), or, as a last resort, git fsck --unreachable --no-reflogs to list dangling commits. Do that within thirty days; gc.pruneExpire defaults to two weeks and gc.reflogExpireUnreachable to thirty days, after which an aggressive gc really does remove the objects.

The branch cleanup one-liner that eats work

The popular incantation git branch --merged | grep -v main | xargs git branch -d has bitten every team that has run it. --merged with no argument means "merged into whatever HEAD is right now", so running it while sitting on a feature branch marks every branch merged into that as disposable. Always name the target explicitly — git branch --merged origin/main — and prefer pruning by upstream state: git fetch --prune then git branch -vv | awk '/: gone\]/ {print $1}' | xargs -r git branch -d, which deletes only branches whose remote counterpart the server has already removed, and which still uses -d so anything unmerged survives.

Tracking branches and upstream configuration

"Upstream" is two config keys, nothing more. branch.<name>.remote names the remote and branch.<name>.merge names the ref on it. Together they let git status print ahead/behind, let bare git pull and git push know where to go, and give you the shorthand @{upstream} (or @{u}) in every revision argument: git log @{u}..HEAD is "what I have that the server does not".

Remote-tracking refs such as origin/main live under refs/remotes/ and are a local cache of what the remote looked like at your last fetch. They are not the remote and they do not update themselves. Every "GitHub says my branch is behind but Git says it is up to date" ticket is a stale cache: run git fetch --prune first, always.

Set upstream with git push -u origin feat/x on the first push, or retroactively with git branch --set-upstream-to=origin/feat/x. Better, set it once and forget it: git config --global push.autoSetupRemote true (Git 2.37+) makes a bare git push on a new branch create the remote branch and set upstream, which removes the most common friction point in a fork-free workflow. Keep push.default at its default of simple, which refuses to push when the local and remote names differ.

branch -vv, read as a status report
$ git fetch --prune
From github.com:acme/checkout
 - [deleted]         (none)     -> origin/feat/retry-budget
$ git branch -vv
* feat/idempotent-orders 9a1c4e2 [origin/feat/idempotent-orders: ahead 2] add key index
  feat/retry-budget      7f2ad31 [origin/feat/retry-budget: gone] widen pool
  main                   e42f009 [origin/main: behind 4] chore: bump undici
  spike/no-upstream      1b30c88 raw notes, never pushed
$ git branch -d feat/retry-budget
Deleted branch feat/retry-budget (was 7f2ad31).
# the hash in that message is the whole recovery path: git branch feat/retry-budget 7f2ad31

How a merge is computed

A merge takes your current HEAD and one or more other commits and produces a tree. Which algorithm runs depends entirely on the shape of the history.

Merge base
The best common ancestor of the two tips, found by git merge-base A B. Everything about a merge follows from it: the two diffs Git compares are base → ours and base → theirs. When history has criss-cross merges there may be several bases (git merge-base --all); the default strategy merges those bases into a synthetic virtual base and recurses.
Fast-forward
The merge base is your current tip: you have done nothing the other side has not. There is nothing to compute, so Git just moves the branch pointer forward. No merge commit exists, and afterwards nothing in history records that a branch was ever involved.
Three-way merge
Both sides have commits the other lacks. Git applies both diffs against the base; hunks that only one side touched apply cleanly, hunks both sides touched in different ways are conflicts. The result is a commit with two parents — first parent your branch, second parent the one you merged.
Octopus
More than two heads at once: git merge topic-a topic-b topic-c selects octopus automatically and produces one commit with four parents. It refuses outright if any pair conflicts, which is the point — it exists for batching known-independent topic branches, not for resolving anything.
FAST-FORWARD — main did nothing since the branch point, so the label just slides forward before after · git merge feat * 9a1c4e2 (feat) * 9a1c4e2 (HEAD -> main, feat) | | * 3c7bb10 * 3c7bb10 | | * e42f009 (HEAD -> main) * e42f009 THREE-WAY — both sides moved, so a commit with two parents is created before after · git merge feat * 9a1c4e2 (feat) * 5d0e8b4 (HEAD -> main) Merge branch 'feat' | * 7f2ad31 (HEAD -> main) |\ |/ | * 9a1c4e2 (feat) * e42f009 * | 7f2ad31 |/ * e42f009 <- merge base

--no-ff, --ff-only and --squash

Git's default is --ff: fast-forward when possible, three-way otherwise. That default silently changes the shape of your history depending on whether a colleague happened to push while you were working, which is a poor property for something you will read during an incident.

--no-ff forces a merge commit even when a fast-forward was available. It is worth the extra commit when the merge is the record that a feature existed as a unit: the merge commit's second-parent side is a bracket around exactly those commits, so git log --first-parent main reads as one line per shipped change, git revert -m 1 <merge> backs the whole feature out in one move, and git log feat --not main still enumerates what the feature contained. It is noise when the "feature" is a one-commit typo fix, and it is actively harmful as a default on a branch that many people merge into, where you end up with a merge bubble per person per day and a graph nobody can read.

--ff-only refuses anything that is not a fast-forward. Set git config --global pull.ff only immediately: it converts the single most common accidental history mutation — git pull quietly building a merge commit because someone pushed while you were reviewing — into an error message you can act on.

--squash is not a merge at all. It computes the same tree as a merge and leaves it staged, but records no second parent and creates no commit; you then git commit yourself. The result is one commit on the target with no link to the source branch, which is why the next merge from the same branch recomputes the same merge base and re-offers every change you already squashed. Squash-merge on the host is the same operation, and the same rule applies: delete the source branch immediately after squashing, and never continue working on it.

Which merge mode, and what it leaves behind
ModeParentsHistory reads asUse it when
--ff (default)None createdA linear run of the feature's own commitsTrivial, single-commit changes; personal branches
--no-ffTwoOne first-parent line per feature, expandableRelease branches, long-lived integration, anything you may need to revert as a unit
--ff-onlyNone createdStrictly linear, or it errorspull, always; and any branch where merge commits are banned by policy
--squashOne (no link to source)One commit, source branch invisibleNoisy work-in-progress branches. Delete the source afterwards.

Strategies, and the difference that costs people data

Since Git 2.34 the default strategy is ort ("ostensibly recursive's twin"), a rewrite of the old recursive. It is markedly faster on large trees because it does not write intermediate indexes and trees to disk, and it handles directory renames and rename-detection correctly in cases where recursive produced surprising results. You will still see -s recursive in old runbooks; it exists, it is slower, and there is no reason to ask for it.

Strategy options are passed with -X and tune how ort behaves. Strategy itself is -s. Confusing the two is the single most expensive mistake in this chapter.

-X ours — a conflict resolution policy

Merges normally. Every hunk that applies cleanly from either side is taken. Only where the two sides conflict does it pick our version without asking. The other branch's non-conflicting work is fully present in the result.

-s ours — a lie recorded in the graph

Ignores the other side entirely. The resulting tree is bit-for-bit your current tree; not one line from the merged branch survives. The commit still gets a second parent, so history claims the branch was merged and every future merge-base agrees. Its legitimate use is deliberately marking a branch as superseded.

How a security fix disappears for six weeks

A release branch has an urgent patch. Someone merges it into main, hits forty conflicts from an unrelated refactor, and reaches for a flag they half-remember: git merge -s ours release/4.2. It completes with no conflicts, the diff against main is empty, CI is green, the pull request shows nothing to review — and the patch is not in main. Worse, main and release/4.2 now share a merge base at that commit, so every subsequent merge agrees the work is already integrated and will never re-offer it. Detection is git log --oneline release/4.2 --not main returning empty when it should not, or git diff main release/4.2 -- path/to/fix still showing the change. Recovery is git revert -m 1 on the bogus merge and merging again properly, or cherry-picking the specific commits. The habit that prevents it: after any merge you did not fully read, run git diff HEAD@{1} HEAD --stat and confirm the file count is not zero.

The other options earn their keep in specific situations. -X theirs is the mirror of -X ours and is the right tool for regenerated artefacts — lockfiles, snapshots, compiled schemas — where one side is authoritative and the merge result will be rebuilt anyway. -X ignore-space-change (and the blunter -X ignore-all-space) makes a merge across a whitespace-only reformat tractable instead of a conflict on every line; it is also how you survive merging a branch that predates a Prettier or gofmt rollout. -X renormalize re-applies .gitattributes line-ending rules to both sides before comparing, which is the fix for a merge that conflicts on every single line of a file after a CRLF change. -X find-renames=40% loosens rename detection when a file was both moved and heavily edited. -X diff-algorithm=histogram often produces smaller, better-aligned conflict regions in code with repeated block structure.

Conflict anatomy, and why zdiff3 changes everything

A conflict is a region where both sides changed the same lines differently. Git writes all the candidate content into the file, bracketed by markers, and leaves the path at stage 1/2/3 in the index instead of stage 0 — which is what git status -sb reports as UU. The default marker style, merge, shows two sections: ours, then theirs. It tells you what the two sides say and nothing at all about what they started from, so you are left guessing which side changed what.

Set git config --global merge.conflictStyle zdiff3 (Git 2.35+; diff3 on older versions) and the middle section appears: the merge base. Now the conflict is a complete statement — this is what the line was, this is what we did to it, this is what they did to it — and resolution becomes a decision rather than a reconstruction. zdiff3 additionally hoists lines that are identical at the start and end of all three sides out of the conflict region, so the markers wrap only the part that genuinely disagrees. There is no downside; the only reason it is not the default is compatibility with tools that parse the two-section form.

plaintext · src/api/orders.ts, merge.conflictStyle = zdiff3
<<<<<<< HEAD                      <- ours: the branch you are merging into
  timeoutMs: 15_000,
  retries: 3,
||||||| e42f009                      <- the merge base: what both sides started from
  timeoutMs: 5_000,
  retries: 3,
=======
  timeoutMs: 5_000,
  retries: 5,
>>>>>>> feat/idempotent-orders     <- theirs: the branch named in the merge command

Read against the base, that conflict resolves in five seconds: we raised the timeout, they raised the retries, neither touched the other's line, and the answer is timeoutMs: 15_000 with retries: 5. Without the middle section you would have had to open the log to work out which number moved. Note also that ours and theirs are defined by the operation, not by ownership: during a rebase or a cherry-pick the roles invert, because Git is replaying your commits on top of their branch, so "ours" is the upstream you are landing on. Checking git status, which names the operation in progress, is faster than reasoning about it.

Resolving, restarting, aborting

List the conflicted paths precisely with git diff --name-only --diff-filter=U — never by grepping for markers, which finds them in test fixtures and documentation too. Then choose a resolution route per file.

For a file where one side is simply correct — a lockfile, a generated client, a minified bundle — git checkout --ours -- path or git checkout --theirs -- path takes that stage wholesale (git restore --ours -- path is the modern spelling). Neither merges anything; they discard the other side of that file completely, so never use them on hand-written source without reading the diff first. For real code, resolve in an editor or run git mergetool, which invokes the three-pane tool configured in merge.tool (vimdiff, meld, nvim, vscode, kdiff3) with base, ours and theirs loaded. Set git config --global mergetool.keepBackup false unless you enjoy committing .orig files.

If you make a mess of a file mid-resolution, you do not have to abort the whole merge: git checkout --merge -- path (or git restore --merge -- path) regenerates the conflicted file from the three stages still sitting in the index, discarding your partial edits. git checkout --conflict=zdiff3 -- path does the same and rewrites the markers in the style you name, which is the fastest way to add the base section to a conflict you opened before you knew about zdiff3.

To finish, git add each resolved file — that collapses stages 1/2/3 back to stage 0 — then git merge --continue (which opens the message editor) or plain git commit. To walk away, git merge --abort restores the working tree and index to the pre-merge state; it needs MERGE_HEAD, so it only works before you commit. If you have already committed a merge you regret, git reset --hard ORIG_HEAD undoes it — ORIG_HEAD is written by every merge, rebase and reset for exactly this purpose — and if it is already pushed and shared, git revert -m 1 <merge-sha> is the non-rewriting fix.

a conflict, start to finish
$ git switch main && git merge --no-ff feat/idempotent-orders
Auto-merging src/api/orders.ts
CONFLICT (content): Merge conflict in src/api/orders.ts
Auto-merging pnpm-lock.yaml
CONFLICT (content): Merge conflict in pnpm-lock.yaml
Automatic merge failed; fix conflicts and then commit the result.
$ git diff --name-only --diff-filter=U
pnpm-lock.yaml
src/api/orders.ts
$ git checkout --theirs -- pnpm-lock.yaml && pnpm install --lockfile-only
# regenerated artefact: take one side, then rebuild it. Never hand-merge a lockfile.
$ git mergetool src/api/orders.ts
$ git add pnpm-lock.yaml src/api/orders.ts
$ git diff --cached --stat
 pnpm-lock.yaml     |  84 ++++++---
 src/api/orders.ts  |  31 +++--
# non-zero, so the merge is not a silent no-op
$ git merge --continue
[main 5d0e8b4] Merge branch 'feat/idempotent-orders'
# wrong result? git reset --hard ORIG_HEAD

rerere: stop resolving the same conflict

Reuse recorded resolution. With rerere.enabled true, Git records the pre-image and post-image of every conflict you resolve into .git/rr-cache/. The next time it meets a conflict with an identical pre-image, it replays your resolution automatically. This is transformative in exactly one situation, and it is a common one: a long-lived branch that you repeatedly rebase or repeatedly merge main into, where the same three conflicts reappear every single time.

Add rerere.autoupdate true so replayed resolutions are staged rather than merely written to the working tree. git rerere status lists paths it is tracking, git rerere diff shows what it changed, and git rerere forget path deletes a recorded resolution — the command you need the moment you realise the resolution you taught it was wrong. The cache is local and per-repository; it is never pushed, expires after sixty days for resolved entries and fifteen for unresolved (gc.rerereResolved, gc.rerereUnresolved), and is safe to delete outright.

Trust, then verify

rerere resolves silently and prints only a one-line Resolved 'path' using previous resolution. If the surrounding code has moved on since you taught it, a stale resolution can reintroduce a deleted line or drop a new one and you will merge it without ever seeing a conflict. Read git diff --cached before completing any merge where rerere spoke up. Enable it globally anyway — the time it saves on a two-week branch is measured in hours.

Interview · merge base, in three questions

"Your feature branch shows a conflict in a file you never touched. Why?" The answer is that conflicts are computed against the merge base, not against the tip: if main changed that file since you branched and your branch also carries a change to it from an earlier rebase, cherry-pick or squash-merge that duplicated the work, both diffs from the base touch the same lines. The follow-ups separate people who have read the model from people who have memorised commands: "what does git merge-base main HEAD return, and what happens if there are two answers?" — ort merges the multiple bases into a virtual base and recurses. And "you squash-merged this branch to main last week and are now merging it again — what does Git think?" — the squash created no second parent, so the merge base is still the original branch point and every already-shipped change is re-offered, usually as conflicts against itself. That is the real reason squash-merged branches must be deleted, not the tidiness of the branch list.

Branches: create, inspect, retire
CommandWhat it doesNotes

Merging and conflict resolution

ini · ~/.gitconfig — the branching and merging defaults worth having
[merge]
	conflictStyle = zdiff3   ; show the merge base inside conflict markers (2.35+)
[pull]
	ff = only                ; never build a merge commit behind your back
[push]
	default = simple
	autoSetupRemote = true   ; bare `git push` creates and tracks the branch (2.37+)
[branch]
	sort = -committerdate    ; most recently touched branch first
[rerere]
	enabled = true
	autoupdate = true
[mergetool]
	keepBackup = false
[fetch]
	prune = true             ; delete origin/* refs the server no longer has

Set those seven blocks once and the remaining friction in day-to-day branching is real disagreement about code, which is the only kind worth your attention. Rebasing, cherry-picking and the rest of history rewriting are the subject of their own chapter; everything there assumes the merge-base model established here.

G04

Rebase, cherry-pick & rewriting history

Rebase never moves a commit. It replays each commit's diff onto a different parent and writes brand-new objects with brand-new hashes, leaving the originals stranded in the object database. Every rule, every trap and every recovery path in this chapter is a consequence of that single fact.

The model: replay, not relocation

A commit's SHA is a hash over its tree, its parent list, the author and committer identities, both timestamps and the message. Parentage is inside the hash, so a commit cannot be handed a new parent — Git can only build a new commit that resembles the old one and points elsewhere. That is the whole of rebase: walk the range, compute each patch, apply it on top of the growing new base, commit the result. The originals are not deleted; they merely stop being reachable and sit in the object database until git gc expires them, which is why the reflog can always bring them back.

Two things follow immediately. First, the committer timestamp changes on every replayed commit even when the content is identical, so the new hashes differ even for a rebase that resolves no conflicts. Second, because each patch is applied in turn, you can hit the same conflict repeatedly — once per replayed commit — where a merge would have asked you once. That is not a bug; it is the price of pretending the work was written against the newer base.

BEFORE AFTER git switch feature && git rebase main * 9c41de add retry budget feature * 04f7ab add retry budget feature * 77b21a extract backoff helper * e2d5c1 extract backoff helper | * 1f9a03 bump client library main * 1f9a03 bump client library main |/ * 3b7a19 * 3b7a19 merge base * 0a11c7 * 0a11c7 9c41de and 77b21a still exist, unreferenced, reachable only through the reflog until gc
Upstream
In git rebase <upstream>, the commit whose history is excluded. The replayed range is <upstream>..HEAD.
Onto
The commit the replayed range is stacked on. Defaults to <upstream>; --onto decouples the two.
Backend
Since Git 2.26 rebase uses the merge backend (the sequencer) by default rather than git am. It handles renames and conflicts far better; rebase.backend=apply restores the old one.
Todo list
The plain-text script at .git/rebase-merge/git-rebase-todo that the sequencer executes line by line. Interactive rebase is nothing more than letting you edit that file first.

The golden rule, stated precisely

The rule is never rewrite commits that other people have based work on. It is not "never rebase main", and it is not "never force-push". The failure mode is specific: when you replace commit 9c41de with 04f7ab, anyone whose branch has 9c41de as an ancestor now holds work whose base no longer exists upstream. Their next git pull sees two diverged histories, merges them, and both copies of every commit reappear on the shared branch — the duplicate-commit history that shows up in every post-incident log review.

The nuance that matters day to day: a personal feature branch is fine to rebase and force-push even after it has been pushed, because "pushed" is not the same as "depended on". A branch nobody else has checked out, with no branches stacked on it, is yours to rewrite as often as you like. The test is not visibility, it is dependency. Ask: has anyone branched from this, or is a long-lived integration branch merged from it? If yes, the answer is git revert, not git rebase.

The shared-branch rewrite

Rebasing a branch that a colleague has already branched from is the most common self-inflicted Git incident. Their commits now have a parent that only exists in their clone; when they push, the review UI shows your commits twice with different hashes, and a "helpful" merge later re-lands the pre-rebase versions of files you had already fixed. Recovery is manual: everyone with a dependent branch runs git rebase --onto <new-base> <old-base> <their-branch> using the pre-rewrite hash from git reflog. Protect shared branches at the server instead — GitHub's "Do not allow force pushes" makes the rule mechanical.

Interactive rebase in full

Interactive rebase opens the todo list in your editor before the sequencer runs it. One line per commit, oldest at the top — the opposite order to git log, which catches people out. Deleting a line drops the commit; reordering lines reorders the replay. The verbs:

Todo-list verbs (git rebase -i)
VerbEffectUse it when
pickReplay the commit unchanged.Default for every line.
rewordReplay it, then open the editor for the message only.Fixing a typo or adding a ticket reference.
editReplay it, then stop with the commit applied so you can amend the content.Splitting a commit, or removing a debug line that shipped mid-branch.
squashFold into the previous commit, opening an editor with both messages concatenated.Combining commits whose messages both say something.
fixupFold into the previous commit and discard this message.The overwhelmingly common case: "fix review comment", "typo", "oops".
dropDiscard the commit entirely.Removing an experiment. Same as deleting the line, but explicit and auditable.
execRun a shell command after that commit is applied; a non-zero exit stops the rebase.exec go build ./... after every commit, so bisect stays meaningful.
breakStop at this point with a clean tree, awaiting --continue. (Git 2.20+)Inspecting or testing at a chosen mid-branch state.

Two more verbs appear only with --rebase-merges: label/reset to mark and return to points in the graph, and merge to recreate a merge commit. That flag replaced --preserve-merges, which was deprecated in 2.21 and removed in 2.35 — if a script still passes it, it now fails outright.

cleaning up a six-commit branch before review
$ git log --oneline main..HEAD
9c41de add retry budget to http client
3ea0b7 fix lint
b18c4f wip debugging, remove me
d5f210 extract backoff helper
77b21a add timeouts module
e2d5c1 typo in readme

$ git rebase -i main
# the todo buffer, oldest first -- edit the verbs, not the hashes
pick   e2d5c1 typo in readme
pick   77b21a add timeouts module
fixup  d5f210 extract backoff helper
drop   b18c4f wip debugging, remove me
fixup  3ea0b7 fix lint
reword 9c41de add retry budget to http client
exec   go test ./internal/http/...

error: could not apply 3ea0b7... fix lint
hint: Resolve all conflicts manually, mark them resolved with
hint: "git add/rm <pathspec>", then run "git rebase --continue".

$ git status --short
UU internal/http/client.go

$ git diff --name-only --diff-filter=U | xargs $EDITOR
$ git add internal/http/client.go
$ git rebase --continue
Executing: go test ./internal/http/...
ok      acme/internal/http      0.412s
Successfully rebased and updated refs/heads/feature.

$ git log --oneline main..HEAD
04f7ab feat(http): add per-request retry budget (PAY-812)
a90cc3 add timeouts module
7be004 typo in readme

If the rebase goes wrong at any point, git rebase --abort restores the branch and the working tree exactly as they were. Once it has finished, ORIG_HEAD and the branch reflog still name the pre-rebase tip: git reset --hard ORIG_HEAD or git reset --hard feature@{1}. Nothing is lost until gc expires unreachable objects, which defaults to 90 days for reflog-named commits and 14 days for the rest.

Two settings that pay for themselves

git config --global rerere.enabled true records how you resolved each conflict hunk and replays it automatically the next time the same hunk appears — during a long rebase that means solving each conflict once instead of once per commit. rebase.missingCommitsCheck = error makes Git refuse a todo list where you deleted a line by accident rather than writing drop. Pair both with merge.conflictstyle = zdiff3, which shows the merge base in the conflict markers.

--autosquash: a clean history without editing a todo list

Editing todo lists by hand does not scale to a branch that survives three rounds of review. The disciplined alternative is to record the intent at commit time. git commit --fixup=<sha> writes a commit whose subject is fixup! <original subject>; --squash=<sha> does the same with squash!. When you later rebase with --autosquash, Git reorders those commits to sit directly beneath their targets and pre-sets the verb to fixup or squash. The todo list arrives already correct.

Set rebase.autoSquash = true so you never have to remember the flag. To let it run with no editor at all, GIT_SEQUENCE_EDITOR=true git rebase -i --autosquash main — that portable trick accepts the generated todo list untouched and works on every Git old enough to have the flag. Git 2.32 added --fixup=amend:<sha> (fold in the change and rewrite that commit's message) and the fixup -C verb, which keeps the fixup's message instead of the original's.

review round two: three fixups, one squash, no manual editing
$ git log --oneline main..HEAD
a90cc3 add timeouts module
04f7ab feat(http): add per-request retry budget (PAY-812)

$ git add internal/http/client.go
$ git commit --fixup=04f7ab
[feature 6d1e77] fixup! feat(http): add per-request retry budget (PAY-812)

$ git add internal/http/timeouts.go
$ git commit --fixup=a90cc3
[feature c02b48] fixup! add timeouts module

$ git rebase -i --autosquash main
# generated todo -- nothing to edit, just save and quit
pick   04f7ab feat(http): add per-request retry budget (PAY-812)
fixup  6d1e77 fixup! feat(http): add per-request retry budget (PAY-812)
pick   a90cc3 add timeouts module
fixup  c02b48 fixup! add timeouts module
Successfully rebased and updated refs/heads/feature.

$ git range-diff main @{1} HEAD
1:  04f7ab ! 1:  b7715c feat(http): add per-request retry budget (PAY-812)
    +    if budget.Remaining() == 0 {
    +        return ErrRetryBudgetExhausted
# range-diff proves the rebase changed only what you intended

git range-diff (Git 2.19+) compares two ranges of commits and shows a diff of diffs. Run it after every non-trivial rebase against @{1} — the pre-rebase tip — and a botched conflict resolution that silently dropped a hunk becomes visible in seconds instead of at deploy time.

--onto: moving a branch off the wrong base

git rebase --onto <newbase> <upstream> <branch> is the one form worth memorising because it is the only way out of "I branched from the wrong place". Read it as: take the commits in <upstream>..<branch> and replay them on <newbase>. The <upstream> argument here does nothing but define which commits to exclude.

git rebase --onto main spike topic replay only spike..topic BEFORE AFTER * c1a2b3 report: csv export topic * 7be004 report: csv export topic * b0d19f report: totals * 41cc8d report: totals * 5f2ee1 spike: throwaway parser * 1f9a03 bump client library main * 33ab7c spike: scratch schema spike * a1c3f2 * a1c3f2 main spike is untouched -- still two commits, still branched from a1c3f2

The same form drops a commit from the middle of a branch (git rebase --onto <sha>^ <sha>, which excludes exactly that one commit) and rescues colleagues after someone rewrote a base branch beneath them.

--update-refs for stacked branches

Stacked branches — feature/a, then feature/b built on it, then feature/c — used to make rebasing miserable: rebasing the tip left the intermediate branch pointers dangling on the old, now-unreachable commits, and you re-pointed each one by hand. Git 2.38 added --update-refs. During the rebase the sequencer inserts update-ref refs/heads/feature/a lines into the todo list at the right positions and moves every branch that pointed into the rewritten range.

Turn it on permanently with rebase.updateRefs = true. One caveat with teeth: it moves the local branches, but each one still needs its own force-push, and a rewritten stack must be pushed base-first or the review UI will briefly show every parent branch's commits inside the child's pull request.

Rebase versus merge: a team decision, not a taste

This argument is settled per repository, written into CONTRIBUTING.md, and enforced by the forge's merge-button settings — not relitigated per pull request. Both sides give up something real.

The honest trade-off
DimensionRebase / linear historyMerge / true history
Readabilitygit log --oneline is a straight list that reads like a changelog.Requires --graph and practice; busy repositories become a lattice.
BisectEvery commit is a candidate and, with exec-verified builds, each one is testable.git bisect still works, but lands on merge commits and untested intermediate states.
Blame & revertReverting one feature means reverting a contiguous run of commits.One git revert -m 1 of the merge undoes the whole feature atomically.
ContextLost: the fact that the work was written against an older base, and the true integration date, are gone.Preserved exactly. The merge commit records who integrated what, when, against which base.
ConflictsResolved per commit; the same hunk can fight you repeatedly without rerere.Resolved once, in one place, recorded in the merge commit.
HashesRewritten. Any external reference — deploy tags, incident tickets, CI artefacts keyed on SHA — goes stale.Stable. A reviewed commit keeps the hash it was reviewed under.
AuditWeaker: the merged commits are not the reviewed commits.Stronger: the merge commit's parent is exactly the tip that passed review.

The workable middle, and what most teams converge on: rebase locally to tidy your own branch before review, then integrate with a merge commit or a squash-merge on the server. Squash-merge gives a linear main without anyone force-pushing a shared branch — at the cost of collapsing the branch to a single commit, which is a poor fit for large changes that genuinely wanted three reviewable steps. Whatever you pick, set pull.rebase = true or pull.ff = only globally; the default merging git pull is how accidental merge commits get into main.

Interview · "when would you rebase, and when would you not?"

The answer they want has three parts. One: rebase is a rewrite, so the question is never taste, it is whether anyone depends on these commits — my own branch, yes; anything protected or branched-from, never. Two: name the recovery path unprompted — --abort mid-flight, ORIG_HEAD or the branch reflog afterwards, and range-diff to verify the result. Three: distinguish rewriting from reverting, and say that on main after a bad deploy the answer is git revert even though it leaves an uglier history. Candidates who recite "never rebase public branches" without being able to define "public" get found out by the follow-up: "my branch is on the remote and CI has run against it — is that public?" It is not; being pushed is not the same as being depended upon.

Force-pushing safely

A rewritten branch cannot fast-forward, so publishing it requires force. git push --force overwrites whatever is on the server, including commits you have never seen. --force-with-lease makes the push conditional: it sends the value your remote-tracking ref refs/remotes/origin/<branch> currently holds, and the server refuses unless that is still the branch's actual tip.
