If a colleague pushed in the meantime, the lease is stale and the push is rejected instead of eating their commit.

The race it does not cover is the one that bites in practice. The lease is checked against your remote-tracking ref, and anything that fetches updates that ref — an IDE's background auto-fetch, a git fetch in another terminal, a helpful git pull on a different branch with fetch.prune — silently refreshes your idea of the remote tip without you ever seeing those commits. The lease then passes and the force-push destroys work. Git 2.30 added --force-if-includes, which additionally requires that the remote tip you are about to overwrite is reachable from your own branch's reflog: proof that the commits were actually in your working history, not merely fetched behind your back. Enable it once, globally, with push.useForceIfIncludes = true; it is implied whenever --force-with-lease is used without an explicit expected value.

a lease saving a colleague's commit
$ git push --force-with-lease
 ! [rejected]        feature -> feature (stale info)
error: failed to push some refs to 'github.com:acme/payments.git'

$ git fetch origin
$ git log --oneline HEAD..origin/feature
ab9012 test: cover retry budget exhaustion
# someone pushed a test onto this branch -- --force would have deleted it

$ git rebase origin/feature
$ git push --force-with-lease
To github.com:acme/payments.git
 + 04f7ab...b7715c feature -> feature (forced update)

$ git push --force-with-lease=feature:04f7ab
# the paranoid form: name the exact hash you expect to overwrite,
# which no background fetch can invalidate on your behalf
Rebase, replay and publish
CommandWhat it doesNotes
git rebase mainReplays main..HEAD onto main.care New hashes. Fine on a branch nobody has forked.
git rebase -i mainSame, but lets you edit the todo list first.care Todo is oldest-first, the reverse of git log.
git rebase --onto main spike topicReplays spike..topic onto main.care The only clean fix for branching off the wrong base.
git rebase -i --autosquash mainPre-arranges fixup!/squash! commits under their targets.care Prefix GIT_SEQUENCE_EDITOR=true to skip the editor.
git rebase --update-refs mainMoves every stacked branch pointing into the rewritten range.care Git 2.38+. Each branch still needs its own push.
git rebase --continueResumes after a conflict or an edit/break stop.safe Refuses while unmerged paths remain.
git rebase --abortRestores the branch and working tree to the pre-rebase state.safe Works at any point mid-rebase.
git rebase --skipDrops the commit currently being applied and carries on.destructive Silently discards that commit's changes. Confirm with range-diff.
git reset --hard ORIG_HEADUndoes a completed rebase.care Also loses uncommitted work; git stash first.
git range-diff main @{1} HEADDiffs the pre- and post-rebase versions of the branch.safe Read-only. The verification step people skip.
git push --force-with-leaseForce-pushes only if the remote tip matches your remote-tracking ref.care Defeated by background fetches unless paired with if-includes.
git push --force-if-includes --force-with-leaseAlso requires the overwritten tip to be in your reflog.care Git 2.30+. Make it the default via push.useForceIfIncludes.
git push --forceOverwrites the remote branch unconditionally.destructive Deletes colleagues' commits with no warning. Recovery needs their reflog or the forge's ref log.
ini · ~/.gitconfig — the rewrite-safety block
[pull]
	rebase = true            # never create an accidental merge on pull
[rebase]
	autoSquash = true        # honour fixup!/squash! without the flag
	autoStash = true         # stash and restore local changes around a rebase
	updateRefs = true        # move stacked branches (Git 2.38+)
	missingCommitsCheck = error   # refuse a todo list with deleted lines
[push]
	default = simple
	useForceIfIncludes = true     # harden --force-with-lease (Git 2.30+)
[rerere]
	enabled = true           # replay conflict resolutions during long rebases
[merge]
	conflictStyle = zdiff3   # show the merge base in conflict markers

cherry-pick: one commit, somewhere else

Cherry-pick is rebase's single-commit sibling: it computes a commit's patch and applies it to HEAD as a new commit with a new hash. Its real job is backporting — a fix lands on main, and the same fix must reach release/2.7 without dragging along the twenty commits that came after it.

Ranges use the standard exclusive notation: git cherry-pick A..B takes everything after A up to and including B, so to include A itself write A^..B. Add -x whenever the destination is a branch other people will read: it appends (cherry picked from commit <sha>) to the message, which is the only durable link back to the original and the thing that lets you answer "is this fix in 2.7?" six months later with git log --grep. Leave -x off for purely local moves, where the referenced hash may never be public.

Cherry-pick and revert
CommandWhat it doesNotes
git cherry-pick 04f7abApplies one commit's patch as a new commit on HEAD.care Duplicates content if the commit later arrives by merge.
git cherry-pick -x 04f7abSame, recording the source hash in the message.care Always use on release branches; it is the audit trail.
git cherry-pick 3b7a19^..04f7abApplies an inclusive range in order.care Without ^ the first commit is excluded.
git cherry-pick -n 04f7abApplies to index and worktree without committing.safe For taking part of a change, or combining several.
git cherry-pick --abortRestores the state from before the pick.safe --quit stops but keeps what was applied.
git cherry origin/release/2.7 mainLists commits on main not yet in the release branch, by patch id.safe Answers "what still needs backporting?".
git revert 04f7abCreates a new commit applying the inverse patch.safe Adds history rather than rewriting it. Correct on shared branches.
git revert -m 1 4c7e0bReverts a merge, keeping the first parent's line of history.care -m 1 = the branch you merged into. Read the trap below.
git revert --no-commit A^..BStages the inverse of a whole range as one change.care Commit it yourself with a message naming the incident.

revert, and the reverted-merge trap

On a shared branch, revert is the answer. It writes a new commit containing the inverse patch: history grows, no hash changes, nobody's clone diverges, and the audit trail shows both that the change landed and that it was withdrawn. That is what you want at 02:00 with a bad deploy on main.

Reverting a merge commit needs -m because a merge has two parents and Git cannot guess which mainline you want to keep. -m 1 means "keep the first parent" — the branch you merged into, normally main — and therefore undoes everything the side branch brought in. Then comes the trap.

after git revert -m 1 4c7e0b * 9d21fa Revert "Merge branch 'feature'" main * 4c7e0b Merge branch 'feature' |\ | * ba3e91 feature: part two feature | * d40b77 feature: part one * | 1f9a03 bump client library |/ * 3b7a19 git merge feature -> "Already up to date." ba3e91 is already an ancestor of main, so nothing is replayed. The code is gone; the graph insists it arrived.
Production failure · the feature that vanished twice

The classic sequence: a feature merge causes an incident, someone reverts the merge, the bug is fixed on the feature branch, the branch is merged again — and the fix ships without the feature it was fixing, because only the new commits replay. QA passes (the fix's tests are narrow), the release goes out, and the feature is missing in production with a green pipeline and a history that says it merged twice. The fix is one of two moves: revert the revert (git revert 9d21fa) immediately before re-merging, which restores the original changes and lets subsequent work merge normally; or rebuild the branch on a fresh base so its commits get new hashes and are no longer ancestors of main. Decide which at revert time and write it in the incident notes — the trap is only expensive when it is discovered weeks later.

filter-repo: rewriting a repository for real

Removing a 400 MB dataset committed in 2021, or a credential that sat in config/prod.env for eleven commits, means rewriting every commit from that point forward — all branches, all tags, all of it. git filter-branch was the historical tool; it is deprecated (Git 2.24 prints a warning pointing elsewhere) because it is glacially slow, forks a shell per commit per ref, and has enough safety footguns that most invocations found online silently corrupt something. Use git filter-repo instead: a single Python tool, orders of magnitude faster, correct about tags, annotated tags and mailmaps, and it deliberately refuses to run on anything but a fresh clone so you always keep an unrewritten copy.

bash · removing a leaked secret and a large blob
# 0. ROTATE THE CREDENTIAL FIRST. The rewrite is cleanup, not containment.

# 1. work from a fresh mirror; filter-repo refuses a repo with local state
git clone --mirror git@github.com:acme/payments.git payments-rewrite.git
cd payments-rewrite.git

# 2a. drop paths from every commit that ever touched them
git filter-repo --invert-paths \
  --path config/prod.env \
  --path-glob 'dumps/*.sql'

# 2b. or keep the file and redact the string in place
printf 'AKIAIOSFODNN7EXAMPLE==>AWS_KEY_REDACTED\n' > /tmp/expr.txt
git filter-repo --replace-text /tmp/expr.txt

# 2c. or sweep every blob above a size threshold
git filter-repo --strip-blobs-bigger-than 10M

# 3. verify: old-to-new hash mapping, and the new size
head -3 .git/filter-repo/commit-map
git count-objects -vH

# 4. publish. filter-repo removes 'origin' on purpose -- name it explicitly
git push --mirror git@github.com:acme/payments.git

Then comes the part that is not a Git problem. Every clone in existence still contains the old objects, every open pull request is now based on commits that no longer exist upstream, CI caches keyed on commit SHAs are stale, and any deploy tooling that pins a SHA needs updating. Treat it as a scheduled change, not a quick fix.

01 · Contain
Rotate the secretassume it is already compromised
02 · Announce
Freeze windowmerge or close open PRs first
03 · Rewrite
filter-repo on a mirrorkeep the original clone untouched
04 · Verify
commit-map + grepconfirm the string is gone from all refs
05 · Publish
push --mirrorlift branch protection, then restore it
06 · Re-clone
Every engineer, freshno rebasing old clones onto the rewrite
Rewriting does not un-leak a secret

On GitHub, GitLab and Bitbucket the old commits often remain fetchable by their exact SHA long after the rewrite — unreachable objects survive until the host garbage-collects, forks keep their own copies, and pull request refs (refs/pull/<n>/head) can pin them indefinitely. On GitHub you must open a support request to have the stale objects purged, and forks are outside your control entirely. Anything scraped from a public repository by a bot was scraped within minutes. Rotation is the control; history rewriting is housekeeping so it does not leak again.

The through-line: rebase, cherry-pick and filter-repo all do the same thing at different scales — replay content as new commits — and each therefore breaks anyone who depends on the old ones. Rewriting freely on work that is yours alone is a professional habit that produces reviewable branches and a bisectable main. Rewriting anything shared is a coordinated operation with a rollback plan, or it is git revert.

G05

Undoing anything

Almost nothing in Git is destroyed by the command you just regretted — commits become unreferenced, not deleted, and the reflog remembers where every branch has been for ninety days. The short list of operations that genuinely lose data is at the end of this chapter, and every single one of them destroys something that was never committed.

The model: three trees and one graph

Every undo in Git is a question about which of four things you want to change, and confusion about undoing is almost always confusion about these four.

The object database
Immutable content-addressed blobs, trees and commits under .git/objects. Nothing here is ever modified. Objects are only ever added, and removed much later by git gc once nothing references them.
Refs
Branch and tag names — pointers into the graph. "Deleting a branch" deletes a pointer, not the commits. "Rewriting history" writes new commits and repoints a name at them.
The index
Also called the staging area or cache: a single file, .git/index, holding the tree that the next commit will record. It is a full snapshot, not a diff.
The working tree
The files on disk. The only one of the four that Git does not version. Whatever is here and nowhere else is the only thing you can truly lose.

Read that last line again, because it is the whole chapter. Committed work is recoverable; staged work is usually recoverable; unstaged work in the working tree is not. The commands below are grouped by which trees they touch, and the risk markers follow the same logic: an operation is marked destructive when it can overwrite the working tree, and safe when the worst case is a new commit or a moved pointer that the reflog can put back.

The decision table

Find the row that matches what you actually want, then read the model sections underneath for why it works. The commands assume Git 2.23 or newer, where git switch and git restore split the overloaded git checkout into two comprehensible tools.

"I want to undo…" — the operational index
CommandI want to…Notes
git restore --staged src/app.pyUnstage a file, keeping my editssafe Copies the file from HEAD into the index only. Working tree untouched. Old spelling: git reset HEAD src/app.py.
git restore src/app.pyThrow away my uncommitted edits to one filedestructive Overwrites the file from the index. If the edits were never staged, they are gone for good.
git restore :/Throw away every uncommitted edit in the repositorydestructive :/ means "from the repository root". Run git stash --include-untracked first if there is any doubt.
git commit --amendFix the message of the commit I just madecare Writes a new commit object with a new hash. Fine before pushing; needs a force-push after.
git commit --amend --no-editAdd a forgotten file to the last commitcare Stage the file first. --no-edit keeps the existing message and skips the editor.
git reset --soft HEAD~1Undo the last commit but keep the changes stagedsafe Moves the branch pointer only. The commit survives in the reflog.
git reset HEAD~1Undo the last commit and unstage the changessafe --mixed is the default. Files on disk are untouched.
git reset --hard HEAD~1Undo the last commit and discard the changes entirelydestructive The commit is in the reflog; any uncommitted work on top of it is not.
git revert 9f2c1abUndo a commit that has already been pushed to a shared branchsafe Adds a new commit with the inverse diff. The only correct undo on a protected branch.
git revert -m 1 4d81e0cUndo a merge commit on a shared branchcare -m 1 keeps the first-parent side. The branch cannot simply be re-merged afterwards.
git push --force-with-leaseUndo a pushed commit on a branch that is genuinely minedestructive Reset locally first. Refuses if the remote moved since your last fetch — see the chapter on rebase.
git filter-repo --invert-paths --path secrets.envRemove a file from every commit in historydestructive Every hash after the first touch changes. Rotate the credential regardless — see the callout below.
git branch feature 9f2c1abRecover a branch I deletedsafe Take the hash from the "Deleted branch (was …)" message or from git reflog.
git reset --hard ORIG_HEADRecover from a bad rebase, merge, reset or pulldestructive ORIG_HEAD is set by all four. Discards uncommitted work as it goes.
git stash listRecover a stash I droppedsafe If it is not listed, the stash commit is dangling — use git fsck, below.
git restore --source=9f2c1ab~1 -- db/schema.sqlRecover a file deleted three commits agocare Find the deleting commit with git log --diff-filter=D first.
git fsck --lost-found --no-reflogsFind work when even the reflog has nothingsafe Read-only. Writes dangling objects into .git/lost-found/.
git clean -ndSee which untracked files a clean would deletesafe Always run the -n dry run before -f. Untracked files are not in any tree.

reset: three modes, precisely

The single most useful mental model in Git: git reset moves the current branch pointer to a commit, and then optionally drags the index and the working tree along with it. The three modes are simply how far down that chain the drag goes.

What each reset mode writes
ModeBranch / HEADIndexWorking treeUse it when
--softmoveduntoucheduntouchedSquashing the last n commits into one: reset, then commit again.
--mixed (default)movedreset to targetuntouchedUndoing a commit to re-stage it in smaller pieces.
--hardmovedreset to targetoverwrittenAbandoning everything since a known-good commit. The only mode that destroys.
--keepmovedreset to targetkept if uncommittedMoving the branch without losing local edits; aborts if they would be clobbered.
--mergemovedreset to targetkept where unaffectedBacking out of a half-applied merge while keeping unrelated edits.

Two behaviours surprise people. First, reset with a pathspec does not move HEAD at all: git reset HEAD~1 -- src/app.py only rewrites that path in the index, and only --mixed semantics are legal in that form. Second, --hard never deletes untracked files — it overwrites tracked ones. A working tree that looks "clean" after a hard reset can still be full of untracked build output, which is why git clean exists as a separate, equally sharp tool.

The afternoon that vanished

The most common real data loss in Git is not an exotic rebase. It is an engineer with four hours of unstaged work who runs git reset --hard or git checkout -- . to clear an unrelated conflict. There is no reflog entry, no dangling object and no recovery path, because the content never entered the object database. Git will not warn you, and the command prints a cheerful HEAD is now at …. The two defences are habitual — git stash --include-untracked before anything that rewrites the working tree, and git add early even on work you are not ready to commit, because a staged blob is a real object that git fsck can find later.

restore: the file-level tool

git restore exists because git checkout did two unrelated jobs and people picked the wrong one under pressure. Restore copies file content from a source into a destination, and takes explicit flags for both.

restore, by source and destination
CommandWhat it doesNotes
git restore app.pyIndex → working treedestructive Default destination is --worktree. Discards unstaged edits.
git restore --staged app.pyHEAD → indexsafe Pure unstage. Disk untouched.
git restore --staged --worktree app.pyHEAD → index and working treedestructive Full revert of one file to its committed state.
git restore --source=HEAD~3 app.pyAn arbitrary commit → working treecare Leaves the file modified relative to HEAD, ready to inspect or commit.
git restore --patch app.pyDiscard selected hunks interactivelycare The surgical alternative to throwing the whole file away.
git checkout -- app.pyThe pre-2.23 spelling of the first rowdestructive The -- is not optional: without it, a file named like a branch resolves as a branch.

revert: the only undo for published history

Reset rewrites; revert appends. On any branch other people build on, appending is the only acceptable option, because rewriting a shared branch reintroduces the old commits through everyone else's next pull. git revert 9f2c1ab computes the inverse of that commit's diff, applies it to the working tree, and commits the result with a generated message. The original commit stays in history — that is the point, and it is what makes the change auditable.

reset removes the commit from the branch revert adds an inverse commit a1f2c9---b7d3e0 main a1f2c9---b7d3e0---9f2c1ab---c3e0d5 main \ ^ 9f2c1ab unreferenced, Revert "add rounding" in the reflog only Use the left form only when the branch is yours alone. Use the right form on main, always.

Two operational notes. Reverting a merge needs -m 1 to say which parent is the mainline, and the result is that the merged branch is now recorded as merged-and-undone; re-merging it later brings back nothing, so you must revert the revert or rebuild the branch. And git revert can conflict, exactly like a cherry-pick, when later commits touched the same lines — git revert --abort gets you back to a clean tree, --continue proceeds after you resolve.

The reflog: the safety net

Every time HEAD moves — commit, checkout, reset, rebase, merge, pull, amend — Git appends a line to .git/logs/HEAD recording the old hash, the new hash, and what caused the move. Each branch gets its own log under .git/logs/refs/heads/. This is why "I lost a commit" is nearly always false: the commit is still an object, and the reflog still names it.

The reflog is local, per-clone and not pushed. A colleague's fresh clone has no record of your reflog, and a CI runner cloning with --depth 1 has none at all. Entries expire on git gc: ninety days for entries whose commits are still reachable (gc.reflogExpire) and thirty days for unreachable ones (gc.reflogExpireUnreachable). Unreferenced loose objects are pruned after two weeks (gc.pruneExpire). Nothing on that list is instant, so the practical window for recovery is generous — provided nobody has run git gc --prune=now.

recovering from an accidental hard reset
$ git commit -am "invoice rounding fix"
[main 9f2c1ab] invoice rounding fix
 1 file changed, 12 insertions(+), 3 deletions(-)

$ git reset --hard HEAD~3
HEAD is now at 4d81e0c chore: bump rubocop

$ git log --oneline -1
4d81e0c chore: bump rubocop
# three commits, including the fix made ten seconds ago, are off the branch

$ git reflog
4d81e0c HEAD@{0}: reset: moving to HEAD~3
9f2c1ab HEAD@{1}: commit: invoice rounding fix
7a30b8e HEAD@{2}: commit: extract Money.round_half_even
c11de42 HEAD@{3}: commit: failing spec for banker's rounding
4d81e0c HEAD@{4}: commit: chore: bump rubocop

$ git diff HEAD HEAD@{1} --stat
# confirm this is the right target BEFORE moving anything
 app/models/money.rb  | 41 +++++++++++++---
 spec/money_spec.rb   | 28 ++++++++++++

$ git branch rescue 9f2c1ab
# non-destructive: park the lost tip on a name, then decide

$ git reset --hard 9f2c1ab
HEAD is now at 9f2c1ab invoice rounding fix

Note the shape of that session. The recovery is one command; the discipline is the two commands around it — git diff to verify the target before moving, and git branch rescue so that a second mistake costs nothing. git reset --hard ORIG_HEAD would have worked here too, since reset sets ORIG_HEAD, but it only ever remembers one step back.

Working the reflog
CommandWhat it doesNotes
git reflogEvery movement of HEAD, newest firstsafe Shorthand for git reflog show HEAD.
git reflog show featureEvery position of one branch pointersafe Deleted with the branch, so use the HEAD reflog after a branch deletion.
git reflog --date=isoAbsolute timestamps instead of HEAD@{2} ordinalssafe Essential when correlating with a deploy or an incident timeline.
git log -g --oneline --allThe reflog as a log, with full formattingsafe Accepts --grep, so you can search lost commit messages.
git show HEAD@{1}Inspect where HEAD was one movement agosafe @{2.hours.ago} also works, and is per-repository, not global time.
git reset --hard ORIG_HEADUndo the last reset, merge, rebase or pulldestructive One level deep only. Discards uncommitted work.
git reflog expire --expire=now --allErase the safety netdestructive Followed by git gc --prune=now this is genuinely irreversible. Only for scrubbing a leaked secret.

When even the reflog cannot help

Two cases fall outside the reflog: a stash you dropped (dropping rewrites the stash reflog) and a commit created inside a rebase or a filter that no ref ever pointed at. Both leave objects in the database with nothing referencing them, and git fsck finds them. Pass --no-reflogs, or everything the reflog still holds is reported as reachable and drowns the signal.

bash · digging dangling objects out of the object store
# List every object nothing points at. --lost-found also writes them
# into .git/lost-found/commit/ and .git/lost-found/other/ as real files.
git fsck --full --no-reflogs --lost-found

# Typical output:
#   dangling commit 8c1f4ab0d2e5c9a71b3d0e4f6a2c8d9e0b1f3a5c
#   dangling blob   3f7e2b1c9d0a4e8f6b2c1d0a9e8f7b6c5d4a3b2e

# Inspect a dangling commit before resurrecting it.
git show --stat 8c1f4ab

# Give it a name so gc can never touch it again.
git branch recovered-work 8c1f4ab

# A dangling BLOB is a file that was git add-ed but never committed.
# You get the content, not the filename — Git never recorded one.
git cat-file -p 3f7e2b1 > recovered-file.txt

# Find a dropped stash: stash commits carry a WIP message.
git fsck --no-reflogs --unreachable |
  awk '/commit/ {print $3}' |
  xargs -n1 git log --no-walk --grep='WIP on' --oneline

git stash apply 8c1f4ab   # or: git branch stash-rescue 8c1f4ab

That dangling-blob case is the one worth internalising. If you ever ran git add on a file, its content is a permanent object until gc prunes it, and git fsck will hand it back. If you never staged it, there is no object, and the honest answer is that Git cannot help you — check your editor's local history instead (JetBrains IDEs keep one, and VS Code's timeline view keeps per-file snapshots), because that is a filesystem feature, not a Git one.

The stash as an undo tool

A stash is not a special data structure — it is one or two real commits (three with --include-untracked) parented on your current HEAD, with refs/stash pointing at the newest and a reflog holding the rest. That is why stash@{2} works, and why a dropped stash is recoverable by hash. Used deliberately, it is the cheapest possible pre-flight before any destructive command.

  • git stash push -m "wip: rounding" — name it. Unnamed stashes become an archaeological problem within a week.
  • git stash push --include-untracked — the default ignores untracked files, which is exactly the work a subsequent git clean will delete.
  • git stash apply rather than pop — pop drops the stash on success, and a conflicted pop can leave you with neither a clean tree nor a stash entry you trust.
  • git stash push -- path/to/file — stash a subset, so an urgent hotfix does not drag the rest of your work with it.
  • git stash branch fix/rounding — create a branch at the stash's parent and apply it there, the correct move when the stash no longer applies cleanly to the current tip.

Recovering a file deleted three commits ago

The trap is that git log -- path stops at the deletion, because after it the path does not exist. Find the deleting commit explicitly, then read the file out of its parent.

resurrecting a deleted path
$ git log --oneline --diff-filter=D -- db/schema.sql
b7d3e0f chore: drop legacy schema dump

$ git show --stat b7d3e0f -- db/schema.sql
 db/schema.sql | 812 ------------------------------------------

$ git restore --source=b7d3e0f~1 -- db/schema.sql
# the caret/tilde matters: the file exists in the PARENT, not in the deleting commit

$ git status --short
A  db/schema.sql

Use git log --all --full-history -- db/schema.sql when the file was moved or lived only on a branch that was later merged; without --full-history, history simplification can hide the commit you need entirely.

Rewriting history does not un-leak a secret

git filter-repo --invert-paths --path .env removes the file from every commit and every hash changes, which forces every clone to be re-cloned. What it does not do is remove the blob from your hosting provider. GitHub keeps pull-request refs under refs/pull/ that you cannot rewrite, forks retain their own copies, and the object stays served until support garbage-collects the repository on request. Anyone who cloned or ran CI in the window still has it. Rotate the credential first, then rewrite history second — and treat the rewrite as tidying, never as remediation.

What genuinely loses data

Everything else in this chapter is reversible. These are not, or are only reversible within a window:

  1. Discarding never-staged working-tree changes. git reset --hard, git restore, git checkout -- ., and a conflicted checkout that overwrites. No object was ever written, so nothing can be recovered.
  2. git clean -fdx. Deletes untracked and ignored files — local .env files, uploaded fixtures, credentials, anything you were about to add. Run -nd first, every time.
  3. git reflog expire --expire=now --all plus git gc --prune=now. Deliberately deletes unreachable objects immediately. This is the only common way to destroy a commit.
  4. Force-pushing over commits that exist in no other clone. Recoverable only from the other person's reflog, or your host's ref log if it keeps one. If neither has it, the commits are gone from the server for good.
  5. Deleting the only clone. A repository with no remote, or a remote nobody has fetched, is one rm -rf from non-existence. Git is not a backup system.
  6. A bad conflict resolution. The original commits survive, but the new ones silently omit a hunk. Nothing is deleted and nothing warns you — git range-diff before and after a rewrite is the only cheap check.

Every item on that list argues the same discipline: commit early and often, even badly. A messy series of WIP commits can be squashed in thirty seconds; four hours of unstaged edits cannot be reconstructed at all. Commit hygiene is a rewriting problem, and rewriting is easy. Recovery of work that was never recorded is not a problem — it is an outcome.

Interview: "walk me through reset --soft, --mixed and --hard"

The weak answer recites the flags. The strong answer draws the three trees first — HEAD, index, working tree — states that all three modes move the branch pointer and they differ only in how far down the chain the change propagates, and then names the practical consequence: --soft is how you squash, --mixed is how you re-split a commit, and --hard is the only mode that can lose anything. Expect two follow-ups. "How do you get it back?" — the reflog, with the caveat that it is local, per-clone, and expires. "When would you use revert instead?" — the moment anyone else has the commit as an ancestor, because reset rewrites the branch and their next pull restores what you removed. Candidates who add that git reset with a pathspec does not move HEAD at all are demonstrating they have read the model rather than memorised recipes.

G06

Remotes, fetch, pull & push

A remote is a named URL plus a default refspec, and every sync is the same two-step: copy objects, then update refs under a policy. Hold that model and rejected pushes, phantom branches and the endless Merge branch 'main' into main commits stop being folklore and become arithmetic you can do in your head.

The distributed model

A clone is not a working copy of a server. It is a complete repository: the full object database, its own refs, its own reflog, its own configuration. You can commit, branch, merge, bisect and rewrite history on a laptop in a tunnel with no network, because nothing you do locally consults a server. The only operations that need one are fetch, push and their derivatives.

What a clone additionally carries is a bookmark of what the server looked like the last time you asked. That bookmark is the remote-tracking ref namespace, refs/remotes/origin/*. It is not the server. It is a cache of the server, and it is only ever refreshed by fetch. Ninety per cent of confusion about remotes is someone reading origin/main as “what is on GitHub right now” instead of “what was on GitHub when I last fetched”.

Remote
A name in .git/config bound to a URL and one or more refspecs. origin is a convention created by clone, not a keyword; you can rename it freely.
Remote-tracking ref
A local, read-only ref such as refs/remotes/origin/main, written only by fetch, pull and successful push. You never commit onto it.
Upstream (tracking) branch
A per-branch pairing stored as branch.<name>.remote and branch.<name>.merge. It is what makes bare git push, git pull and the ahead/behind counts in git status work.
Refspec
[+]<src>:<dst> — copy the ref (or glob of refs) at src to the ref at dst. The leading + permits a non-fast-forward update of the destination.
Bare repository
A repository with no working tree: the contents of .git promoted to the top level. Servers hold bare repos because there is no checkout to get out of sync with pushes.
Managing remotes
CommandWhat it doesNotes
git remote -vLists remotes with their fetch and push URLssafe Two lines per remote; they can differ.
git remote add upstream git@github.com:acme/api.gitAdds a second remote for the fork workflowsafe Adds a config stanza only — fetches nothing yet.
git remote rename origin ghRenames the remote and rewrites its tracking refscare Also rewrites branch.*.remote. Scripts hard-coding origin will break.
git remote remove upstreamDrops the remote and all refs/remotes/upstream/*care Local branches survive; their upstream pairing does not.
git remote set-url origin git@github.com:acme/api.gitSwitches protocol or host without re-cloningsafe The classic HTTPS-to-SSH migration.
git remote set-url --push origin git@github.com:me/api.gitFetch from one URL, push to anothercare Read from upstream, write to your fork. Surprising to a colleague reading your config.
git remote show originQueries the server: HEAD, stale refs, push mappingsafe Network call. Add -n to use only cached data.
git remote set-head origin --autoRepoints origin/HEAD after the default branch is renamedsafe Fixes the stale origin/HEAD -> origin/master symref.

The fork workflow is just two remotes with different rights: origin is your fork, which you can push to, and upstream is the canonical repository, which you can only read. Keeping them straight is entirely a matter of never letting a local branch track upstream by accident.

ini · .git/config after adding a second remote
[remote "origin"]
	url = git@github.com:me/payments-api.git
	fetch = +refs/heads/*:refs/remotes/origin/*
[remote "upstream"]
	url = https://github.com/acme/payments-api.git
	fetch = +refs/heads/*:refs/remotes/upstream/*
	pushurl = no-push-to-upstream
[branch "main"]
	remote = origin
	merge = refs/heads/main

Refspecs, and why they demystify everything

The fetch line above is the whole trick. It says: take every ref under refs/heads/ on the server and write it under refs/remotes/origin/ here, forcibly (+), because the server is allowed to rewrite its own branches and our cache must follow. That single line explains why remote branches appear in a namespace of their own, why they cannot be checked out directly, and why they update without asking.

Push uses the same grammar in the other direction, with one critical asymmetry: push has no implicit +. A push whose destination would not fast-forward is rejected, which is the mechanism that stops you silently deleting a colleague's commits.

Refspec forms you will actually type
RefspecDirectionEffect
+refs/heads/*:refs/remotes/origin/*fetchThe default. Mirror every server branch into the tracking namespace.
refs/heads/main:refs/heads/mainpushThe long form of git push origin main.
HEAD:refs/heads/release-2026-09pushPublish the current commit under a different remote name. Useful when local naming is scratch.
:refs/heads/old-featurepushEmpty source means delete the destination — the original spelling of --delete.
+refs/heads/main:refs/heads/mainpushForce. Equivalent to --force for that ref only, with none of the safety of a lease.
refs/pull/*/head:refs/remotes/origin/pr/*fetchGitHub-specific: fetch every pull request head locally for review.
refs/notes/*:refs/notes/*bothNotes, replace refs and other non-branch namespaces are never synced unless you ask.
Read the output as refspecs

Every line Git prints during a transfer is a refspec result: 3f2a1c9..8d41b07 main -> origin/main is a fast-forward, + 9ab1e30...4d0c77f main -> origin/main (forced update) means somebody rewrote history on the server, and * [new branch] means the destination did not exist. The three dots in a forced update are the tell.

Fetch versus pull

git fetch downloads objects and moves remote-tracking refs. It does not touch your working tree, your index, or any branch you have checked out. There is no state in which running it makes your situation worse, which is why it should be the reflex and pull the deliberate act.

git pull is fetch followed by an integration step — merge by default, rebase if configured — executed against a working tree you have not yet looked at. Everything people dislike about pull is that second half running before they had a chance to see what arrived.

01 · Negotiate
Ref advertisementserver lists refs; client says what it has
02 · Transfer
Packfileonly missing objects, delta-compressed
03 · Index
Write objectsinto .git/objects, nothing user-visible yet
04 · Update
refs/remotes/*fetch stops here — always safe
05 · Integrate
merge or rebasepull continues; working tree changes

The default integration is a merge, and when your branch has diverged from its upstream that merge produces a commit whose message is Merge branch 'main' of github.com:acme/api into main. It records nothing anybody will ever want: no feature, no decision, no review. On a busy trunk you get one per developer per day, the history turns into a braid, and git log --oneline becomes unreadable. This is the single largest source of accidental noise in mainline history.

* a19f3e2 (HEAD -> main) Merge branch 'main' of github.com:acme/api into main |\ | * 8d41b07 (origin/main) fix: retry budget on 429 * | 4c7d0e1 feat: idempotency keys on POST /charges |/ * 3f2a1c9 chore: bump http client to 6.2.1 | (and one of these per pull, forever)

Configure the behaviour once, globally, and stop thinking about it. pull.ff=only is the strictest and the best default for people who want to be told when something needs attention: it fast-forwards when it can and refuses otherwise, leaving you to choose --rebase or --merge with full information.

bash · one-time global setup
# Refuse to invent a merge commit behind your back.
git config --global pull.ff only

# Or: always rebase local commits on top of what you fetched.
git config --global pull.rebase true
git config --global rebase.autoStash true   # stash/unstash dirty tree around it

# Prune deleted remote branches on every fetch.
git config --global fetch.prune true

# Push the current branch to a same-named branch, creating upstream if needed.
git config --global push.default simple
git config --global push.autoSetupRemote true   # Git 2.37+

Since Git 2.27 an unconfigured pull on diverged branches warns; on modern releases it stops outright with fatal: Need to specify how to reconcile divergent branches. That is an improvement, not a bug — it exists precisely because the silent merge was doing damage.

Never rebase a pull on a shared branch

pull --rebase is right for your own commits on a trunk you have not published. It is wrong when the commits being replayed are already pushed and other people have them, because rebasing rewrites their hashes and your next push will be a force-push that orphans their copies. The rule is behavioural, not technical: rebase what only you have, merge what others have.

Push, upstreams and force safety

git push -u origin feature/x pushes and records the upstream in one go; every later git push and git pull on that branch needs no arguments. push.default=simple, the default since Git 2.0, pushes only the current branch and only to a remote branch of the same name, refusing when the names differ — a deliberate guard against the old matching behaviour that pushed every same-named branch you happened to have.

Push and fetch operations
CommandWhat it doesNotes
git fetch --all --pruneUpdates every remote and deletes stale tracking refssafe The safest first command of any confusing morning.
git push -u origin feature/xPublishes the branch and sets its upstreamsafe Unnecessary once push.autoSetupRemote is on.
git push origin HEADPushes the current commit to a same-named remote branchsafe Immune to typing the wrong branch name.
git push origin --delete feature/xDeletes the branch on the servercare Recoverable: the commit lives in your clone; re-push it.
git push origin :refs/heads/feature/xColon-refspec form of the same deletioncare Omitting the branch name here would push, not delete. Type carefully.
git push origin v2.4.0Pushes one tagsafe Tags are never pushed by plain git push.
git push --follow-tagsPushes commits plus annotated tags that point into themsafe Better than --tags, which also ships local scratch tags.
git push origin :refs/tags/v2.4.0Deletes a published tagdestructive Consumers who already fetched keep it; releases built from it may break. Prefer a new tag.
git push --force-with-leaseForce-pushes only if the remote is where you last saw itcare Fails with (stale info) if someone pushed since your last fetch.
git push --force-with-lease --force-if-includesAlso requires your rewrite to contain what you fetchedcare Git 2.30+. Closes the background-fetch hole described below.
git push --forceOverwrites the remote ref unconditionallydestructive Recovery depends on the server reflog or another clone. Ban it on shared branches.
git push --dry-run --porcelainShows exactly which refs would movesafe Use before any force on a branch you did not create.
The lease your editor quietly breaks

--force-with-lease compares the remote ref against your remote-tracking ref, not against anything you have read. VS Code, JetBrains IDEs and any fetch in a cron job or shell prompt update that tracking ref in the background. If a colleague pushes at 10:02 and your editor auto-fetches at 10:03, the lease at 10:04 is measured against their commit — it passes, and the force-push destroys it. This has taken out shared release branches in the wild. Fixes, in order of preference: turn off auto-fetch on repositories where you force-push, add --force-if-includes (Git 2.30+), or pin the lease explicitly with git push --force-with-lease=main:3f2a1c9. Recovery, if it happens: the overwritten commit is still in the pusher's reflog and in every other clone, and GitHub's PushEvent API or a protected-branch audit log records the old SHA.

Tracking branches and ahead/behind arithmetic

git status saying Your branch is ahead of 'origin/main' by 2 commits is not a server query. It is two rev-list counts computed against the merge base of your branch and its cached upstream. Ahead means commits you have that the cache does not; behind means commits the cache has that you do not. Both numbers are as stale as your last fetch, which is why a colleague can be four commits ahead of you while your status cheerfully reports up to date.

bash · doing the arithmetic yourself
# behind <tab> ahead, relative to the merge base
git rev-list --left-right --count origin/main...main

# every branch, its upstream, and its divergence in one view
git branch -vv

# machine-readable version for scripts and prompts
git for-each-ref --format='%(refname:short) %(upstream:short) %(upstream:track)' refs/heads

# delete every local branch whose upstream is gone after a prune
git fetch --prune
git branch -vv | awk '/: gone]/ {print $1}' | xargs -r git branch -d
Interview · "status says up to date, so why was my push rejected?"

The answer they want is the cache. git status compares main against refs/remotes/origin/main, a local file last written by your last fetch; git push compares against the server as it is this millisecond. A strong follow-up: what does git fetch change about the working tree? Nothing. And what is the only ref git push updates locally? The remote-tracking ref for the branch it just pushed — which is exactly why a successful push leaves you back at up to date.

Worked example: resolving a rejected push

rejected push, diagnosed and resolved
$ git push
To github.com:acme/payments-api.git
 ! [rejected]        main -> main (fetch first)
error: failed to push some refs to 'github.com:acme/payments-api.git'
hint: Updates were rejected because the remote contains work that you do
hint: not have locally. Integrate the remote changes before pushing again.

$ git fetch origin
remote: Enumerating objects: 17, done.
# objects arrive; nothing in the working tree moves
   3f2a1c9..8d41b07  main       -> origin/main

$ git status -sb
## main...origin/main [ahead 1, behind 3]

$ git log --oneline --left-right --graph main...origin/main
< 4c7d0e1 feat: idempotency keys on POST /charges
> 8d41b07 fix: retry budget on 429
> b0f9d22 test: cover partial capture
> 61ce4aa chore: bump http client to 6.2.1

$ git pull --rebase
Successfully rebased and updated refs/heads/main.
# my one commit replayed on top of their three; new hash, same change

$ git status -sb
## main...origin/main [ahead 1]

$ git push
   8d41b07..a19f3e2  main -> main

Three details matter. fetch was run before deciding anything, so the divergence was inspected rather than guessed at. --left-right showed which commits were whose, so the choice of rebase over merge was informed. And no force was needed, because the rebase moved only a commit that had never been published — the distinction that keeps this manoeuvre safe.

If the rejection had said (non-fast-forward) instead of (fetch first), the remote branch would have been rewritten under you, and the correct move is to inspect origin/main and ask who force-pushed before doing anything else. If it says (stale info), a lease failed and someone pushed since your last fetch. If it says protected branch hook declined, your branch protection is doing its job.

Pruning

Deleting a branch on the server does not delete your tracking ref. Left alone, a clone accumulates hundreds of origin/feature/* refs for branches merged and removed months ago, which slows tab-completion, pollutes git branch -a, and makes --all log views useless.

Cleaning up stale references
CommandWhat it doesNotes
git fetch --pruneFetches, then removes tracking refs whose server branch is gonesafe Only deletes refs/remotes/*, never your local branches.
git remote prune origin --dry-runLists what a prune would remove, without fetchingsafe Good habit before the real thing.
git fetch --prune --prune-tagsAlso deletes local tags removed upstreamcare Will delete local-only tags that share the tag namespace.
git config --global fetch.prune trueMakes pruning the default for every fetchsafe Set it and forget it.

Clone shapes: shallow, single-branch, partial

A full clone of a large monorepo can be gigabytes and minutes. Three orthogonal knobs trim it, and choosing wrongly produces a repository that works until the one command that needs the missing data.

Which trimmed clone to use
FlagWhat it omitsRight forBreaks
--depth 1All history beyond the tipThrowaway CI builds and container image stepsgit merge-base, git describe, blame, any diff against a base branch
--shallow-since=2026-01-01History before a dateCI that must diff against a recent baseOlder tags and long-lived release branches
--single-branchEvery branch but oneDeployment checkouts, release pipelinesAnything comparing branches; implied by --depth
--filter=blob:noneFile contents, fetched on demandDeveloper clones of huge repos; full history, fastOffline work and git log -p across many commits — each blob is a round trip
--filter=tree:0Trees and blobsLog-and-graph-only tooling, mirroring for analysisAlmost any checkout; very chatty on demand

Partial clone needs uploadpack.allowFilter enabled server-side; GitHub, GitLab and Bitbucket Data Center all support it. Pair --filter=blob:none with git sparse-checkout set --cone path/one path/two when only part of the tree matters. A shallow clone can be repaired in place with git fetch --deepen 100 or git fetch --unshallow; a partial clone backfills automatically the moment something needs a missing object.

The CI failure everyone hits once

GitHub Actions' actions/checkout defaults to fetch-depth: 1. Any job that runs git diff origin/main...HEAD, computes a changed-files list, or calls git describe --tags for a version string will fail with fatal: no merge base or silently produce an empty change set that skips every test. Set fetch-depth: 0 on those jobs, or fetch the base branch explicitly with git fetch --no-tags --depth=1 origin main. An empty changed-files list that skips tests is worse than a red build, because it goes green.

Protocols, credentials and signatures

HTTPS and SSH move identical bytes; they differ only in how you prove who you are. HTTPS sends a token, cached by a credential helper — osxkeychain on macOS, manager on Windows, libsecret or cache --timeout=3600 on Linux. Never credential.helper store: it writes the token in cleartext to ~/.git-credentials. GitHub has not accepted account passwords over HTTPS since August 2021, so an HTTPS remote means a personal access token or an OAuth-backed helper such as gh auth login.

SSH sends a key. Generate ed25519, load it into an agent so the passphrase is typed once per session, and verify with ssh -T git@github.com. On corporate networks that block port 22, GitHub answers SSH on ssh.github.com port 443. HTTPS wins through restrictive proxies and for tokens with fine-grained scopes and expiry; SSH wins for daily developer use and avoids token rotation. For CI, prefer short-lived tokens (an OIDC-minted app token, or a deploy key scoped to one repository) over anything long-lived in an environment variable.

bash · identity and signing setup
# SSH key, agent, and macOS keychain persistence
ssh-keygen -t ed25519 -C "masud@example.com"
ssh-add --apple-use-keychain ~/.ssh/id_ed25519
ssh -T git@github.com

# Port 443 fallback when 22 is blocked, in ~/.ssh/config
#   Host github.com
#     Hostname ssh.github.com
#     Port 443

# Rewrite HTTPS URLs to SSH everywhere (handy for vendored submodules)
git config --global url."git@github.com:".insteadOf "https://github.com/"

# Sign commits with the SSH key you already have (Git 2.34+)
git config --global gpg.format ssh
git config --global user.signingkey ~/.ssh/id_ed25519.pub
git config --global commit.gpgsign true
git config --global tag.gpgsign true
git config --global gpg.ssh.allowedSignersFile ~/.config/git/allowed_signers

git log --show-signature -1
git verify-commit HEAD

user.email is free text. Nothing stops anyone from committing as your address and pushing it, which is precisely why regulated organisations require signatures: a signed commit binds the content to a key the organisation has enrolled, and branch protection can then reject anything unsigned. SSH signing is the low-friction option because the key already exists; GPG remains common where a corporate PKI or an existing web of trust is in play. Note that rebasing, squash-merging and any server-side rewrite discard the original signature — a repository that requires signed commits also needs its merge queue or bot to re-sign what it produces.

Mirrors and bare repositories

git clone --bare gives you a repository with no working tree and no remote-tracking namespace. git clone --mirror goes further: it sets remote.origin.fetch = +refs/*:refs/* and remote.origin.mirror = true, so it copies every ref exactly as it is — branches, tags, notes, pull-request refs — and a later git remote update keeps it identical. That is the correct tool for a backup, a read-only replica, or moving a repository between forges.

bash · migrating a repository between hosts
git clone --mirror https://github.com/acme/payments-api.git
cd payments-api.git
git remote set-url --push origin git@gitlab.internal:acme/payments-api.git
git push --mirror
push --mirror deletes

git push --mirror makes the destination match the source exactly, which includes deleting every remote ref that is not present locally. Run it against a live repository from a clone taken an hour earlier and every branch and tag created since disappears in one command, with no confirmation and no local trace. Use it only against an empty destination, and only from a mirror clone taken moments before. If it has already happened, recovery is the destination host's reflog or ref-restore tooling (GitHub Support can restore recently deleted refs; GitLab keeps them until housekeeping runs) — act within hours, not days.

The five-line configuration that prevents most of this chapter

pull.ff=only, fetch.prune=true, push.default=simple, push.autoSetupRemote=true, and rebase.autoStash=true. Set them once in ~/.gitconfig and the daily loop becomes: git fetch, look, then choose. Fetch early, fetch often; integrate on purpose.

G07

Stash, worktrees, submodules & large files

Every tool in this chapter exists because a single working tree and a single object store stopped being enough — two tasks at once, two repositories in one checkout, or files too large for a pack. Each one has a precise model underneath, and each one loses data in a specific way when you use it without knowing that model.

Stash: a commit you refused to name

A stash is not a clipboard, a buffer, or a temporary file. It is a real commit — usually two or three of them — and understanding that single fact answers almost every question people have about stashing.

When you run git stash push, Git builds a commit that records the state of your index, then builds a merge commit whose first parent is HEAD, whose second parent is that index commit, and whose tree is your working tree. With -u it builds a third parent holding the untracked files. The resulting commit id is written to refs/stash, and the previous value of that ref is pushed down the refs/stash reflog. That is the entire mechanism. The stack you think you have is a reflog, which is why stash@{2} is reflog syntax and not array indexing, and why dropping the middle entry renumbers the ones above it.

Two consequences follow immediately. First, your working tree is restored by a hard reset to HEAD after the commit is made — a stash is save then discard, not move. Second, git stash drop deletes a reflog entry, not a commit; the objects survive until garbage collection, so a dropped stash is recoverable.

Stash — the full surface
CommandWhat it doesNotes
git stash push -m "wip: partial capture"Stashes tracked modifications with a readable labelcare Working tree is hard-reset to HEAD afterwards. git stash save is deprecated since 2.16.
git stash push -uAlso stashes untracked filescare Untracked files are deleted from the tree once recorded. See the callout below.
git stash push -aAlso stashes ignored filesdestructive Will happily swallow node_modules/, target/ and .env. Almost never what you want.
git stash push -pInteractive hunk selection, like add -psafe The right tool for splitting one dirty tree into two pieces of work.
git stash push --stagedStashes only what is in the indexsafe Git 2.35+. The inverse of --keep-index.
git stash push --keep-index -- src/chargesStashes a pathspec, leaving the index intactcare The pre-commit test idiom: stash everything not staged, run tests, restore.
git stash list --date=relativeShows the stack with agessafe Anything older than a fortnight is a branch you never made.
git stash show -p --include-untracked stash@{1}Prints the stash as a patchsafe Bare show gives a diffstat only. --include-untracked needs 2.32+.
git stash apply stash@{0}Replays the stash, keeping the entrycare Can conflict. The entry survives, so a failed apply costs nothing.
git stash popapply then drop, but only if apply succeededcare On conflict the entry is kept and printed — people then forget it and re-pop stale work later.
git stash branch fix/capture stash@{0}Creates a branch at the stash's base commit and applies theresafe The escape hatch when the base has moved too far to apply cleanly.
git stash drop stash@{0}Removes one reflog entrydestructive Recover with git fsck --unreachable; see the recovery recipe.
git stash clearDeletes the whole stackdestructive No confirmation, no output. Same recovery path, but you must identify commits by date.
git stash createBuilds the stash commit and prints its hash without touching refs or the treesafe The scripting primitive; pair with git stash store.

Prefer apply to pop. The two differ only in that pop deletes the entry on success, and that deletion is exactly the thing you cannot undo casually. Apply, look at the result, run the tests, and drop deliberately once you believe it. The keystroke you save with pop is not worth the class of incident where a clean apply lands on the wrong branch and you have already destroyed the record of what you had.

stash, inspect, apply, verify, drop
$ git status -s
 M src/charges/capture.rs
?? src/charges/fixtures/partial.json

$ git stash push -u -m "wip: partial capture"
Saved working directory and index state On main: wip: partial capture

$ git stash list
stash@{0}: On main: wip: partial capture
stash@{1}: WIP on main: 4c7d0e1 feat: idempotency keys

$ git cat-file -p stash@{0} | head -4
tree 9b1c4e2f0a7d5c3e8f1b2a6d4c9e7f0b3a5d8c21
parent a19f3e2          # HEAD when I stashed
parent 6f0d3b8          # the index state
parent 2c7e91a          # the untracked files, because of -u

$ git stash apply stash@{0}
Auto-merging src/charges/capture.rs
CONFLICT (content): Merge conflict in src/charges/capture.rs
# apply never drops — the entry is untouched, so this cost nothing

$ git checkout --merge -- src/charges/capture.rs
$ git stash branch fix/partial-capture stash@{0}
Switched to a new branch 'fix/partial-capture'
# applied cleanly at the ORIGINAL base commit, then dropped the entry for me

$ git stash list
# empty
stash -u followed by drop deletes files that exist nowhere else

Untracked files have never been committed. git stash push -u records them in the third parent and then removes them from disk; git stash drop then unlinks the only ref that named them. The files are gone from your editor, gone from the tree, and invisible to git log. Recovery, while the objects survive: git fsck --unreachable --no-reflog, look for the most recent unreachable commit, then git show --stat <sha> to identify it and git stash apply <sha> to restore it. The window is gc.pruneExpire, two weeks by default, and it closes instantly if anyone runs git gc --prune=now. Never stash files you have not at least added to the index.

One more foot-gun worth naming: rebase.autoStash and merge.autoStash create and pop stashes on your behalf. When the auto-pop conflicts, Git leaves the entry in the stack and prints a line most people scroll past. If a rebase ends with a clean tree that is missing your uncommitted work, look at git stash list before you look anywhere else.

Worktrees: stop stashing to look at someone else's branch

The stash exists to free your working tree. Worktrees remove the need to free it at all. git worktree add gives you a second, third or tenth checkout of the same repository, each with its own HEAD, index, working directory and per-worktree reflog, all sharing one object database and one set of refs.

Mechanically: the linked worktree's .git is a file, not a directory, containing a single line such as gitdir: /Users/mk/src/payments-api/.git/worktrees/review. That administrative directory holds HEAD, index, ORIG_HEAD and logs/; everything expensive — objects, packs, refs, most configuration — stays in the main repository. Adding a worktree to a two-gigabyte repository costs a checkout, not a clone.

For reviewing a colleague's branch this is strictly better than stashing. Your own half-finished work never moves. Your build cache, node_modules, running dev server and open editor buffers stay exactly as they were. You can run their test suite and yours simultaneously. And when you are done you delete a directory rather than replaying a merge of your own uncommitted changes.

Worktrees
CommandWhat it doesNotes
git worktree add ../api-review feat/retry-budgetNew checkout of an existing branch in a sibling directorysafe Fails if that branch is checked out elsewhere.
git worktree add -b hotfix/tls ../api-hotfix origin/mainCreates the branch and checks it out in one stepsafe The incident-response idiom: a clean tree at origin/main in seconds.
git worktree add --detach ../api-bisectDetached checkout, no branch consumedsafe Correct for bisecting, building an old tag, or reading history.
git worktree listPaths, checked-out commits, branches, prunable markerssafe Add --porcelain for scripts.
git worktree remove ../api-reviewDeletes the directory and its admin datacare Refuses if the tree is dirty or has submodules; --force overrides and those edits are unrecoverable.
git worktree pruneCleans admin entries whose directories are gonesafe What you run after deleting a worktree with rm -rf.
git worktree move ../api-review ../review-retryRelocates a worktree and fixes both pointerssafe Use this instead of mv.
git worktree lock ../api-usb --reason "on removable disk"Stops prune from reclaiming a temporarily absent worktreesafe Essential for network shares and external drives.
git worktree repairRewrites the .git file and admin pointers after paths changesafe Git 2.30+. The fix after moving the main repository directory.

The one real constraint: a branch may be checked out in only one worktree at a time. Git enforces this because two trees sharing one branch ref would let a commit in one silently invalidate the index of the other. The error is explicit:

reviewing a colleague's branch without disturbing your own
$ git worktree add ../api-review main
fatal: 'main' is already used by worktree at '/Users/mk/src/payments-api'
# older Git says: fatal: 'main' is already checked out at ...

$ git worktree add ../api-review feat/retry-budget
Preparing worktree (checking out 'feat/retry-budget')
HEAD is now at 8d41b07 fix: retry budget on 429

$ cat ../api-review/.git
gitdir: /Users/mk/src/payments-api/.git/worktrees/api-review

$ git worktree list
/Users/mk/src/payments-api          a19f3e2 [main]
/Users/mk/src/payments-api-review   8d41b07 [feat/retry-budget]

$ git -C ../api-review status -s
# clean — my own dirty tree over here is completely untouched

$ git worktree remove ../api-review
$ git worktree list
/Users/mk/src/payments-api          a19f3e2 [main]

Practical notes. Untracked build artefacts are not shared, so each worktree pays its own node_modules or target/ — deliberate, and the reason the isolation works. Tools that assume .git is a directory occasionally misbehave in a linked worktree; almost all modern tooling is fine. git gc is repository-wide and honours the reflogs of every worktree, so a commit referenced only from another worktree's reflog is safe. Configuration is shared unless you enable extensions.worktreeConfig and use git config --worktree. And submodules are per-worktree: a new worktree needs its own git submodule update --init --recursive.

Interview · "your tree is dirty and a colleague needs a review now"

The weak answer is git stash. The strong answer is git worktree add, and the reason it is strong is the object store: a linked worktree shares objects and refs, so it costs a checkout rather than a clone, and your work in progress is never serialised, replayed or risked. Expect the follow-up: what actually is a stash? A commit — a merge commit whose parents are HEAD, the index state, and optionally the untracked files — pointed at by refs/stash, with the stack implemented as that ref's reflog. Say that, and then say that drop removes a reflog entry rather than the commit, which is why git fsck --unreachable recovers a dropped stash. Candidates who know the stash is a commit almost always know the rest of the object model too.

Submodules: a pinned pointer to another repository

A submodule is one tree entry. When you run git submodule add, Git records in the superproject's tree an entry with mode 160000 — a gitlink — whose hash is a commit id belonging to a completely different repository, and writes the path-to-URL mapping into .gitmodules. The superproject stores no files from the submodule and none of its objects. It stores forty hexadecimal characters and a URL.

superproject payments-api submodule vendor/sdk * 8d41b07 chore: bump sdk to 2.4.0 --> * c3e0d5f (tag: v2.4.0) | | * a19f3e2 feat: retry budget * 61ce4aa fix: connection timeouts | (pointer unchanged) | * 4c7d0e1 chore: vendor the sdk --> * b0f9d22 (tag: v2.3.1) The superproject tree holds only: 160000 commit c3e0d5f vendor/sdk No blobs, no history, no objects from the submodule. One pointer.

Everything surprising about submodules follows from that. The pointer names a commit, not a branch, so git submodule update checks out that commit and leaves the submodule's HEAD detached. Commit inside a submodule without first creating a branch and your work is reachable from nothing; the next submodule update moves HEAD and your commits become garbage-collectable. This is the single most common way people lose work in a submodule, and the recovery is the reflog of the submodule repository: git -C vendor/sdk reflog, then git -C vendor/sdk switch -c rescue <sha>.

Submodules
CommandWhat it doesNotes
git submodule add https://github.com/acme/sdk vendor/sdkClones it, writes .gitmodules, stages the gitlinkcare Commit .gitmodules and the gitlink together or the repo is broken for everyone else.
git clone --recurse-submodules git@github.com:acme/api.gitClone plus init plus checkout of every submodulesafe Add -j8 to clone submodules in parallel.
git submodule update --init --recursivePopulates submodules in an already-cloned repositorysafe The command every onboarding document forgets.
git submodule status --recursivePrints each pointer with a state prefixsafe - not initialised, + checked out at a different commit, U conflicted.
git submodule update --remote vendor/sdkFetches and moves to the tip of submodule.<name>.branchcare Defaults to the remote's default branch. This changes the pointer — you must still commit it.
git submodule foreach --recursive 'git switch main && git pull'Runs a shell command in every submodulecare Non-zero exit aborts the walk unless you append || true.
git submodule sync --recursivePushes changed .gitmodules URLs into .git/configsafe Required after a host migration; without it clones keep using the old URL.
git push --recurse-submodules=on-demandPushes submodule commits before the superproject commit that needs themsafe Set push.recurseSubmodules=check as a minimum. See the callout.
git submodule deinit -f vendor/sdkRemoves the working tree and the .git/config entrydestructive Uncommitted submodule edits are lost. The submodule's own objects survive under .git/modules/vendor/sdk.
git config --global submodule.recurse trueMakes pull, checkout and switch update submodules automaticallycare Removes most day-to-day surprise; also silently discards detached submodule work.
git config --global diff.submodule logShows submodule bumps as commit lists instead of two hashessafe Turns an unreviewable diff into a reviewable one.
The outage: a gitlink pointing at a commit that exists only on your laptop

You commit in vendor/sdk, bump the superproject pointer, and push the superproject — but not the submodule. Your machine builds perfectly because the object is in your local clone. Every CI runner and every colleague gets fatal: remote error: upload-pack: not our ref c3e0d5f or fatal: Needed a single revision … reference is not a tree, and the pipeline fails on checkout before a single test runs. Main is now un-buildable for the whole team by a commit that looks like a one-line change. Prevention is one setting: git config --global push.recurseSubmodules check, which refuses the superproject push until the submodule commit is published. Recovery is to push the submodule commit — and if that branch was rewritten or the work is gone, to revert the superproject bump immediately and restore the previous pointer, rather than trying to reconstruct the missing commit while the team is blocked.

Now the honest assessment. Submodules work, they are built in, and they genuinely solve the pinned-dependency problem. They also impose real costs:

  • Onboarding. A plain git clone produces empty directories and a confusing build failure. Every new contributor hits it once.
  • CI. Nothing recurses by default. GitHub Actions needs submodules: recursive on actions/checkout, and a private submodule additionally needs credentials that reach a second repository — a deploy key, an app token, or a url.<base>.insteadOf rewrite — because the default job token is scoped to one repository. This is the most common broken-pipeline cause in submodule repositories.
  • Perpetual dirtiness. modified: vendor/sdk (new commits), (modified content) and (untracked content) are three different states with three different fixes, and the difference is not obvious. Build output written inside a submodule makes the superproject permanently dirty until you set diff.ignoreSubmodules dirty or fix the ignore rules.
  • Merge conflicts on the pointer. Two branches bumping the same submodule conflict on forty characters with no useful three-way merge; you resolve by choosing a commit by hand and verifying it contains both changes.
  • Branch switching. Moving between superproject branches with different pointers leaves the submodule at the old commit unless submodule.recurse is on.
yaml · .github/workflows/ci.yml — checkout with a private submodule
- uses: actions/checkout@v4
  with:
    submodules: recursive
    fetch-depth: 0
    # the default GITHUB_TOKEN cannot read a second private repo
    token: ${{ secrets.SUBMODULE_READ_TOKEN }}
- name: Verify submodule pointers are published
  run: git submodule foreach --recursive 'git cat-file -e HEAD^{commit}'
Choosing between submodules and the alternatives
ApproachHow it worksUse it whenCost
SubmoduleGitlink to an exact commit of another repoGenuinely separate lifecycles; you need the exact upstream history; the dependency is also consumed elsewhereOnboarding, CI credentials, detached HEAD, dirty-state noise
Subtree mergegit subtree add --prefix=vendor/sdk … --squash copies the files into your historyConsumers must get everything from one clone with no extra commandsRepository grows; contributing changes back needs subtree push and discipline
Package registryPublish a versioned artefact; depend on it by version and lockfileThe dependency has a release cadence and a stable interface — the default answerNeeds registry infrastructure and a release process
MonorepoOne repository, one commit, many projectsChanges routinely span components and you want atomic cross-cutting commitsBuild tooling and repository-scale problems — see sparse checkout below
VendoringCopy the code in and record the source commit in a text fileSmall, rarely updated, forked-in-practice dependenciesManual updates; drift from upstream goes unnoticed

The rule of thumb: if the dependency has releases, use a package registry. If it does not and the coupling is tight, prefer a monorepo. Reach for submodules when you need a specific commit of a repository you do not control, and accept that you are also signing up for the tooling work.

Git LFS: pointers in history, bytes on a server

Git stores whole objects, deduplicated by content hash and delta-compressed within packs. That model is excellent for text and hostile to a 300 MB binary that changes every sprint: every version is a fresh incompressible blob, every clone downloads all of them forever, and the repository never gets smaller.

Git LFS solves this with a clean/smudge filter plus a pre-push hook. On commit, the clean filter replaces the file's content with a small text pointer; the real bytes go to an LFS server via a separate batch API on push. On checkout, the smudge filter swaps the pointer back for the bytes. Git's history contains only pointers — a few hundred bytes per version — so the pack stays small.

plaintext · what is actually committed for a tracked file
version https://git-lfs.github.com/spec/v1
oid sha256:4d7a2148a1f0c9b3e6d5028fa4c1b7e93d0f6a25c8e4b1d7f309a2c6b845e0f3
size 314572800
Git LFS
CommandWhat it doesNotes
git lfs installWrites the filter.lfs.* stanza into ~/.gitconfigsafe Per user and machine, not per repository. Every contributor and every CI image must run it.
git lfs track "*.psd"Adds a pattern to .gitattributescare Quote the glob or the shell expands it. Commit .gitattributes in the same commit.
git lfs ls-filesLists the LFS-managed files in the current checkoutsafe A * marker means the real content is present; - means you have a pointer only.
git lfs statusShows which staged files will be cleaned to pointerssafe Run before committing a new binary type.
git lfs pull --include "assets/ui/**"Downloads content for a subset of pointerssafe Pairs with a skip-smudge clone to avoid fetching the world.
git lfs migrate info --everything --above=10MBReports which extensions dominate history, by sizesafe Always the first command. Read-only.
git lfs migrate import --everything --include="*.psd,*.mp4"Rewrites all history, converting matching blobs to pointersdestructive Every commit gets a new hash. Same blast radius as filter-repo — see the recovery note.
git lfs migrate import --no-rewrite path/to/big.binConverts files in a new commit, leaving history alonecare History keeps the old fat blobs; the repo does not shrink, but nobody has to re-clone.
git lfs migrate export --include="*.psd"Converts pointers back to real blobs in historydestructive The exit door from LFS. Also a full rewrite.
git lfs pruneDeletes local LFS objects that are old and pushedcare Local cache only — it never touches the server. Re-download with lfs pull.
git lfs envPrints endpoint, credentials helper and filter configurationsafe First stop when CI produces pointer files.
A clone without LFS gives you 130-byte files, and nothing warns you

If git lfs install has not run on that machine, the smudge filter is not configured, checkout writes the pointer text to disk, and your build reads a three-line ASCII file where a texture, model or installer should be. The symptoms are absurd — invalid PNG signature, a video player rejecting a file, a Docker build embedding a 130-byte binary — and none of them mention LFS. Diagnose with file assets/logo.psd and git lfs env; fix with git lfs install && git lfs pull. The second trap is the bill: LFS bandwidth is metered separately from repository storage, and a CI job that clones with LFS on every push can exhaust a monthly quota in days, after which every clone fails with batch response: This repository is over its data quota — including developers'. Mitigate with GIT_LFS_SKIP_SMUDGE=1 on clone plus a targeted git lfs pull --include, a persistent CI cache of .git/lfs/objects, and lfs.fetchexclude for assets the build does not need.

01 · Survey
Find the weightgit lfs migrate info --everything
02 · Provision
LFS server & quotastorage and bandwidth, both
03 · Freeze
Lock branchesrewrite invalidates every open PR
04 · Mirror
Back up firstgit clone --mirror, kept offline
05 · Migrate
import --everythingon the mirror, never a working clone
06 · Verify
Size, CI, checkoutlfs ls-files on a fresh clone
07 · Re-clone
Humans and runnersevery old clone is now wrong

git lfs migrate import --everything rewrites every commit, so every hash changes, every branch and tag must be force-pushed, and every existing clone and open pull request is invalidated. Treat it as the history-rewrite operation it is: announce a window, freeze merges, work on a mirror clone, and keep that mirror until the team has re-cloned — it is your only recovery path if the migration is wrong. Note too that rewriting history does not reclaim server storage on its own; the old objects persist on the forge until its garbage collection runs, which on most hosts means opening a support request.

Sparse checkout and partial clone

LFS addresses files that are individually large. A different problem is a repository that is large in aggregate — millions of files, decades of history — where the pain is clone time, checkout time and the cost of git status. Two independent mechanisms address it, and they compose.

Partial clone
Server-side filtering at clone: --filter=blob:none omits all file contents (fetched on demand), --filter=tree:0 omits trees as well. The remote is recorded as a promisor and missing objects are fetched lazily when something asks for them.
Sparse checkout
Client-side filtering of the working tree: the index still lists every path, but only files matching your patterns are written to disk. Cone mode restricts patterns to whole directories, which makes the matching fast enough for very large trees.
Shallow clone
--depth 1 truncates history. Cheap and blunt: no git log, no merge-base, no blame. Fine for a build runner, wrong for a developer.
Promisor fetch
The on-demand round trip a blobless clone makes when you finally need content. It is why git log -p or git blame in such a clone can be slow, and why it fails outright offline.
Working with very large repositories
CommandWhat it doesNotes
git clone --filter=blob:none git@github.com:acme/mono.gitFull history and trees, file contents on demandsafe The best general-purpose choice for a huge repo; history stays intact.
git clone --filter=tree:0 --depth 1 …Minimal clone for a buildcare Anything historical becomes a network call, or fails when the runner has no egress.
git clone --filter=blob:none --sparse …Blobless clone that checks out only the root directorysafe Then sparse-checkout set the directories you need.
git sparse-checkout set --cone services/payments libs/httpRestricts the working tree to those directoriescare Files outside the cone are removed from disk. Uncommitted edits there are lost.
git sparse-checkout add services/ledgerWidens the cone without rewriting the listsafe Git 2.25+.
git sparse-checkout listPrints the active patternssafe Truth lives in .git/info/sparse-checkout.
git sparse-checkout reapplyRe-applies patterns after a merge or checkout resurrects filessafe The fix for "why is that directory back?".
git sparse-checkout disableRestores the full working treesafe The recovery path for an over-aggressive set — everything committed comes back.
git maintenance startSchedules background prefetch, commit-graph and incremental repacksafe Git 2.30+. The largest easy win on a big repository.
git config feature.manyFiles trueEnables index v4, untracked cache and lazy index loadingcare Umbrella setting; its members may change between versions.

Two cautions. A sparse checkout still commits the whole tree — you are hiding files, not excluding them, so a merge can produce changes in paths you cannot see; that is normal and correct. And a partial clone makes availability a dependency: an operation that would have been local now needs the server, so a blobless clone plus an outage means git blame stops working. For developer laptops, --filter=blob:none with cone-mode sparse checkout and background maintenance is the combination that scales; for CI, prefer a shallow, treeless, cached clone and never let a runner depend on history it did not ask for.

Defaults worth setting once

push.recurseSubmodules=check and diff.submodule=log if you have submodules; submodule.recurse=true only once the team knows detached HEAD. git lfs install in your base CI image, not in a pipeline step. And reach for git worktree add rather than git stash the moment a second task appears — stash is for the sixty seconds before you pull, not for parking work you intend to come back to. Work you intend to come back to is called a branch.

G08

Forensics: bisect, blame & log
