Every production incident eventually reduces to two questions: when did this start, and what changed. Git already holds the answer — the difficulty is that the three tools that extract it search fundamentally different things, and reaching for the wrong one costs hours you do not have at 03:00.

Three searches, three domains

The reason engineers flail during a regression hunt is not missing flags. It is that they treat blame, log and bisect as interchangeable ways of "looking at history", when each searches a different space and only one of them can observe behaviour at all.

blame
Searches lines. For each line currently in a file, it names the most recent commit that produced it. It cannot see a line that was deleted, and it cannot tell you whether the commit it names is the one that broke anything.
log
Searches diff text and metadata. With -S, -G, --grep and -L it finds commits whose content or message matches a pattern. It is a full-text index over every change ever made — but only over text you can describe in advance.
bisect
Searches behaviour. It runs your test at chosen commits and performs a binary search over the commit graph. It is the only one of the three that can find a regression you cannot characterise textually — a timing change, a dependency bump, an ordering-dependent bug.

The practical corollary: if you can describe the symptom as a string, start with log -S; if you can describe it only as a test, start with bisect; use blame last, to understand why the change you found was made, not to find it. Blame is an attribution tool wearing an investigation tool's clothes.

01 · Reproduce
Write a probeexit 0 good, exit 1 bad, under 60s
02 · Bound
Known good, known bada release tag you actually trust
03 · Verify ends
Run the probe at bothif the good end fails, your bounds are wrong
04 · Bisect
Binary searchlog₂(n) builds, not n
05 · Confirm
Revert the culpritdoes the symptom actually disappear?
06 · Attribute
blame and log -Swhy the change was made
07 · Reach
tag --containswhich shipped releases carry it

Bisect: binary search over behaviour

Bisect maintains two sets of commits — those known good and those known bad — and repeatedly checks out a commit whose testing maximally shrinks the remaining candidate set. Because the "goodness" of a commit is assumed monotonic (good, then bad, and never back again), each test halves the search space. Four thousand commits therefore cost twelve builds, not four thousand: log₂(4000) ≈ 11.97, and you cannot do a fractional build.

The arithmetic is worth internalising because it changes what you consider feasible. A ten-minute build across 4,000 commits is two hours of bisect — an afternoon's work, entirely automatable. Linearly, it would be twenty-seven days. This is why a slow, correct probe still beats a fast, manual read of the diff.

Why twelve, and what each step costs
TestCandidates beforeGit printsRemaining after
140132006 revisions left … (roughly 11 steps)2006
220061003 revisions left … (roughly 10 steps)1003
5250125 revisions left … (roughly 7 steps)125
1073 revisions left … (roughly 2 steps)3
1210 revisions left … (roughly 0 steps)0 — culprit named

Automation and the exit-code contract

git bisect run replaces you with a script, and the contract it obeys is unusual enough to be a common source of wrong answers. The exit code is not a boolean.

What git bisect run does with your exit code
Exit codeInterpreted asConsequence
0goodThe commit and all its ancestors leave the candidate set
1–124, 126, 127badIncludes command not found (127) and not executable (126)
125skipUntestable — Git tries a neighbouring commit instead
128–255abortThe whole bisect stops, state preserved for git bisect log
bash · scripts/bisect-probe.sh
#!/usr/bin/env bash
# Exit-code contract for `git bisect run`:
#   0 = good, 1 = bad, 125 = untestable/skip, 128+ = abort the bisect.
set -u

# Commits that do not build are UNTESTABLE, not bad. Report 125.
if ! go build ./... >/dev/null 2>&1; then
  exit 125
fi

# A missing tool exits 127, which git reads as BAD. Guard it explicitly.
command -v curl >/dev/null 2>&1 || exit 125

./bin/api --port 18080 &
api_pid=$!
trap 'kill "$api_pid" 2>/dev/null' EXIT

for _ in $(seq 40); do
  curl -sf localhost:18080/healthz >/dev/null 2>&1 && break
  sleep 0.25
done

status=$(curl -s -o /dev/null -w '%{http_code}' \
  -X POST localhost:18080/v1/charges \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"amount_cents":100,"currency":"gbp"}')

[ "$status" = "201" ] && exit 0   # good
exit 1                            # bad
the same search, unattended
$ git bisect start main v4.7.0
Bisecting: 2006 revisions left to test after this (roughly 11 steps)

$ git bisect run ./scripts/bisect-probe.sh
running './scripts/bisect-probe.sh'
Bisecting: 1003 revisions left to test after this (roughly 10 steps)
running './scripts/bisect-probe.sh'
error: build failed on 3f9c2a1 — treated as untestable (exit 125)
Bisecting: 501 revisions left to test after this (roughly 9 steps)
...
8a1f3c9 is the first bad commit
bisect run success

# Resume a session later, on another machine, or after a mistake:
$ git bisect replay /tmp/bisect-CHG-4411.log
we are not bisecting.
Bisecting: 501 revisions left to test after this (roughly 9 steps)
Data-loss trap in probe scripts

The single most expensive mistake in bisect automation is putting git clean -xdf at the top of the probe "to get a clean build". Bisect will run that script ten to twenty times against your own working tree, and -x deletes ignored files: your .env, local SQLite databases, generated certificates, editor state, and any untracked file you had not yet added. None of it was ever in Git, so there is no recovery path — no reflog, no fsck, nothing. If a clean tree is genuinely required, bisect inside a throwaway worktree (git worktree add ../bisect-tmp --detach) or a container, never in the checkout you work in. The same applies to any probe that runs git checkout, git stash or git reset --hard: those commands also corrupt bisect's own state, and the session ends up testing commits you never chose.

Why bisect blamed the wrong commit

Three failure modes, all producing a confident, wrong answer. Exit 127. A typo in a binary name, a probe that is not chmod +x, or a tool absent from older commits' toolchain returns 126 or 127, which Git reads as bad; the search then walks backwards and names the earliest commit in the range. Flakiness. A test that fails one run in five turns a monotonic search into a random walk; bisect has no notion of confidence and will not re-test. Loop the assertion inside the probe until the signal is deterministic. Non-monotonic history. If a bug was introduced, fixed, and reintroduced, bisect finds a transition, not the one you care about — git bisect log plus git bisect view will show the suspicious topology. Always finish by reverting the named commit on a scratch branch and confirming the symptom disappears.

Making a repository bisectable

Bisect is not a tool you deploy during an incident; it is a property your repository either has or does not. Three practices decide it, and all three are paid for in normal working weeks, not on the night of the outage.

  1. Small, coherent commits. The output of a bisect is one commit. If that commit is a 4,000-line squash of a fortnight's work, you have converted a search problem into a code-review problem and gained little. Commits that do one thing turn the answer into a diff you can read in a minute.
  2. A green build at every commit. Every commit that does not compile is a forced skip, and skips inflate the result from a commit to a range. Enforce it by rebasing rather than merging fixup commits into a branch, and by running CI on each commit of a pull request — not only on its tip.
  3. A fast reproduction script in the repository. The probe belongs in scripts/, under version control, taking its parameters from the environment. Written during the incident it takes forty minutes; written in advance it takes zero, and it stays correct because it is the same script CI runs.

There is a fourth, subtler requirement: the probe must test the behaviour, not the current test suite. Checking out a commit from six months ago also checks out that commit's tests, which may not contain the assertion you care about. The robust pattern is a probe that lives outside the tree — copied in from /tmp, or invoked with git bisect run bash /tmp/probe.sh — so that the same test runs against every commit.

Interview · "how many builds, and when does bisect lie?"

The expected first answer is the arithmetic: ⌈log₂(4000)⌉ = 12 builds, because each verdict discards half the candidates. The answer that distinguishes an operator is the second half — bisect's correctness rests on three assumptions, and naming them is the point of the question. First, monotonicity: exactly one good→bad transition exists in the range. Second, testability: every commit builds, or is honestly reported as 125 rather than falling through to "bad". Third, determinism: the probe returns the same verdict for the same commit every time. Break any one and bisect still terminates, still prints "is the first bad commit", and is still wrong. Follow up with the topology caveat: on a merge-heavy history, bisect walks the full DAG and may land inside a feature branch that was never on main alone, which is what --first-parent exists to avoid.

Blame, and how to stop a reformat destroying it

Blame answers "which commit last produced this line". Run bare, it answers it badly: a whitespace change, a lint autofix, a file split or a language-wide formatter rewrites every line and buries the real author under a commit called chore: run prettier. Four flags restore its usefulness, and they should be your default rather than an escalation.

git blame
CommandWhat it doesNotes
git blame -w -- internal/payments/retry.goIgnores whitespace-only changes when assigning linessafe make this reflexive; costs nothing
git blame -w -M -- internal/payments/retry.goAlso detects lines moved or copied within the filesafe defeats "I reordered the functions"
git blame -w -C -C -- internal/payments/retry.goDetects lines moved from other files, including files the commit did not modifycare the second -C widens the search and slows it noticeably
git blame -w -C -C -C -- internal/payments/retry.goSearches every file in every commit for the origincare minutes on a large repo — pair it with -L
git blame -L 88,104 -- internal/payments/retry.goRestricts blame to a line rangesafe also accepts -L 88,+20 and -L 88,-10
git blame -L :chargeWithRetry:internal/payments/retry.goRestricts blame to a named functionsafe uses the funcname regex for the file's language
git blame -L '/func chargeWithRetry/,+40' -- internal/payments/retry.goRange anchored on a regex matchsafe survives the line numbers shifting
git blame --ignore-rev 9f2c8b71a4d3e05c -- src/app.tsAttributes past one specific commitsafe Git 2.23+
git blame --ignore-revs-file .git-blame-ignore-revs -- src/app.tsAttributes past every commit listed in the filesafe entries must be full 40-character SHAs
git config blame.ignoreRevsFile .git-blame-ignore-revsMakes that the default for this clonesafe local config — it is not carried by the repository, so document it
git config blame.markIgnoredLines truePrefixes ? on lines whose attribution was inferredsafe pair with blame.markUnblamableLines for *
git blame --reverse v4.0.0..HEAD -- internal/payments/retry.goFor each line, the last commit in which it still existedcare the child of that commit is the one that removed the line
git blame v4.7.0 -- internal/legacy/gateway.goBlames a file as it stood at an older commitsafe the only way to blame a file that no longer exists
git blame --porcelain -- src/app.tsStable machine-readable outputsafe what editor plugins consume; --line-porcelain repeats the header per line
git log -L 88,104:internal/payments/retry.goThe full history of a line range, every commit that touched itsafe usually what people actually wanted instead of blame

A mass reformat is a one-day decision with a multi-year cost, and .git-blame-ignore-revs is how you pay it down. Commit the file at the repository root, list the reformat SHAs with a dated comment, and set the config in the onboarding script. GitHub reads the same file for its own blame view, so the fix applies in the browser too.

plaintext · .git-blame-ignore-revs
# Revisions listed here are skipped by `git blame`.
# Enable locally:  git config blame.ignoreRevsFile .git-blame-ignore-revs
# Entries MUST be full 40-character object names; abbreviations make blame abort.

# 2025-03-14  repo-wide prettier 3 reformat, no behavioural change
9f2c8b71a4d3e05c6b1a8f4d2e7c3b9a0d5e6f18

# 2025-06-02  gofmt sweep after the Go 1.24 upgrade
3c7a1e59d8b2f406a9c5e3d1b7f8a2c4e6d09b53
Reverse blame

git blame --reverse START..END inverts the direction of the search: instead of walking back to find where a line came from, it walks forward from START to find where each line stopped existing. Read the output carefully — the commit shown is the last one in which the line survived, so the deletion happened in one of its children. For a whole file the blunter tool is faster: git log --diff-filter=D --oneline -- path/to/file names the deleting commit, and git show <sha>^:path/to/file prints the contents as they were the moment before.

Log as a search index

Most of what people do by scrolling through git log is a query they did not know how to write. The pickaxe family turns history into a searchable corpus.

-S counts occurrences: it shows only commits where the number of occurrences of the string changed, which means introduction and removal, skipping every commit that merely moved the line about. -G takes a regular expression and matches any commit whose diff text contains it, moves included. The difference matters: -S'DEFAULT_TIMEOUT' answers "when did this constant appear or vanish"; -G'DEFAULT_TIMEOUT' answers "which commits ever touched a line mentioning it". Both accept a pathspec, both accept --all to search every ref rather than the current branch, and both are far faster than they look because Git only diffs the commits it must.

Log, and answering "where did this come from"
CommandWhat it doesNotes
git log -S'DEFAULT_TIMEOUT' --oneline --allCommits where that string's occurrence count changedsafe the pickaxe; the first thing to try
git log -S'timeout_[0-9]+' --pickaxe-regex --onelineTreats the pickaxe argument as a regexsafe still counting occurrences, not matching text
git log -G'time\.Sleep\(' --onelineAny commit whose diff text matches the regexsafe catches pure moves that -S hides
git log -S'x' --pickaxe-allShows every file in a matching commit, not only the matching onessafe useful when the change spans files
git log --follow --oneline -- internal/pay/charge.goHistory across renamescare exactly one pathspec, and it is a similarity heuristic
git log --diff-filter=D --oneline -- internal/legacy/gateway.goThe commit that deleted a pathsafe other filters: A added, M modified, R renamed
git show 8a1f3c9^:internal/legacy/gateway.goPrints a file as it was before a commitsafe the recovery route for deleted content
git log --oneline --source --allAnnotates each commit with the ref it was reached fromsafe answers "which branch is this commit on"
git name-rev --name-only 8a1f3c9Names a commit relative to the nearest ref, e.g. tags/v4.8.0~12safe cheap; works on any reachable object
git describe --contains 8a1f3c9The first tag that contains the commitsafe the direct answer to "which release has the fix"
git describe --tags --always --dirtyA build stamp for the current checkoutsafe bake this into artefacts; it makes every later forensic query trivial
git branch -a --contains 8a1f3c9Every local and remote branch containing the commitsafe --no-contains inverts it
git tag --contains 8a1f3c9 | sort -V | head -1Earliest release carrying the changesafe sort -V because tags sort lexically otherwise
git log --merges --ancestry-path 8a1f3c9..mainThe merges on the path from a commit to a branchsafe the last line is the merge that brought it in
git rev-list --count v4.7.0..mainHow many commits separate two pointssafe the number that sizes your bisect
git log --oneline --no-merges v4.7.3..v4.8.0What shipped between two releasessafe add --first-parent for the release-branch narrative

Two dots, three dots, and the inversion nobody warns you about

The same syntax means near-opposite things to log and to diff, and this is responsible for more wrong answers during incidents than any other Git detail. In log, two dots is asymmetric set subtraction and three dots is the symmetric difference. In diff, two dots is a plain comparison of two trees and three dots is the merge-base comparison — the "what did this branch add" view that a pull request shows you.

o---o---o---o A (main) / ---o---o---o--X \ o---o---o B (feature/charge-retry) X = git merge-base A B git log A..B 3 commits reachable from B, not from A git log B..A 4 commits reachable from A, not from B git log A...B 7 commits symmetric difference; --left-right marks < and > git diff A..B tree(A) vs tree(B) A's 4 commits appear as REMOVALS git diff A...B tree(X) vs tree(B) only B's 3 commits <-- what a PR shows

The operational consequence: when you ask "what does this branch change", git diff main feature/x is almost always the wrong command. If main has advanced since the branch forked, that diff presents everyone else's work as deletions in your branch, and a reviewer reading it will reject a perfectly good change. git diff main...feature/x is the correct question, and it is exactly what GitHub renders under Files changed. Conversely, when you ask "what commits are on this branch", git log main..feature/x — two dots — is right, and three dots would include main's commits too.

Mnemonic

Three dots always involves the merge base. In log it means "everything on either side of the base"; in diff it means "everything on the right side of the base". Two dots never involves the merge base at all: in log it is set subtraction, in diff it is two trees compared directly, which is why git diff A..B and git diff A B are identical.

Shortlog for release notes

git shortlog groups commits by author and is the fastest honest changelog you can produce. It reads from a range like any other log command, so it composes with tags, pathspecs and --no-merges.

release notes in three commands
$ git shortlog -sne --no-merges v4.7.3..v4.8.0
    62  R. Okonjo <rokonjo@example.com>
    41  L. Ferrante <lferrante@example.com>
     9  dependabot[bot] <support@github.com>

$ git shortlog --no-merges v4.7.3..v4.8.0 -- internal/payments
R. Okonjo (4):
      payments: reuse one http.Client across retry attempts
      payments: cap the retry budget per idempotency key

$ git log --no-merges --format='* %s (%h)' v4.7.3..v4.8.0 > CHANGELOG.frag
$ git shortlog -sn --group=trailer:Co-authored-by v4.7.3..v4.8.0
     7  L. Ferrante

--group arrived in Git 2.29 and can group by author, committer or any trailer, which is how you credit pair-programmed and co-authored work that a plain author count hides. Add .mailmap at the repository root to collapse the four spellings of the same person's email into one line.

The question-to-command table

Incident work is mostly translation: someone asks a question in English, and you need the command before your attention moves on. This is the lookup.

What you are asked, and what you type
Question you are askedCommandWhy that one
"When did this stop working?"git bisect run ./scripts/bisect-probe.shOnly bisect tests behaviour; log₂(n) builds
"When did this constant change?"git log -S'MAX_RETRIES' --oneline --allPickaxe finds introduction and removal, ignoring moves
"Which commits ever touched this pattern?"git log -G'time\.Sleep\(' --onelineRegex over diff text, moves included
"Who wrote this line, and why?"git blame -w -C -C -L 88,104 -- src/pay.goWhitespace and cross-file moves ignored
"How did this function get like this?"git log -L :chargeWithRetry:src/pay.goEvery commit touching the range, not only the last
"When was this file deleted, and what was in it?"git log --diff-filter=D -- path then git show SHA^:pathBlame cannot see lines that no longer exist
"When did this line disappear?"git blame --reverse v4.0.0..HEAD -- pathForward search; the child of the named commit removed it
"Which release first contains this fix?"git describe --contains 8a1f3c9Names the nearest tag containing the commit
"Is this on main yet?"git branch -a --contains 8a1f3c9Reachability, not the commit date
"Which merge brought it in?"git log --merges --ancestry-path 8a1f3c9..main | tail -1Restricts to the path between the two points
"What version is this running binary?"git describe --tags --always --dirtyOnly works if you stamped it at build time
"What does this PR actually change?"git diff main...feature/xThree-dot diff = merge base versus branch tip
"What is going into tonight's release?"git log --oneline --no-merges v4.8.0..release/4.9Two-dot log = commits on the right, not the left
"Who owns this subsystem now?"git shortlog -sne --no-merges --since=6.months -- internal/paymentsRecent activity, not the CODEOWNERS file's fiction
"Which ref does this dangling SHA belong to?"git name-rev --name-only 8a1f3c9Cheap; falls back to undefined if unreachable

One closing discipline. Forensics ends when you can state, in one sentence, which commit changed the behaviour, which releases contain it, and what the reverting diff looks like. Anything short of that — a suspicion, a plausible-looking blame line, a commit that "seems related" — is a hypothesis, and the cost of testing it is one git revert --no-commit on a scratch branch followed by the same probe you already wrote. Do that before you tell an incident channel you have found the cause.

G09

Hooks, config & ergonomics

Git has exactly two extension points: a cascade of configuration files that changes what commands do, and a directory of scripts it executes at fixed moments. Everything in this chapter — enforcing standards, killing repetitive friction, keeping two identities on one laptop — is one of those two, and the single most expensive misunderstanding is believing that a hook enforces anything.

The model: config decides, hooks observe

Before any command runs, Git assembles one flat key-value namespace by reading a fixed sequence of files, later ones overriding earlier ones. Then, at a dozen defined points during the command, it looks for an executable file with a specific name and runs it. That is the whole extension surface. There is no plugin API, no event bus, and no way for a repository to make your Git behave differently without your consent — which is a security property, not an oversight, and it is also why hooks cannot be an enforcement mechanism.

Keep the two straight and most confusion evaporates. Config is data Git reads; it is cheap, cascading, and inspectable with one command. Hooks are code Git executes; they are local, per-clone, never transferred by clone or fetch, and bypassable with a flag. Config makes Git less tedious for you. Hooks make mistakes cheaper to catch. Only the server enforces.

Client-side hooks: what each is genuinely good for

A fresh git init populates .git/hooks/ with a dozen *.sample files. Git ignores anything with that suffix; a hook is live when a file with the bare name exists and is executable. The name must match exactly, it must have a shebang or be a binary, and on Unix it must have the execute bit — a hook that silently does nothing is nine times out of ten chmod +x.

01 · Hook
pre-commitruns before the message editor · non-zero aborts
02 · Hook
prepare-commit-msgedits the message file before you see it
03 · You
editorskipped with -m or --no-edit
04 · Hook
commit-msgvalidates the finished message · non-zero aborts
05 · Hook
post-commitnotification only · exit status ignored
The client-side hooks worth writing, and the ones worth avoiding
HookArguments / stdinCan it abort?Genuinely good for
pre-commitnone; inspect the index with git diff --cachedYesFormatting, linting and secret scanning on staged content only. The workhorse.
prepare-commit-msg$1 message file, $2 source (message, template, merge, squash, commit), $3 SHA when amendingYesInjecting the ticket ID parsed from the branch name, or a Co-authored-by trailer. Not validation.
commit-msg$1 path to the message fileYesConventional Commits, subject-length limits, required trailers. Rewriting the file in place is legal.
pre-push$1 remote name, $2 URL; stdin lines of local-ref local-sha remote-ref remote-shaYesThe expensive checks — type-check, unit tests, "is this main?". Runs once, not per commit.
post-checkout$1 old HEAD, $2 new HEAD, $3 flag (1 branch switch, 0 file checkout)NoReinstalling dependencies when a lockfile changed. Guard on $3 or it fires on every git restore.
post-merge$1 is 1 for a squash mergeNoThe same lockfile job for git pull. Pair it with post-checkout; neither covers the other.
post-rewrite$1 is amend or rebase; stdin pairs of old and new SHANoUpdating external systems that recorded commit hashes after a rebase.
pre-rebase$1 upstream, $2 branch being rebasedYesRefusing to rebase a branch that has already been published. Rarely worth the friction.

Three behaviours cause most surprises. First, pre-commit sees the working tree, not the index — if you lint the files on disk you have just validated content that is not being committed, which is why every serious framework stashes unstaged changes before running. Second, --no-verify (-n) skips pre-commit and commit-msg on git commit, and skips pre-push on git push. Third, commits replayed by git rebase go through the sequencer rather than through git commit, so per-commit hooks do not fire for them at all.

Keep pre-commit under about two seconds. Anything slower gets bypassed habitually, and a hook that everyone bypasses is worse than no hook, because the team believes the check is running. Push the slow work to pre-push, and the authoritative work to CI.

The secret that shipped anyway

The canonical failure is a team that adds gitleaks as a pre-commit hook, declares secret management solved, and six weeks later has an AWS key in main — committed by someone whose IDE commits through libgit2 (which does not run hooks at all), or who typed -n to get past a slow linter. Once the blob is in the object database it is in every clone, every fork and every CI cache; rewriting history with git filter-repo does not un-leak it. The credential must be rotated, always, before anything else. The hook is a convenience that saves you the rotation on a good day. The enforcement must be a server-side push protection rule and a scanner in CI, because those are the only checks the author cannot skip.

Hooks are not cloned — which is why teams use a manager

.git/hooks/ lives inside .git, which is not part of any tree, so nothing in it is versioned, pushed, or received by a clone. A new joiner gets the .sample files and nothing else. This is deliberate: if clone installed executable scripts from a stranger's repository, cloning would be arbitrary code execution.

a hook that works locally and exists nowhere else
$ cat .git/hooks/pre-commit
#!/bin/sh
if git diff --cached -S'debugger' --name-only | grep -q . ; then
  echo "blocked: debugger statement in staged changes" >&2; exit 1
fi

$ chmod +x .git/hooks/pre-commit
$ git add b.js && git commit -m "add b"
blocked: debugger statement in staged changes

# It works. Now clone the repository the way a colleague would.
$ git clone -q . ../clone1
$ ls ../clone1/.git/hooks/ | grep -v sample
# (nothing)

$ git -C ../clone1 config core.hooksPath
# (unset — so .git/hooks is used, and it is empty)

# The fix: a versioned directory, opted into once per clone.
$ git config core.hooksPath .githooks

core.hooksPath (Git 2.9+) points Git at a directory of your choosing. Set it to a tracked .githooks/ and the scripts become reviewable, versioned code. The cost is one setup command per clone, which belongs in the project's bootstrap script — make setup, npm run prepare, whatever developers already run before their first build. Note that core.hooksPath replaces .git/hooks rather than adding to it, and a value set globally silently disables every repository's hooks; that is worth checking first when hooks mysteriously stop firing.

Beyond a handful of scripts, use a manager. All three solve the same problems — installation, per-language toolchains, running only on staged files, and restoring the working tree if a hook rewrites a file.

pre-commit (Python)

Language-agnostic despite the name. Declares hooks as pinned Git repositories, builds isolated environments for each, and stashes unstaged changes so hooks see exactly the staged content. Best when the repo spans several languages. pre-commit autoupdate bumps the pins.

lefthook (Go)

A single static binary, no runtime to install, genuinely parallel, and the fastest of the three. {staged_files} and stage_fixed make formatting-then-restaging a one-liner. Best when speed matters or the team is polyglot but not Python-centric.

husky + lint-staged (Node)

The default in the JavaScript ecosystem. Since v9 a hook is just a shell file in .husky/; the prepare lifecycle script is what installs it on npm install, which is the whole trick. Only sensible if Node is already a hard dependency.

yaml · lefthook.yml
# Staged files only. Fast checks at commit, slow ones at push.
pre-commit:
  parallel: true
  jobs:
    - name: format
      glob: "*.{ts,tsx,js,jsx,json,css,md}"
      run: npx prettier --write {staged_files}
      stage_fixed: true          # re-add what the formatter changed

    - name: lint
      glob: "*.{ts,tsx}"
      run: npx eslint --max-warnings=0 {staged_files}

    - name: secrets
      run: gitleaks protect --staged --redact --no-banner

commit-msg:
  jobs:
    - name: conventional
      run: npx commitlint --edit {1}

pre-push:
  jobs:
    - name: types
      run: npx tsc --noEmit
    - name: no-direct-main
      run: test "$(git branch --show-current)" != "main"

# Install once per clone (put this in the project bootstrap script):
#   lefthook install
# `jobs:` needs lefthook 1.10+; older versions spell it `commands:`.
yaml · .pre-commit-config.yaml
repos:
  - repo: https://github.com/pre-commit/pre-commit-hooks
    rev: v5.0.0
    hooks:
      - id: trailing-whitespace
      - id: end-of-file-fixer
      - id: check-merge-conflict     # catches a committed <<<<<<< marker
      - id: check-added-large-files
        args: ["--maxkb=512"]

  - repo: https://github.com/gitleaks/gitleaks
    rev: v8.21.2
    hooks:
      - id: gitleaks

  - repo: https://github.com/astral-sh/ruff-pre-commit
    rev: v0.7.4
    hooks:
      - id: ruff
        args: ["--fix"]
      - id: ruff-format

# pre-commit install --install-hooks -t pre-commit -t commit-msg -t pre-push
# pre-commit run --all-files      # the same checks, in CI, unskippable

That last line is the point. Whatever the hooks run, run it again in CI with the identical configuration. The hook exists to give the author a two-second answer instead of a four-minute one; CI exists to make the answer binding.

Server-side hooks, and why rulesets replaced them

On the receiving repository, three hooks bracket a push. pre-receive runs once with every ref update on stdin as old-sha new-sha ref-name and can reject the entire push atomically. update runs once per ref and can reject that ref alone. post-receive runs after the refs have moved and cannot reject anything — it is for notifications, mirroring and deploy triggers. A fourth, proc-receive (Git 2.29+), is what lets forges implement pull requests as pushes to a magic ref.

These are the only checks a developer cannot bypass, because they execute on hardware the developer does not control. They are also, on GitHub and GitLab, largely obsolete for application teams: pre-receive hooks require GitHub Enterprise Server or a self-managed GitLab instance, they run on the git host's critical path, a bug in one blocks every push organisation-wide, and they are invisible to the people they affect. Platform rulesets — branch protection, required status checks, required reviews, push rules, path and file-size restrictions, required signatures, push protection for secrets — cover most of the same ground declaratively, apply across many repositories at once, and report failures in the UI where the author is already looking.

Where to put a given rule
RuleRight placeWhy not elsewhere
Code is formattedClient hook + CI checkServer hooks cannot fix the file; a failing push with no autofix is pure friction.
Commit message conventionClient commit-msg + CI on the PR titleSquash merges replace the message anyway; enforce where the merge message is authored.
No secretsPlatform push protection + CI scanClient-only means --no-verify defeats it. This is the case that must be unskippable.
No force-push to mainRuleset / branch protectionA client hook protects one clone; the rule must hold for CI bots and the web UI too.
Signed commits requiredRulesetVerification needs the organisation's enrolled keys, which only the server has.
Max file size, banned pathsRuleset push rules, plus check-added-large-filesOnce the blob is received, every future clone pays for it — reject at the boundary.
Bespoke internal policy the platform cannot expresspre-receive on a self-managed hostAccept the operational cost knowingly; it is on the push path for everyone.

Configuration levels, and where a value actually came from

Git reads config files in a fixed order and the last writer wins. Later files override earlier ones; within a single file, a later line overrides an earlier one.

System
/etc/gitconfig or $(prefix)/etc/gitconfig. Written with --system. Set by the package manager or a fleet management tool, rarely by you.
Global
~/.gitconfig, or $XDG_CONFIG_HOME/git/config (usually ~/.config/git/config) if it exists. --global writes to ~/.gitconfig unless the XDG file already exists. This is where almost everything below belongs.
Local
.git/config in the repository. Written with --local, the default for a bare git config inside a repo. Remotes and per-project identity live here.
Worktree
.git/config.worktree, active only after git config --local extensions.worktreeConfig true. The only way to give two worktrees of one repository different settings — most usefully a different core.sparseCheckout.
Command line
git -c key=value … beats all of them, for one invocation. The right way to test a setting before adopting it.

Environment variables sit alongside: GIT_CONFIG_GLOBAL and GIT_CONFIG_SYSTEM redirect or (set to /dev/null) disable those files, which is how you reproduce a colleague's "clean machine" behaviour without touching your own.

config archaeology — why is this the wrong email?
$ git config --show-origin --show-scope --get user.email
global  file:/Users/dev/.gitconfig    personal@example.com

# Wrong address for a work repo. Where should it have come from?
$ git config --show-origin --show-scope --get-regexp 'user\.'
global  file:/Users/dev/.gitconfig    user.name  Ada Byron
global  file:/Users/dev/.gitconfig    user.email personal@example.com

# The conditional include never fired. Check the condition:
$ git rev-parse --show-toplevel
/Users/dev/Work/acme/payments-api
# ...but the includeIf said gitdir:~/work/ — and gitdir is case-sensitive.

$ git config --global --edit    # fix to gitdir/i:~/work/ , then re-check
$ git config --show-origin --show-scope --get user.email
global  file:/Users/dev/.config/git/work.inc    ada@acme.io

# Note the scope says "global" but the file is the include. That is the
# only way to see which included file won, and it is why --show-origin
# beats --list for debugging.

$ git config --list --show-origin | grep -c .
147
$ git config --get-all credential.helper    # multi-valued: order matters
osxkeychain

Two flags do all the diagnostic work. --show-origin names the file a value came from, following includes; --show-scope (Git 2.26+) labels it system, global, local, worktree or command. For multi-valued keys such as credential.helper and remote.*.fetch, --get returns the last value only — use --get-all, and remember that setting an empty value resets an inherited list. Git 2.46 added the friendlier git config get / set / unset / list subcommands; the flag forms still work everywhere.

The settings worth turning on for everyone

Each of these fixes a specific recurring annoyance or a specific failure. None changes what a commit contains, so none of them can start an argument.

Global defaults, with the justification
CommandWhat it doesNotes
git config --global rerere.enabled trueRecords how you resolved a conflict and replays it when the same conflict reappearscare Pays for itself on any long-lived rebase. Cache in .git/rr-cache, local only. See the warning below.
git config --global merge.conflictStyle zdiff3Shows the merge base between the two sides, with common lines hoisted out of the markerssafe Git 2.35+; use diff3 before that. Turns "which side is right?" into a question you can actually answer.
git config --global pull.ff onlyMakes git pull fail rather than silently merge or rebasesafe The strict option: fetch, look, then choose. pull.rebase true is the alternative if you always want linear.
git config --global rebase.autoStash trueStashes and restores dirty state around a rebase or a rebasing pullsafe Removes the "cannot rebase: you have unstaged changes" dance.
git config --global push.autoSetupRemote trueFirst push of a new branch creates the upstream without -usafe Git 2.37+. Kills the most-typed error message in Git.
git config --global push.default simplePushes the current branch to its same-named upstream, and nothing elsesafe Default since 2.0; set it explicitly so an inherited matching from an old system file cannot push every branch at once.
git config --global fetch.prune trueDeletes remote-tracking refs for branches removed on the serversafe Stops git branch -r accumulating years of dead branches. Leave fetch.pruneTags off — tags are shared and deletions are usually mistakes.
git config --global diff.algorithm histogramBetter hunk boundaries than the default myerssafe Noticeably fewer nonsense diffs in brace-heavy and indentation-heavy code. Marginally slower; irrelevant at normal file sizes.
git config --global init.defaultBranch mainNames the first branch of new repositoriessafe Git 2.28+. Also silences the hint printed by every git init.
git config --global rebase.autosquash truegit rebase -i automatically orders and marks fixup! commitssafe Makes git commit --fixup=<sha> a complete workflow rather than half of one.
git config --global rebase.updateRefs trueMoves intermediate branch refs along when rebasing a stacksafe Git 2.38+. Essential for stacked PRs; without it every branch below the tip is left pointing at abandoned commits.
git config --global help.autocorrect promptOffers the intended command when you typo onecare prompt needs 2.42+. A number means deciseconds until it runs anyway, and immediate runs it with no pause — never use those on a machine with production remotes.
git config --global commit.verbose truePuts the full staged diff below the message in the editorsafe The cheapest possible self-review; the diff is stripped before the commit is written.
git config --global user.useConfigOnly trueRefuses to guess an identity from the hostnamesafe Turns a wrong-email commit into an upfront error. Mandatory if you use conditional includes.
git config --global branch.sort -committerdategit branch lists most recently used firstsafe Git 2.19+. Pair with tag.sort=version:refname so v1.10 sorts after v1.9.
git config --global transfer.fsckObjects trueValidates objects received over the wiresafe Catches corruption and some malformed-object attacks at fetch time rather than months later.
rerere replays what you told it, not what you meant

rerere keys a resolution on the text of the conflict, not on its meaning. If the same lines conflict again for a different reason, it silently reapplies the old resolution and reports a clean merge — the most plausible mechanism by which a fix disappears without anyone force-pushing anything. Two defences: read git rerere diff before trusting a merge that resolved itself, and never set rerere.autoUpdate, so a replayed resolution still has to be staged by hand. To recover, git rerere forget <path> drops the recorded resolution and re-conflicts the file; rm -rf .git/rr-cache clears everything, and costs nothing but the replays.

Line endings: core.autocrlf or, better, .gitattributes

core.autocrlf is a per-developer setting for a per-repository problem, which is why mixed-platform teams that rely on it produce pull requests where every line has changed. Set the policy in a tracked .gitattributes instead: the repository then behaves identically for everyone, including CI and the web UI, regardless of what any individual has configured.

ini · .gitattributes
# Normalise to LF in the object database; check out native on Windows.
* text=auto

# Files that must keep LF everywhere, or the tool breaks.
*.sh        text eol=lf
Dockerfile  text eol=lf
*.pem       text eol=lf

# Files that must keep CRLF.
*.bat       text eol=crlf
*.ps1       text eol=crlf

# Never touch these, and never try to diff them.
*.png       binary
*.pdf       binary
*.parquet   binary

# Ergonomics: better hunk headers, and hide generated files from PR diffs.
*.py        diff=python
*.md        diff=markdown
package-lock.json  linguist-generated=true -diff

Adding .gitattributes to an existing repository does not rewrite what is already committed. Run git add --renormalize . once and commit the result as a single, clearly labelled change, on a quiet day, with no other work in flight — it will touch a large fraction of the tree.

Signing: SSH is the low-friction path

user.email is free text and nothing validates it, so commit authorship is an assertion, not a fact. Signing binds a commit to a key the organisation has enrolled, and a ruleset can then reject anything unsigned. Since Git 2.34 you can sign with the SSH key you already push with, which removes the historical reason teams skipped signing.

bash · SSH commit signing, end to end
git config --global gpg.format ssh
git config --global user.signingkey ~/.ssh/id_ed25519.pub
git config --global commit.gpgsign true
git config --global tag.gpgsign true

# Verification needs a local trust list; without it Git cannot check
# anything and says so:
#   error: gpg.ssh.allowedSignersFile needs to be configured and exist
#          for ssh signature verification
git config --global gpg.ssh.allowedSignersFile ~/.config/git/allowed_signers

mkdir -p ~/.config/git
printf '%s %s %s\n' "ada@acme.io" 'namespaces="git"' \
  "$(cut -d' ' -f1,2 ~/.ssh/id_ed25519.pub)" >> ~/.config/git/allowed_signers

git commit --allow-empty -m "check signing"
git log --show-signature -1     # Good "git" signature for ada@acme.io
git verify-commit HEAD          # exit 0, no output, when valid

Three caveats. The forge needs the key uploaded as a signing key — on GitHub that is a separate entry from the authentication key, even for the identical file, and missing it is why a correctly signed commit shows as unverified. allowedSignersFile is a local trust list, so it answers "do I trust this key?" for you alone; the organisation's answer lives in the platform. And any operation that rewrites a commit — rebase, squash-merge, a bot amending a trailer — discards the original signature, so a repository that requires signed commits must have its merge queue re-sign what it produces.

Aliases, including the shell escape hatch

An alias whose value starts with ! is executed by the shell instead of being treated as Git arguments. Four rules matter. It runs from the top level of the working tree, not your current directory — $GIT_PREFIX holds the path you were in. Arguments are appended to the end of the string, so if you need them anywhere else, wrap the body in a throwaway function: "!f() { …; }; f". Inside a shell alias, git resolves to the same Git binary, so nested calls are safe. And an alias can never shadow a real command: Git resolves built-ins first, so alias.status is simply ignored.

Aliases that earn their place
CommandWhat it doesNotes
git config --global alias.s "status -sb"Short status with branch and tracking infosafe The one you will type fifty times a day.
git config --global alias.lg "log --oneline --graph --decorate -20"A readable graph of recent historysafe Add --all when hunting for a branch you cannot find.
git config --global alias.amend "commit --amend --no-edit"Folds staged changes into the last commit, message unchangedcare Rewrites; needs a force-push if already published.
git config --global alias.fixup "commit --fixup"Marks a commit as a fix to an earlier onesafe Combined with rebase.autosquash, git rebase -i does the ordering.
git config --global alias.please "push --force-with-lease --force-if-includes"Force-push that refuses if the remote moved since your last fetchdestructive Still a rewrite. --force-if-includes (2.30+) closes the gap where a fetch alone would defeat the lease.
git config --global alias.undo "reset --soft HEAD~1"Uncommits the last commit, keeping everything stagedsafe Nothing leaves the object database; the reflog holds the old tip.
git config --global alias.root "rev-parse --show-toplevel"Prints the repository rootsafe cd "$(git root)" is the portable way home.
git config --global alias.sync '!git fetch --prune && git rebase --autostash @{u}'Brings the current branch up to date against its upstreamcare Shell alias. Fails cleanly if there is no upstream, which is the behaviour you want.
git config --global alias.gone '!git branch -vv | grep ": gone]" | awk "{print \$1}" | xargs -r git branch -d'Deletes local branches whose upstream was removedcare Run git fetch --prune first. -d refuses unmerged branches — never change it to -D.
Interview · "we require conventional commits — how do you enforce it?"

The answer that fails is "a commit-msg hook". The answer that lands names the trust boundary first: hooks are local files that clone never delivers, that --no-verify skips, that libgit2-based GUIs do not run at all, and that a rebase does not fire per replayed commit — so a hook is a fast feedback mechanism, never a control. Enforcement has to run where the author has no vote: a required status check on the pull request, a platform ruleset, or a pre-receive hook on a self-managed host. Then close the loop: distribute the hook through core.hooksPath or a manager so the local and remote checks are the same configuration, and — the detail that separates people who have shipped this — point out that if you squash-merge, the commit that lands on main takes the pull request title, so validating individual commit messages enforces nothing about the history you actually keep. Validate the PR title. A good follow-up is "what if someone commits a secret anyway?" — rotate the credential first; history rewriting is cleanup, not remediation.

One machine, two identities

includeIf (Git 2.13+) pulls in a file only when a condition holds, which is how one laptop keeps work and personal commits separate without ever typing git config user.email again. The condition is evaluated per repository, so it belongs in the global file.

gitdir:~/work/
True when the repository lives under that path. The trailing slash means "and everything below" — without it the pattern must match exactly, which is the usual reason an include appears not to work.
gitdir/i:~/work/
Same, case-insensitively. On macOS and Windows the filesystem is case-insensitive but this pattern is not, so ~/Work/ will not match gitdir:~/work/.
onbranch:release/**
True when the checked-out branch matches (Git 2.23+). Useful for stricter settings on release branches.
hasconfig:remote.*.url:git@github.com:acme/**
True when any remote URL matches (Git 2.36+). Keys off where the code lives rather than where you cloned it, which is the more robust condition.

Set user.useConfigOnly=true and leave user.email out of the base file entirely. Then a repository that matches no include fails loudly with fatal: no email was given and auto-detection is disabled, instead of quietly attributing work to the wrong identity — which, on a public repository, is the kind of mistake that requires a history rewrite to undo.

Credential helpers

A credential helper answers Git's request for a username and password over HTTPS from an OS keystore, so tokens never sit in a file or a shell variable. They are multi-valued and tried in order, and can be scoped per host.

Credential helpers by platform
CommandWhat it doesNotes
git config --global credential.helper osxkeychainStores tokens in the macOS Keychainsafe Ships with Git for macOS and the Xcode command line tools.
git config --global credential.helper managerGit Credential Manager: OAuth flows, MFA, Azure DevOps, GitHub, Bitbucketsafe Bundled with Git for Windows; installable on macOS and Linux.
git config --global credential.helper libsecretStores tokens in the GNOME keyring on Linuxsafe Ships as source under /usr/share/doc/git/contrib/credential/libsecret on Debian-family distros; may need make.
git config --global credential.helper "cache --timeout=3600"Holds credentials in a memory-only daemon for an hourcare The fallback when there is no keystore — a container, a jump host. Nothing touches disk.
gh auth setup-gitRegisters the GitHub CLI as the helper for GitHub hostssafe Token rotation becomes the CLI's problem. Adds a scoped credential.https://github.com.helper entry.
git config --global credential.helper storeWrites the token in cleartext to ~/.git-credentialsdestructive Never. It survives backups, sync clients and screen shares; treat any machine where it has run as having leaked the token.
git config --global --unset-all credential.helperClears an inherited list before setting a new onesafe Helpers accumulate; check with --get-all first.
git credential-cache exitForgets cached credentials immediatelysafe The first step when a token has been rotated and Git keeps sending the old one.

On CI, use none of these. A short-lived token minted from OIDC, or a deploy key scoped to one repository, is the correct shape; a long-lived personal access token in an environment variable inherits the blast radius of the human it belongs to.

The starting ~/.gitconfig

Paste this, change the name, and delete the parts you disagree with. Every line above is justified in the sections above.

Adopt it in the right order

Test any setting with git -c key=value … before writing it. Roll config out to a team through documentation and a bootstrap script, never by editing anyone's ~/.gitconfig for them — a value nobody can explain is a value nobody can debug, and git config --show-origin is only useful if the origin is a file the developer knows exists. And whenever a check is worth having, put the fast copy in a hook and the binding copy in CI, from the same configuration file.

G10

Branching strategies

A branching strategy is not a diagram, it is a policy about batch size and how long unintegrated work is allowed to exist. Choose it by asking how often you can ship and how many versions you must support at once; every other consideration — pretty history, tidy lanes, ceremony — is downstream of those two answers.

What you are actually choosing

Every strategy on offer is a different answer to one question: how long may a change sit outside the mainline? A branch is unmerged inventory. While it exists it accrues merge cost against everything else in flight, it is untested in combination with its peers, and it holds finished work that customers cannot use. The diagrams differ; the economics are the whole argument.

The DORA research programme (the State of DevOps reports and the book Accelerate) found, across many years of survey data, that trunk-based development predicts higher software delivery performance — specifically, teams with fewer than three active branches in the application's repository, branches living less than a day before merging, and no long-lived code freezes. It is correlational, self-reported survey data rather than a controlled experiment, and you should say so out loud when someone waves it at you. But the causal story is mundane and independently believable: small batches shorten lead time, shorten feedback, shrink the blast radius of any one change, and make a failed change trivially attributable and trivially revertible. Nothing about that mechanism depends on the survey being right.

So the strategies below are ranked, in effect, by how much unmerged inventory they normalise. Where a strategy carries more inventory, it should be because your product forces it — a store review queue, an on-premise customer on last year's version, a certification cycle — not because it looked orderly on a blog post.

Trunk-based development

One branch matters: main. Work happens on branches that live hours, not days, and merge at least daily. Anything too big to finish in a day ships incomplete but inert, hidden behind a feature flag. main is releasable at every commit, and in the strong form every merge deploys to production automatically.

TRUNK-BASED · branches live hours; main is releasable at every commit * 7d41a9 (HEAD -> main) deploy #4183 · 16:52 |\ | * c02e6f feat: new rounding, dark behind flag billing_v2 |/ cut 15:10 · merged 16:50 * 1b9c04 deploy #4182 · 15:07 |\ | * 88af13 fix: null deref in the session cache |/ * 3f5e21 deploy #4181 · 11:26 no release branch · CI tags main · rollback = redeploy the previous artefact

Optimises for lead time and change-failure recovery. Release process: there is no release event — CI builds an artefact from every commit on main, tags it, and a deployment pipeline promotes it. Hotfix path: identical to the normal path, because the normal path is minutes long. Fix forward on main; if forward is too slow, redeploy the previous artefact by digest. Failure mode: flag debt and a permanently red trunk. If main is broken for hours at a time, the strategy collapses — nobody can release and everyone's branches rot. It demands fast, reliable CI (single-digit minutes), a merge queue once more than a handful of people are merging, and the cultural willingness to revert first and diagnose second.

GitHub Flow

One long-lived main, feature branches off it, pull request, review, merge, deploy from main. It is trunk-based development with the discipline about branch lifetime quietly removed — which is exactly where teams get into trouble, because the branch that was supposed to live a day lives a fortnight.

GITHUB FLOW · one long-lived branch; feature branches live days * a91f30 (main) Merge pull request #812 --> deploys production |\ | * 4c7e12 review round 3 | * 2ad9b8 address comments | * e5510a feat: bulk export feature/bulk-export · 4 days old |/ * 6b2d77 (main) Merge pull request #809

Optimises for review quality and a low-ceremony contribution model — it is the natural shape for open source, where contributors work in forks and maintainers cannot coordinate branch lifetimes anyway. Release process: merge equals release, or a tag on main triggers one. Hotfix path: a branch off main, expedited review, merge, deploy. Failure mode: review latency becomes lead time. A two-day review turnaround silently converts this into a strategy with a two-day batch size, and the symptom appears as merge conflicts and "works on my branch" integration bugs rather than as a review complaint. Measure time-from-first-commit-to-merge; if the median is above a day, you have a queueing problem, not a Git problem.

GitLab Flow: main plus environment branches

Keep main as the integration branch, then add downstream branches that are environments: staging, production. Code only ever flows one way — merge main into staging to promote, staging into production to release. The branch tip is a record of what is deployed where, which is the entire appeal: it is auditable with git log alone, and deployment permission reduces to write permission on a ref, which branch protection already understands.

GITLAB FLOW · code flows one way: main --> staging --> production main *--*--*--*--*--*--*--* every merged pull request \ \ staging *-----------* merge main into staging when QA is ready \ production * merge staging into production to release a cherry-pick into production that is not on main is a future regression

Optimises for demonstrable provenance under audit and for organisations where a human gate before production is non-negotiable. Release process: a merge, gated by whatever approval you attach to the ref. Hotfix path: the honest one is fix on main and promote quickly; the tempting one is to commit straight onto production, and that is the trap the strategy sets. Failure mode: environment branches drift. The moment one commit exists on production that is not an ancestor of main, the branch stops being a promotion record and becomes a fourth codebase. Add a CI check that fails if git merge-base --is-ancestor origin/production origin/main does not hold, and treat a failure as an incident.

There is a strong argument that environment branches are a workaround for not having artefact promotion. If your pipeline can already promote an immutable build by digest and record which digest is in which environment, the branches add ceremony and a drift risk while buying you nothing you did not already have.

GitFlow, and why it is usually the wrong answer

Vincent Driessen's 2010 model: two permanent branches (master for released code, develop for integration), plus three temporary kinds (feature/*, release/*, hotfix/*). Features merge into develop; a release branch is cut from develop, stabilised, then merged into master and tagged, and merged back into develop; hotfixes branch from master and likewise merge into both.

GITFLOW · two permanent branches, three temporary kinds master *-------------------* v1.4.0----------* v1.4.1 \ / / release *--*--*--/ hotfix *--/ / \ / develop *--*--*-------*--*----*--*--* \ / feature *--* release and hotfix must merge into BOTH master and develop — the step teams forget

Driessen himself added a note to the original post saying that if you are delivering continuously to a web application, this is not the model you want. Take him at his word. GitFlow was designed for software with explicit, versioned, mutually-supported releases on a slow cadence — installed desktop applications, on-premise servers, firmware — where master must always describe a shipped artefact somebody is running today. Optimises for supporting multiple released versions concurrently and for a long stabilisation window. Release process: cut release/1.4, freeze features, absorb only fixes, tag on master. Hotfix path: branch from the tag on master, fix, tag v1.4.1, merge to master and back to develop. Failure mode: develop is a second trunk that nobody deploys, so integration debt accumulates in it invisibly; the mandatory double merge-back is done by hand and therefore eventually skipped; and the release freeze is exactly the long-lived code freeze the delivery evidence says is expensive. For a service where you control the only running copy, you are paying the whole cost of multi-version support to support one version.

The same four strategies, read as inventory policy
StrategyLong-lived branchesTypical branch lifeRelease is…Characteristic failure
Trunk-based1HoursA non-event; every commit is a candidateFlag debt; a red trunk stops everyone
GitHub Flow1DaysThe merge itselfReview latency becomes lead time
GitLab Flow2–4DaysA merge into an environment refEnvironment branches drift from main
GitFlow2 permanent + release linesDays to weeksA branch, a freeze, a tag, two merge-backsSkipped merge-backs; develop as shadow trunk

Feature flags: the mechanism that makes trunk-based possible

You cannot merge daily if merging means releasing an unfinished feature. Flags break that coupling: deploy becomes a technical event that happens continuously, and release becomes a business decision made by flipping a value. Incomplete work ships dark — the code path exists, is compiled, is covered by tests with the flag both on and off, and returns the old behaviour in production until you say otherwise.

The related technique for larger changes is branch by abstraction: introduce a seam, put both implementations behind it, migrate callers incrementally on the mainline, then delete the old side. It is the same idea applied to structure rather than behaviour, and it is how you replace a data layer without a six-week branch.

Flags are also inventory, and the same discipline applies: every flag has an owner and an expiry from the moment it is created.

yaml · config/flags/billing_v2.yml
key: billing_v2
type: release          # release | ops | experiment | permission
owner: payments-team
created: 2026-08-14
expires: 2026-10-14    # CI fails the build after this date
default: false
rollout:
  internal: true
  percentage: 10
cleanup_issue: PAY-3120
  1. Create with an expiry. A flag record with no owner and no date is permanent by default. The expiry is enforced by a CI job that fails the build, not by a reminder.
  2. Ship dark. Merge with default: false. Tests run the suite with the flag both off and on; a flag that is only ever tested in one state is a code path you have not tested.
  3. Ramp. Internal users, then a percentage, then all. Each step is a config change with no deploy, which is the point.
  4. Bake. Leave it at 100% for long enough to cover a weekly traffic cycle before touching the code.
  5. Remove the branch, then the flag. Delete the dead code path first and merge that; delete the flag record in a second change. Doing both at once means a rollback of the cleanup restores a flag whose record no longer exists.

Distinguish flag types, because their lifetimes differ. Release flags are temporary by definition and should be measured in weeks. Ops kill-switches (degrade the recommendation service, disable a costly report) are permanent on purpose. Experiment flags die with the experiment. Permission flags are product entitlements and are not really flags at all. Only the first category is debt; conflating them means either the kill-switches get deleted or the release flags never do.

Knight Capital, 1 August 2012

Knight Capital deployed new order-routing code to eight production servers and missed one. The new code reused a flag that, on the un-updated server, still activated a dormant function called Power Peg — dead code left in the codebase for eight years. The stale server began sending millions of unintended orders. In roughly 45 minutes the firm lost about $460m (per the SEC's 2013 order) and effectively ceased to exist as an independent company. Two lessons, both about hygiene rather than cleverness: never repurpose a flag key — retire it and mint a new one — and delete dead code paths rather than leaving them reachable behind a switch. A flag that has been at 100% for a year is not a safety net, it is an armed trap.

Release branches and the cherry-pick trap

Even a trunk-based team acquires release branches the moment something outside its control gates the release: an app store review queue, an embedded device, a customer who upgrades quarterly. The rule is narrow and worth stating precisely: a release branch is a place fixes are copied to, never a place they originate. Fix on main, verify on main, then git cherry-pick -x onto the release branch. The -x flag records the source SHA in the message, which is what makes the later audit possible.

The trap is the reverse order. Under pressure, someone fixes directly on release/2.14 because that is what is on fire. It ships, the incident closes, and nobody back-ports. Three weeks later release/2.15 is cut from main — which never received the fix — and the same bug ships again, now with an incident report claiming it was already fixed.

hotfix on a release branch, then the audit for what never came back
$ git switch -c hotfix/csrf release/2.14
Switched to a new branch 'hotfix/csrf'

$ git cherry-pick -x 5f0a2c1
[hotfix/csrf 9b41ee7] fix: reject POST without matching CSRF token
# -x appended: (cherry picked from commit 5f0a2c1...) — the audit trail

$ git switch release/2.14 && git merge --no-ff hotfix/csrf
$ git tag -a v2.14.3 -m "v2.14.3 CSRF fix"
$ git push --follow-tags origin release/2.14

# The audit. Which commits are on the release branch and NOT on main,
# ignoring anything already cherry-picked in either direction?
$ git log --cherry-pick --right-only --oneline main...release/2.14
7ab4410 fix: clamp retry backoff at 30s
# shipped in 2.14.1 six weeks ago, never back-ported. 2.15 will regress.

$ git cherry -v main release/2.14 | grep '^+'
+ 7ab4410 fix: clamp retry backoff at 30s

$ git switch main && git cherry-pick -x 7ab4410
$ git branch --contains 7ab4410
  main
* release/2.14
Release branches, hotfixes and back-port auditing
CommandWhat it doesNotes
git switch -c release/2.15 mainCuts a release line from the current trunksafe cut late; a branch cut early is a freeze in disguise
git cherry-pick -x 5f0a2c1Copies one commit and records its origincare always -x on a release branch; without it the audit below is guesswork
git cherry-pick -x A^..BCopies an inclusive rangecare order matters; conflicts stop mid-range, resolve then --continue
git log --cherry-pick --right-only --oneline main...release/2.15Commits only on the release branch, ignoring equivalent patchessafe the three-dot form is required; run it in CI on every release
git cherry -v main release/2.15Same question, patch-id based; + means not upstreamsafe older, terser, no rename detection
git branch -a --contains 5f0a2c1Every branch containing that exact commitsafe misses cherry-picks — they are different SHAs
git merge-base --is-ancestor origin/production origin/mainExit 0 if production has not drifted aheadsafe the CI gate for environment branches
git config rerere.enabled trueRecords conflict resolutions for replaysafe pays for itself when the same fix conflicts on three release lines
git branch -D release/2.13Deletes a finished release line locallydestructive harmless only if the tag exists — the tag is what keeps the commits reachable
Delete the branch, keep the tag

Commits are kept alive by reachability, and a release branch is often the only ref pointing at a shipped tree. Delete release/2.13 before tagging it and those commits become unreachable; they survive in the reflog for 90 days and then git gc removes them for good. On the server there is no reflog to fall back on. Tag first, push the tag (git push origin v2.13.4, or git push --follow-tags), verify with git ls-remote --tags origin, and only then delete the branch.

Versioning and tagging

Semantic versioning (MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH) is a contract with people who consume your artefact: major means "I broke your build", minor means "new capability, still compatible", patch means "bug fix only". It earns its keep for libraries, SDKs, APIs and anything with an external consumer. For a single-deployable web service with no external consumer, it communicates nothing — you cannot break your own compatibility, and every release is somewhere between minor and patch. Calendar versioning (2026.09.0, or Ubuntu's YY.MM) is more honest there because the only question anyone actually asks is "how old is this?". Pre-release identifiers (1.4.0-rc.1) sort before the release under SemVer precedence rules; build metadata after a + is ignored for precedence entirely, so 1.4.0+build.9 and 1.4.0+build.10 are the same version, which is a common source of surprise in package resolvers.

Conventional Commits let the bump be computed rather than argued about: fix: yields a patch, feat: a minor, and a ! after the type or a BREAKING CHANGE: footer yields a major. Tools that implement this include semantic-release, release-please, changesets (best for monorepos with independently versioned packages), git-cliff and cocogitto. The caveat is real: the version is only as good as the commit messages, so enforce them at the gate with commitlint and remember that squash-merging means the pull request title becomes the commit subject — lint that too, or your release notes will be a list of "update stuff".

Tags
CommandWhat it doesNotes
git tag v1.4.0Lightweight tag: a ref file containing a commit SHAcare no author, date, message or signature — do not use it for a release
git tag -a v1.4.0 -m "1.4.0"Annotated tag: a real tag object with tagger, date and messagesafe the correct form for anything you ship
git tag -s v1.4.0 -m "1.4.0"Annotated and cryptographically signedsafe needs user.signingkey; set tag.gpgsign=true to make it the default
git tag -v v1.4.0Verifies the signaturesafe SSH signing needs gpg.ssh.allowedSignersFile
git describe --tags --always --dirtyHuman build identifier, e.g. v1.4.0-12-gc02e6fsafe without --tags it considers annotated tags only — bake it into the binary
git push origin v1.4.0Pushes one tagsafe a plain git push pushes no tags at all
git push --follow-tagsPushes annotated tags reachable from the pushed commitssafe ignores lightweight tags — another reason to annotate
git tag -f v1.4.0 <sha>Moves an existing tagdestructive clones do not update moved tags; two people now build different code from one version
git push --delete origin v1.4.0Removes the tag from the remotedestructive anyone who already fetched keeps it; released versions are immutable — supersede, never re-cut
Annotated, always

A lightweight tag is indistinguishable from a branch tip in the ref store: no tagger, no date, no message, nothing to sign. Six months later, "who cut v1.4.0 and when?" has no answer. git describe ignores lightweight tags unless you pass --tags, and --follow-tags refuses to push them. Configure the habit away: git config --global tag.gpgsign true forces annotation as a side effect, because a signed tag is necessarily a tag object.

One artefact, many environments

Build once, on merge to main, and promote that exact artefact — identified by content digest, not by a moving tag — through every environment. Rebuilding per environment is the single most common way teams destroy the value of their own testing, and it is worth naming precisely why it is wrong: a rebuild resolves dependencies again. A floating base image (node:22-slim), an unpinned transitive dependency, a package index that published a new patch an hour ago, a compiler on a different runner — any one of them means the bytes you tested in staging are not the bytes now in production. You did not promote a release, you performed an untested build directly into production and called it a promotion.

01 · Build
Once, on mainoutput: ghcr.io/acme/api@sha256:9f2c…
02 · Attest
Sign + SBOMcosign, provenance bound to the digest
03 · Dev
Deploy digestconfig from the environment, not the image
04 · Staging
Automated gatesmoke + contract tests against that digest
05 · Approve
Human gateonly where regulation or risk requires it
06 · Production
Same digestrollback = redeploy the previous digest

The corollary is that nothing environment-specific may be baked into the artefact. Configuration, endpoints and secrets arrive at runtime from the environment. The usual objection comes from front-end builds, where bundlers inline import.meta.env or process.env values at build time — which appears to force a rebuild per environment. It does not: serve a small /config.js from the same origin, or substitute placeholders in the container entrypoint before the server starts. The rebuild is a workaround for a solvable packaging problem, and it costs you the guarantee that staging tested production's code.

yaml · .github/workflows/promote.yml
name: promote
on:
  workflow_dispatch:
    inputs:
      digest:
        description: Digest produced by the build workflow, e.g. sha256:9f2c...
        required: true
      environment:
        type: choice
        options: [staging, production]

jobs:
  promote:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    environment: ${{ inputs.environment }}   # approval + secrets live on the environment
    steps:
      - name: Verify provenance before promoting
        run: |
          cosign verify \
            --certificate-identity-regexp '^https://github.com/acme/api/' \
            --certificate-oidc-issuer https://token.actions.githubusercontent.com \
            ghcr.io/acme/api@${{ inputs.digest }}

      - name: Deploy the same bytes
        run: |
          kubectl set image deployment/api \
            api=ghcr.io/acme/api@${{ inputs.digest }} --record
          kubectl rollout status deployment/api --timeout=5m
Interview: "which branching strategy would you use, and why?"

Naming GitFlow unprompted is the classic tell that a candidate has read a diagram rather than run a release. The strong answer refuses the question as posed and asks two counter-questions first: how many versions must be supported in production simultaneously, and what stands between a merge and a customer — an app store review, a hardware ship date, a regulator, or nothing? One supported version and nothing in the way gives trunk-based development with feature flags. Several supported versions gives release branches with disciplined, audited back-ports. A hard human gate gives artefact promotion, and environment branches only if the audit story genuinely requires refs. Expect the follow-up "how do you hotfix?" — the answer that earns the point is that the hotfix path should be the ordinary path, and if it is not, your ordinary path is too slow and that is the actual defect. A candidate who then volunteers the back-port audit (git log --cherry-pick --right-only in CI) has clearly been burned by a regression and fixed the process afterwards.

Choosing

Team size, cadence and product type mapped to a recommendation
ContextUseBecause
1–10 engineers · one web service · deploy on mergeTrunk-based, flags, no release branchCoordination cost is near zero; batch size is the only lever left
10–80 engineers · one deployable · daily or betterTrunk-based + merge queue + flagsThe queue keeps main green without lengthening branch life
Any size · web service · manual QA sign-off requiredGitHub Flow, promote artefacts by digestGate the promotion, not the branch; the tag records what shipped
Regulated · deployment authority must map to a refGitLab Flow environment branches + drift check in CIAuditors can read git log; drift is the risk you must automate against
Mobile app · store review · N versions in the wildmain + short release branches + audited cherry-picksRelease timing is outside your control; back-porting is unavoidable
On-premise / embedded · customers on old versions · LTS linesLong-lived maintenance branches per line; GitFlow is defensibleThis is the workload GitFlow was actually designed for
Library or SDK with external consumersmain + SemVer tags + maintenance branches for old majorsThe version number is a compatibility contract, so it must mean something
Open source with drive-by contributorsGitHub Flow: fork, pull request, squash-mergeYou cannot govern the branch lifetimes of people you have never met

Two tests to apply to whatever you choose. First, count the refs that must be manually kept in sync — every one of them is a place a fix can be stranded, so if the count exceeds two, put a CI check on each pair. Second, time a hotfix from the moment someone types the fix to the moment it is serving traffic. If that number is measured in hours because of the branching model rather than because of your test suite, the model is the incident's root cause and no diagram will change that.

G11

Code review that works

Review is the only engineering practice that touches every change you ship, which makes it the single largest lever on both defect rate and cycle time — and the one most often run on folklore. Treated as an engineering process with inputs you control and outputs you measure, it gets faster and catches more; treated as a ritual approval, it becomes a queue that slows delivery without improving anything.

What review is actually for

Ask a team why they review code and you will hear "to catch bugs". That is the weakest of the four things review does, and the one automation is steadily taking over. Empirically, reviewers find roughly a quarter of the defects that reach production; type checkers, tests, fuzzers and static analysis find more, more cheaply, and without a human waiting. If defect detection were the whole purpose, the honest conclusion would be to stop reviewing and spend the time on tests.

The other three outputs are what justify the cost:

Knowledge distribution
Review is the cheapest mechanism you have for ensuring more than one person understands a subsystem. Every approval is a second engineer who has read the code recently enough to page in the context during an incident. A team where one person reviews everything in billing/ has a bus factor of one and a review process that is actively concealing it.
Collective ownership
Code that has been read by others is code others feel entitled to change. Unreviewed code becomes someone's private territory, and territory does not get refactored.
Design feedback while it is still cheap
The expensive mistakes are structural: a schema that cannot be migrated, an endpoint that leaks an internal identifier, a queue consumer that is not idempotent. These are visible in a diff and nearly invisible in a test suite. A reviewer's most valuable sentence is usually a question about the approach, not a defect in a line.
A record of intent
The PR description and the review thread are the only place the rejected alternatives are written down. Two years later, that is why the code looks the way it does.

Once you accept that framing, several arguments settle themselves. Review latency matters more than review depth, because a stale review distributes no knowledge. Approving a change that is better than what exists is correct, because the alternative is not perfection but the status quo. And anything a machine can decide should never occupy a human's attention, because human attention is the scarce input.

Size is the dominant variable

Nothing else you can change moves review outcomes as much as the number of lines under review. The pattern is consistent across two decades of industry data and every internal measurement I have seen: defect discovery per line is roughly flat up to a few hundred changed lines, then falls off a cliff. Beyond about 400 lines, reviewers stop reading and start skimming; beyond about 1,000, approval time drops, because a diff nobody can hold in their head produces "LGTM" rather than questions. A 2,000-line pull request does not get four times the scrutiny of a 500-line one. It gets less, in total.

What changes as a diff grows (typical, not universal — measure your own)
Changed linesReviewer behaviourObserved outcome
Under 50Reads every line, often twiceHighest defect density found; review in minutes
50–200Reads everything, reasons about structureThe sweet spot; design feedback still happens
200–400Reads carefully, loses the whole-change pictureLine defects found, structural ones missed
400–1000Skims; anchors on the first files in the listLater files effectively unreviewed
Over 1000Approves on trust, or leaves it in the queue for daysLongest latency and lowest defect discovery

The second-order effect is worse than the first. Large PRs sit unreviewed, so authors keep working on top of them, so the next PR is larger and depends on the unmerged one, so rebases multiply and conflicts compound. Size and latency form a feedback loop, and the only place to break it is size.

Stacking: the technique that makes small PRs possible
