Turning a commit into an artefact somebody else can trust. The rule that everything else follows from: build once, promote the same digest.

Build once, promote everywhere

WRONG — rebuild per environment main ──▶ build ──▶ staging different image └──▶ build ──▶ production different image, untested Base image moved, a transitive dependency floated, a build cache differed. You tested one artefact and shipped another. RIGHT — one build, promoted by digest main ──▶ build ──▶ ghcr.io/acme/api@sha256:9f3c… ├──▶ staging (same digest) ├──▶ canary (same digest) └──▶ prod (same digest) The thing that passed the tests is byte-for-byte the thing running. Tags are labels that move; the DIGEST is the identity.
yaml · build, sign, attest, then promote
jobs:
  build:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    permissions:
      contents: read
      packages: write
      id-token: write          # for keyless signing and provenance
      attestations: write
    outputs:
      digest: ${{ steps.push.outputs.digest }}
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: docker/setup-buildx-action@v3
      - uses: docker/login-action@v3
        with:
          registry: ghcr.io
          username: ${{ github.actor }}
          password: ${{ secrets.GITHUB_TOKEN }}

      - id: push
        uses: docker/build-push-action@v6
        with:
          push: true
          tags: |
            ghcr.io/acme/api:${{ github.sha }}
            ghcr.io/acme/api:latest
          cache-from: type=gha
          cache-to: type=gha,mode=max
          provenance: true          # SLSA build provenance
          sbom: true                # software bill of materials

      # Keyless signing: identity comes from the OIDC token, so there is no
      # private key to store, rotate or leak.
      - run: cosign sign --yes ghcr.io/acme/api@${{ steps.push.outputs.digest }}

      - uses: actions/attest-build-provenance@v1
        with:
          subject-name: ghcr.io/acme/api
          subject-digest: ${{ steps.push.outputs.digest }}

  deploy-staging:
    needs: build
    uses: ./.github/workflows/deploy.yml
    with:
      environment: staging
      image: ghcr.io/acme/api@${{ needs.build.outputs.digest }}   # digest, not tag

Verifying at deploy time

bash · a signature nobody checks is decoration
cosign verify ghcr.io/acme/api@sha256:9f3c... \
  --certificate-identity-regexp '^https://github.com/acme/api/.github/workflows/release.yml@refs/heads/main$' \
  --certificate-oidc-issuer https://token.actions.githubusercontent.com

# The two flags ARE the security. Without them you have verified that
# *somebody* signed it — which is true of an attacker's image too.
# The identity regex pins WHICH workflow, in WHICH repo, on WHICH branch.

# Enforce it in the cluster so an unsigned image cannot run at all:
#   Kyverno / Sigstore policy-controller admission webhook
gh attestation verify oci://ghcr.io/acme/api@sha256:9f3c... --repo acme/api
Tags are mutable; digests are not

ghcr.io/acme/api:v1.2.3 can be overwritten — by a rerun, by a mistake, or by someone with write access to the registry. Deploying by tag means you cannot prove what is running, and a rollback to "v1.2.3" may not restore what v1.2.3 was last week. Deploy by digest, and enable immutable tags in the registry so releases cannot be silently republished.

Releases and versioning

CommandDoes
git tag -s v1.4.0 -m "release"Signed, annotated tag — the release marker
gh release create v1.4.0 --generate-notesRelease with notes generated from merged PRs
gh release create v1.4.0 ./dist/*.tar.gzAttach build artefacts
gh release create v1.4.0 --prereleaseMarks it as not production-ready
gh release download v1.4.0 -p '*.tar.gz'Fetch assets
gh release list --limit 10Recent releases
yaml · release notes that write themselves
# .github/release.yml — categorises generated notes by PR label
changelog:
  exclude:
    labels: [ignore-for-release, dependencies]
  categories:
    - title: Breaking changes
      labels: [breaking]
    - title: Features
      labels: [feature, enhancement]
    - title: Fixes
      labels: [bug, fix]
    - title: Other
      labels: ['*']
Semantic versioning
MAJOR breaks, MINOR adds, PATCH fixes. Only meaningful if you actually treat a removed field as MAJOR — most teams do not, which is why consumers stop trusting the number.
Conventional commits
feat:, fix:, feat!: in the squash title. Lets semantic-release or release-please compute the next version and the changelog with no human judgement.
Changesets
Better for monorepos: contributors add a small file describing the change and its bump level; the release job aggregates them across packages.
Calendar versioning
2026.04.1. Honest for continuously deployed services where "breaking" is not a meaningful axis.

Packages and registry hygiene

yaml · untagged images grow forever and cost money
  cleanup:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    permissions: { packages: write }
    steps:
      - uses: actions/delete-package-versions@v5
        with:
          package-name: api
          package-type: container
          min-versions-to-keep: 30
          delete-only-untagged-versions: true
# Every PR build that pushes an image leaves an untagged layer set behind.
# On a busy repo that is tens of gigabytes a month, and nobody notices until
# the storage bill or a quota does.

Supply chain, in practical terms

ControlPreventsEffort
Pin actions to a full SHAa compromised action tag stealing your secretslow — Dependabot updates them
Lockfile-only installs (npm ci)a floating transitive dependency changing under younone
--ignore-scripts in CIinstall-time script execution from a malicious packagelow; some packages genuinely need them
SBOM generationnot knowing what you shipped when a CVE landslow — one build flag
Provenance attestationan artefact of unknown origin reaching productionlow with OIDC
Signature verification at admissionan unsigned or foreign image runningmoderate — a policy controller
Base image pinned by digesta base image changing between buildslow
yaml · pin third-party actions by SHA
      # A tag is a moving pointer. Whoever controls the action repo can
      # repoint v4 at anything, and it runs with your GITHUB_TOKEN.
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4                                   # convenient
      - uses: actions/checkout@692973e3d937129bcbf40652eb9f2f61becf3332  # v4.1.7
      #        ^ immutable, and Dependabot still proposes updates with the
      #          version in a trailing comment so it stays readable
Interview follow-up

“How do you know that what is running in production is what you tested?” The chain: build once and record the digest; promote that digest through every environment rather than rebuilding; sign it with a keyless signature bound to the workflow identity; attach provenance and an SBOM; and verify the signature at admission so an unsigned image cannot start. The important sentence is the last one — the signature is worthless until something refuses to run without it.

H06

GitHub security features

The platform ships good controls that are mostly off by default. The highest-risk surface is not your code — it is Actions, because a workflow is arbitrary code running with a token that can push to your repository.

The Actions threat model

A workflow is code execution with: · a GITHUB_TOKEN that may have write access · every repository secret the job can read · network access to your cloud, via OIDC or stored credentials The three ways an attacker gets in: 1 pull_request_target with a checkout of the PR head Runs with the BASE repo's secrets and a writable token, but checks out code from a FORK. Any drive-by contributor executes on your runner. 2 script injection ${{ github.event.* }} interpolated into a `run:` block. A PR title containing shell metacharacters is executed. (See the fundamentals chapter.) 3 a compromised third-party action `uses: some/action@v1` where v1 is repointed at malicious code. It inherits the job's token and secrets.
yaml · the pull_request_target trap, and the safe pattern
# CATASTROPHIC — do not do this
on: pull_request_target
jobs:
  build:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
        with:
          ref: ${{ github.event.pull_request.head.sha }}   # ← FORK'S CODE
      - run: npm ci && npm test                            # ← EXECUTES IT
      # npm ci runs the fork's install scripts, with your secrets in the
      # environment and a writable GITHUB_TOKEN. Full compromise, from anyone
      # who can open a pull request.

# SAFE — use plain pull_request. Forks get NO secrets and a read-only token.
on: pull_request
permissions: { contents: read }
jobs:
  test:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4      # the merge ref, no secrets, read-only
      - run: npm ci && npm test

# If you genuinely need base-repo context (labelling, commenting), use
# pull_request_target but NEVER check out or execute the fork's code:
on: pull_request_target
permissions: { pull-requests: write }
jobs:
  label:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/labeler@v5       # operates on metadata only
The rule, stated simply

Never execute untrusted code in a workflow that holds secrets. pull_request is safe because forks get neither. pull_request_target is safe only if you never check out or run the fork's code. Anything that needs both — running a fork's tests and posting results with a token — must be split into two workflows communicating through an artifact and workflow_run.

Token permissions

yaml · least privilege, per job
# Organisation setting: default GITHUB_TOKEN permissions = read-only.
# Then elevate deliberately, per job, never at workflow level "just in case".

permissions: {}                    # start from nothing

jobs:
  test:
    permissions: { contents: read }

  publish:
    permissions:
      contents: read
      packages: write              # only this job can push images
      id-token: write              # only this job can assume a cloud role

  comment:
    permissions: { pull-requests: write }   # can comment, cannot push code

Scanning

FeatureFindsConfigure
Secret scanningcredentials committed to the repoon for public repos; enable for private. Turn on push protection — it blocks the push rather than reporting afterwards
Dependabot alertsknown CVEs in dependenciesfree; the value is in triage, not the alert
Dependabot updates—group updates or you drown in PRs; auto-merge patch bumps with green CI
CodeQLinjection, path traversal, unsafe deserialisationdefault setup is one click; custom queries for your own patterns
Dependency reviewa PR introducing a vulnerable or badly-licensed packagean action; blocks at review time rather than after merge
yaml · Dependabot configured so people read it
version: 2
updates:
  - package-ecosystem: npm
    directory: /
    schedule: { interval: weekly, day: monday }
    open-pull-requests-limit: 5
    groups:                          # ← the setting that saves the feature
      production-patches:
        dependency-type: production
        update-types: [patch]
      dev-dependencies:
        dependency-type: development
    ignore:
      - dependency-name: typescript
        update-types: [version-update:semver-major]

  - package-ecosystem: github-actions   # keeps SHA-pinned actions current
    directory: /
    schedule: { interval: weekly }

  - package-ecosystem: docker
    directory: /
    schedule: { interval: weekly }
Alert fatigue is a security failure

Forty open Dependabot PRs is functionally the same as having the feature switched off — nobody reads them, and the one that matters is indistinguishable from the thirty-nine that do not. Group updates, auto-merge patch bumps that pass CI, ignore noise you have consciously accepted, and route the remainder to a named owner. A smaller number of alerts that someone actually acts on beats complete coverage that everyone ignores.

When a secret is found

incident order — rotation first, always
1 ROTATE  the credential is compromised from the moment it was pushed.
   Assume it was scraped within seconds — public repos are monitored
   continuously by people looking for exactly this.

2 ASSESS  what could it reach? check the provider's audit log for use
   between the push time and the rotation.

3 PURGE   git filter-repo, force-push, everyone re-clones.
   This is hygiene, not containment — every existing clone still has it.

4 PREVENT enable push protection; add gitleaks to pre-commit;
   move the value to a secrets manager.

# Find what is already there before someone else does:
$ gitleaks detect --source . --redact --report-format sarif -r leaks.sarif
$ gh api repos/acme/api/secret-scanning/alerts --jq '.[] | [.state, .secret_type] | @tsv'

Self-hosted runners

Never attach a self-hosted runner to a public repository

Anyone can open a pull request, and on a public repo that means anyone can run code on your runner. If it is persistent, they can leave something behind for the next job — including one from a private repository sharing the same runner pool. If it sits in your VPC, they have a foothold inside your network. Use ephemeral, single-job runners in an isolated network, or use GitHub-hosted runners for anything public.

Interview follow-up

“What is the biggest security risk in a GitHub organisation?” Not the source code — it is CI. A workflow is arbitrary code with a token and secrets, so the three questions are: can a fork cause code to execute with secrets (pull_request_target), can untrusted text reach a shell (script injection), and can a third-party action be repointed (unpinned uses:). Add least-privilege token permissions and push protection, and you have covered the paths that actually get exploited.

H07

Issues, projects & automation

The planning layer is worth exactly as much as the discipline behind it. Automation is what supplies the discipline, because conventions that depend on people remembering decay within a quarter.

Issue forms beat free text

yaml · .github/ISSUE_TEMPLATE/bug.yml
name: Bug report
description: Something is broken
labels: [bug, needs-triage]
body:
  - type: input
    id: version
    attributes: { label: Version, placeholder: "2.14.3" }
    validations: { required: true }

  - type: dropdown
    id: environment
    attributes:
      label: Environment
      options: [production, staging, local]
    validations: { required: true }

  - type: textarea
    id: reproduce
    attributes:
      label: Steps to reproduce
      description: The exact sequence, from a clean state
    validations: { required: true }

  - type: textarea
    id: expected
    attributes: { label: Expected vs actual }
    validations: { required: true }

  - type: input
    id: request-id
    attributes:
      label: Request ID
      description: From the error page or response header — this is what makes it findable in logs
Required fields are the whole point

A free-text bug template gets "it doesn't work" and then three round trips to establish version, environment and reproduction. A form with required fields makes those round trips impossible to skip. The request id field is the highest-value one: it turns a vague report into a log query and a trace, which is usually the difference between a five-minute diagnosis and an afternoon.

Labels as a small, enforced taxonomy

DimensionValuesApplied by
Typebug feature chore docstemplate, then triage
Priorityp0 p1 p2 p3triage only — a human decision
Areaarea:payments area:authautomated from changed paths
Statusneeds-triage blocked readyautomation + humans
Sizesize/xs … size/xlautomated from diff size

Keep it under about twenty labels. A taxonomy nobody can hold in their head is applied inconsistently, and inconsistent labels are worse than none because they create false confidence in the filters built on them.

Automating triage

yaml · label by path, size, and route to owners
name: triage
on:
  pull_request_target:            # safe here: metadata only, no checkout
    types: [opened, synchronize, reopened]

permissions:
  pull-requests: write
  contents: read

jobs:
  label:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/labeler@v5          # reads .github/labeler.yml

      - uses: codelytv/pr-size-labeler@v1
        with:
          xs_max_size: 20
          s_max_size: 100
          m_max_size: 300
          l_max_size: 600
          message_if_xl: |
            This PR changes more than 600 lines. Review quality drops sharply
            above this size — consider splitting it into stacked PRs.

      - name: Warn on missing tests
        if: contains(github.event.pull_request.labels.*.name, 'bug')
        run: |
          FILES=$(gh pr diff ${{ github.event.number }} --name-only)
          if ! grep -qE '(test|spec)' <<< "$FILES"; then
            gh pr comment ${{ github.event.number }} \
              --body "This fixes a bug but changes no tests. Is there a regression test?"
          fi
        env: { GH_TOKEN: '${{ secrets.GITHUB_TOKEN }}' }
yaml · .github/labeler.yml
'area:payments':
  - changed-files: [{ any-glob-to-any-file: ['services/payments/**'] }]
'area:infra':
  - changed-files: [{ any-glob-to-any-file: ['infra/**', '.github/workflows/**'] }]
'needs-migration-review':
  - changed-files: [{ any-glob-to-any-file: ['db/migrations/**'] }]
'documentation':
  - changed-files: [{ all-globs-to-all-files: ['**/*.md'] }]   # docs-ONLY changes

Projects v2

Custom fields
Status, iteration, estimate, priority, team. Fields live on the project, not the issue, so one issue can appear in several projects with different values.
Views
Table for triage, board for a sprint, roadmap for planning. Same data, saved filters and grouping.
Built-in workflows
Auto-add matching items, set status on close, move to "In review" when a PR is linked. Enough for most teams without writing any automation.
The GraphQL API
Projects v2 is GraphQL-only — there is no REST equivalent. Anything scripted goes through gh api graphql.
bash · adding an item and setting a field
# Add an issue to a project
gh project item-add 12 --owner acme --url https://github.com/acme/api/issues/451

# Setting a field requires ids, not names — resolve them once and cache them
gh api graphql -f query='
  query($org:String!, $number:Int!) {
    organization(login:$org) { projectV2(number:$number) {
      id
      fields(first:20) { nodes {
        ... on ProjectV2SingleSelectField { id name options { id name } }
      } }
    } }
  }' -f org=acme -F number=12

Measuring delivery honestly

bash · PR cycle time, from real data
gh pr list --state merged --limit 200 \
  --json number,createdAt,mergedAt,additions,deletions,reviews \
  --jq '.[] | {
      n: .number,
      hours: (((.mergedAt | fromdate) - (.createdAt | fromdate)) / 3600 | floor),
      size: (.additions + .deletions),
      reviewers: (.reviews | map(.author.login) | unique | length)
    }' \
| jq -s '{
    median_hours: (map(.hours) | sort | .[length/2|floor]),
    p90_hours:    (map(.hours) | sort | .[length*0.9|floor]),
    median_size:  (map(.size)  | sort | .[length/2|floor]),
    over_500_lines: (map(select(.size > 500)) | length)
  }'

# {"median_hours": 6, "p90_hours": 71, "median_size": 84, "over_500_lines": 23}
#
# The p90 is the number that matters. A median of 6 hours with a p90 of 71
# means most PRs move quickly and a long tail sits for three days — usually
# waiting on one reviewer, or blocked on a flaky check.
DORA metricGet it fromHonest caveat
Deployment frequencydeployments API, or releasescounts deploys, not value delivered
Lead time for changesfirst commit → deployedPR-open-to-merge is a proxy; it excludes the time before opening
Change failure ratedeploys followed by a rollback or hotfixrequires tagging incidents consistently, which is the hard part
Time to restoreincident open → resolvedonly as good as your incident discipline
These are diagnostics, not targets

Set deployment frequency as a team goal and you will get more, smaller, emptier deploys. Set lead time as a target and PRs get opened later. Use the numbers to find where work actually stalls — almost always review latency or a flaky check — and fix that. The metric is a thermometer, not a lever.

Interview follow-up

“How would you improve a team's delivery speed?” Measure first: PR cycle time with its p90, and where the time sits — waiting for review, waiting for CI, or waiting for a human decision. It is almost always review latency, and the two effective interventions are smaller PRs (stacked, with the mechanical change separated) and automated routing via CODEOWNERS so nobody has to work out who should look at it. Adding process is what people try first and it rarely helps.

H08

The gh CLI & API

Everything the web interface does is an API call, and gh is the fastest way to reach it. This is where organisation-wide audits and one-off automation actually get written.

The command surface worth knowing

CommandDoes
gh auth login --scopes 'repo,read:org'Authenticate; gh auth status shows scopes
gh repo clone acme/api -- --depth 1Clone; args after -- go to git
gh repo list acme --limit 500 --json name,isArchivedEnumerate an org
gh pr create --fill --reviewer @acme/platformOpen a PR from commits
gh pr checks --watchLive CI status until it settles
gh pr merge --squash --auto --delete-branchMerge when green care
gh run list --workflow ci.yml --status failureRecent failures
gh run view <id> --log-failedOnly the failing step's log — the one you want
gh run rerun <id> --failedRe-run failed jobs only
gh workflow run deploy.yml -f env=stagingTrigger a dispatch workflow with inputs
gh secret set AWS_ROLE --env productionEnvironment-scoped secret
gh variable list --env stagingNon-secret configuration
gh api ... --paginate --jq '.[]'Anything not covered above
gh browse -n src/index.ts:42Open a file at a line in the browser

Structured output

--json plus --jq is the whole scripting model
$ gh pr list --json number,title,author,isDraft \
      --jq '.[] | select(.isDraft | not) | "\(.number)\t\(.author.login)\t\(.title)"'
451     ada     feat: tiered pricing
452     grace   fix: raise upstream timeout

# --json without --jq gives raw JSON for piping into jq or a script
$ gh run list --workflow ci.yml --limit 50 --json conclusion \
    | jq '[.[] | .conclusion] | group_by(.) | map({(.[0]): length}) | add'
{"success": 41, "failure": 7, "cancelled": 2}   # a 15% failure rate is worth investigating

# gh api reaches everything, with automatic pagination
$ gh api /orgs/acme/members --paginate --jq '.[].login' | wc -l
128

Two audits worth having

bash · repositories with no protection on the default branch
#!/usr/bin/env bash
set -Eeuo pipefail
ORG=${1:?usage: audit-protection.sh ORG}

gh repo list "$ORG" --limit 1000 --no-archived \
  --json name,defaultBranchRef --jq '.[] | select(.defaultBranchRef != null)
        | "\(.name)\t\(.defaultBranchRef.name)"' \
| while IFS=$'\t' read -r repo branch; do
    rules=$(gh api "repos/$ORG/$repo/rules/branches/$branch" --jq 'length' 2>/dev/null || echo 0)
    if [ "$rules" -eq 0 ]; then
      echo "UNPROTECTED  $ORG/$repo ($branch)"
    else
      # Protected, but check whether the bypass list makes it decorative
      bypass=$(gh api "repos/$ORG/$repo/rulesets" \
                 --jq '[.[] | .bypass_actors // [] | length] | add // 0' 2>/dev/null || echo 0)
      [ "$bypass" -gt 0 ] && echo "BYPASSABLE   $ORG/$repo ($bypass actors can bypass)"
    fi
  done
bash · open security alerts across the org
gh api graphql --paginate -f query='
query($org: String!, $cursor: String) {
  organization(login: $org) {
    repositories(first: 50, after: $cursor, isArchived: false) {
      pageInfo { hasNextPage endCursor }
      nodes {
        name
        vulnerabilityAlerts(first: 100, states: OPEN) {
          totalCount
          nodes {
            securityVulnerability {
              severity
              package { name }
              advisory { summary }
            }
          }
        }
      }
    }
  }
}' -f org=acme \
| jq -r '.data.organization.repositories.nodes[]
         | select(.vulnerabilityAlerts.totalCount > 0)
         | . as $r
         | $r.vulnerabilityAlerts.nodes[]
         | select(.securityVulnerability.severity == "CRITICAL")
         | [$r.name, .securityVulnerability.package.name,
            .securityVulnerability.advisory.summary] | @tsv'
REST or GraphQL?

REST for simple, well-covered resources — it is easier to read and --paginate just works. GraphQL when you need fields from several nested levels and REST would mean N+1 requests, as in the query above: one call returns every repository and its alerts, where REST would need one call per repository. The cost is that GraphQL pagination is per-connection and rate limits are computed on query complexity rather than request count.

Rate limits

bash · check before you loop over a thousand repositories
gh api /rate_limit --jq '.resources | {core: .core, graphql: .graphql, search: .search}'

# Primary limits:  5,000 req/hour authenticated; 15,000 for a GitHub App
#                  GraphQL is 5,000 POINTS/hour, not requests
#                  Search is 30 req/minute — far tighter, and easy to hit
#
# SECONDARY limits are the ones that surprise people: too many concurrent
# requests, or too many mutations in a short window, produce a 403 with
# `retry-after`. Respect that header rather than retrying immediately —
# ignoring it escalates to a longer block.

# In scripts: back off, and never run unbounded parallelism against the API
for repo in $(gh repo list acme --limit 500 --json name --jq '.[].name'); do
  gh api "repos/acme/$repo/topics" --jq '.names[]' || true
  sleep 0.2
done

gh inside Actions

yaml · gh is preinstalled; it just needs a token
      - name: Comment the coverage delta
        env:
          GH_TOKEN: ${{ secrets.GITHUB_TOKEN }}   # gh reads GH_TOKEN, not GITHUB_TOKEN
        run: |
          DELTA=$(node scripts/coverage-delta.js)
          gh pr comment "${{ github.event.number }}" --body "Coverage: $DELTA"
    permissions:
      pull-requests: write        # the token needs the scope for the operation

Extensions

gh extensions are just executables named gh-*
$ gh extension install dlvhdr/gh-dash        # a TUI for PRs and issues
$ gh extension list
$ gh extension create acme-audit               # scaffold your own

# An extension is any executable named gh-<name> on PATH. A 20-line shell
# script that your whole team runs as `gh acme-audit` is a real productivity
# win, and it version-controls like anything else.
Interview follow-up

“How would you find every repository in the organisation without branch protection?” The answer demonstrates whether you have automated GitHub or only clicked it: enumerate with gh repo list --json, check rulesets per repo with gh api, and — the detail that distinguishes — also check the bypass list, because a ruleset that everyone can bypass is not protection. Mentioning rate limits and a sleep in the loop shows you have run something like this against a real org.

B01

Linux for operators

Not a tutorial — the subset you reach for when something is wrong at 2am, organised by the question you are trying to answer.

The sixty-second triage

run these in order; each rules out a class of problem
$ uptime
 14:02:31 up 43 days,  load average: 18.4, 12.1, 6.2   # load ≫ cores: something is queueing

$ dmesg -T | tail -30
[Wed Apr 11 13:58] Out of memory: Killed process 2841 (node)   # the answer, often

$ vmstat 1 5
 r  b   swpd   free   buff  cache   si   so    bi    bo   in   cs us sy id wa
18  0      0  81232  12044 891232    0    0     0  4096 8201 12k 92  6  0  2
       ▲                                                              ▲
       r = runnable, 18 > cores → CPU-bound       us 92% → user code

$ iostat -xz 1 3        # %util near 100 and high await → disk-bound
$ free -h               # look at "available", NOT "free" — see below
$ df -h; df -i          # BOTH: inodes exhaust independently of bytes
$ ss -s                 # socket summary; TIME-WAIT in the tens of thousands?
$ top -o %CPU           # now, and only now, look at processes
"free" memory is not the number you want

Linux uses spare RAM as page cache, so free is usually near zero on a healthy machine and that is correct — the cache is reclaimable. The column to read is available, which accounts for what can be freed under pressure. Alerting on low free pages the team every night for a system working exactly as designed.

Processes and signals

CommandDoesNotes
ps aux --sort=-%mem | headTop memory consumerssafe
pgrep -af nodePIDs with full command linebetter than ps | grep
kill -TERM <pid>Ask politely to stopthe process can clean up care
kill -KILL <pid>Terminate immediatelyno cleanup, no flush destructive
kill -HUP <pid>Reload config (by convention)nginx, many daemons
kill -QUIT <pid>Dump state and exitGo prints all goroutine stacks — invaluable on a hang
lsof -p <pid>Open files and socketsfind the fd leak
ss -tlnpWhat is listening, and which processreplaces netstat
strace -f -p <pid> -e trace=networkSyscallsslows the target substantially care
nsenter -t <pid> -n ss -tlnpRun a command in another process's namespacehow you debug inside a container with no tools
The OOM killer and container exit code 137

When memory runs out the kernel picks a victim by oom_score, which favours large recent allocators — usually your application. The process receives no signal it can handle and simply vanishes; in a container this surfaces as exit code 137 with no application log. Look in dmesg -T for the kill line, and check whether the container's memory limit or the host's memory was exhausted, because the remedies differ.

systemd

ini · /etc/systemd/system/api.service
[Unit]
Description=Acme API
After=network-online.target
Wants=network-online.target

[Service]
Type=simple
User=acme
WorkingDirectory=/opt/acme
EnvironmentFile=/etc/acme/api.env
ExecStart=/usr/bin/node /opt/acme/dist/server.js

Restart=on-failure
RestartSec=5
StartLimitIntervalSec=60        # if it fails 5 times in 60s, stop trying —
StartLimitBurst=5               # otherwise a crash loop hides the real error

# Graceful shutdown: SIGTERM, then 30s, then SIGKILL
KillSignal=SIGTERM
TimeoutStopSec=30

# Cheap hardening, no application change required
NoNewPrivileges=true
PrivateTmp=true
ProtectSystem=strict
ProtectHome=true
ReadWritePaths=/var/lib/acme
MemoryMax=2G
LimitNOFILE=65535               # the default 1024 is far too low for a server

[Install]
WantedBy=multi-user.target
CommandDoes
systemctl daemon-reloadRequired after editing any unit file
systemctl enable --now apiStart now and on boot — the two are separate
systemctl status apiState, recent logs, the actual command line
journalctl -u api -fFollow logs
journalctl -u api --since "10 min ago" -p errErrors only, in a window
journalctl -u api -b -1Logs from the previous boot — after a reboot you did not expect
systemd-analyze blameWhat made boot slow
systemctl list-timersTimers — the modern replacement for cron

Disk, and the two ways it fills

"no space left on device" with 40% free
$ df -h /var
Filesystem  Size  Used Avail Use% Mounted on
/dev/sda1   100G   41G   59G  41% /var          # plenty of bytes…

$ df -i /var
Filesystem  Inodes  IUsed IFree IUse% Mounted on
/dev/sda1     6.5M   6.5M     0  100% /var       # …and no inodes
# Millions of tiny session files. Bytes and inodes exhaust independently.

$ sudo du -x --max-depth=1 /var 2>/dev/null | sort -rn | head
# -x stays on one filesystem, so it does not wander into /proc or a mount

# The classic trap: a deleted file still held open by a process. The bytes
# are NOT reclaimed until the fd closes, and du cannot see them.
$ sudo lsof +L1 | head
node  2841  acme  7w  REG  8,1  4294967296  0  /var/log/api.log (deleted)
# Fix: restart the process, or truncate via /proc:
$ sudo truncate -s 0 /proc/2841/fd/7

Text processing you will actually use

bash · log forensics without a log platform
# Top 10 IPs by request count
awk '{print $1}' access.log | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn | head

# Requests per minute, to find the spike
awk '{print substr($4, 2, 17)}' access.log | uniq -c

# 5xx rate by endpoint
awk '$9 ~ /^5/ {print $7}' access.log | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn | head

# p95 response time from a JSON log
jq -r 'select(.durationMs) | .durationMs' app.log \
  | sort -n | awk '{a[NR]=$1} END {print "p95:", a[int(NR*0.95)]"ms"}'

# Follow a live log for a single request id
tail -F app.log | jq -c 'select(.requestId == "req_7c1d")'

# Anything with xargs: use -0 and -print0, or filenames with spaces break
find /var/log -name '*.gz' -mtime +30 -print0 | xargs -0 -r rm --

Permissions

ConceptMeaning
644 / 755owner rw / rwx, group and other read (and execute for directories)
600owner only — correct for keys, .env files and certificates
Directory xpermission to traverse, not to execute. Without it you cannot reach anything inside, even with read
setgid on a directorynew files inherit the group — how shared directories stay usable
Sticky bit (/tmp)only the owner may delete their own files in a world-writable directory
umask 022the default mask; new files are 644, directories 755
Interview follow-up

“A server is slow — what do you check?” The value is in having an order that eliminates classes of cause rather than a list of commands. Load average against core count, then dmesg for OOM kills and disk errors, then vmstat to separate CPU-bound from IO-bound from swapping, then iostat, then memory available, then disk bytes and inodes, then sockets, and only then per-process. Naming the deleted-file-still-open trap or the inode exhaustion case shows you have actually been paged for this.

B02

Networking for operators

"The service is unreachable" has about eight distinct causes. Working through the layers in order finds it in minutes; guessing takes an afternoon.

The unreachable-service checklist

Work OUTWARD from the application, one layer at a time. 1 Does the name resolve? dig +short api.acme.com ↳ no → DNS: record missing, wrong zone, or split-horizon 2 Is the port open? nc -vz api.acme.com 443 ↳ refused → nothing listening, or the process bound to 127.0.0.1 ↳ timeout → firewall or security group is DROPPING (not rejecting) 3 Does TLS complete? openssl s_client -connect host:443 -servername host ↳ no → expired cert, wrong SNI, chain incomplete, protocol mismatch 4 Does HTTP respond? curl -v https://api.acme.com/healthz ↳ 502 → the proxy reached nothing; 504 → upstream too slow 5 Is the app listening correctly? ss -tlnp | grep 3000 ↳ 127.0.0.1:3000 is the single most common cause — bind 0.0.0.0 6 Path MTU / packet loss? mtr -rw api.acme.com Refused vs timeout is the fastest signal you have: connection refused → you REACHED the host, nothing was listening connection timeout → a firewall dropped the packet silently
curl -w is the whole latency breakdown
$ curl -sS -o /dev/null -w '
dns:      %{time_namelookup}s
connect:  %{time_connect}s
tls:      %{time_appconnect}s
ttfb:     %{time_starttransfer}s
total:    %{time_total}s
code:     %{http_code}\n' https://api.acme.com/healthz

dns:      0.004s
connect:  0.021s
tls:      0.089s
ttfb:     1.842s   # connection is fast; the SERVER is slow
total:    1.844s
code:     200

# Test a specific backend, bypassing DNS and the load balancer entirely:
$ curl -v --resolve api.acme.com:443:10.0.3.17 https://api.acme.com/healthz

DNS

CommandDoes
dig +short api.acme.comJust the answer
dig +trace api.acme.comFull delegation from the root — finds a broken NS
dig @8.8.8.8 api.acme.comAsk a specific resolver, bypassing local cache
dig api.acme.com CAAWhich CAs may issue — a common cause of failed cert renewal
dig -x 10.0.3.17Reverse lookup
dig api.acme.com +noall +answer +ttlidSee the remaining TTL — how long a change takes to propagate
TTL is a hint, not a contract

A 60-second TTL does not mean 60-second failover. Resolvers ignore short TTLs, corporate networks cache aggressively, JVMs historically cached forever, and browsers pin connections independently of DNS. Observed DNS-only failover is routinely 5–15 minutes. If your RTO is tighter, you need anycast or a load balancer that shifts traffic at the network layer — not a shorter TTL.

TCP states that matter

ss -s, and what the numbers mean
$ ss -s
TCP:   28451 (estab 812, closed 27102, orphaned 0, timewait 26980)

# 27k TIME-WAIT: this host is CLOSING connections rather than reusing them.
# Each holds an ephemeral port for ~60s. At ~28k usable ports you are close
# to exhaustion, after which new outbound connections fail with EADDRNOTAVAIL.
#
# Cause: HTTP keep-alive is off somewhere — an SDK, a proxy, or a client
# creating a new agent per request. Fix keep-alive rather than tuning sysctls.

$ ss -tan state time-wait | awk '{print $5}' | cut -d: -f1 | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn | head
  26440 10.0.2.44     # one destination — that is the culprit

$ ss -tanp state syn-sent    # stuck connecting: firewall or dead backend
$ ss -tanp | awk '$2 > 0 || $3 > 0'   # non-empty send/recv queues = backpressure
TIME-WAIT
Normal on the side that closes first. Thousands of them means connections are not being reused.
CLOSE-WAIT
The application has not closed its socket after the peer did. Growing CLOSE-WAIT is an application file-descriptor leak, not a network problem.
SYN-SENT
Stuck opening. Firewall dropping, or the destination is gone.
Recv-Q growing
The application is not reading fast enough — backpressure.

TLS

bash · certificate debugging
# Full handshake, chain, and expiry. -servername sets SNI, which matters
# because a shared IP serves different certificates by hostname.
openssl s_client -connect api.acme.com:443 -servername api.acme.com < /dev/null 2>/dev/null \
  | openssl x509 -noout -subject -issuer -dates -ext subjectAltName

# Expiry across a fleet — run it daily and alert at 21 days
for h in api.acme.com www.acme.com admin.acme.com; do
  end=$(echo | openssl s_client -connect "$h:443" -servername "$h" 2>/dev/null \
        | openssl x509 -noout -enddate | cut -d= -f2)
  days=$(( ( $(date -d "$end" +%s) - $(date +%s) ) / 86400 ))
  printf '%-20s %3d days\n' "$h" "$days"
done

# "certificate verify failed" from a server but the browser is happy?
# The browser fills in missing intermediates from its own cache; curl does not.
# The server is sending an INCOMPLETE CHAIN. Fix the server, not the client.
openssl s_client -connect api.acme.com:443 -showcerts < /dev/null | grep -c 'BEGIN CERT'
"Works in the browser, fails in curl" means an incomplete chain

Browsers cache intermediate certificates and can also fetch them via the AIA extension, so a server that omits its intermediate still works for humans and fails for every SDK, container and health check. Always test with openssl s_client -showcerts and confirm the full chain is served — this is one of the most common causes of a deployment that passes manual testing and fails in production.

Cloud networking concepts

ConceptOperational consequence
Security groupStateful — allow inbound and the reply is automatically permitted. Deny by default.
Network ACLStateless — you must allow the return traffic explicitly, including ephemeral ports. The usual cause of "the rule looks right and it still fails".
Private subnetNo route to an internet gateway. Outbound needs a NAT gateway, which is charged per gigabyte and is a frequent surprise on the bill.
Cross-AZ trafficCharged, and adds ~1 ms. Chatty microservices spread across zones can cost more in transfer than in compute.
Private endpointReach a managed service without traversing the internet or a NAT gateway — cheaper and more secure.
MTUOverlay networks and VPNs reduce the usable MTU. Symptom: small requests work, large ones hang. Test with ping -M do -s 1472.

Packet capture, when nothing else explains it

bash · a readable tcpdump
# Connection attempts to a specific backend
sudo tcpdump -nni any 'host 10.0.3.17 and port 5432' -c 100

# SYNs with no reply — a firewall dropping silently
sudo tcpdump -nni any 'tcp[tcpflags] & tcp-syn != 0 and not tcp[tcpflags] & tcp-ack != 0'

# Resets, which usually mean an application or proxy is refusing
sudo tcpdump -nni any 'tcp[tcpflags] & tcp-rst != 0'

# Capture to a file and open it in Wireshark — far easier than reading it live
sudo tcpdump -nni any -w /tmp/capture.pcap 'port 443' -G 60 -W 1

# Inside a container that has no tcpdump, run it in the container's namespace:
sudo nsenter -t "$(docker inspect -f '{{.State.Pid}}' api)" -n tcpdump -nni any port 3000
Interview follow-up

“Service A cannot reach service B — walk me through it.” Go outward layer by layer and say what each result eliminates: resolve the name, test the port, note whether it is refused or timed out (application versus firewall — this single distinction halves the search), complete TLS, then HTTP, then check what the process is actually bound to. Mentioning 127.0.0.1 versus 0.0.0.0 and stateless NACLs needing explicit return rules signals real operational experience rather than a memorised layer model.

B03

Shell scripting that survives

Operational scripts run unattended, often as root, against production. The default shell behaviour — continue after errors, silently expand unset variables — is exactly wrong for that, and every safe script starts by turning it off.

The prologue

bash · the first lines of every script
#!/usr/bin/env bash
set -Eeuo pipefail
IFS=$'\n\t'

#  -E  ERR traps are inherited by functions and subshells
#  -e  exit on any unhandled non-zero status
#  -u  an unset variable is an error, not an empty string
#  -o pipefail  a pipeline fails if ANY stage fails, not only the last
#
# Without pipefail:  curl bad-url | jq .   →  exits 0, because jq succeeded
# on empty input. The script "succeeded" and did nothing.
#
# IFS without a space stops unquoted expansion splitting on spaces — which is
# what turns "My File.txt" into two arguments.

cleanup() {
  local code=$?
  [[ -n "${tmpdir:-}" ]] && rm -rf "$tmpdir"
  exit "$code"          # preserve the original exit code
}
trap cleanup EXIT
trap 'echo "FAILED at line $LINENO: $BASH_COMMAND" >&2' ERR

tmpdir=$(mktemp -d)
Where set -e does not fire

It is weaker than people assume. It does not trigger for a command used as a condition (if cmd, cmd && …, ! cmd), for any command except the last in a pipeline unless pipefail is set, or inside a function whose result is tested. set -e is a safety net, not a guarantee — check the exit status explicitly wherever failure genuinely matters.

Quoting — the source of most shell bugs

bash
file="My Report.pdf"

rm $file            # WRONG: two arguments, "My" and "Report.pdf"
rm "$file"          # right

files=(*.txt)                                   # array handles spaces
for f in "${files[@]}"; do process "$f"; done   # the quotes are essential

# find + read: the only fully safe pattern for arbitrary filenames
while IFS= read -r -d '' f; do
  process "$f"
done < <(find . -name '*.log' -print0)

count="$(wc -l < "$file")"                      # quote command substitution
if (( count > 100 )); then echo large; fi       # no $ needed inside (( ))
if [[ "$name" =~ ^acme-[0-9]+$ ]]; then echo match; fi   # [[ ]] over [ ]

# Parameter expansion replaces most calls to sed
: "${ENVIRONMENT:=staging}"                     # default if unset
: "${API_TOKEN:?API_TOKEN is required}"         # fail loudly, at the top
dir="${path%/*}"                                # strip shortest from the end
name="${path##*/}"                              # strip longest from the start
safe="${input//[^a-zA-Z0-9_-]/}"                # drop disallowed characters

Structure

bash · a deployment script worth copying
#!/usr/bin/env bash
set -Eeuo pipefail
readonly LOCK_FILE=/var/lock/deploy.lock

# Logs go to STDERR so stdout stays clean for the script's real output —
# that is what lets the script compose inside a pipeline.
log()  { printf '%s [%s] %s\n' "$(date -u +%FT%TZ)" "$1" "${*:2}" >&2; }
info() { log INFO "$@"; }
die()  { log ERROR "$@"; exit 1; }

usage() {
  printf '%s\n' \
    'Usage: deploy.sh -e ENV -i IMAGE [-n] [-t SECONDS]' \
    '  -e ENV      staging | production' \
    '  -i IMAGE    image reference — must be a digest, not a tag' \
    '  -n          dry run: print what would happen, change nothing' \
    '  -t SECONDS  rollout timeout (default 300)'
}

retry() {
  local attempts=$1; shift
  local n=0
  until "$@"; do
    n=$((n + 1))
    (( n >= attempts )) && return 1
    local wait=$(( 2 ** n + RANDOM % 3 ))       # backoff with jitter
    log WARN "attempt $n failed, retrying in ${wait}s"
    sleep "$wait"
  done
}

main() {
  local env='' image='' dry_run=0 timeout=300
  while getopts ':e:i:nt:h' opt; do
    case "$opt" in
      e) env=$OPTARG ;;
      i) image=$OPTARG ;;
      n) dry_run=1 ;;
      t) timeout=$OPTARG ;;
      h) usage; exit 0 ;;
      :) die "option -$OPTARG requires an argument" ;;
      \?) usage >&2; die "unknown option -$OPTARG" ;;
    esac
  done

  [[ -n $env ]] || { usage >&2; die '-e is required'; }
  [[ $env =~ ^(staging|production)$ ]] || die "unknown environment: $env"
  [[ $image == *"@sha256:"* ]] || die 'image must be a digest, not a tag'

  # Prevent concurrent deploys. flock releases when the fd closes, including
  # on kill -9 — which a lock file containing a PID does not.
  exec 200>"$LOCK_FILE"
  flock -n 200 || die 'another deploy is in progress'

  info "deploying $image to $env"
  (( dry_run )) && { info 'dry run — stopping here'; return 0; }

  retry 3 kubectl -n "$env" set image deploy/api "api=$image"
  if ! timeout "$timeout" kubectl -n "$env" rollout status deploy/api; then
    log ERROR 'rollout failed — rolling back'
    kubectl -n "$env" rollout undo deploy/api
    exit 1
  fi
  info 'deployed successfully'
}

main "$@"
Idempotent and re-runnable is the design goal

An operational script will be interrupted — a dropped SSH session, a closed laptop, a Ctrl-C. The question is not whether it fails halfway but whether running it again is safe. Prefer declarative operations (kubectl apply, mkdir -p, rsync) over imperative ones, check the desired state before changing it, and make every step safe to repeat.

Things that bite

PatternProblemInstead
cd /some/dirIf it fails, everything after runs in the wrong directory — possibly a rm -rfcd /some/dir || exit 1, or a subshell
rm -rf "$dir/"*If $dir is unset this becomes rm -rf /*set -u, plus an explicit non-empty check
for f in $(ls)Breaks on spaces and newlines in filenamesA glob, or find -print0
cmd 2>&1 > fileOrder matters — this sends stderr to the old stdoutcmd &> file
Parsing lsOutput is for humans and varies by locale and terminalfind, stat, or a glob
No timeout on a remote callThe script hangs forever, usually in CItimeout 30 curl …
Secrets as argumentsVisible in ps to every user on the hostEnvironment variable or a file

Linting and testing

bash
# shellcheck finds most of the table above, statically. Run it in CI and
# treat the findings as errors, not suggestions.
shellcheck -x -S style scripts/*.sh

# bats for behaviour
@test 'rejects a tag instead of a digest' {
  run ./deploy.sh -e staging -i ghcr.io/acme/api:latest
  [ "$status" -eq 1 ]
  [[ "$output" == *'must be a digest'* ]]
}
When to stop writing bash

Roughly: past 150 lines, any non-trivial data structure, any JSON handling beyond a couple of jq calls, anything needing real error types, or anything a second person must maintain. Bash is excellent glue between programs and a poor language for logic. Rewriting a 400-line script in Python usually takes an afternoon and repays it the first time someone has to change it.

Interview follow-up

“What is wrong with this script?” The expected findings are a missing set -Eeuo pipefail, unquoted variables, no timeout on network calls, no lock against concurrent runs, and no cleanup trap. If you can also state why pipefail matters — that curl | jq exits zero when curl fails — you are describing the specific failure rather than reciting the incantation.

B04

Docker fundamentals

A container is a process with a restricted view of the machine. Almost every confusing container behaviour makes sense once you stop thinking of it as a small virtual machine.

What a container actually is

NAMESPACES — what the process can SEE pid its own process tree; your app is PID 1 net its own interfaces, routes and ports mnt its own filesystem view uts its own hostname ipc its own shared memory user uid mapping, when enabled CGROUPS — what the process can USE cpu shares and quota → throttling, not killing memory a hard limit → OOM kill, not throttling pids process count io block device bandwidth That is the whole mechanism. No guest kernel, no hypervisor, no boot. docker run is clone() with flags, plus a chroot, plus cgroup writes. Consequences that surprise people: · the kernel is shared — a kernel panic takes down every container · uname -r reports the HOST kernel · /proc/cpuinfo reports HOST cores, not your CPU limit · your process is PID 1, and PID 1 has no default signal handlers

Images and layers

layers are immutable, content-addressed and shared
$ docker history ghcr.io/acme/api:latest --format '{{.Size}}\t{{.CreatedBy}}'
0B        CMD ["node" "dist/server.js"]
2.1MB     COPY dist ./dist
184MB     RUN npm ci --omit=dev          # the expensive layer
1.4kB     COPY package*.json ./
74.8MB    FROM node:22-slim

# Edit dist/       → only the 2.1MB layer rebuilds.
# Edit package.json → the 184MB layer rebuilds too.
# That is the entire reason manifests are copied before source.

# Two images sharing a base share the bytes on disk and in the registry,
# so pulling the second one is nearly free.

# A TAG is a mutable pointer. A DIGEST is the identity.
$ docker pull ghcr.io/acme/api@sha256:9f3c...   # reproducible
$ docker pull ghcr.io/acme/api:latest           # whatever it means today

The commands, with the flags that matter

CommandDoesNotes
docker run --rm -it img shThrowaway interactive shellwithout --rm you accumulate stopped containers
docker run -p 127.0.0.1:8080:3000host:container, bound to loopbackbare -p 8080:3000 exposes on every interface
docker run --initA real PID 1 that reaps zombies and forwards signalsuse this unless your entrypoint handles signals
docker run --memory 512m --cpus 1.5cgroup limitsmemory kills, CPU throttles — not symmetric
docker run --read-only --tmpfs /tmpImmutable root filesystemcheap, effective hardening
docker run --cap-drop ALLDrop all capabilitiesadd back only what is needed, usually nothing
docker logs -f --since 10m --tail 100 cRecent logscaptures stdout and stderr only
docker stats --no-streamLive CPU, memory, IOthe fastest "is it throttled" check
docker inspect -f '{{.State.ExitCode}}' cWhy it died137 = SIGKILL/OOM, 143 = SIGTERM
docker system dfDisk used by images, containers, volumes, cacherun before pruning
docker system prune -a --volumesReclaim spacedestructive — --volumes deletes data

The PID 1 problem

dockerfile · shell form silently breaks graceful shutdown
# SHELL FORM — becomes: /bin/sh -c "node dist/server.js"
# /bin/sh is PID 1 and does NOT forward SIGTERM to node.
# docker stop waits 10s, then SIGKILLs. Your shutdown handler never ran,
# in-flight requests are dropped, connections are not closed cleanly.
CMD node dist/server.js

# EXEC FORM — node IS PID 1 and receives the signal directly
CMD ["node", "dist/server.js"]

# But PID 1 gets no DEFAULT signal handlers from the kernel: if the app does
# not explicitly handle SIGTERM, nothing happens and it still needs SIGKILL.
# An init process supplies a correct PID 1 and reaps zombie children.
ENTRYPOINT ["/usr/bin/tini", "--"]
CMD ["node", "dist/server.js"]
The symptom is a rolling deploy that drops requests

Everything works locally, and every production deploy produces a small burst of 502s that nobody can reproduce. The cause is that SIGTERM never reached the application, so it never began draining and was killed mid-request. Check with docker inspect --format '{{.Config.Cmd}}' — if it prints a single string rather than an array, you are in shell form.

Networking and storage

Network modeBehaviourUse for
bridge (default)Private network, NAT to the host; ports must be publishedmost things
user-defined bridgeAs above, plus DNS resolution by container namecompose stacks — services reach each other by name
hostNo network isolation; binds directly to host interfacesperformance-sensitive, or very many ports
noneLoopback onlybatch jobs with no network need
StorageBehaviourUse for
Named volumeDocker-managed, survives container removal, correct ownershipdatabases and persistent state
Bind mountA host path; ownership and permissions come from the hostsource code during development
tmpfsMemory only, discarded on stopscratch space in a read-only container
Bind-mount permission mismatches

The container's uid must be able to write the host directory. Running as node (uid 1000) against a directory owned by uid 501 gives EACCES, and the usual "fix" of running as root leaves every created file root-owned on the host. Either align the uid with --user "$(id -u):$(id -g)", or use a named volume, which Docker initialises with the right ownership.

Debugging a container with no shell

distroless has no sh — and that is the point
$ docker exec -it api sh
OCI runtime exec failed: exec: "sh": executable file not found in $PATH

# Attach a debug container that SHARES the target's namespaces: you get
# your tools, and its network, process table and filesystem.
$ docker run -it --rm \
    --pid container:api \
    --network container:api \
    --cap-add SYS_PTRACE \
    nicolaka/netshoot

$ ss -tlnp                            # what is it actually listening on?
$ ls -l /proc/1/root/app              # read its filesystem through /proc
$ tr '\0' '\n' < /proc/1/environ      # its environment, including config

# Kubernetes has this built in:
$ kubectl debug -it pod/api --image=nicolaka/netshoot --target=api

Resource limits and what the app sees

bash · the container does not tell your runtime the truth
docker run --cpus 1 --memory 512m node:22 node -e '
  console.log(require("os").cpus().length);          // 16 — the HOST cores
  console.log(require("os").availableParallelism()); // 1  — respects the cgroup
'
# Runtimes that size thread pools or worker counts from cpus().length will
# fork 16 workers onto a 1-core quota and spend most of their time throttled.

# Check whether you are being throttled — this is the number that matters:
cat /sys/fs/cgroup/cpu.stat | grep throttled
#   nr_throttled 8421      ← the container is hitting its quota constantly
#   throttled_usec 91203441

# And the memory ceiling the kernel will enforce:
cat /sys/fs/cgroup/memory.max
Interview follow-up

“Why does my container get SIGKILLed on every deploy?” Because PID 1 has no default signal handlers, and with a shell-form entrypoint the signal reaches /bin/sh rather than your process at all. A complete answer names both halves — exec form and either an init process or an explicit SIGTERM handler — then connects it to the visible symptom, which is dropped requests during rolling updates rather than anything that looks like a container problem.

B05

Dockerfiles & image optimisation

Two rules produce most of the benefit: order instructions so the expensive layer is cached, and never let build tooling or secrets reach the final image.

Layer ordering is the whole cache story

Every instruction produces a layer. A layer's cache is invalidated when its inputs change — and every layer after it is invalidated too. SLOW FAST COPY . . COPY package*.json ./ RUN npm ci ← 90s RUN npm ci ← 90s, CACHED RUN npm run build COPY . . RUN npm run build ← 12s Any source edit invalidates A source edit invalidates only the COPY, so npm ci reruns. last two layers. Same for pip (requirements.txt), cargo (Cargo.toml + a dummy main), go (go.mod + go.sum), bundler (Gemfile + Gemfile.lock).

A production Dockerfile, annotated

dockerfile · Node service
# syntax=docker/dockerfile:1.7
# Pin the base by digest: a tag can be repointed, and then your build is
# no longer reproducible. Dependabot keeps the digest current.
FROM node:22.11-bookworm-slim@sha256:a1b2c3... AS deps
WORKDIR /app
COPY package.json package-lock.json ./
# --mount=type=cache keeps the package manager's cache OUTSIDE the layer,
# so it speeds up builds without adding a single byte to the image.
RUN --mount=type=cache,target=/root/.npm \
    npm ci --omit=dev --ignore-scripts

FROM node:22.11-bookworm-slim@sha256:a1b2c3... AS build
WORKDIR /app
COPY package.json package-lock.json ./
RUN --mount=type=cache,target=/root/.npm npm ci --ignore-scripts
COPY . .
# --mount=type=secret: the token is available during this RUN and is never
# written to a layer. Putting it in an ARG or ENV bakes it into the image.
RUN --mount=type=secret,id=npm_token \
    NPM_TOKEN="$(cat /run/secrets/npm_token)" npm run build

FROM node:22.11-bookworm-slim@sha256:a1b2c3... AS runtime
ENV NODE_ENV=production
WORKDIR /app

# Copy only artefacts. The compiler, dev dependencies, git history and
# test fixtures stay in the build stage and never ship.
COPY --from=deps  --chown=node:node /app/node_modules ./node_modules
COPY --from=build --chown=node:node /app/dist ./dist
COPY --chown=node:node package.json ./

USER node                       # never run as root
EXPOSE 3000                     # documentation only; it publishes nothing
ENTRYPOINT ["node", "--enable-source-maps", "dist/server.js"]

LABEL org.opencontainers.image.source=https://github.com/acme/api \
      org.opencontainers.image.revision=$GIT_SHA
bash · .dockerignore matters more than people expect
node_modules
.git
.env*
coverage
dist
*.log
.github
**/*.test.ts

# Without this the build CONTEXT — everything sent to the daemon before the
# build starts — includes .git and node_modules. That is frequently hundreds
# of megabytes uploaded on every build, and a real risk of COPY . . baking
# an .env file into a layer where it is extractable forever.

Base image choice

BaseSizeTrade-off
node:22~1.1 GBfull Debian with build tools — for build stages, not runtime
node:22-slim~200 MBthe sensible default: glibc, a shell, a package manager
node:22-alpine~140 MBmusl — native modules need rebuilding; documented DNS and performance differences
distroless/nodejs22~110 MBno shell, no package manager — smallest attack surface, hardest to debug
scratch0only for a statically linked binary (Go, Rust)
Alpine is not automatically the right answer

musl instead of glibc means native modules must be rebuilt, some prebuilt binaries do not exist, and there are long-standing reports of slower DNS resolution and worse performance in allocation-heavy workloads. The -slim images are only tens of megabytes larger and behave like the platform you developed and tested on. Choose alpine deliberately after measuring, not reflexively because the number is smaller.

Secrets, and the layer that remembers

dockerfile · every wrong way, and the right one
# WRONG — visible in `docker history` forever
ARG NPM_TOKEN
RUN npm ci

# WRONG — present in every layer's environment, and in `docker inspect`
ENV AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY=...

# WRONG — deleting in a LATER layer does not remove it from the earlier one.
# The file is still in the image and trivially extractable.
COPY .npmrc .
RUN npm ci && rm .npmrc

# RIGHT — BuildKit secret mount: present only during this RUN, never in a layer
RUN --mount=type=secret,id=npmrc,target=/root/.npmrc npm ci

# RIGHT — SSH agent forwarding for private git dependencies
RUN --mount=type=ssh git clone git@github.com:acme/private-lib.git
proving it to yourself
$ docker build --secret id=npmrc,src=$HOME/.npmrc -t api .

# The wrong version leaves this behind:
$ docker history api:bad --no-trunc | grep -i token
RUN |1 NPM_TOKEN=npm_9f3c8a... npm ci     # extractable by anyone with the image

# Scan the built image before it reaches a registry
$ trivy image --severity HIGH,CRITICAL --exit-code 1 api:latest
$ docker sbom api:latest

Multi-architecture builds

bash
# One manifest, two architectures. Apple Silicon laptops and Graviton
# instances both pull the right one automatically.
docker buildx build \
  --platform linux/amd64,linux/arm64 \
  --tag ghcr.io/acme/api:1.4.0 \
  --push .

# QEMU emulation makes the non-native architecture 5-20x slower to build.
# For anything non-trivial, build each architecture on its own native runner
# and merge the manifests — usually a matrix job plus:
docker buildx imagetools create -t ghcr.io/acme/api:1.4.0 \
  ghcr.io/acme/api:1.4.0-amd64 ghcr.io/acme/api:1.4.0-arm64

Size, and where it actually goes

TechniqueTypical saving
Multi-stage: ship artefacts, not toolchainslargest single win — often 500 MB+
--omit=dev / production install100–300 MB in a Node project
A .dockerignore that excludes .gitvaries; frequently 100 MB+ of context
Combine apt-get update, install and clean in one RUN~40 MB, and avoids a stale package index
Slim or distroless base50–100 MB
Cache mounts instead of committed cachesvaries — and speeds up rebuilds
dockerfile · the apt pattern, in one layer
RUN apt-get update \
 && apt-get install -y --no-install-recommends ca-certificates curl \
 && rm -rf /var/lib/apt/lists/*
# Separate RUN instructions would leave the package index in an earlier layer
# where the cleanup cannot reach it — and would let a cached `update` serve a
# stale index to a later `install`, which fails in confusing ways.
Interview follow-up

“How would you reduce a 1.2 GB image?” In order of impact: multi-stage so the compiler and dev dependencies never ship; a slim base; a .dockerignore that excludes .git and node_modules; production-only installs; and cache mounts rather than committed caches. Then the part that shows judgement — say that you would check docker history first to find where the megabytes actually are, because guessing usually optimises the wrong layer.

B06

Compose & local environments

The acceptance criterion for a development environment is one command from a fresh clone to a working stack with seeded data. Anything less and new engineers lose their first day, repeatedly.

A compose file worth copying

yaml · compose.yaml
name: acme

services:
  api:
    build:
      context: .
      target: dev                    # a stage in the same Dockerfile
    command: npm run dev
    ports: ['3000:3000', '9229:9229']  # app, and the debugger
    environment:
      NODE_ENV: development
      MONGO_URI: mongodb://mongo:27017/acme   # service NAME, not localhost
      REDIS_URL: redis://redis:6379
    env_file: [.env.local]
    volumes:
      - ./src:/app/src:cached        # live reload
      - /app/node_modules            # anonymous volume: keep the CONTAINER's
                                      # node_modules, not the host's. Native
                                      # modules built for Linux would otherwise
                                      # be shadowed by macOS binaries.
    depends_on:
      mongo:  { condition: service_healthy }
      redis:  { condition: service_healthy }
      seed:   { condition: service_completed_successfully }
    develop:
      watch:
        - { action: sync,    path: ./src,          target: /app/src }
        - { action: rebuild, path: ./package.json }

  mongo:
    image: mongo:7
    command: ['--replSet', 'rs0', '--bind_ip_all']   # transactions need a set
    ports: ['27017:27017']
    volumes: [mongo-data:/data/db]
    healthcheck:
      test: ['CMD', 'mongosh', '--quiet', '--eval', "db.adminCommand('ping')"]
      interval: 5s
      timeout: 3s
      retries: 12
      start_period: 10s

  redis:
    image: redis:7-alpine
    command: ['redis-server', '--appendonly', 'yes']
    volumes: [redis-data:/data]
    healthcheck:
      test: ['CMD', 'redis-cli', 'ping']
      interval: 5s
      retries: 10

  seed:
    build: { context: ., target: dev }
    command: npm run db:seed
    depends_on:
      mongo: { condition: service_healthy }
    environment:
      MONGO_URI: mongodb://mongo:27017/acme
    restart: 'no'                    # a one-shot task, not a service

volumes:
  mongo-data:
  redis-data:
Plain depends_on does not wait for readiness

Without a condition it waits only for the container to start, not for the database to accept connections. Your API then crashes on boot, restarts, and works on the third attempt — which people learn to accept as normal. condition: service_healthy plus a real healthcheck is the fix, and service_completed_successfully is how you make migrations and seeds run before the app.

The commands

CommandDoesNotes
docker compose up -d --build --waitBuild, start, and block until healthchecks pass--wait is what makes it usable in CI
docker compose watchSync and rebuild on file changesreplaces most bind-mount reload setups
docker compose logs -f api mongoFollow specific services
docker compose exec api shShell in a running service
docker compose run --rm api npm testOne-off task in a fresh container--rm, or containers accumulate
docker compose configRender the fully merged filethe fastest way to debug override precedence
docker compose ps --format jsonStatus, scriptable
docker compose downStop and remove containersvolumes survive safe
docker compose down -v…and delete the volumesdestructive — this is your database

Overrides for dev, test and CI

bash · one base file, environment-specific layers
# compose.yaml is loaded, then compose.override.yaml AUTOMATICALLY if present.
# Later files win, and lists are REPLACED while maps are MERGED — the source
# of most "why did my override not apply" confusion. Check with:
docker compose config

# Explicit composition for CI: no bind mounts, no exposed ports, fixed tags
docker compose -f compose.yaml -f compose.ci.yaml up -d --wait
docker compose -f compose.yaml -f compose.ci.yaml run --rm api npm run test:integration
docker compose -f compose.yaml -f compose.ci.yaml down -v
yaml · compose.ci.yaml
services:
  api:
    build: { target: runtime }   # the production stage, not dev
    command: node dist/server.js
    volumes: []                  # no source mounting in CI
    ports: []                    # nothing published; the network is internal
  mongo:
    ports: []
    tmpfs: [/data/db]            # in-memory: much faster, and disposable
Run the same stack in CI

Using the same compose definition for integration tests means the environment developers debug in is the environment CI fails in. The alternative — mocks locally, containers in CI, or the reverse — guarantees a category of bug that only appears in one of them. For tests started from code rather than from a shell, testcontainers gives the same containers with programmatic lifecycle.

Development performance

ProblemCauseFix
Slow file access on macOS/WindowsBind mounts cross a virtualisation boundary:cached, VirtioFS, or compose watch which syncs rather than mounts
node_modules conflictsHost binaries built for macOS shadow the container's Linux onesAn anonymous volume at /app/node_modules
Rebuilds on every changeWatching package.json or the whole contextScope watch paths; sync source, rebuild only on manifest change
Containers eating diskAccumulated images, build cache, dangling volumesdocker system df, then prune deliberately
Ports already in useA previous stack, or a host servicecompose down; bind to 127.0.0.1; use distinct ports per project

The limits of Compose

Compose is not a production orchestrator

It has no rolling updates, no health-gated rollout, no rescheduling on node failure, no horizontal scaling across machines, and no secret management beyond files on disk. Teams that ship it to production end up with a single host, downtime on every deploy, and no way to roll back except git checkout and rebuild. It is an excellent development and CI tool; production wants an orchestrator, a managed platform, or at minimum systemd units with a real deployment process.

Interview follow-up

“How do you make a new engineer productive on day one?” git clone && docker compose up --wait and the stack is running with seeded data — no manual database installation, no version mismatches, no README with fourteen steps. The details that make it real are healthchecks with condition: service_healthy, a seed service gated on service_completed_successfully, and using the same compose files in CI so local and CI cannot drift.

B07

CI/CD pipeline design

A pipeline is a system with its own requirements: fast feedback, deterministic results, and one artefact that flows from commit to production unchanged. Most pipelines are accumulated rather than designed, which is why they are slow and nobody trusts them.

The stage model

01 · Fast
Lint, types, unitunder 2 min, on every push
02 · Gate
Must passblocks the PR
03 · Build
The artefactonce, tagged by digest
04 · Verify
Integration, scanagainst that artefact
05 · Gate
Merge queuetests the combination
06 · Staging
Deploy + smokesame digest
07 · Gate
Approvalor automated canary
08 · Prod
Progressivesame digest
Build once, promote the same digest

Rebuilding per environment means the thing you tested is not the thing you shipped — a base image moved, a transitive dependency floated, a cache differed. The artefact must be produced once, identified by its digest, and promoted unchanged through every environment. Everything else in a pipeline is negotiable; this is not.

What belongs in each stage

StageContainsBudgetBlocks?
Fast feedbackformat, lint, type check, unit tests< 2 minyes
Buildcompile, container image, SBOM, sign< 5 minyes
Verifyintegration tests against real dependencies in containers, contract diff, image scan< 10 minyes
Deploy stagingrollout, smoke tests, migration dry-run< 5 minyes
Extendede2e, load, accessibility, cross-browsercan be slowno — run nightly or on a schedule
Deploy productionprogressive rollout with automated analysisas slow as it needs to beyes
Only block on what is both fast and reliable

A twenty-minute end-to-end suite that fails 8% of the time for environmental reasons does not improve quality — it trains everyone to click re-run without reading. Put slow or flaky checks on a schedule with an owner, and reserve the blocking gate for checks that are quick and trustworthy. A pipeline people trust is worth more than one with complete coverage that they route around.

Speed

LeverEffectCost
Cancel superseded runs on a PRlarge — most runs on a busy repo are obsoletenone
Dependency + layer cachingoften 60–80% off install and buildcache storage; key design
Test sharding across a matrixnear-linearmore concurrent minutes; needs balanced shards
Affected-project detection (monorepo)very largethe skipped-required-check trap
Run integration tests in parallel with the image scanmoderatenone — they are independent
Bigger runnerssub-linear — 4× cores rarely gives 4×higher per-minute rate
yaml · sharding, with balanced splits
  test:
    strategy:
      fail-fast: false
      matrix:
        shard: [1, 2, 3, 4]
    steps:
      - run: npx jest --shard=${{ matrix.shard }}/4 --coverage
      # Naive alphabetical splitting puts all the slow suites in one shard and
      # the wall-clock is then the slowest shard. Split by RECORDED DURATION —
      # most runners support a timing manifest from the previous run.
      - uses: actions/upload-artifact@v4
        with: { name: coverage-${{ matrix.shard }}, path: coverage/ }

  coverage:
    needs: test
    steps:
      - uses: actions/download-artifact@v4
        with: { pattern: coverage-*, merge-multiple: true, path: coverage }
      - run: npx nyc merge coverage && npx nyc report --check-coverage --lines 80

Determinism

A pipeline that fails randomly is worse than no pipeline, because it destroys the signal. The sources of non-determinism, in order of frequency: 1 flaky tests timing, shared state, real network, ordering assumptions 2 floating versions `npm install` not `npm ci`; `:latest` base images; unpinned actions 3 real time tests that break at midnight, on a leap day, or in another timezone → inject a clock 4 randomness unseeded → seed it and print the seed on failure 5 shared environments two pipelines mutating the same staging database 6 network unit tests reaching the internet → forbid it Quarantine policy: a test that fails twice in a week without a code change is moved to a quarantined suite with a ticket and a two-week deadline. Not deleted, not ignored — but it stops blocking merges. Without a policy, teams simply learn to re-run, and every real failure is missed.

Secrets in pipelines

ApproachRisk
Long-lived cloud keys in repo secretsnever expire, work from anywhere, invisible when leaked
Environment-scoped secrets + required reviewersa human gates production access
OIDC federationnothing durable is stored — the standard for cloud deploys
Vault with a short-lived tokensame idea, for non-cloud secrets
Masking is not containment

CI platforms redact known secret values from logs, but that only covers exact matches. A secret that is base64-encoded, JSON-embedded, split across lines, or printed by a dependency's debug output appears in plain text. Never echo a secret to test it, never enable verbose modes on tools that print their configuration, and treat build logs as public.

Measuring the pipeline

bash · where the time actually goes
gh run list --workflow ci.yml --limit 100 --json name,conclusion,startedAt,updatedAt \
  --jq '.[] | select(.conclusion != null)
        | [(.updatedAt|fromdate) - (.startedAt|fromdate), .conclusion] | @tsv' \
| awk -F'\t' '{d[NR]=$1; if ($2!="success") f++} END {
    n=asort(d);
    printf "runs %d  failure rate %.1f%%  median %ds  p90 %ds\n",
      n, f*100/n, d[int(n/2)], d[int(n*0.9)]
  }'

# The two numbers that matter:
#   p90 duration  — the tail is what developers actually experience
#   failure rate  — above ~5% without real bugs means flakiness, and the
#                   pipeline has stopped being a signal
PR feedback
< 10 minbeyond this, people context-switch away
Fast stage
< 2 minthe lint/type/unit gate
Flake rate
< 1%above 5% the signal is gone
Commit to prod
< 60 minincluding gates and rollout
Interview follow-up

“Design a CI/CD pipeline for this service.” Lead with the artefact rule — build once, promote by digest — because everything else follows from it. Then the stage order with a budget per stage, what blocks versus what runs on a schedule, and the two determinism requirements (lockfile-only installs and pinned tool versions). Finish with the operational half that people forget: a flaky-test quarantine policy, and OIDC instead of stored cloud credentials.

B08

Deployment strategies

You cannot test your way to zero bad deploys. What you can do is make the blast radius small and the reversal fast — which is why rollback speed matters more than release confidence.

The strategies

StrategyMechanismExtra capacityRollbackTwo versions live
Recreatestop all, start allnoneredeployno — downtime
Rollingreplace instances in batchesmaxSurgeminutesyes
Blue/greenfull parallel stack, switch traffic100%secondsbriefly
Canarysmall % of traffic, then widensmallsecondsyes, for longer
Shadowmirror traffic, discard responses100% of the new stackn/ayes, invisibly
CANARY, with automated analysis t+0 deploy v2 alongside v1, 0% traffic t+1m shift 5% ─┐ t+6m compare ──┤ error rate v2 vs v1 (not vs an absolute threshold) v1 vs v2 │ p95 latency v2 vs v1 │ saturation v2 vs v1 └ ONE business metric (checkout success, signup rate) t+7m pass → 25% → 50% → 100% fail → shift to 0% and roll back automatically Comparing v2 against v1 on the same time window is the essential detail. An absolute threshold ("error rate under 1%") fires during an unrelated incident and passes during a quiet minute. A comparison controls for everything happening in the environment at that moment.

Feature flags separate deploy from release

javascript · the deploy is not the release
// Code ships dark. Enabling it is a runtime decision, reversible in seconds
// without a deploy, a build, or a rollback.
if (await flags.enabled('new-pricing-engine', { tenantId, userId })) {
  return newPricing(cart);
}
return legacyPricing(cart);

// Progressive exposure: internal → 1% → 10% → 50% → 100%
// Each step is a config change, watched for a period, trivially reversed.

// Flags must FAIL SAFE. If the flag service is unreachable, the default
// must be the known-good path — never an exception, never the new code.
const enabled = await flags.enabled(key, ctx).catch(() => false);

// And they must be REMOVED. A flag older than one release cycle is
// permanent branching disguised as a toggle: two code paths, both of which
// must be tested forever. Put an expiry date in the flag's metadata and
// fail CI when it passes.
Flags are the real rollback mechanism

Rolling back a deployment reverts everything in it, including unrelated changes that were fine. Turning off one flag reverts exactly the thing that broke, in seconds, with no rebuild and no rollout. For anything risky, the flag is the control and the deploy is just delivery.

The database constraint that governs everything

During any rolling deploy, two versions of your code share one database. Therefore the deployed code must tolerate BOTH schemas — which forces expand/contract and is what actually makes rollback possible. 1 EXPAND add the new column/field; write BOTH; read the old ↳ old code unaffected · rollback free 2 MIGRATE backfill (throttled, resumable); switch reads via a FLAG ↳ rollback = flip the flag back · still writing both 3 CONTRACT stop writing the old; drop it — a LATER release, once no running version reads it ↳ the only irreversible step, deliberately isolated A migration bundled with the code that needs it removes your ability to roll back at exactly the moment you most want to.

Version skew — the parts people miss

SurfaceThe problemFix
Queue payloadsv2 enqueues a new shape; a v1 worker consumes itdeploy consumers before producers; version the payload; old workers reject rather than misread
Cache entriesv2 writes a new object shape to a key v1 readsversion the key (user:v3:123) so the versions simply miss each other
Sessionsa user's next request hits the other versionadditive changes only during the rollout window
API responsesa single-page app loaded from v1 keeps calling v2additive only; remove fields a release later
Static assetsv1's HTML references a chunk v2 deletedfingerprinted filenames, and keep old assets for a grace period
The asset-404 deploy failure

A user has the page open from v1. You deploy v2, which replaces the JavaScript bundles. The user navigates, the app requests a chunk that no longer exists, and the page breaks — for everyone with a tab open, not just during the rollout. Keep previous fingerprinted assets available for at least a release cycle, and handle the chunk-load error in the client by reloading.

Draining, and where the 502s come from

yaml · the ordering that eliminates rollout errors
    spec:
      terminationGracePeriodSeconds: 45     # must exceed drain + in-flight work
      containers:
        - name: api
          lifecycle:
            preStop:
              exec:
                # Endpoint removal is ASYNCHRONOUS. Without this sleep the
                # process stops accepting while the load balancer is still
                # sending — which is exactly what a rollout 502 is.
                command: ['sh', '-c', 'sleep 10']
          readinessProbe:
            httpGet: { path: /readyz, port: 3000 }
            periodSeconds: 2
            failureThreshold: 2
    strategy:
      rollingUpdate:
        maxSurge: 25%
        maxUnavailable: 0                   # never lose capacity mid-deploy

Choosing

SituationStrategyWhy
Routine change, good testsrolling + flagscheapest; flags cover the risk
Risky change, measurable impactcanary with automated analysiscatches what tests cannot — real traffic
