Infrastructure or runtime upgradeblue/greeninstant reversal matters more than the cost
Rewrite of a critical pathshadow first, then canaryvalidate against production traffic with no user impact
Stateful servicerolling, one at a time, with a PDBquorum must be preserved throughout
Interview follow-up

“How do you deploy without downtime?” The mechanical answer is readiness-gated rolling updates with maxUnavailable: 0 and a preStop drain. The answer that shows depth adds the two constraints that actually make it work: the database must be expand/contract so both versions can run against one schema, and every asynchronous surface — queues, caches, sessions, static assets — has its own version-skew story. Finish with the point that a one-minute rollback is worth more than any amount of pre-release confidence.

B09

Terraform & infrastructure as code

Terraform's model is simple: a desired state, a recorded state, and a plan that reconciles them. Almost every Terraform incident is a state file problem, which is why state deserves more care than the configuration.

The model

configuration (.tf) what you want + state (.tfstate) what Terraform believes exists, and the mapping │ from resource address → real cloud id + refresh what actually exists now ▼ plan ── the diff, which you review ──▶ apply State is the dangerous artefact. Lose it and Terraform no longer knows which resources it owns — a subsequent apply tries to CREATE everything, and will fail on name collisions or, worse, orphan the originals. Corrupt it and it may try to destroy production. Therefore, always: remote backend · locking · versioning · encryption.
hcl · backend and provider pinning
terraform {
  required_version = "~> 1.9"

  required_providers {
    aws = { source = "hashicorp/aws", version = "~> 5.60" }
  }

  backend "s3" {
    bucket       = "acme-tfstate"
    key          = "prod/api/terraform.tfstate"   # one key per state
    region       = "eu-west-2"
    encrypt      = true
    use_lockfile = true      # S3-native locking (no DynamoDB table needed)
    # Enable BUCKET VERSIONING — it is the only way back from a corrupt state.
  }
}

Splitting state

Split byWhy
LifecycleThe VPC changes yearly; the application changes daily. Sharing a state means every app deploy plans against the network, which is slow and risky.
Blast radiusA mistake in the app state cannot destroy the database. This is the main argument.
OwnershipThe platform team owns networking; product teams own their services.
EnvironmentSeparate state per environment, always — ideally separate accounts.
One giant state is the most common Terraform mistake

A single state containing the VPC, the cluster, the databases and forty services takes minutes to plan, locks the whole organisation while one person applies, and puts every resource within reach of one bad terraform destroy. Split it early — merging states later is easy, splitting them means state mv across backends and a nervous afternoon.

count versus for_each

hcl · the difference that destroys resources
# count indexes by POSITION.
variable "buckets" { default = ["logs", "media", "backups"] }
resource "aws_s3_bucket" "bad" {
  count  = length(var.buckets)
  bucket = var.buckets[count.index]
}
# State holds: [0]=logs [1]=media [2]=backups
# Now remove "logs" from the list:
#   [0] logs → media   → Terraform plans to DESTROY logs and RENAME media
#   [1] media → backups
#   [2] backups → gone
# Removing the FIRST element rewrites every subsequent resource.

# for_each indexes by KEY. Removing one touches only that one.
resource "aws_s3_bucket" "good" {
  for_each = toset(var.buckets)
  bucket   = each.key
}
# State holds: ["logs"] ["media"] ["backups"]
# Remove "logs" → exactly one destroy, nothing else moves.

# Use count ONLY for a conditional single resource:
resource "aws_nat_gateway" "this" {
  count = var.enable_nat ? 1 : 0
}

Lifecycle meta-arguments

hcl
resource "aws_db_instance" "main" {
  identifier = "acme-prod"
  # ...

  lifecycle {
    prevent_destroy = true        # apply REFUSES to delete this. Use it on
                                  # every database and every state bucket.

    ignore_changes = [
      password,                   # rotated out of band
      tags["LastModified"],       # written by another system
    ]

    # For resources where a replacement must not leave a gap
    create_before_destroy = true
  }
}

# Replacing a single resource without touching anything else:
#   terraform apply -replace=aws_instance.web[0]
# (`taint` is deprecated in favour of -replace)

The commands

CommandDoesNotes
terraform init -upgradeDownload providers and modulesrun after any version change safe
terraform fmt -recursive -checkFormattingput -check in CI
terraform validateSyntax and type checkingno cloud access needed safe
terraform plan -out=tfplanCompute the diff and save itsafe
terraform apply tfplanApply exactly that planthe only safe way — see below care
terraform state listWhat Terraform thinks it ownssafe
terraform state mv A BRename without recreatingafter a refactor care
terraform state rm AForget a resource, leave it alivehand it to another state care
terraform import A idAdopt an existing resourceprefer import blocks, which are planned
terraform destroyDelete everything in this statedestructive — never in production
Always apply a saved plan

terraform apply without a plan file re-plans at apply time, so what you review and what executes are two different computations separated by however long the approval took. In between, someone can change a variable, a provider can return different data, or a resource can drift. Save the plan in CI, post it to the pull request, and apply that file after approval — this is the single most important Terraform CI practice.

Terraform in CI

yaml · plan on PR, apply on merge, no stored credentials
jobs:
  plan:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    permissions: { id-token: write, contents: read, pull-requests: write }
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: aws-actions/configure-aws-credentials@v4
        with:
          role-to-assume: arn:aws:iam::123456789012:role/tf-plan   # READ-ONLY
          aws-region: eu-west-2
      - uses: hashicorp/setup-terraform@v3
      - run: terraform init
      - run: terraform validate
      - id: plan
        run: |
          terraform plan -out=tfplan -no-color -detailed-exitcode | tee plan.txt
        continue-on-error: true
      - uses: actions/upload-artifact@v4
        with: { name: tfplan, path: tfplan }
      - name: Comment the plan
        run: |
          {
            echo '### Terraform plan'
            echo '```'
            tail -c 60000 plan.txt
            echo '```'
          } > body.md
          gh pr comment "${{ github.event.number }}" --body-file body.md
        env: { GH_TOKEN: '${{ secrets.GITHUB_TOKEN }}' }

  apply:
    needs: plan
    if: github.ref == 'refs/heads/main'
    environment: production            # required reviewers gate the apply
    permissions: { id-token: write, contents: read }
    steps:
      - uses: actions/download-artifact@v4
        with: { name: tfplan }
      - run: terraform apply -auto-approve tfplan   # the SAVED plan

Policy as code

bash · check the plan, not the configuration
# Checking the .tf files misses anything computed. Check the PLAN OUTPUT,
# which is the actual set of changes.
terraform show -json tfplan > plan.json
conftest test plan.json --policy policy/

# Example rules worth enforcing:
#   · no security group with 0.0.0.0/0 on port 22
#   · every S3 bucket has encryption and public access blocked
#   · every resource carries owner and cost-centre tags
#   · no resource of type aws_db_instance is being destroyed
#   · instance types are drawn from an approved list

Drift

somebody changed it in the console
$ terraform plan -detailed-exitcode
# exit 0 = no changes · 1 = error · 2 = changes present
# Run this on a schedule and alert on exit 2 — that is drift detection.

~ aws_security_group.api
  ~ ingress {
      ~ cidr_blocks = ["10.0.0.0/8"] -> ["0.0.0.0/0"]
    }

# Someone opened it in the console during an incident and never reverted.
# Terraform will close it on the next apply — which is the point, but it
# means the next unrelated deploy silently reverts an emergency change.
# Drift detection turns that surprise into a ticket.
Interview follow-up

“Someone deleted the Terraform state file — what now?” First, restore it: the backend bucket has versioning enabled, so it is a previous-version restore. If it is genuinely gone, you must rebuild it by importing every resource — hours of work, and the reason versioning and locking are non-negotiable. The follow-up worth volunteering is that this is exactly why state is split by blast radius: rebuilding one service's state is a bad afternoon, while rebuilding a single state containing the entire estate is a bad week.

B10

Configuration management & Ansible

Containers reduced the need for configuration management; they did not remove it. Image building, bare metal, network devices, databases and one-off fleet operations all still need something that can converge a machine to a described state.

Where it fits

ConcernToolBoundary
Create the machineTerraformdeclarative, owns the resource lifecycle
Configure what is on itAnsible, Chef, Puppet, Saltconverges state inside the machine
Bake an imagePacker + Ansibleconfiguration runs once, at build time
Run and schedule workloadsKubernetes, Nomadthe container era's answer
One-off fleet operationsAnsible ad-hoc"restart this on 200 hosts, in batches"
Immutable beats convergent, where you can have it

Rebuilding an image and replacing the instance removes configuration drift entirely — there is no long-lived machine to drift. Use configuration management to build the image rather than to repair running servers. Where you genuinely have pets (databases, network appliances, on-premises hardware), convergence is still the right model.

Idempotency is the design property

yaml · modules converge; shell commands do not
    # NOT idempotent — appends a duplicate line on every run
    - name: Add config
      shell: echo "max_connections = 200" >> /etc/pg/postgresql.conf

    # Idempotent — the module checks before changing, and reports whether
    # it changed anything. That report is what makes `--check` meaningful.
    - name: Set max_connections
      ansible.builtin.lineinfile:
        path: /etc/pg/postgresql.conf
        regexp: '^max_connections'
        line: 'max_connections = 200'
        validate: '/usr/lib/postgresql/16/bin/postgres --check-config -f %s'
      notify: restart postgres        # handler runs ONCE, at the end, only
                                       # if something actually changed

    # When you must use a command, make it idempotent yourself:
    - name: Initialise the cluster
      ansible.builtin.command: /usr/bin/initdb -D /var/lib/pg
      args:
        creates: /var/lib/pg/PG_VERSION     # skip if this exists

A role, structured

yaml · roles/api/tasks/main.yml
- name: Create the service user
  ansible.builtin.user:
    name: "{{ api_user }}"
    system: true
    shell: /usr/sbin/nologin

- name: Install the release
  ansible.builtin.unarchive:
    src: "{{ api_artifact_url }}"
    dest: /opt/acme
    remote_src: true
    owner: "{{ api_user }}"
  notify: restart api

- name: Render the unit file
  ansible.builtin.template:
    src: api.service.j2
    dest: /etc/systemd/system/api.service
    mode: '0644'
  notify:
    - reload systemd
    - restart api

- name: Enable and start
  ansible.builtin.systemd_service:
    name: api
    enabled: true
    state: started
    daemon_reload: true

- name: Wait for health
  ansible.builtin.uri:
    url: "http://127.0.0.1:{{ api_port }}/healthz"
    status_code: 200
  register: health
  retries: 30
  delay: 2
  until: health.status == 200

Rolling operations across a fleet

yaml · serial, with a failure budget
- hosts: api
  serial: "20%"                 # 20% of the fleet at a time
  max_fail_percentage: 10       # abort the whole play above 10% failures
  order: shuffle                # avoid always hitting the same host first

  pre_tasks:
    - name: Remove from the load balancer
      community.general.haproxy:
        state: disabled
        host: "{{ inventory_hostname }}"
        socket: /var/run/haproxy.sock
      delegate_to: "{{ item }}"
      loop: "{{ groups['loadbalancers'] }}"

  roles:
    - api

  post_tasks:
    - name: Return to the load balancer
      community.general.haproxy: { state: enabled, host: "{{ inventory_hostname }}" }
      delegate_to: "{{ item }}"
      loop: "{{ groups['loadbalancers'] }}"
Run --check --diff first, and know its limits

--check is a dry run and --diff shows the exact file changes — always run them against production first. But check mode is not perfect: a task whose result depends on an earlier task's change will report incorrectly, and command/shell tasks are skipped entirely unless you set check_mode: false. Treat it as a strong signal, not a proof.

Variables and secrets

SourcePrecedenceUse for
Role defaultslowestsensible values every role should ship with
group_vars/allloworganisation-wide settings
group_vars/<group>mediumper-environment
host_vars/<host>higherthe genuine exception
-e on the command linehighestone-off overrides
bash · Vault, and why it is a floor rather than an answer
ansible-vault encrypt_string --stdin-name db_password
ansible-vault edit group_vars/production/secrets.yml
ansible-playbook site.yml --vault-password-file ~/.vault-pass

# Vault encrypts values in the repository, which is better than plaintext.
# Its limits: one shared password, no per-secret access control, no audit of
# who read what, and rotation means re-encrypting and redeploying.
# For anything beyond a small team, pull secrets at run time instead:
- name: Fetch from the secrets manager
  ansible.builtin.set_fact:
    db_password: "{{ lookup('amazon.aws.aws_secret', 'prod/db/password') }}"
  no_log: true            # or the value appears in verbose output

Testing and performance

CommandDoes
ansible-lintStatic checks, including idempotency smells — put it in CI
molecule testConverge a role in a container, then assert; runs it twice to prove idempotency
ansible-playbook --syntax-checkParse only
ansible-playbook --list-hosts --limit prodConfirm the target set before you touch it safe
ansible-playbook --tags migrateRun a subset
ANSIBLE_PIPELINING=true, forks = 50The two settings that most improve speed on a large fleet
Interview follow-up

“Is configuration management still relevant?” Yes, but its centre of gravity moved: it builds images rather than repairing servers, and it operates the things containers cannot — bare metal, databases, network devices, and fleet-wide one-off operations. The judgement being tested is whether you reach for convergence where immutability would be simpler. If a machine can be replaced instead of repaired, replace it.

B11

Secrets management

Most organisations solve storage and never solve rotation. A secret you cannot rotate in an hour is a secret you cannot respond to a leak with — and leaks are the case the whole system exists for.

The lifecycle

generate ─▶ store ─▶ distribute ─▶ use ─▶ rotate ─▶ revoke ─▶ audit most teams stop here The questions that reveal maturity: · How long would it take to rotate the production database password? · Who read that secret last week? · If a laptop is stolen tonight, what must be rotated by morning? · Which secrets have never been rotated? If the answers are "a day", "no idea", "we would have to think", and "all of them" — storage was solved and the lifecycle was not.

Anti-patterns, and why each is dangerous

PatternWhy it fails
In the repositoryPermanent — history keeps it after deletion, and every clone has a copy
In a container image layerExtractable from the registry by anyone who can pull; deleting in a later layer does not remove it
In a Kubernetes manifest in Gitbase64 is encoding, not encryption
In CI environment variables, long-livedNo expiry, readable by any workflow in the repo, invisible in an audit
In a shared password manager, copy-pastedNo audit, no rotation, and it is now in three people's clipboards and shell history
Passed as a command-line argumentVisible in ps to every user on the host, and in shell history

Storage options

OptionStrengthWeakness
Cloud secrets managerIAM-integrated, audited, rotation hooksper-secret cost; cloud-specific
Vaultdynamic secrets, leases, fine-grained policy, cloud-agnosticyou now operate Vault, including its unseal story
Kubernetes Secretsnative, easybase64 only; needs encryption at rest and tight RBAC
SOPS / sealed-secretsencrypted in Git, so GitOps still worksrotation means re-encrypting and committing
External Secrets Operatorsyncs a manager into the cluster; nothing secret in Gitanother controller to run

Dynamic secrets — the strongest pattern

bash · a credential that did not exist a minute ago
# Vault creates a NEW database user on request, with a lease. Nothing durable
# is stored anywhere, and a leak expires by itself.
vault read database/creds/api-readonly
#   lease_id     database/creds/api-readonly/8f3a...
#   lease_duration  1h
#   username     v-token-api-readonly-x7Kd9
#   password     A1b2C3...

# The credential is unique per request, so an audit log shows exactly which
# workload used which credential — impossible with one shared password.

# The same idea, without Vault, for cloud access:
#   · IRSA / workload identity in Kubernetes
#   · OIDC federation from CI
# Both mint short-lived cloud credentials from a workload's verified identity,
# which removes the stored secret entirely rather than protecting it better.
The best secret is the one that does not exist

Every static credential is a thing to store, distribute, rotate, audit and eventually leak. Workload identity replaces it with a verifiable assertion — this pod, in this cluster, running as this service account — exchanged for a credential that expires in minutes. Wherever the platform supports it, that is strictly better than any amount of care around a stored key.

Rotation without downtime

A rotation that requires a synchronised restart is one nobody performs. The dual-secret window makes it routine: 1 add the NEW secret alongside the old; verification accepts BOTH 2 deploy — every instance now accepts old and new 3 switch signing/issuing to the new secret 4 deploy — nothing is issued with the old one 5 wait for the longest-lived artefact signed with the old to expire 6 remove the old secret Applies to JWT signing keys, webhook signing secrets, API keys and session cookies alike. Build the array-of-valid-secrets shape from day one: verify(token, secrets) // any of them sign(payload, secrets[0]) // always the first

Kubernetes

yaml · External Secrets Operator, so nothing secret is in Git
apiVersion: external-secrets.io/v1beta1
kind: ExternalSecret
metadata: { name: api-secrets, namespace: prod }
spec:
  refreshInterval: 1h              # picks up rotations automatically
  secretStoreRef: { name: aws-secrets, kind: SecretStore }
  target:
    name: api-secrets              # the Kubernetes Secret it creates
    creationPolicy: Owner
  data:
    - secretKey: DATABASE_URL
      remoteRef: { key: prod/api/database-url }
    - secretKey: JWT_PRIVATE_KEY
      remoteRef: { key: prod/api/jwt-private-key }
---
# Mount as FILES, not environment variables:
#   · env vars appear in `kubectl describe pod`, crash dumps and child processes
#   · mounted files update in place when the Secret changes; env vars do not
    volumeMounts:
      - { name: secrets, mountPath: /run/secrets, readOnly: true }
Kubernetes Secrets are base64, not encrypted

kubectl get secret -o yaml | base64 -d is the entire attack. They are only meaningfully protected once you enable encryption at rest in etcd and restrict who can read Secrets in that namespace via RBAC — and by default a great many roles can. Check with kubectl auth can-i get secrets --as=system:serviceaccount:prod:default; the answer surprises people.

Detection and response

find them, then respond in the right order
# Scan history — the leak is usually already committed
$ gitleaks detect --source . --log-opts="--all" --redact

# Block the next one at commit time
$ cat .pre-commit-config.yaml
repos:
  - repo: https://github.com/gitleaks/gitleaks
    rev: v8.18.4
    hooks: [{ id: gitleaks }]

# And server-side, which the developer cannot skip
# GitHub → Settings → Code security → Secret scanning → Push protection

# WHEN ONE IS FOUND, in this order:
1 ROTATE   it is compromised from the moment it was pushed
2 ASSESS   provider audit logs between push time and rotation
3 PURGE    filter-repo, force-push, everyone re-clones
4 PREVENT  push protection, pre-commit, move it to a manager
Purging first is the common mistake — it feels like containment and is not.
Interview follow-up

“How do you manage secrets?” The weak answer names a storage product. The strong answer describes the lifecycle and then argues for removing the secret entirely where possible — workload identity and OIDC instead of stored keys, dynamic short-lived credentials where a static one is unavoidable, and a dual-secret window so rotation is routine rather than an outage. Finish with detection: push protection plus a pre-commit scanner, and the rotate-first incident order.

B12

DevSecOps & policy as code

Shifting security left works only if the findings are actionable. A pipeline that blocks on four hundred medium-severity advisories teaches everyone to bypass the gate, which is worse than not having one.

The scanning layers

TypeFindsWhenBlock?
Secret scanningcredentials in codepre-commit + pushyes, always
SCAknown CVEs in dependenciesPR + nightlycritical only, and only if reachable
SASTinjection, path traversal, unsafe deserialisationPRhigh confidence rules only
IaC scanningpublic buckets, open security groups, missing encryptionPR, on the planyes — high signal, low noise
Container scanningOS and language package CVEson buildcritical, with a fixable filter
DASTruntime issues against a live environmentnightly on stagingno — slow and noisy
Licence compliancea copyleft dependency in a proprietary productPRyes, by policy
"Vulnerable" is not the same as "exploitable"

A critical CVE in a transitive dependency you never call is not a critical risk to you. Blocking on raw severity produces a queue of unactionable work and trains people to add blanket suppressions. Filter by reachability where the tooling supports it, filter by fixability always (a CVE with no patch cannot be actioned by a blocking gate), and use VEX statements to record "not affected, because" as data rather than as a comment nobody finds.

yaml · a scan gate that stays credible
      - name: Scan the image
        uses: aquasecurity/trivy-action@master
        with:
          image-ref: ghcr.io/acme/api@${{ needs.build.outputs.digest }}
          severity: CRITICAL,HIGH
          ignore-unfixed: true          # cannot act on an unpatched CVE
          exit-code: '1'
          format: sarif
          output: trivy.sarif

      # Upload regardless, so findings are visible even when not blocking
      - uses: github/codeql-action/upload-sarif@v3
        if: always()
        with: { sarif_file: trivy.sarif }
yaml · suppressions with an expiry, not a permanent hole
# .trivyignore.yaml
vulnerabilities:
  - id: CVE-2024-12345
    statement: |
      Reachable only via the XML parser, which we do not enable.
      Upstream fix expected in 3.2.0.
    expired_at: 2026-06-01        # after this date the build fails again

# An expiry date is what turns a suppression from "we ignored it" into
# "we made a decision and set a reminder". Without it, every suppression
# is permanent and the ignore file becomes the real security posture.

Policy as code

bash · Rego against a Terraform plan
# policy/security_groups.rego
package terraform.security

deny contains msg if {
  resource := input.resource_changes[_]
  resource.type == "aws_security_group"
  rule := resource.change.after.ingress[_]
  cidr := rule.cidr_blocks[_]
  cidr == "0.0.0.0/0"
  rule.from_port == 22
  msg := sprintf("%s exposes SSH to the internet", [resource.address])
}

deny contains msg if {
  resource := input.resource_changes[_]
  resource.type == "aws_db_instance"
  "delete" in resource.change.actions
  msg := sprintf("%s would be DESTROYED — refusing", [resource.address])
}

# Run against the PLAN, not the .tf files: only the plan contains the
# computed values, module expansions and the actual set of actions.
terraform show -json tfplan | conftest test - --policy policy/
yaml · Kyverno, enforcing at admission
apiVersion: kyverno.io/v1
kind: ClusterPolicy
metadata: { name: production-baseline }
spec:
  validationFailureAction: Enforce      # start with Audit, then switch
  background: true                      # also report on existing resources
  rules:
    - name: images-from-approved-registries
      match: { any: [{ resources: { kinds: [Pod] } }] }
      validate:
        message: "Images must come from ghcr.io/acme"
        pattern:
          spec:
            containers:
              - image: "ghcr.io/acme/*"

    - name: no-latest-tag
      match: { any: [{ resources: { kinds: [Pod] } }] }
      validate:
        message: "Deploy by digest, not by a mutable tag"
        pattern:
          spec:
            containers:
              - image: "*@sha256:*"

    - name: require-resource-limits
      match: { any: [{ resources: { kinds: [Pod] } }] }
      validate:
        message: "Memory limits are required"
        pattern:
          spec:
            containers:
              - resources: { limits: { memory: "?*" } }

    - name: no-privileged
      match: { any: [{ resources: { kinds: [Pod] } }] }
      validate:
        message: "Privileged containers are not permitted"
        pattern:
          spec:
            =(securityContext): { =(privileged): "false" }
Roll policy out in audit mode first

Switching straight to Enforce on an existing cluster blocks deployments people are relying on, at a moment they did not choose, with an error message they have never seen. Run in Audit, report what would have been blocked, give teams a deadline to fix it, then enforce. The same applies to a blocking CI gate: report for two weeks, then block.

Supply chain

ControlPreventsEffort
Pin CI actions to a full SHAA compromised action tag exfiltrating secretslow — Dependabot maintains it
Lockfile-only installsA floating transitive dependency changing under younone
--ignore-scriptsInstall-time code execution from a malicious packagelow; a few packages genuinely need them
Base image pinned by digestThe base changing between buildslow
SBOM per buildNot knowing what shipped when a CVE landsone flag
Provenance attestationAn artefact of unknown origin reaching productionlow with OIDC
Verification at admissionAn unsigned or foreign image runningmoderate — and it is what makes the rest real
A signature nobody verifies is decoration

Signing every image and never enforcing verification adds a step to the pipeline and no security. The control is the admission policy that refuses to run an image without a valid signature from the expected workflow identity. Until that exists, the signing is a preparation for security rather than security.

Threat modelling in the review

Fifteen minutes against the architecture diagram, using STRIDE at the trust boundaries — not against the codebase, because the expensive flaws are architectural and a scanner will never find them. Spoofing can something claim to be another service? Tampering can data be modified in transit or at rest? Repudiation can an action be denied? is there an audit trail? Information disclosure what leaks — including through error messages, timing, and 403-versus-404? Denial of service what is expensive to request, and is it bounded? Elevation of privilege can a user become an admin? is authz per-object? Output: three or four specific mitigations with owners. Not a document.
Interview follow-up

“How do you stop the security gate becoming noise?” Filter by fixability and reachability rather than raw severity; block only on the categories with high signal — secrets, IaC misconfiguration, critical fixable CVEs — and report the rest; require every suppression to carry a justification and an expiry date; and give findings a named owner rather than a shared queue. The underlying principle is that a gate people route around is worse than no gate, because it also creates the belief that the problem is covered.

R01

Kubernetes: the model

Kubernetes is a set of control loops. You declare desired state, controllers continuously reconcile actual state towards it, and there is no imperative deploy step anywhere. Everything else follows from that one idea.

The control loop

you ──apply──▶ API server ──▶ etcd (the ONLY component that writes etcd) ▲ │ watch ┌───────────┼────────────┬──────────────┐ ▼ ▼ ▼ ▼ deployment replicaset scheduler kubelet (per node) controller controller assigns pods starts containers │ │ to nodes reports status └───────────┴──────────┴───────────────┘ each observes, compares, acts, repeats Consequences worth internalising: · nothing is imperative — kubectl apply records intent; a controller acts · everything is eventually consistent — a pod is not running when apply returns · the system self-heals — delete a pod and the ReplicaSet recreates it · drift is corrected automatically — manual edits are reverted by the controller · a failure is usually a controller that cannot reach desired state, and it says so in kubectl describe events — which is where to look first

Objects and the workload hierarchy

Deployment ──manages──▶ ReplicaSet ──manages──▶ Pod ──▶ container(s) rollout history replica count the scheduling unit revision, strategy one per revision shares network + volumes StatefulSet stable identity (api-0, api-1), ordered start/stop, one PersistentVolumeClaim per replica that SURVIVES deletion DaemonSet exactly one pod per node — log shippers, node exporters Job run to completion, with retries CronJob a Job on a schedule (watch concurrencyPolicy and history limits) Never create a bare Pod. Nothing recreates it when the node dies.
Labels
Identifying metadata used by selectors — how a Service finds its pods and a Deployment finds its ReplicaSet. Changing a Deployment's selector is immutable and requires recreating it.
Annotations
Non-identifying metadata for tools: checksums, ingress configuration, controller hints. Never selected on, so free to change.
ownerReferences
How cascading deletion works — deleting a Deployment garbage-collects its ReplicaSets and their Pods.
Finalizers
A controller registers one to do cleanup before deletion completes. A finalizer whose controller is gone is why a namespace sits in Terminating forever.

Pod lifecycle, and the ordering that causes dropped requests

STARTUP Pending ─▶ init containers (sequential, must all succeed) ─▶ containers start, startupProbe runs (liveness is suspended) ─▶ readinessProbe passes ─▶ Endpoint added ─▶ traffic arrives SHUTDOWN — these two happen CONCURRENTLY, and that is the whole problem ┌─ endpoint removal propagates to every kube-proxy (asynchronous, ~seconds) └─ preStop hook runs, then SIGTERM is sent to PID 1 If the process exits on SIGTERM immediately, it stops accepting while the load balancer is still sending → connection refused → 502s on every deploy. Fix: a preStop sleep long enough for endpoint removal to propagate, and a terminationGracePeriodSeconds that exceeds preStop + in-flight work. After the grace period: SIGKILL, unconditionally.
yaml · probes configured so they help rather than harm
          startupProbe:              # buys time for a slow start WITHOUT
            httpGet: { path: /livez, port: 3000 }   # a lax liveness probe
            failureThreshold: 30
            periodSeconds: 2         # up to 60s to come up

          livenessProbe:             # "is this process wedged?" — NOTHING ELSE
            httpGet: { path: /livez, port: 3000 }
            periodSeconds: 10
            failureThreshold: 3

          readinessProbe:            # "should I get traffic right now?"
            httpGet: { path: /readyz, port: 3000 }
            periodSeconds: 5
            failureThreshold: 2

          lifecycle:
            preStop:
              exec: { command: ['sh', '-c', 'sleep 10'] }
Never check a dependency in the liveness probe

Liveness failure means restart this container. If the probe touches the database, a thirty-second failover restarts your entire fleet at once — losing all in-flight requests, dropping every connection pool, and hitting the recovering database with a reconnection stampede. Liveness answers "is the process wedged". Readiness answers "should I receive traffic". Conflating them turns a dependency wobble into an outage you caused.

Resources: requests, limits, and the asymmetry

CPUMemory
Requestwhat the scheduler reserves; the share under contentionwhat the scheduler reserves
Exceeding the limitthrottled — paused until the next periodOOMKilled — terminated, exit 137
Symptomlatency spikes with CPU well under the limitcontainer vanishes, no application log
Diagnosecontainer_cpu_cfs_throttled_seconds_totalkubectl describe pod → OOMKilled; dmesg
Common practiceset requests, often no limit for latency-sensitive servicesalways set a limit
Guaranteed
requests == limits for every container. Evicted last.
Burstable
requests < limits. The usual case.
BestEffort
nothing set. Evicted first under node pressure — never for anything that matters.

kubectl as an API client

CommandDoes
kubectl get pods -o wide --sort-by=.status.startTimePods with node and IP, newest last
kubectl describe pod XEvents at the bottom — where the answer usually is
kubectl get events --sort-by=.lastTimestampCluster-wide recent events
kubectl logs X -c app --previousLogs from the crashed container
kubectl logs -l app=api --tail=50 --prefixAll pods of a Deployment, labelled
kubectl rollout status deploy/api --timeout=120sBlock until rolled out
kubectl rollout undo deploy/apiRoll back care
kubectl diff -f manifest.yamlWhat would apply change? safe
kubectl explain pod.spec.containers.resourcesSchema documentation from the server
kubectl auth can-i --list --as=system:serviceaccount:prod:apiWhat can that identity do?
kubectl debug -it pod/api --image=nicolaka/netshoot --target=apiTools inside a distroless pod
kubectl get pod X -o jsonpath='{.spec.containers[*].image}'Precise field extraction

The failures you will actually see

StatusCauseCheck
PendingNo node fits: insufficient CPU/memory, taints, node selector, unbound PVCkubectl describe pod → the scheduler's reason
ImagePullBackOffWrong tag, private registry, missing imagePullSecretevents; try the pull manually
CrashLoopBackOffThe process exits repeatedlylogs --previous — the current container has no logs yet
OOMKilled (137)Exceeded the memory limitraise the limit, or fix the leak; check the runtime's heap setting
Error then restartNon-zero exitlogs; check the command and args are exec-form
Running but no trafficReadiness failing, or the Service selector does not matchkubectl get endpoints svc/api — empty means selector or readiness
Terminating foreverA finalizer whose controller is goneinspect metadata.finalizers; remove only when you understand why
EvictedNode under memory or disk pressureBestEffort pods go first; set requests
Interview follow-up

“A pod is not receiving traffic although it is Running — what do you check?” Work from the Service inwards: kubectl get endpoints first, because an empty endpoint list immediately separates the two causes — either the Service selector does not match the pod's labels, or readiness is failing. Then the readiness probe's path, port and the address the process actually bound to. Naming get endpoints as the first command is what distinguishes someone who has debugged this from someone who has read about Services.

R02

Kubernetes: networking & traffic

The network model has three rules and everything else is implementation. Debugging it means walking from the outside in and knowing which command eliminates which layer.

The model

1 Every pod gets its own routable IP. 2 Pods communicate pod-to-pod without NAT. 3 Nodes can reach every pod without NAT. A CNI plugin implements this. What differs between plugins is policy enforcement, performance and observability — not the rules above. SERVICE is a stable virtual IP plus a load-balancing rule. It is NOT a proxy process. kube-proxy programmes iptables or IPVS rules on every node, so traffic to the ClusterIP is rewritten in the kernel to one of the ready pod IPs. Nothing sits in the path. client ──▶ ClusterIP 10.96.4.11:80 │ kernel DNAT (iptables/IPVS) ├──▶ pod 10.244.1.7:3000 ├──▶ pod 10.244.2.3:3000 └──▶ pod 10.244.3.9:3000 ← membership = ready endpoints only

Service types

TypeReachable fromUse for
ClusterIPinside the clusterthe default; service-to-service
NodePortany node IP on a high portrarely directly — the substrate for LoadBalancer
LoadBalancerthe internet, via a cloud LBone per exposed service; costs a real load balancer each
ExternalName—a CNAME to something outside the cluster
headless (clusterIP: None)DNS returns pod IPs directlyStatefulSets, and clients that do their own load balancing
kube-proxy load balances per connection, not per request

With HTTP/1.1 and a new connection per request this is invisible. With HTTP/2, gRPC or any client using persistent connections, one connection is pinned to one pod for its lifetime — so a client with two long-lived connections will hammer two pods while eight sit idle, and scaling up changes nothing. The fixes are a headless service with client-side load balancing, a proxy that understands HTTP/2, a service mesh, or periodically cycling connections with a max-age.

DNS

bash · names, and the resolution cost people miss
# Full form
api.prod.svc.cluster.local

# Within the same namespace, "api" is enough — but that shortcut is why
# resolution can be slow. /etc/resolv.conf contains:
#   search prod.svc.cluster.local svc.cluster.local cluster.local
#   options ndots:5
#
# ndots:5 means any name with FEWER than 5 dots is tried against every
# search domain FIRST. Looking up "api.acme.com" (2 dots) becomes:
#   api.acme.com.prod.svc.cluster.local   NXDOMAIN
#   api.acme.com.svc.cluster.local        NXDOMAIN
#   api.acme.com.cluster.local            NXDOMAIN
#   api.acme.com                          ✓        ← the 4th query
# Four queries per external lookup, ×2 for A and AAAA. On a busy service
# this is a measurable latency and a real load on CoreDNS.

# Fix: a trailing dot makes it absolute, or lower ndots per pod:
#   https://api.acme.com./v1/...
    dnsConfig:
      options: [{ name: ndots, value: '2' }]

Ingress and Gateway API

yaml · Gateway API — the successor, with roles separated
# Platform team owns the Gateway: listeners, certificates, IP addresses
apiVersion: gateway.networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: Gateway
metadata: { name: public, namespace: infra }
spec:
  gatewayClassName: envoy
  listeners:
    - name: https
      protocol: HTTPS
      port: 443
      tls:
        certificateRefs: [{ name: acme-wildcard-tls }]
      allowedRoutes:
        namespaces: { from: Selector, selector: { matchLabels: { public: 'true' } } }
---
# Application team owns the route, in its own namespace
apiVersion: gateway.networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: HTTPRoute
metadata: { name: api, namespace: prod }
spec:
  parentRefs: [{ name: public, namespace: infra }]
  hostnames: ['api.acme.com']
  rules:
    - matches: [{ path: { type: PathPrefix, value: /v1 } }]
      backendRefs:
        - { name: api, port: 80, weight: 90 }      # traffic splitting is
        - { name: api-canary, port: 80, weight: 10 } # first-class, not an annotation
      timeouts: { request: 30s }
Why Gateway API replaced Ingress

Ingress expressed almost everything beyond host-and-path routing through controller-specific annotations, so a manifest was not portable and there was no schema to validate. Gateway API makes timeouts, retries, header manipulation and traffic splitting typed fields, and separates infrastructure concerns (the Gateway) from application concerns (the HTTPRoute) so the two can be owned by different teams with different RBAC.

NetworkPolicy

yaml · default deny, then allow explicitly
apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: NetworkPolicy
metadata: { name: default-deny-all, namespace: prod }
spec:
  podSelector: {}                 # every pod in the namespace
  policyTypes: [Ingress, Egress]  # deny both directions
---
apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: NetworkPolicy
metadata: { name: api-allow, namespace: prod }
spec:
  podSelector: { matchLabels: { app: api } }
  policyTypes: [Ingress, Egress]
  ingress:
    - from:
        - namespaceSelector: { matchLabels: { name: infra } }
          podSelector: { matchLabels: { app: gateway } }
      ports: [{ protocol: TCP, port: 3000 }]
  egress:
    - to: [{ podSelector: { matchLabels: { app: mongo } } }]
      ports: [{ protocol: TCP, port: 27017 }]
    - to: [{ namespaceSelector: { matchLabels: { name: kube-system } } }]
      ports: [{ protocol: UDP, port: 53 }, { protocol: TCP, port: 53 }]
      # Forgetting to allow DNS egress is THE classic mistake: every name
      # lookup fails and the symptom looks like the application is broken.
NetworkPolicy does nothing without a CNI that enforces it

The API accepts the object, kubectl get networkpolicy shows it, and if your CNI does not implement policy enforcement it has no effect whatsoever. There is no warning and no error. Verify by actually attempting a connection that should be denied — do not assume the manifest is doing anything.

Debugging, outside in

each step eliminates a layer
1 # Does the Service have endpoints?
$ kubectl get endpointslice -l kubernetes.io/service-name=api
# EMPTY → selector mismatch or readiness failing. This one check
# separates the two most common causes immediately.

2 # Do the labels actually match?
$ kubectl get svc api -o jsonpath='{.spec.selector}'; echo
$ kubectl get pods --show-labels -l app=api

3 # Does DNS resolve, from inside the cluster?
$ kubectl run -it --rm dns --image=nicolaka/netshoot --restart=Never -- \
    nslookup api.prod.svc.cluster.local

4 # Can we reach the pod DIRECTLY, bypassing the Service?
$ kubectl exec -it deploy/client -- curl -s http://10.244.1.7:3000/healthz
# works → the Service or kube-proxy is the problem
# fails → the application or the pod network is the problem

5 # Bypass everything and hit the pod from your laptop
$ kubectl port-forward pod/api-7d9f 8080:3000

6 # What is the process actually bound to?
$ kubectl debug -it pod/api --image=nicolaka/netshoot --target=api -- ss -tlnp
# 127.0.0.1:3000 → the single most common cause. Bind 0.0.0.0.

Service mesh — when it earns its cost

Gives youCosts
Automatic mTLS between servicesA sidecar (or an eBPF layer) per pod: memory, latency, complexity
Retries, timeouts, circuit breaking without library changesAnother control plane to upgrade and debug
Traffic splitting for canariesFailures now have a new place to hide
Uniform golden-signal metrics per serviceA steep operational learning curve

Justified when you have many services in several languages and genuinely need consistent mTLS and traffic policy without touching every codebase. Not justified for five services in one language, where a shared client library and Gateway API traffic splitting deliver most of the value at a fraction of the operational cost.

Interview follow-up

“Service A cannot reach service B — walk me through it.” Start with kubectl get endpointslice, because an empty list splits the problem in two instantly. Then labels, DNS from inside the cluster, direct pod IP to separate Service problems from application problems, and finally what the process is bound to. Volunteering the ndots:5 DNS behaviour or the per-connection load balancing issue with gRPC signals real operating experience rather than a memorised diagram.

R03

Kubernetes: config, storage & security

Everything a workload needs besides an image. Two details cause most of the surprises: mounted config updates while environment variables do not, and a Secret is base64 rather than encrypted.

ConfigMaps and Secrets

As environment variablesAs mounted files
Updates when the object changesno — fixed at container startyes, after a kubelet sync (~60 s)
Visible in kubectl describe podyes — including secret values in some pathsno
Inherited by child processesyes — and by anything that dumps the environmentno
Appears in crash dumpsfrequentlyno
Verdictfine for non-secret configuse for secrets, always
yaml · forcing a rollout when config changes
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
spec:
  template:
    metadata:
      annotations:
        # Changing a ConfigMap does NOT restart pods. Without this, an
        # updated config silently applies to new pods only — so half the
        # fleet has the old value and nobody notices until behaviour differs.
        checksum/config: "{{ sha256sum .Files.Get 'config.yaml' }}"
    spec:
      containers:
        - name: api
          envFrom:
            - configMapRef: { name: api-config }
          volumeMounts:
            - { name: secrets, mountPath: /run/secrets, readOnly: true }
      volumes:
        - name: secrets
          secret:
            secretName: api-secrets
            defaultMode: 0400
Secrets are base64-encoded, not encrypted

kubectl get secret api-secrets -o jsonpath='{.data.PASSWORD}' | base64 -d is the whole attack. They are only meaningfully protected once encryption at rest is enabled in etcd and RBAC restricts who can read Secrets in that namespace — and by default a surprising number of roles can. Check with kubectl auth can-i get secrets --as=system:serviceaccount:prod:default before assuming otherwise.

Storage

PersistentVolumeClaim ── binds ──▶ PersistentVolume ──▶ real disk what the pod asks for the actual volume StorageClass provisions PVs on demand and decides: · the underlying disk type (gp3, io2, premium SSD) · reclaimPolicy Delete (default!) or Retain · volumeBindingMode Immediate or WaitForFirstConsumer Two settings that lose data or break scheduling: reclaimPolicy: Delete — deleting the PVC deletes the DISK. Use Retain for anything stateful, and accept manual cleanup as the price. volumeBindingMode: Immediate — the volume is created before a pod is scheduled, so it may land in an availability zone with no capacity, and the pod can then never be scheduled. Use WaitForFirstConsumer so the volume follows the pod. Access modes: ReadWriteOnce is one NODE (not one pod). ReadWriteMany needs a shared filesystem — most block storage does not support it, and assuming it does is a common design error.
Deleting a StatefulSet does not delete its PVCs

That is deliberate — it lets you recreate the StatefulSet and reattach the data. It also means storage accumulates silently, and that recreating a StatefulSet with a different volume size fails because the existing PVCs are immutable in that field. Clean up deliberately, and know that kubectl delete pvc on a Retain class leaves the PV behind too.

RBAC

yaml · least privilege for a workload
apiVersion: v1
kind: ServiceAccount
metadata: { name: api, namespace: prod }
automountServiceAccountToken: false     # most apps never call the API server
---
apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1
kind: Role                              # Role = namespaced; ClusterRole = global
metadata: { name: api-reader, namespace: prod }
rules:
  - apiGroups: ['']
    resources: [configmaps]
    resourceNames: [api-config]         # ← narrow to the SPECIFIC object
    verbs: [get, watch]
---
apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1
kind: RoleBinding
metadata: { name: api-reader, namespace: prod }
subjects: [{ kind: ServiceAccount, name: api, namespace: prod }]
roleRef: { kind: Role, name: api-reader, apiGroup: rbac.authorization.k8s.io }
audit what an identity can actually do
$ kubectl auth can-i --list --as=system:serviceaccount:prod:api
$ kubectl auth can-i delete pods --as=system:serviceaccount:prod:api -n prod
no

# Find bindings to cluster-admin — the finding in most RBAC audits
$ kubectl get clusterrolebinding -o json \
  | jq -r '.items[] | select(.roleRef.name=="cluster-admin")
           | [.metadata.name, (.subjects[]?.name // "-")] | @tsv'

# Find service accounts that can read secrets cluster-wide
$ kubectl get clusterrole -o json | jq -r '.items[]
    | select(.rules[]? | (.resources[]? == "secrets") and (.verbs[]? | . == "get" or . == "list"))
    | .metadata.name'

Pod security

yaml · a securityContext worth defaulting to
    spec:
      securityContext:                  # pod level
        runAsNonRoot: true
        runAsUser: 10001
        fsGroup: 10001
        seccompProfile: { type: RuntimeDefault }
      containers:
        - name: api
          securityContext:              # container level
            allowPrivilegeEscalation: false
            readOnlyRootFilesystem: true
            capabilities: { drop: ['ALL'] }
          volumeMounts:
            - { name: tmp, mountPath: /tmp }    # read-only root needs writable
            - { name: cache, mountPath: /app/.cache }  # scratch space somewhere
      volumes:
        - { name: tmp, emptyDir: {} }
        - { name: cache, emptyDir: {} }
yaml · Pod Security Admission — enforce it at the namespace
apiVersion: v1
kind: Namespace
metadata:
  name: prod
  labels:
    pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce: restricted
    pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce-version: latest
    pod-security.kubernetes.io/warn: restricted     # warn on kubectl apply
    pod-security.kubernetes.io/audit: restricted    # record in the audit log
# Levels: privileged (no restrictions) | baseline (blocks known escalations)
#         | restricted (hardened: non-root, no capabilities, seccomp)
#
# Roll out as warn+audit FIRST, see what would break, then enforce.

Workload identity

yaml · cloud access with no stored credentials
apiVersion: v1
kind: ServiceAccount
metadata:
  name: api
  namespace: prod
  annotations:
    # The pod's projected service-account token is exchanged for temporary
    # cloud credentials. No access key exists anywhere to leak or rotate.
    eks.amazonaws.com/role-arn: arn:aws:iam::123456789012:role/prod-api
# The IAM trust policy pins the exact namespace and service account:
#   "sub": "system:serviceaccount:prod:api"
# A loose condition here lets ANY pod in the cluster assume the role —
# the same failure mode as a loose OIDC subject in CI.

Multi-tenancy, and its honest limit

ControlProvides
Namespace + RBACAPI-level isolation — who can see and change what
ResourceQuotaA cap on total CPU, memory, storage and object counts per namespace
LimitRangeDefault and maximum requests/limits, so a pod cannot omit them
NetworkPolicyNetwork isolation between namespaces
Pod Security AdmissionPrevents privilege escalation to the node
Namespaces are not a security boundary against hostile code

They are excellent for separating teams that trust each other. They are not sufficient for untrusted workloads: containers share the host kernel, so a kernel vulnerability crosses every namespace on that node. For genuinely hostile multi-tenancy you need separate clusters, or sandboxed runtimes such as gVisor or Kata containers with their own kernel boundary. Saying this distinction out loud is what separates a considered answer from a checklist.

Interview follow-up

“How do you handle secrets in Kubernetes?” Start by saying what a Secret is not — base64 is encoding, so it needs encryption at rest and tight RBAC before it protects anything. Then: mount as files rather than environment variables, so values update in place and do not appear in describe output or crash dumps; sync from an external manager so nothing secret lives in Git; and use workload identity for cloud access so the highest-value credentials do not exist at all.

R04

Kubernetes: operating a cluster

Day-two work: getting pods onto the right nodes, scaling on a signal that means something, and upgrading without dropping traffic.

Scheduling

yaml · spread across zones, keep replicas apart, tolerate a taint
    spec:
      # Spread evenly across zones. maxSkew 1 means no zone may hold more
      # than one replica above the least-loaded one.
      topologySpreadConstraints:
        - maxSkew: 1
          topologyKey: topology.kubernetes.io/zone
          whenUnsatisfiable: DoNotSchedule       # ScheduleAnyway = best effort
          labelSelector: { matchLabels: { app: api } }

      affinity:
        podAntiAffinity:
          # PREFERRED, not required: with required, a 5-replica Deployment on
          # a 3-node cluster leaves 2 pods permanently Pending.
          preferredDuringSchedulingIgnoredDuringExecution:
            - weight: 100
              podAffinityTerm:
                topologyKey: kubernetes.io/hostname
                labelSelector: { matchLabels: { app: api } }

      # Taints repel; tolerations permit. Used to reserve nodes for a workload
      # class — GPUs, spot instances, memory-optimised.
      tolerations:
        - { key: workload, operator: Equal, value: batch, effect: NoSchedule }

      # priorityClassName decides who gets evicted under pressure
      priorityClassName: production
Required anti-affinity is how you get permanently Pending pods

requiredDuringScheduling with topologyKey: hostname means one replica per node, full stop. Scale to more replicas than nodes and the surplus sits Pending forever with a scheduler message nobody reads. Use preferred with a high weight, or topology spread with ScheduleAnyway, unless the constraint is genuinely non-negotiable.

Autoscaling

ScalerScalesOnCaveat
HPAreplica countCPU, memory, or custom metricsCPU is a poor signal for I/O-bound services
VPArequests and limitsobserved usageconflicts with HPA on the same resource; recreates pods
Cluster AutoscalernodesPending podsreactive — a pod waits for a node to boot
KarpenternodesPending pods, but chooses the instance typefaster and cheaper; bin-packs and consolidates
KEDAreplicas, including to zeroqueue depth, stream lag, external metricsthe right answer for workers
yaml · scale a worker on the thing that matters
apiVersion: keda.sh/v1alpha1
kind: ScaledObject
metadata: { name: worker, namespace: prod }
spec:
  scaleTargetRef: { name: worker }
  minReplicaCount: 1
  maxReplicaCount: 50
  cooldownPeriod: 300
  triggers:
    - type: redis
      metadata:
        address: redis.prod.svc:6379
        listName: bull:emails:wait
        listLength: '100'          # one replica per 100 queued jobs
# Queue depth is a LEADING indicator: it rises before latency does.
# CPU on a worker that spends its time waiting on I/O never moves,
# so a CPU-based HPA would never scale it at all.

Disruptions and upgrades

Voluntary node drain, cluster upgrade, deliberate eviction → a PodDisruptionBudget constrains these Involuntary node crash, kernel panic, out-of-memory, hardware failure → a PDB does nothing here. Only replicas across failure domains protect you. The upgrade dance 1 upgrade the control plane first (it supports older kubelets, not newer) 2 add new nodes on the new version 3 cordon an old node kubectl cordon node-3 4 drain it kubectl drain node-3 --ignore-daemonsets \ --delete-emptydir-data --grace-period=60 ↳ evictions respect PDBs; a too-strict PDB makes drain hang forever 5 remove the node, repeat 6 check for removed APIs BEFORE any of this: kubectl-convert, pluto A version-skew rule to remember: kubelet may be up to three minor versions behind the API server, never ahead. Upgrading nodes first breaks the cluster.
yaml · a PDB that permits maintenance
apiVersion: policy/v1
kind: PodDisruptionBudget
metadata: { name: api, namespace: prod }
spec:
  # maxUnavailable is safer than minAvailable: with minAvailable: 3 and only
  # 3 replicas, NO pod may ever be evicted and every drain hangs indefinitely.
  maxUnavailable: 1
  selector: { matchLabels: { app: api } }

Capacity and cost

the requests-versus-usage gap is where the money is
$ kubectl top pods -n prod --sort-by=memory
$ kubectl describe node node-3 | grep -A6 'Allocated resources'

Allocated resources:
  cpu     6200m (77%)      memory  11Gi (71%)   # what is REQUESTED

$ kubectl top node node-3
  cpu     1180m (14%)      memory  4.1Gi (26%)   # what is USED

# 77% requested, 14% used. The scheduler cannot place more pods, while
# the node is almost idle. This gap is typically the single largest line
# of waste in a cluster — and it is invisible unless you compare the two.

# Right-size from observed usage, not from a guess at the p99:
#   requests ≈ p50 usage · limits ≈ p99 usage (memory) · no CPU limit

Backup and recovery

WhatHowNote
etcdetcdctl snapshot save, off-clusterthe entire cluster state; test the restore
Workloads + volumesVeleroschedules, and can restore into a different cluster
ManifestsGitwith GitOps the cluster is rebuildable by re-syncing
Persistent dataThe database's own backupsvolume snapshots are not application-consistent
The real disaster-recovery test

Can you rebuild the cluster from an empty account? If the manifests are in Git, the infrastructure is in Terraform, secrets are in a manager and data is in database backups, the answer is yes and the procedure is documented. If any of those live only in the cluster, you have a single point of failure that no amount of node redundancy addresses.

Node problems

SymptomLikely causeCheck
NotReadykubelet down, network partition, disk pressurekubectl describe node → conditions; the kubelet journal
Pods EvictedMemory or disk pressure on the nodeBestEffort goes first; set requests
DiskPressureImage cache or logs filled the diskkubelet garbage collection thresholds; log rotation
PIDPressureA process fork bomb, or an app leaking threadsset a pids limit per pod
Pods stuck TerminatingNode gone; the kubelet cannot confirm deletiona force delete only removes the API object, not the process
Interview follow-up

“Your pods are Pending — why?” Read the scheduler's message in kubectl describe pod; it says exactly which predicate failed. The usual causes are insufficient allocatable CPU or memory, a taint with no matching toleration, a node selector or affinity nobody can satisfy, an unbound PVC in the wrong zone, or a required anti-affinity that cannot be met with the current node count. The point being tested is whether you read the event or start guessing.

R05

Helm, Kustomize & GitOps

Two problems: generating manifests for several environments without copy-paste, and getting them into a cluster in a way that is auditable and reversible.

Helm

yaml · a template, and where the sharp edges are
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: {{ include "api.fullname" . }}
  labels: {{- include "api.labels" . | nindent 4 }}
spec:
  replicas: {{ .Values.replicaCount }}
  template:
    metadata:
      annotations:
        # Force a rollout when config changes — Helm will not do this for you
        checksum/config: {{ include (print $.Template.BasePath "/configmap.yaml") . | sha256sum }}
    spec:
      containers:
        - name: api
          image: "{{ .Values.image.repository }}@{{ .Values.image.digest }}"
          resources: {{- toYaml .Values.resources | nindent 12 }}
          {{- with .Values.env }}
          env: {{- toYaml . | nindent 12 }}
          {{- end }}

# nindent vs indent: nindent adds a NEWLINE first. Using indent after a
# key on the same line produces valid-looking YAML that parses wrongly.
# This is the most common Helm bug, and it is a consequence of templating
# a whitespace-significant format with a text templater.
CommandDoesNotes
helm template . -f values-prod.yamlRender locally, apply nothingalways do this first safe
helm diff upgrade api . -f values-prod.yamlWhat would changeplugin; treat it as mandatory
helm upgrade --install api . --atomic --wait --timeout 5mInstall or upgrade, roll back on failure--atomic is the flag that matters care
helm rollback api 3Revert to a revisionrevisions live in cluster Secrets care
helm history apiRevisions and their status
helm lint .Static checksin CI
Helm's real problems, stated plainly

It templates a whitespace-significant format with a text templater, so indentation bugs are structural rather than accidental. Values files become an unversioned, undocumented API that grows without bound. Release state lives in cluster Secrets, so a failed upgrade can leave a release pending-upgrade and require manual intervention. And a chart with heavy conditional logic is unreadable. It remains the right tool for distributing third-party software; it is a poor tool for your own manifests.

Kustomize

yaml · overlays, no templating at all
# base/kustomization.yaml
resources: [deployment.yaml, service.yaml]
commonLabels: { app: api }

# overlays/production/kustomization.yaml
namespace: prod
resources: [../../base]
replicas: [{ name: api, count: 6 }]
images:
  - name: api
    newName: ghcr.io/acme/api
    digest: sha256:9f3c...          # bumped by CI on every release

# Generators append a CONTENT HASH to the name, so a config change produces
# a new ConfigMap name and therefore triggers a rollout automatically —
# the thing Helm needs a checksum annotation to fake.
configMapGenerator:
  - name: api-config
    literals: [LOG_LEVEL=info, FEATURE_X=true]

patches:
  - target: { kind: Deployment, name: api }
    patch: |-
      - op: replace
        path: /spec/template/spec/containers/0/resources/limits/memory
        value: 2Gi
Kustomize for your own services
  • Real YAML — your editor, linter and schema validation all work
  • No indentation class of bug
  • Generators trigger rollouts on config change automatically
  • Built into kubectl
Helm for third-party software
  • It is how upstream ships (Prometheus, cert-manager, ingress controllers)
  • Versioned, discoverable charts with an upgrade path
  • Values give a supported configuration surface
  • You are consuming a chart, not maintaining one

GitOps

PUSH CI holds cluster credentials and runs kubectl apply · CI needs production access — a large, permanent blast radius · drift is invisible: a manual change persists until someone notices · "what is running?" is answered by asking the cluster PULL (GitOps) a controller IN the cluster reconciles to a Git repo · CI only writes to Git — it never touches the cluster · drift is corrected automatically, continuously · "what is running?" is answered by reading a commit · rollback is git revert, with a review and an audit trail · disaster recovery is: point a new cluster at the repo ┌──────────┐ image digest ┌──────────┐ watch ┌──────────┐ │ CI │ ────commit──────▶ │ Git │ ◀───────── │ Argo/Flux│ └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └────┬─────┘ │ apply ┌────▼─────┐ │ cluster │ └──────────┘
yaml · an Argo CD Application with self-healing
apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1
kind: Application
metadata: { name: api-prod, namespace: argocd }
spec:
  project: production
  source:
    repoURL: https://github.com/acme/deploy
    targetRevision: main
    path: overlays/production
  destination: { server: https://kubernetes.default.svc, namespace: prod }
  syncPolicy:
    automated:
      prune: true          # delete resources removed from Git
      selfHeal: true       # revert manual changes — the point of GitOps
      allowEmpty: false    # refuse to delete everything if the path is wrong
    syncOptions: [CreateNamespace=true, PruneLast=true]
    retry:
      limit: 5
      backoff: { duration: 5s, factor: 2, maxDuration: 3m }
  revisionHistoryLimit: 20
selfHeal makes kubectl edit useless — deliberately

Scaling a Deployment by hand during an incident works for about thirty seconds, then Argo reverts it and everyone is confused at the worst possible moment. The team must know this before it happens. Emergency changes go through Git, or through an explicit, documented break-glass procedure that disables sync for that application first. This is a culture change as much as a tooling one.

Repository structure

LayoutProsCons
Separate deploy repoApp CI cannot touch cluster config; clean audit of what deployed whenTwo PRs for a change that spans both
Same repo as the appOne PR, atomicEvery app commit triggers a sync evaluation; harder to give the platform team ownership
One repo per environmentStrong separation; different reviewersPromotion means copying between repos

The common shape is a separate deploy repository with a directory per environment. CI builds the image and opens (or pushes) a commit updating the digest for staging; promotion to production is a pull request that copies that digest forward — which makes promotion reviewable and gives you a complete history of what ran where.

Progressive delivery

yaml · Argo Rollouts — canary with automated analysis
apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1
kind: Rollout
spec:
  strategy:
    canary:
      steps:
        - setWeight: 5
        - pause: { duration: 5m }
        - analysis:
            templates: [{ templateName: success-rate }]
        - setWeight: 25
        - pause: { duration: 5m }
        - setWeight: 50
        - pause: { duration: 10m }
      analysis:
        templates: [{ templateName: success-rate }]
        startingStep: 2
---
apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1
kind: AnalysisTemplate
metadata: { name: success-rate }
spec:
  metrics:
    - name: success-rate
      interval: 1m
      successCondition: result[0] >= 0.99
      failureLimit: 2                     # two consecutive failures → abort
      provider:
        prometheus:
          address: http://prometheus.monitoring:9090
          query: |
            sum(rate(http_requests_total{job="api",status!~"5..",version="{{args.version}}"}[2m]))
            /
            sum(rate(http_requests_total{job="api",version="{{args.version}}"}[2m]))
Interview follow-up

“Why GitOps rather than kubectl apply from CI?” Three concrete reasons: CI no longer needs cluster credentials, which removes the largest standing privilege in most pipelines; drift is corrected continuously rather than discovered during an incident; and the answer to "what is running in production" is a commit hash rather than a query. The cost is real and worth naming — selfHeal means manual intervention no longer works, so you need a documented break-glass procedure before you need it.

R06

Metrics & Prometheus

Metrics answer "is the system healthy" cheaply enough to alert on. The one constraint that governs everything is cardinality — and exceeding it takes down your monitoring at exactly the moment you need it.

The data model

A metric is a NAME plus a set of LABELS. Every unique label combination is a separate time series with its own memory and storage. http_requests_total{method="GET", route="/orders/:id", status="200"} Cardinality = the product of every label's distinct values. method(5) × route(40) × status(8) = 1,600 series ✔ …add pod(50) = 80,000 series ⚠ …add user_id(2,000,000) = 160 billion ✘ A label with unbounded values does not degrade Prometheus gradually — it exhausts memory and the server dies. NEVER label with: user id · request id · full URL · email · session · error message · trace id · IP address · timestamp The route PATTERN is fine. The route with the id substituted is not.

The four metric types

TypeForQuery withNote
Counterthings that only increase: requests, errors, bytesalways rate()the raw value is meaningless — it resets on restart
Gaugevalues that go up and down: queue depth, connections, temperaturedirectly, or avg_over_time
Histogramdistributions: latency, response sizehistogram_quantile()server-side buckets; aggregatable across instances
Summarydistributionsthe quantile directlyclient-side quantiles — cannot be aggregated. Prefer histograms.
Why summaries are almost always the wrong choice

A summary computes quantiles inside each instance. You cannot average a p99 across ten instances — the mathematics does not work, and the resulting number is meaningless. A histogram ships bucket counts, which do add up, so histogram_quantile over a sum across instances gives a correct fleet-wide quantile. The cost is choosing buckets in advance; the benefit is a number you can actually aggregate and alert on.

Instrumenting a service

javascript · the RED metrics, with bounded labels
import { Counter, Histogram, Gauge, register } from 'prom-client';

const httpDuration = new Histogram({
  name: 'http_request_duration_seconds',      // _seconds, not _ms — convention
  help: 'HTTP request duration',
  labelNames: ['method', 'route', 'status'],  // ALL bounded
  // Buckets must bracket your SLO threshold, or you cannot measure it.
  // If the SLO is "99% under 500ms", there must be a 0.5 bucket.
  buckets: [0.005, 0.01, 0.025, 0.05, 0.1, 0.25, 0.5, 1, 2.5, 5, 10],
});

app.use((req, res, next) => {
  const end = httpDuration.startTimer();
  res.on('finish', () => {
    end({
      method: req.method,
      // The ROUTE PATTERN. req.originalUrl would be unbounded cardinality.
      route: req.route?.path ?? 'unmatched',
      status: res.statusCode,
    });
  });
  next();
});

// Saturation — the leading indicator that utilisation misses
new Gauge({
  name: 'db_pool_pending',
  help: 'Requests waiting for a database connection',
  collect() { this.set(pool.pendingCount); },
});

app.get('/metrics', async (_req, res) => {
  res.type(register.contentType).end(await register.metrics());
});
// Never expose /metrics publicly — bind it to an internal port.

PromQL

bash · the queries you will write repeatedly
# Request rate by route
sum by (route) (rate(http_requests_total{job="api"}[5m]))

# Error RATIO — always a ratio, never a raw count
sum(rate(http_requests_total{job="api",status=~"5.."}[5m]))
  / sum(rate(http_requests_total{job="api"}[5m]))

# p99 latency, aggregated correctly across every instance.
# Note: sum by (le, ...) — the `le` label MUST be preserved or the quantile
# calculation is nonsense.
histogram_quantile(0.99,
  sum by (le, route) (rate(http_request_duration_seconds_bucket{job="api"}[5m])))

# Saturation: pods at their CPU limit
rate(container_cpu_cfs_throttled_seconds_total{namespace="prod"}[5m]) > 0

# Prediction: will the disk fill within four hours?
predict_linear(node_filesystem_avail_bytes{mountpoint="/"}[1h], 4*3600) < 0

# Change detection: is the current rate much worse than a week ago?
sum(rate(http_requests_total{status=~"5.."}[5m]))
  > 3 * sum(rate(http_requests_total{status=~"5.."}[5m] offset 1w))
rate() vs irate()
rate averages over the whole window and is what you want for alerting and graphing. irate uses only the last two samples — spiky, and misleading on a dashboard.
rate() vs increase()
Same computation; increase is rate × window. Both handle counter resets correctly, which is why you never subtract counter values yourself.
Window size
Must be at least four times the scrape interval, or you get gaps. With a 15 s scrape, [1m] is the practical minimum and [5m] is the usual choice.
by vs without
by keeps only the listed labels; without drops them. without is safer when new labels appear.

Recording and alerting rules

yaml · precompute the expensive queries, alert on the symptom
groups:
  - name: api
    interval: 30s
    rules:
      # Recording rules make dashboards fast and alert expressions readable
      - record: job:http_error_ratio:rate5m
        expr: |
          sum(rate(http_requests_total{job="api",status=~"5.."}[5m]))
          / sum(rate(http_requests_total{job="api"}[5m]))

      - record: job:http_latency_p99:rate5m
        expr: |
          histogram_quantile(0.99,
            sum by (le) (rate(http_request_duration_seconds_bucket{job="api"}[5m])))

      - alert: ApiHighErrorRate
        expr: job:http_error_ratio:rate5m > 0.05
        for: 5m                       # avoid paging on a transient blip
        labels: { severity: page, team: platform }
        annotations:
          summary: 'API error ratio {{ $value | humanizePercentage }}'
          runbook: https://runbooks.acme.com/api-errors
          dashboard: https://grafana.acme.com/d/api

      - alert: QueueOldestJobAge
        expr: queue_oldest_job_age_seconds > 900
        for: 5m
        labels: { severity: page }
        annotations:
          summary: 'Oldest job is {{ $value | humanizeDuration }} old'
          # Depth is ambiguous; AGE is the signal that catches dead workers.

Alertmanager

yaml · routing, grouping and inhibition
route:
  group_by: [alertname, cluster, namespace]
  group_wait: 30s          # collect related alerts before the first notification
  group_interval: 5m
  repeat_interval: 4h
  receiver: slack-platform
  routes:
    - matchers: [severity="page"]
      receiver: pagerduty
      continue: true       # ALSO send to Slack
    - matchers: [severity="ticket"]
      receiver: jira

inhibit_rules:
  # If the whole cluster is down, do not also page for every service in it.
  - source_matchers: [alertname="ClusterDown"]
    target_matchers: [severity=~"page|ticket"]
    equal: [cluster]

Scaling Prometheus

ProblemApproach
Too many series for one serverShard by scrape target; or Mimir/Thanos with a global query layer
Retention beyond ~2 weeksRemote write to long-term storage; keep local retention short
Querying across clustersThanos Query or Mimir — federation does not scale for this
Slow dashboardsRecording rules; the fix is almost never a bigger server
Cardinality creeping upAlert on prometheus_tsdb_head_series; use topk on count by (__name__) to find the offender
Interview follow-up

“Your Prometheus keeps running out of memory — why?” Cardinality, almost always, and almost always from one new label. Find it with topk(10, count by (__name__)({__name__=~".+"})), then look at which label exploded. The underlying cause is nearly always an id, a URL or an error message used as a label value. Mentioning that the fix is to move that dimension to logs or traces — rather than to add memory — shows you understand what each signal is for.

R07

Logging, tracing & OpenTelemetry

Metrics tell you something is wrong. Traces tell you where. Logs tell you what happened to one specific request. Using the wrong one for a question is how investigations take hours.

Structured logging

javascript · the fields every line should carry
logger.info({
  // identity — which code, where
  service: 'orders-api', version: gitSha, env: 'production', region: 'eu-west-2',
  // correlation — the fields that make an investigation possible
  requestId, traceId, spanId, tenantId, userId,
  // the event
  event: 'order.placed', orderId, totalCents, durationMs,
}, 'order placed');

// Redaction happens at the EMITTER, not at the log store. By the time it
// reaches the store it has crossed a network and been written to disk.
const logger = pino({
  redact: {
    paths: ['req.headers.authorization', 'req.headers.cookie',
            '*.password', '*.token', '*.cardNumber', '*.ssn', '*.email'],
    censor: '[redacted]',
  },
});
Two logging mistakes that cost real money

Logging full request and response bodies. At a few thousand requests per second this is terabytes a month, priced per gigabyte ingested — teams routinely discover their logging bill exceeds their compute bill — and you have almost certainly stored personal data you now have obligations about. Log identifiers and sizes; put body logging behind a per-request debug flag. Logging inside a hot loop. One line per iteration in a batch job shipped on Friday is a very expensive Monday.

The collection pipeline

app ──▶ stdout (JSON, one object per line) │ never write log FILES from a container — you then own │ rotation, disk pressure and shipping ▼ node agent Fluent Bit / Vector / Promtail │ · enriches with pod, namespace, node, labels │ · parses, redacts again as a backstop, samples ▼ store indexed (Elasticsearch / OpenSearch) + arbitrary full-text search, fast − index size ≈ data size; expensive at volume label-indexed (Loki) + cheap: only labels are indexed, content is compressed chunks − you must know the labels; grep-style search over a wide window is slow Retention tiers are where the cost is controlled: 7 days hot (searchable) → 30 days cold (object storage) → delete

Tracing

trace 7c1d POST /checkout 820 ms ├─ auth.verify 12 ms ├─ cart.get 30 ms ├─ pricing.quote 240 ms │ ├─ catalogue.batchGet 25 ms │ └─ tax.calculate 210 ms ◀── here ├─ inventory.reserve 45 ms └─ payment.authorise 480 ms ◀── and here └─ POST https://api.stripe.com/v1/payment_intents 470 ms Two spans account for 690 of 820 ms. No amount of staring at metrics would have told you that tax.calculate was the internal problem and the payment provider was the external one.
javascript · context propagation across the boundaries people forget
// Auto-instrumentation covers HTTP and most database clients. The three
// boundaries it does NOT cover are where traces silently break:

// 1. QUEUE — inject the context into the message
const carrier = {};
propagation.inject(context.active(), carrier);
await queue.add('send-receipt', { orderId, _otel: carrier });

// 2. CONSUMER — continue the same trace, possibly hours later
const parent = propagation.extract(context.active(), job.data._otel);
await tracer.startActiveSpan('send-receipt', {}, parent, async (span) => {
  try { await sendReceipt(job.data.orderId); }
  catch (err) { span.recordException(err); span.setStatus({ code: 2 }); throw err; }
  finally { span.end(); }
});

// 3. BATCH — one span per batch with a count attribute, not one per item.
// A span per item in a 10,000-item batch produces a trace nobody can read
// and a bill nobody expected.
tracer.startActiveSpan('reindex.batch', { attributes: { 'batch.size': items.length } },
  async (span) => { await reindex(items); span.end(); });

Sampling

StrategyDecidesKeepsCost
Headat the first spana random N% — mostly successful, fast requestscheap; the interesting traces are usually discarded
Tailwhen the trace completes100% of errors, 100% of slow, N% of the restthe collector must buffer every span until the trace ends
Rate-limitedper servicea fixed traces/secondpredictable spend; loses proportionality
yaml · OpenTelemetry Collector with tail sampling
processors:
  tail_sampling:
    decision_wait: 10s            # must exceed your p99 trace duration
    num_traces: 100000
    policies:
      - name: errors
        type: status_code
        status_code: { status_codes: [ERROR] }      # keep ALL errors
      - name: slow
        type: latency
        latency: { threshold_ms: 1000 }             # keep ALL slow traces
      - name: baseline
        type: probabilistic
        probabilistic: { sampling_percentage: 1 }   # 1% of everything else

  batch: { timeout: 5s, send_batch_size: 1024 }

  # Drop noisy spans before they cost anything
  filter/health:
    traces:
      span:
        - 'attributes["http.route"] == "/healthz"'
Tail sampling is the one that is worth the complexity

Head sampling at 1% keeps a random one-in-a-hundred requests, which are overwhelmingly the fast, successful ones — so when a user reports a slow request, the trace almost certainly was not kept. Tail sampling keeps every error and every slow trace plus a baseline, at a similar total volume. The cost is that the collector must buffer spans until the trace completes, which needs memory and a decision_wait longer than your slowest trace.

Correlation — the part that makes it usable

FromToMechanism
AlertDashboardA dashboard annotation on the alerting rule
Metric spikeA representative traceExemplars — a trace id attached to a histogram bucket
TraceLogs for that requesttraceId on every log line
Log lineThe traceSame field, followed in reverse
User reportEverythingThe x-request-id returned in the response header
