Exemplars are the highest-value integration nobody configures

Without them, seeing a p99 spike on a dashboard means guessing at a time range and searching for a slow trace by hand. With them, you click the spike and land on a trace that produced it. It is a small amount of configuration in the instrumentation and the storage backend, and it changes an investigation from twenty minutes to two.

Cost control without going blind

Logs
identifiers onlybodies behind a debug flag
Traces
tail, ~1%plus 100% of errors and slow
Metrics
bounded labelsroute pattern, never an id
Retention
7d / 30d / deletetiered, not uniform
Interview follow-up

“A user reports a slow request from an hour ago — how do you investigate?” Ask for the request id from the error page, which is why it is in the response header and on every log line. That gives the trace id, the trace gives the waterfall, and the waterfall names the span. If there is no request id, the fallback is metrics to narrow the window and route, then a tail-sampled slow trace from that period. The answer demonstrates whether the correlation was designed in or is being improvised.

R08

SLOs, alerting & on-call

An SLO turns reliability from an opinion into a number you can budget against. Without one, every alert threshold is a guess and every "is it good enough?" conversation is unresolvable.

The terms

SLI
A measurement of one aspect of quality, expressed as good events ÷ valid events. "The proportion of requests returning a non-5xx status."
SLO
A target for an SLI over a window. "99.9% over 28 rolling days." Internal, and deliberately below 100%.
SLA
A contract with financial consequences. Always looser than the SLO, so breaching the SLO is an internal warning rather than a refund.
yaml · SLIs as ratios, with the exclusions stated
availability:
  good:  sum(rate(http_requests_total{job="api",status!~"5.."}[5m]))
  valid: sum(rate(http_requests_total{job="api",status!~"4.."}[5m]))
  # 4xx excluded from "valid": a client sending malformed requests is not
  # us being unavailable. But 429 SHOULD count against you — that is your
  # capacity, not their bug. Decide this explicitly and write it down.

latency:
  good:  sum(rate(http_request_duration_seconds_bucket{le="0.5",job="api"}[5m]))
  valid: sum(rate(http_request_duration_seconds_count{job="api"}[5m]))
  # "99% of requests complete within 500ms" — a RATIO, never an average.
Why an average-latency SLO is worthless

Take 100 requests: 99 at 50 ms and one at 30 seconds. The average is 350 ms, comfortably inside a 500 ms target — while one user waited half a minute and left. Averages are dominated by the majority and structurally blind to the users having a bad time. Always express latency as the fraction of requests under a threshold.

Error budgets

99%
7h 18mper 30 days — internal tools
99.9%
43m 12sthe usual business-API target
99.95%
21m 36sneeds real redundancy and automated failover
99.99%
4m 19sno manual step in any recovery path

The budget is what you are permitted to spend. Consistently spending none of it means the target is too strict and you are over-investing in reliability at the cost of shipping. The policy that makes it real must be agreed in advance, when nobody is under pressure:

  • Budget healthy — ship freely, take reasonable risks.
  • Below 25% remaining — reliability work is prioritised over features until it recovers.
  • Exhausted — feature freeze; only fixes and reliability work ship.
  • One incident consumed more than half — a postmortem with owned action items is mandatory.

Burn-rate alerting

A static threshold ("error rate > 1%") pages at 3am for a blip that consumed 0.2% of the month, and stays silent through a slow leak that will exhaust the budget by Thursday. Burn rate = how fast you are consuming the budget relative to even spend. burn 1 → you exactly exhaust the budget at the end of the window burn 14.4 → you exhaust a 30-day budget in ~2 days Two windows per alert: a LONG one for signal, a SHORT one so it resolves quickly once you fix it. PAGE burn ≥ 14.4 over 1h AND burn ≥ 14.4 over 5m ↳ 2% of the monthly budget in an hour. Something is broken NOW. TICKET burn ≥ 6 over 6h AND burn ≥ 6 over 30m ↳ a real degradation, and nobody needs to wake up for it.
yaml · the rules
      - alert: ApiErrorBudgetFastBurn
        expr: |
          job:slo_error_ratio:rate1h  > (14.4 * 0.001)
          and
          job:slo_error_ratio:rate5m  > (14.4 * 0.001)
        for: 2m
        labels: { severity: page, slo: api-availability }
        annotations:
          summary: 'Burning error budget 14x — 2% of the month in the last hour'
          runbook: https://runbooks.acme.com/api-availability

      - alert: ApiErrorBudgetSlowBurn
        expr: |
          job:slo_error_ratio:rate6h  > (6 * 0.001)
          and
          job:slo_error_ratio:rate30m > (6 * 0.001)
        for: 15m
        labels: { severity: ticket, slo: api-availability }

What deserves to wake someone

SignalActionWhy
SLO fast burnpageusers are affected now
All instances failing readinesspagetotal outage imminent
Queue oldest-job age > 15 minpagework has silently stopped
Disk > 85% and risingpagepredictable, imminent, catastrophic on arrival
Certificate expiring in 14 daysticketplenty of time; paging is cruelty
SLO slow burnticketreal, but daylight is soon enough
A pod restarteddashboardKubernetes did its job
CPU > 80%dashboarda cause, not a symptom — alert on the symptom
Three tests every alert must pass

Actionable — there is something a human can do right now. Urgent — it cannot wait until morning. Documented — it links to a runbook naming the first three commands. An alert failing any of the three should be deleted or downgraded. A rotation that fires twenty times a night trains people to dismiss the one that matters, which is strictly worse than having no alerting.

On-call

PracticeWhy
Minimum 6 people in a rotationFewer means someone is on call every fortnight, which is unsustainable
The developers of a service carry its pagerThe fastest route to fewer alerts is making the people who can fix them feel them
A written handover per shiftOngoing issues, silences set, changes in flight, anything fragile
Time off after a bad nightNot a favour — a fatigued responder makes the next incident worse
Alert review every sprintDelete what did not need a human; that is the whole mechanism for keeping the rotation liveable
Onboarding via shadowingNobody's first page should be their first incident

Postmortems

  1. Timeline first, timestamped from the scribe's notes: started, detected, mitigated, resolved.
  2. The two gaps are the findings. Time-to-detect and time-to-mitigate. If detection took forty minutes, the alerting is the problem regardless of what broke.
  3. Contributing factors, not a root cause. Real incidents have several. "The deploy" is never the whole answer; the missing canary gate is closer to it.
  4. Blameless. The question is what made the mistake easy, not who made it. Anything else guarantees the next person hides the next incident.
  5. Action items with owners and dates, in the same backlog as features. Untracked actions are a fiction everyone participates in.
Interview follow-up

“How do you decide what to alert on?” Start from the SLO rather than from the system: alert on the symptom users experience — availability and latency burn rate — and leave causes to dashboards you consult once paged. Then the three tests: actionable, urgent, documented. If you can also explain why multi-window burn-rate beats a static threshold in both directions — no page for a harmless blip, and a page for a slow leak that a threshold would miss — you have described a rotation people can live with.

R09

Incident response

Incidents are handled well when the procedure was agreed beforehand. Improvising roles, severity and communication while production is down is how a twenty-minute outage becomes a four-hour one.

Severity, decided in advance

SevCriteriaResponse
SEV1Total outage, data loss, or a security breachPage immediately, all hands, exec notified, public status page
SEV2Major feature broken, or severe degradation for many usersPage, incident channel, status page
SEV3Minor feature broken, or a workaround existsTicket, business hours
SEV4Cosmetic or internal onlyBacklog

Write the criteria down. Debating severity during an incident wastes the minutes that matter, and a team without agreed criteria consistently under-calls — because declaring a SEV1 feels dramatic when you are the one who has to do it.

Roles

RoleDoesDoes not
Incident commanderCoordinates, decides, tracks actions, keeps the timeline movingDebug. The moment the IC opens a terminal, nobody is coordinating.
Operations leadMakes the changes — the only person touching productionCommunicate externally
Communications leadStatus page, stakeholders, support, updates on a cadenceDebug
ScribeTimestamps every action, observation and decisionAnything else
One person doing all four is how incidents last four hours

They debug, and while they are deep in a log the stakeholders are not updated, the timeline is not recorded, and two people start making conflicting changes because nobody is coordinating. For a small team, at minimum split commander from operator — one person decides and communicates, the other types. It feels like overhead for ten minutes and then it is obviously right.

The first ten minutes

1 ACKNOWLEDGE take the page; stop it re-routing to someone else 2 ASSESS how many users, which functions, since when 3 DECLARE severity, open a channel, page the roles you need 4 COMMUNICATE an initial update within 5 minutes, even if it is only "we are investigating" — silence is worse 5 ASK WHAT CHANGED deploys, flags, config, migrations, a provider's status page, a certificate expiry, a cron job 6 MITIGATE before you understand it — see below Do NOT begin by reading code. The first six steps take four minutes and frequently resolve the incident outright, because most incidents are a recent change and the mitigation is to undo it.

Mitigation before diagnosis

Users do not care why. Restore service, then investigate with the pressure off. The levers, in order of speed: 1 roll back the deploy seconds — and the most likely cause 2 disable the feature flag seconds — surgical, reverts one thing 3 fail over minutes — region, replica, provider 4 scale up minutes — if it is capacity 5 shed load immediate — protect the core, drop the rest 6 disable the expensive path turn off search, recommendations, exports The trap: "let me just understand it first, then I will know what to fix." Thirty minutes later you understand it and the outage is thirty minutes longer. Understanding is what the postmortem is for.
The one exception

Do not blindly roll back when the deploy included an irreversible migration, and do not restore from backup without first understanding whether the data is corrupt or merely inaccessible — restoring over good data makes a recoverable incident permanent. When the mitigation is itself destructive, take the extra five minutes and have a second person confirm.

Communication

plaintext · a status update template
[INVESTIGATING] 14:05 UTC
We are investigating reports of errors when placing orders. Checkout may
fail for some customers. Browsing and existing orders are unaffected.
Next update by 14:20.

[IDENTIFIED] 14:18 UTC
We have identified the cause and are applying a fix. Approximately 15% of
checkout attempts have failed since 13:52. Next update by 14:35.

[MONITORING] 14:31 UTC
A fix has been applied and error rates have returned to normal. We are
monitoring. Next update by 15:00.

[RESOLVED] 15:02 UTC
This is resolved. Between 13:52 and 14:29, ~15% of checkout attempts
failed. Affected orders were not charged. We will publish a postmortem.

RULES
 · every update carries a TIME FOR THE NEXT ONE — and you meet it, even
   with nothing new to say. Silence is read as "nobody is working on it".
 · write for customers, not engineers: "checkout may fail", not
   "the pricing service is returning 503 from a connection pool timeout"
 · never speculate on cause publicly before you are certain
 · state the impact WINDOW and the SCOPE — people need to know if they
   were affected

Playbooks by incident shape

ShapeSignatureFirst moves
Bad deployStarted at a deploy timestamp; one serviceRoll back. Do not diagnose first.
Dependency outageErrors from one upstream; their status pageCircuit-break, serve degraded, communicate the dependency
CapacityLatency climbing with traffic; saturation metrics highScale out, shed load, disable expensive endpoints
Cascading failureMultiple services failing in sequence; retry amplificationShed load at the edge; disable retries; restart from the bottom of the dependency graph
Data corruptionWrong values, not errorsStop writes first. Assess scope before any restore.
SecurityUnexpected access, leaked credentialRotate, revoke, preserve evidence, escalate to security — do not "clean up" first
Slow burnGradual degradation over hours or daysLook for a leak, a queue backing up, a disk filling, or data growth

A runbook that is usable at 3am

plaintext · runbooks/api-high-error-rate.md
# API high error rate

## Verify it is real (30 seconds)
  kubectl -n prod get pods -l app=api
  open https://grafana.acme.com/d/api-overview

## First three commands
  1. gh run list --workflow deploy.yml --limit 5      # recent deploys?
  2. kubectl -n prod rollout history deploy/api       # what changed?
  3. curl -s https://flags.acme.com/recent            # recent flag changes?

## Mitigations, in order
  Roll back:      kubectl -n prod rollout undo deploy/api
  Disable flag:   ./scripts/flag.sh disable new-pricing-engine
  Scale:          kubectl -n prod scale deploy/api --replicas=20
  Shed load:      ./scripts/shed.sh --percent 30

## Escalate to
  @platform-oncall  ·  #incident-api  ·  DB issues: @data-oncall

## Known causes, most recent first
  2026-03-14  connection pool exhaustion after a Mongo failover — see PM-42
  2026-01-08  a bad feature flag rollout — see PM-38

WHAT MAKES A RUNBOOK USELESS: prose explaining the architecture. At 3am
nobody reads paragraphs. Commands, in order, that can be copy-pasted.
Interview follow-up

“Walk me through a production incident you handled.” Structure it as detect, assess, mitigate, resolve, learn — and make sure mitigation comes before diagnosis in your telling, because that ordering is what is being assessed. Name the specific signal that alerted you, the first thing you tried, and how long each phase took. Then finish on what changed afterwards: the guardrail that now prevents it, not "we were more careful". An incident story with no systemic follow-up suggests the same incident is still possible.

R10

Databases for operators

You may not write the queries, but you will be paged when the database is the problem — and the database is very often the problem, because it is the one component that cannot simply be restarted or scaled out.

Connections

The arithmetic nobody does until it fails: pods × pool size = total connections 40 pods × 50 = 2 000 connections Postgres allocates memory per connection and a few hundred is a lot. MongoDB Atlas tiers cap connections in the hundreds. Autoscaling to 40 pods therefore takes the database down — and it happens at peak, which is exactly when autoscaling fires. Fixes, in order 1 size the pool from per-pod CONCURRENCY, not optimism (10-20 is typical) 2 put a POOLER in front (PgBouncer, RDS Proxy, Atlas connection pooling) 3 alert on connection count as a percentage of the limit, before the cap PgBouncer modes: session one server connection per client session — little gain transaction server connection returned after each transaction — the big win, but prepared statements, advisory locks, LISTEN and session variables break. Applications must be aware.

Replication

PropertyAsynchronousSynchronous
Write latencylocal only+ the round trip to the replica
Data loss on failoverpossible — anything not yet shippednone, for acknowledged writes
Availabilitya replica failure is invisiblea replica failure can block writes
Typical useread replicas, cross-regiona same-zone standby for a financial system
Replica lag causes bugs that look like application bugs

A user updates their profile, the write goes to the primary, the next read goes to a replica that is 400 ms behind, and the UI shows the old value. Support cannot reproduce it because their replica happened to be current. Either route reads to the primary for a short window after a write, use a causally-consistent session, or return the updated object from the write and skip the read. Monitor lag as a first-class metric and alert on it — an unmonitored replica silently serving stale data is worse than one that is down.

Migrations that do not take production down

OperationRiskSafe approach
Add a nullable columnlowSafe on modern Postgres and MySQL
Add a column with a defaulthistorically a full table rewritePostgres 11+ and MySQL 8 handle it in place; on older versions add nullable then backfill
Add an indexlocks writesCREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY (Postgres); a rolling build on a replica set (Mongo)
Add NOT NULLfull scan under lockAdd a CHECK constraint NOT VALID, validate separately, then convert
Change a column typerewriteNew column, dual-write, backfill, switch reads, drop — expand/contract
Rename anythingbreaks running codeNever rename. Add, dual-write, migrate reads, drop later.
Drop a columnirreversibleA separate, later deploy — once no running version references it
Backfill a large tablelock contention, replication lagBatch by primary key, sleep between batches, watch lag, make it resumable
sql · a backfill that does not cause an incident
-- Never: UPDATE orders SET total_cents = total * 100;
-- On 200M rows that is one enormous transaction: lock contention, WAL flood,
-- replication lag in the minutes, and no way to stop it cleanly.

-- Instead: batched, resumable, throttled, and observable.
DO $$
DECLARE
  last_id bigint := 0;
  batch   int := 5000;
  touched int;
BEGIN
  LOOP
    UPDATE orders o
       SET total_cents = (o.total * 100)::bigint
     WHERE o.id > last_id
       AND o.id <= last_id + batch
       AND o.total_cents IS NULL;         -- idempotent: a rerun is harmless
    GET DIAGNOSTICS touched = ROW_COUNT;
    last_id := last_id + batch;
    EXIT WHEN touched = 0 AND last_id > (SELECT max(id) FROM orders);
    COMMIT;                                -- short transactions
    PERFORM pg_sleep(0.1);                 -- let replicas catch up
  END LOOP;
END $$;

What to monitor

MetricWatch forWhy it matters
Connections used / max> 80%the cap is a hard wall; new connections simply fail
Replication lag> a few secondsstale reads, and a larger data-loss window on failover
Cache hit ratiofallingthe working set no longer fits in memory — reads go to disk
Slow query countrisingusually a missing index or a data-volume threshold crossed
Lock waits / deadlocksany sustainedcontention; often a long transaction holding a lock
Longest transaction age> a few minutesblocks vacuum, holds locks, bloats the database
Disk used and IOPStrenda full disk on a database is a hard outage with a slow recovery
Bloat / vacuum lag (Postgres)growingautovacuum falling behind eventually causes transaction wraparound
the queries to run when paged
# What is running right now, longest first
SELECT pid, now()-query_start AS dur, state, wait_event_type, left(query,80)
FROM pg_stat_activity WHERE state != 'idle' ORDER BY dur DESC LIMIT 10;

# The expensive queries overall — this is where to look BEFORE resizing
SELECT calls, mean_exec_time, total_exec_time, left(query,80)
FROM pg_stat_statements ORDER BY total_exec_time DESC LIMIT 10;

# Who is blocking whom
SELECT blocked.pid AS blocked, blocking.pid AS blocking,
       left(blocked.query,60) AS blocked_query
FROM pg_stat_activity blocked
JOIN pg_stat_activity blocking ON blocking.pid = ANY(pg_blocking_pids(blocked.pid))
WHERE cardinality(pg_blocking_pids(blocked.pid)) > 0;

# Kill a runaway — cancel first, terminate only if it will not stop
SELECT pg_cancel_backend(12345);      # polite
SELECT pg_terminate_backend(12345);   # drops the connection

Backups

MethodRPORestore timeNote
Logical dumpsince the last dumpslow — hours for large dataportable across versions; fine for small databases
Volume snapshotsince the last snapshotfastmust be crash-consistent or quiesced
Continuous archiving (PITR)secondsbase restore + replaythe standard for anything that matters
Delayed replicathe delay windowpromote — minutesthe best defence against a bad migration or an accidental DELETE
An untested backup is not a backup

The failure modes are all discovered during a restore: the backup was of the wrong database, it silently failed for six weeks, the encryption key lived only in the environment you lost, the restore takes eleven hours and your RTO is one, or the schema no longer matches the application. Schedule a restore drill, restore into a scratch environment, run the application against it, and record the measured RTO. Until you have done that, you have a backup job, not a recovery capability.

Interview follow-up

“The database is slow — what do you do?” Look at what is running now and what is expensive overall before touching anything: pg_stat_activity for the current picture, pg_stat_statements for the cumulative one, and blocking relationships for contention. Check connections against the cap and replication lag, because both masquerade as slowness. The point being tested is whether you reach for evidence or for a bigger instance — resizing hides a missing index for one release and then costs the same again.

R11

Cloud foundations

The primitives are similar across providers; the names differ. AWS terms are used as the concrete example, but the reasoning transfers.

Account structure is your primary blast radius control

One account per environment, minimum. Not one account with tags. ┌─ management ─────────────────────────────────────────┐ │ organisation root · SCPs · consolidated billing │ └──┬──────────┬──────────┬──────────┬──────────────────┘ ▼ ▼ ▼ ▼ security shared prod staging sandbox logs DNS, CI workloads workloads experiments audit registry (separate (hard budget GuardDuty account) cap) Why an ACCOUNT rather than a tag or a namespace: · a compromised credential is bounded by the account · quotas are per-account, so staging cannot exhaust production's limits · billing is separated without any tagging discipline · "delete everything in staging" cannot reach production · an SCP can make production changes literally impossible from elsewhere

Identity

IdentityUse forAvoid because
IAM user + access keynothingnever expires, works from anywhere, invisible in logs as a person
SSO + assumed rolehumans—
Instance profileEC2 workloads—
IRSA / workload identityKubernetes pods—
OIDC federationCI/CD—
Service control policyGuardrails that even an account admin cannot bypass—
json · policy evaluation, and the rule that matters
{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [
    {
      "Sid": "AllowReadWriteInOwnPrefix",
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": ["s3:GetObject", "s3:PutObject"],
      "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::acme-uploads/tenants/${aws:PrincipalTag/tenant}/*"
    },
    {
      "Sid": "DenyUnencryptedUploads",
      "Effect": "Deny",
      "Action": "s3:PutObject",
      "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::acme-uploads/*",
      "Condition": { "StringNotEquals": { "s3:x-amz-server-side-encryption": "aws:kms" } }
    }
  ]
}
Explicit deny always wins

The evaluation order is: an explicit Deny anywhere — identity policy, resource policy, SCP, permission boundary — beats every Allow. That makes Deny the tool for guardrails you want to be unbypassable: deny unencrypted uploads, deny actions outside approved regions, deny deleting CloudTrail. Everything else is allow-by-exception from an implicit deny.

Networking

VPC 10.0.0.0/16 across three availability zones public 10.0.0.0/20 ALB, NAT gateway route → internet gateway private 10.0.16.0/20 application, pods route → NAT gateway data 10.0.32.0/20 databases, cache no route out at all Decisions that show up on the bill or in an incident: NAT gateway charged per hour AND per gigabyte. One per AZ for availability, and it is frequently a top-five line item. Use VPC endpoints for S3, ECR, Secrets Manager and the rest — traffic then never traverses NAT at all. Cross-AZ charged per gigabyte in both directions. Chatty services spread across three zones can cost more in transfer than in compute. Topology-aware routing keeps traffic local. CIDR sizing cannot be changed later without a rebuild. A /24 that seemed generous runs out of pod IPs the first time you autoscale. Size for ten times what you expect.
Security groupNetwork ACL
Statestateful — the reply is automaticstateless — you must allow return traffic explicitly
Rulesallow onlyallow and deny, evaluated in order
Applies toan interfacea subnet
Common bug—forgetting ephemeral ports 1024-65535 on the return path

Compute options

OptionGood forWatch out
Virtual machinesfull control, legacy, licensed softwareyou patch and manage them
Managed Kubernetesmany services, portability, a platform teamreal operational cost; upgrades every few months
Serverless containers (Fargate, Cloud Run)steady services without node managementhigher per-unit cost; less control over placement
Functionsevent-driven, spiky, gluecold starts, and one database connection pool per concurrent invocation
Functions plus a relational database is a specific trap

Each concurrent invocation is its own process with its own connection. A spike to 500 concurrent executions opens 500 connections to a database that permits 200, and every one of them fails. Use a connection proxy (RDS Proxy, PgBouncer), a data API, or a runtime that pools outside the function. This is the single most common serverless architecture mistake.

Storage and data transfer

TypeUseCost driver
Object (S3)blobs, backups, static assets, data lakestorage class, requests, egress
Block (EBS)a disk for one instanceprovisioned size and IOPS, whether used or not
File (EFS)shared POSIX access across instancesexpensive; usually a sign the design wants object storage
json · a lifecycle policy is free money
{
  "Rules": [
    { "Id": "tier-and-expire",
      "Status": "Enabled",
      "Filter": { "Prefix": "logs/" },
      "Transitions": [
        { "Days": 30,  "StorageClass": "STANDARD_IA" },
        { "Days": 90,  "StorageClass": "GLACIER_IR" }
      ],
      "Expiration": { "Days": 400 } },

    { "Id": "clean-failed-uploads",
      "Status": "Enabled",
      "AbortIncompleteMultipartUpload": { "DaysAfterInitiation": 7 } }
  ]
}
Incomplete multipart uploads are invisible and billed

A failed large upload leaves parts in the bucket that do not appear in a normal listing and are charged as storage indefinitely. On a bucket receiving user uploads this quietly accumulates for years. The abort rule above is two lines and is one of the highest-return storage changes available.

Quotas

Quotas are a real operational constraint, not a formality

Elastic IPs, VPCs per region, rules per security group, Lambda concurrency, EC2 instances per family, API request rates — all capped, many at surprisingly low defaults, and several requiring a support ticket with a lead time. They are discovered during an incident or a scale-up, which is the worst possible moment. Inventory the quotas your architecture depends on, monitor usage against them, and request increases before you need them.

Interview follow-up

“How would you structure a cloud environment for a new company?” Lead with account separation, because it is the control that bounds everything else: separate accounts per environment under an organisation, SSO with assumed roles for humans, and no long-lived access keys anywhere. Then a VPC with public, private and data subnets across three zones with CIDRs sized ten times larger than expected. Then guardrails as SCPs — deny unencrypted storage, deny regions you do not operate in, deny disabling audit logging — because those are the rules that must hold even against an account administrator.

R12

Scaling & performance operations

Capacity is a measurement problem before it is a provisioning problem. The question is never "how many servers" — it is "what is the first thing that saturates, and what number tells me it is close".

The capacity model

provision = peak × (1 / (1 − failure domain)) × (1 + headroom) peak measured from last month, not estimated 3 AZs, survive 1 ⇒ each AZ runs at ≤ 66% at peak headroom 25% for spikes, deploys, and being wrong So a steady-state utilisation around 50% is not waste — it is the cost of surviving the failure you designed for. A fleet running at 85% has no capacity to lose a zone, and will discover that during the incident. Little's Law L = λ × W concurrency = arrival rate × latency 500 rps × 200 ms = 100 concurrent requests in flight Halve latency to 100 ms → 50 concurrent → half the fleet Latency work IS capacity work, and it is usually cheaper than instances.

Load testing that tells you something

javascript · k6 — ramp to find the knee, do not pick a number
import http from 'k6/http';
import { check, sleep } from 'k6';

export const options = {
  stages: [
    { duration: '2m', target: 100 },   // warm up — caches, JIT, pools
    { duration: '5m', target: 100 },   // baseline
    { duration: '5m', target: 400 },   // ramp
    { duration: '5m', target: 800 },   // find the knee
    { duration: '5m', target: 1200 },  // past it
    { duration: '3m', target: 0 },     // recovery: does it come back?
  ],
  thresholds: {
    http_req_failed:   ['rate<0.01'],
    http_req_duration: ['p(95)<500', 'p(99)<1500'],
  },
};

export default function () {
  // A realistic MIX, not one endpoint. Hammering /healthz proves nothing.
  const r = Math.random();
  if (r < 0.7)      http.get(`${__ENV.BASE}/v1/products?page=1`);
  else if (r < 0.95) http.get(`${__ENV.BASE}/v1/products/${randomId()}`);
  else               http.post(`${__ENV.BASE}/v1/orders`, JSON.stringify(order()),
                               { headers: { 'content-type': 'application/json' } });
  sleep(Math.random() * 2);            // think time — users are not a tight loop
}
TestAnswers
LoadDoes it meet the SLO at expected peak?
StressWhere is the knee, and what breaks first?
SoakDoes it degrade over hours? (leaks, disk, connection growth)
SpikeDoes it survive 10× in 30 seconds, and recover?
BreakpointWhat is the absolute ceiling before failure?
A load test against an empty database proves nothing

Queries that are fast against ten thousand rows are frequently catastrophic against ten million, because that is where a missing index starts mattering and where the working set stops fitting in cache. Test with production-scale data volume and a realistic cache state. The other common invalidation is testing with no think time — a tight loop produces unrealistic connection reuse and cache hit rates, and flatters the result.

Reading the result

throughput │ ┌──────────── plateau: saturated │ ╱ │ │ ╱ │ │ ╱ │ ← the KNEE is the number you provision against │ ╱ │ │ ╱ │ └────────────┴──────────────▶ offered load latency │ ╱ ← climbing steeply = queueing, not compute │ ╱ │─────────────────╱ └──────────────────────────▶ offered load Beyond the knee, throughput is flat and latency rises — you are queueing. Adding load past that point adds only latency and error rate. Then find WHICH resource saturated (the USE method): CPU near limit / throttled → compute-bound connection pool pending > 0 → pool-bound (not the database!) database CPU or IO high → database-bound event-loop delay climbing → the process itself network throughput at the cap → bandwidth

Autoscaling in practice

SignalGood forProblem
CPUcompute-bound servicesuseless for I/O-bound — saturated at 30% CPU
Requests per podmost HTTP servicesneeds a known per-pod capacity from a load test
p95 latencydirect proxy for user experiencelagging — you scale after it degrades
Queue depth / lagworkersthe best signal there is: it leads
Concurrent connectionsWebSocket services—
Scale up fast, scale down slowly

Asymmetry is deliberate. Scaling up late costs an outage; scaling down early costs a thrash cycle where you remove capacity and immediately need it back, paying the cold-start penalty twice. Use a zero-second stabilisation window upward and five minutes downward. And remember the lag that autoscaling cannot remove: node provisioning plus image pull plus application start is frequently two to four minutes — which is why scheduled scaling ahead of a predictable peak beats reacting to it.

The bottleneck sequence

Most systems saturate in this order, and knowing it tells you what to measure next rather than what to guess: 1 application CPU / event loop → scale out; it is stateless, so this is easy 2 connection pool → pods × pool exceeds the database limit → a pooler, or fewer queries per request 3 database CPU or IO → indexes, caching, read replicas 4 the single writer → the hard wall: shard, or move work off the primary (CQRS, async, precomputation) 5 network / egress → CDN, compression, keep traffic in-AZ Each stage takes longer to fix than the last. Stage 1 is an afternoon; stage 4 is a quarter. That is the argument for choosing a shard key early even when you do not need one yet.

Degradation under overload

javascript · shed by priority, not at random
// When you cannot serve everything, choose deliberately what to drop.
// Random shedding drops checkout as readily as analytics.
const PRIORITY = {
  '/v1/checkout': 0,      // never shed
  '/v1/orders':   1,
  '/v1/products': 2,
  '/v1/search':   3,
  '/v1/recommendations': 4,
  '/v1/analytics': 5,     // shed first
};

app.use((req, res, next) => {
  const lag = loopDelay.percentile(99) / 1e6;    // ms
  if (lag < 20) return next();                    // healthy

  // Degrade progressively: at 20ms drop priority 5, at 100ms drop everything
  // above priority 1.
  const threshold = lag > 100 ? 1 : lag > 50 ? 3 : 4;
  if ((PRIORITY[req.route?.path] ?? 3) > threshold) {
    shed.inc({ route: req.route?.path });
    res.set('retry-after', '5');
    return res.status(503).json({ error: 'temporarily unavailable' });
  }
  next();
});
Interview follow-up

“How do you know how many servers you need?” Measure per-instance capacity with a ramping load test using a realistic traffic mix and production-scale data, find the knee rather than a comfortable number, then provision for peak plus the loss of one failure domain plus headroom — which lands around 50% steady-state utilisation. Then name the first bottleneck and the metric that warns you before it arrives. The answer that fails is one with no measurement in it.

R13

Disaster recovery & resilience

Resilience is what keeps small failures small. Disaster recovery is what you do when that fails. They are different budgets, different drills, and different conversations with the business.

RPO and RTO come from the business, not from engineering

← RPO ──────────┤ disaster ├────────── RTO → how much data you the event how long until can afford to lose you are serving again RPO is set by backup and replication — how recent the last good copy is. RTO is set by process and automation — how fast you can stand it up. The pattern you can afford: Backup & restore RPO hours RTO hours-days $ backups in another region, rebuild on demand Pilot light RPO minutes RTO ~1 hour $$ database replicating continuously, compute switched off Warm standby RPO seconds RTO ~10 min $$$ a smaller full stack running, scale it up and cut over Active-active RPO ~0 RTO ~0 $$$$ both regions serving; failover is a routing change Asking for "zero downtime, zero data loss" without naming the cost is how DR plans get written and never funded. Quote the tier and the price.
The number that is actually true is the one you last measured

A stated RTO of one hour that has never been executed is a guess. Restore a production-sized backup into a scratch environment on a schedule and record the wall-clock time — restore duration scales with data size, and a database that restored in twenty minutes last year may take six hours now. That measured number is your RTO. Everything else is an aspiration.

Backups

PropertyRequirementWhy
3-2-13 copies, 2 media/systems, 1 off-sitecorrelated failure kills co-located copies
Separate accountbackups not deletable by production credentialsa compromised or mistaken prod role must not reach them
Immutableobject lock / retention holdransomware deletes backups first
Encryptedat rest, with a key you controla backup is a full copy of your data
Point-in-timeWAL/binlog shipping, not only snapshotslets you restore to the second before the bad migration
Testedautomated restore drill on a schedulesilently-corrupt backups are common
bash · a restore drill that runs itself and reports a number
#!/usr/bin/env bash
# Weekly. Restores the latest backup into a throwaway instance, verifies it,
# records the duration, and tears it down. Failure pages the on-call.
set -euo pipefail

STAMP=$(date +%Y%m%d-%H%M)
TARGET="dr-drill-${STAMP}"
START=$(date +%s)

LATEST=$(aws rds describe-db-snapshots --db-instance-identifier prod-db \
  --snapshot-type automated --query 'reverse(sort_by(DBSnapshots,&SnapshotCreateTime))[0].DBSnapshotIdentifier' \
  --output text)

aws rds restore-db-instance-from-db-snapshot \
  --db-instance-identifier "$TARGET" \
  --db-snapshot-identifier "$LATEST" \
  --db-instance-class db.r6g.large --no-multi-az
aws rds wait db-instance-available --db-instance-identifier "$TARGET"

HOST=$(aws rds describe-db-instances --db-instance-identifier "$TARGET" \
  --query 'DBInstances[0].Endpoint.Address' --output text)

# Verification is the part people skip. "It started" is not "it is correct."
psql "host=$HOST dbname=app user=drill" <<'SQL'
  \set ON_ERROR_STOP on
  SELECT 1 FROM orders  LIMIT 1;                       -- tables exist
  SELECT count(*) > 0 FROM users;                      -- data present
  SELECT max(created_at) > now() - interval '2 days'   -- data is RECENT
    FROM orders;
SQL

DURATION=$(( $(date +%s) - START ))
echo "restore_drill_seconds $DURATION" | curl --data-binary @- \
  "${PUSHGATEWAY}/metrics/job/dr_drill"
echo "RTO measured: $((DURATION / 60)) minutes from snapshot ${LATEST}"

aws rds delete-db-instance --db-instance-identifier "$TARGET" --skip-final-snapshot

Resilience patterns in the request path

PatternPreventsRule of thumb
Timeoutthreads held forever by a hung dependencyevery network call, always; tighter than the caller's
Retry + jittertransient blipsonly idempotent calls; exponential with full jitter; a small budget
Circuit breakerhammering something already downopen on error rate, half-open probe to recover
Bulkheadone slow dependency consuming the whole poolseparate pools/concurrency limits per dependency
Load sheddingcollapse under overloadreject early, by priority (see R12)
Fallbacktotal failure of a non-critical featurestale cache, empty list, default — degrade, do not error
Retries are how a small outage becomes a large one

Three layers each retrying three times is twenty-seven requests for one user action. When a dependency slows down, retries multiply load precisely when it is least able to take it — a retry storm. Two defences: retry budgets (retries capped at, say, 10% of total requests, so retries stop when everything is failing) and retrying at one layer only. Always add full jitter; synchronised retries recreate the thundering herd you were avoiding.

Testing failure deliberately

The game day loop 1 Hypothesis "If the primary database fails over, requests degrade for <30s and no orders are lost." 2 Blast radius smallest that tests the hypothesis. Staging first, then production off-peak, with a stop button. 3 Inject kill the pod · fail over the database · add 500ms latency · black-hole a dependency · fill a disk 4 Observe did the alert fire? was the runbook right? did the dashboard show it? how long until a human understood? 5 Fix the finding is usually not the failure itself — it is a missing alert, a wrong runbook, or a timeout that was never configured. The most valuable game day output is a list of things you believed that turned out not to be true.
Start with the dependency inventory, not with the tooling

Before installing anything, list every external dependency and answer one question for each: what happens to a user request if this is completely unavailable for ten minutes? Most teams find at least one dependency they assumed was optional that is in fact on the critical path — an auth provider, a feature-flag service, a metrics sink that blocks on write. Those are your first game days.

Multi-region reality

The hard part is state, and it is a business decision

Stateless services in two regions is a weekend. The database is the problem. Async replication means the failover loses whatever had not shipped — you must decide, in advance and with the business, whether that is acceptable and what to do with the writes. Synchronous replication across regions adds the round-trip to every write, so a 70 ms inter-region link makes every write at least 70 ms slower, permanently, in exchange for zero loss. Active-active additionally requires conflict resolution because two regions will accept conflicting writes. Do not adopt multi-region for reliability alone until you have exhausted multi-AZ — it multiplies operational surface far more than it multiplies uptime.

Interview follow-up

“Your primary region is gone. Walk me through it.” Say the numbers first: our RPO is N minutes and our RTO is M, both measured in the last drill, not estimated. Then declare the incident and cut DNS or global routing to the standby, promote the replica, verify with the same checks the drill uses, and communicate the data-loss window explicitly. Finish on the part interviewers listen for: the failback plan, because coming back is harder than going — the standby now holds the authoritative writes.

R14

Cost engineering & platform work

Two subjects that arrive together at the same point in a company's life: the bill becomes a real number, and the DevOps team becomes a bottleneck. Both are solved by making the right thing the easy thing.

Where the money actually goes

A typical over-spending bill, roughly in order of how much is recoverable: Idle and forgotten 20-40% non-prod running nights and weekends, unattached volumes, old snapshots, load balancers with no targets, dev clusters Over-provisioning 15-30% requests set to "what it might need" rather than measured usage On-demand for steady load 10-30% baseline that never varies, paid at the most expensive rate available Data transfer 5-20% cross-AZ chatter, NAT gateway, egress Storage class 5-15% year-old logs in the hot tier Observability 5-15% every log line at full retention, high-cardinality metrics Observability is the one that surprises people: a logging bill larger than the compute bill it observes is common, and it happens quietly.
LeverTypical savingEffort
Shut non-prod outside business hours60-70% of non-proda cron job
Storage lifecycle policies40-70% of storageone policy
Delete orphans (volumes, snapshots, IPs)varies, often largea script
Rightsize from measured p9520-40% of computeneeds data
Committed use / savings plans30-60% of the baselinea finance decision
Spot for stateless and batch60-90% of that workloadneeds interruption handling
Log sampling and tiering50-80% of logginga config change
VPC endpoints instead of NATmost NAT trafficterraform
Commit to the floor, not the average

Savings plans and reserved capacity are a commitment to spend, and unused commitment is pure waste. Commit only to the baseline you are certain of — the minimum spend over the last twelve months — and leave everything above it on-demand or spot. Companies burned by over-committing usually committed to their average rather than their floor, then shrank.

Making cost visible

hcl · tags are the entire attribution story
// Untagged resources are unattributable, and unattributable spend is nobody's
// problem. Enforce at the provider so it cannot be forgotten.
provider "aws" {
  default_tags {
    tags = {
      Environment = var.environment
      Team        = var.owning_team     # who gets the report
      Service     = var.service_name    # what it is
      CostCenter  = var.cost_center     # who pays
      ManagedBy   = "terraform"
      Repo        = var.repo_url        # where to fix it
    }
  }
}

// And make untagged resources impossible, not merely discouraged:
//   an SCP denying RunInstances without a CostCenter tag
//   a Kyverno/OPA policy rejecting namespaces without an owner label
A monthly report nobody reads changes nothing

Cost work fails when it is a spreadsheet sent to engineers who cannot connect a line item to a decision they made. What works: per-team dashboards showing their own spend, anomaly alerts that fire on a 30% week-over-week jump in a specific service, and a cost estimate posted on the pull request that introduces the change. Feedback at the moment of the decision beats a report thirty days later.

Unit economics

Absolute spend is a bad signal — it should grow as you grow. The real health metric is cost per unit of business value: cost per thousand requests cost per active user per month cost per order processed cost per gigabyte ingested Total spend up 40% with cost per order down 15% is a company doing well. Total spend flat with cost per order up 30% is a company in trouble that looks fine on the finance dashboard. Track one of these per service, and put it on the same dashboard as the latency and error rate. It ends the "are we spending too much" argument by replacing it with a number.

Platform engineering

Why the platform appears Stage 1 everyone does their own ops works to ~15 engineers Stage 2 one DevOps team does everyone's bottleneck at ~40 Stage 3 a platform teams use themselves scales Stage 2 is where most companies are stuck. The failure is structural, not effort: every deployment, secret, database and dashboard routes through a queue of three people, and their throughput is fixed while demand is not. Golden path, not golden cage ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │ the paved road: a template repo that gives you │ │ CI, deploy, dashboards, alerts, secrets, DB — │ │ working — in under an hour │ └────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ ↑ 90% of services take this and never think about it ↓ 10% need something else and may leave the road, accepting that they own what they build Mandating the road produces resentment and shadow infrastructure. Making it clearly the fastest route produces adoption.
The platform providesSo a team never has to
Service template + scaffolding CLIcopy a half-remembered pipeline from another repo
Standard pipeline as a reusable workflowreinvent build, scan, sign, deploy
Deploy as a self-service actionfile a ticket
Dashboards and alerts generated from a manifestlearn PromQL to get a p95 chart
Environment provisioningknow what a VPC is
A service catalogue with ownershipask in Slack who owns this
The platform is a product and its users can refuse it

Treat internal engineers as customers: interview them, measure adoption, track time-to-first-deploy for a new service, and run a satisfaction survey. The metric that matters is how long from an empty repository to production traffic. If it is days, teams will route around you. If it is under an hour, they will not want to. A platform nobody adopts is a very expensive way of having built nothing.

Measuring the whole thing

DORA metricEliteWhat a bad number usually means
Deployment frequencyon demand, many per daybatching — which makes every deploy riskier
Lead time for change< 1 hourslow CI, manual approvals, review queues
Change failure rate0-15%weak tests, no staging parity, big batches
Time to restore< 1 hourno fast rollback, no ownership, poor observability
These four move together

The counter-intuitive finding from the research is that speed and stability are correlated, not traded off. Teams deploying many times a day have lower failure rates, because small changes are easier to review, easier to reason about and trivial to roll back. If someone proposes deploying less often to be safer, that is the argument to make.

Interview follow-up

“Our cloud bill doubled. What do you do?” Attribute before you cut: find out what grew, using tags and cost-explorer grouping, and whether it grew in step with traffic. If cost per order is flat, the bill doubling may be correct and the conversation is about efficiency, not waste. If it grew out of step, the usual suspects in order are idle non-production, a logging or metrics change, a retry loop or runaway job, and a storage class. Then name the durable fix — per-team visibility and anomaly alerts — because a one-time cleanup regrows within two quarters.

R15

Interviews, checklists & the road on

A closing chapter with three jobs: how DevOps interviews are actually structured, the checklists worth keeping, and an honest map of what to learn next.

What the rounds are testing

RoundLooks likeActually assessing
Linux & troubleshooting“a server is at 100% CPU, no dashboards, go”a method, not memorised commands
Scriptingparse logs, call an API, write a small toolerror handling, idempotency, readability
CI/CD design“design the pipeline for this service”stages, gates, artifact promotion, rollback
Infra & cloudwhiteboard a VPC / cluster / account layoutblast radius, identity, cost awareness
Kubernetes“the pod is CrashLooping”whether you have operated it or only read about it
Incident / on-call“production is down, you are IC”calm, structure, communication, mitigate-first
Behavioural“tell me about an outage you caused”blamelessness and honesty
The strongest signal in any of these rounds

Say what you would measure before saying what you would change. Candidates who jump to a fix are guessing; candidates who name the metric, the command, or the dashboard that would confirm the hypothesis are operating. This single habit separates mid-level from senior answers more reliably than any body of knowledge.

The troubleshooting method, portable to any question

1 Scope everyone or some? one host, one AZ, one version, one tenant? started when? what changed then? 2 Correlate deploy · config · migration · traffic · dependency · certificate · scheduled job. Something changed. 3 USE per resource for CPU, memory, disk, network, and each pool: Utilisation · Saturation · Errors 4 Narrow bisect the request path — LB → ingress → app → cache → database → third party. Halve the search space each step. 5 Mitigate roll back, scale, shed, fail over. Before you understand it. Diagnosis is not the emergency. 6 Prove confirm with the metric you named in step 1. 7 Prevent the alert, the test, or the guardrail that catches it next time, written down while it is fresh.
bash · the first sixty seconds on an unfamiliar box
uptime                      # load vs core count — is it even busy?
dmesg -T | tail -30         # OOM kills, disk errors, link flaps
vmstat 1 5                  # r = runnable (CPU), b = blocked (IO), si/so = swap
mpstat -P ALL 1 3           # one hot core = single-threaded; %steal = noisy neighbour
pidstat -u -r -d 1 3        # which process, and is it CPU, memory or IO
iostat -xz 1 3              # %util near 100 with high await = disk saturated
free -h                     # available, not free; check swap movement
df -h; df -i                # BOTH — inodes exhaust while bytes look fine
ss -s; ss -tan state time-wait | wc -l      # socket exhaustion, TIME_WAIT pile-up
ip -s link                  # interface errors and drops
journalctl -p err --since -1h --no-pager | tail -40

# then, on the process:
ss -tanp | grep :8080 | wc -l               # connection count
cat /proc/$PID/limits | grep 'open files'   # FD ceiling
ls /proc/$PID/fd | wc -l                    # and how close it is

Fifteen questions, answered in one line each

Rebase or merge?
Rebase private work to keep history readable; merge shared branches; never rebase what others have pulled.
reset vs revert?
Reset rewrites history (local only); revert adds a new commit that undoes — the only safe option on a shared branch.
Blue-green vs canary?
Blue-green swaps all traffic at once and rolls back instantly; canary shifts a percentage and catches faults that only appear under real traffic.
Why is a container not a VM?
Shared kernel, namespaces and cgroups for isolation — so it starts in milliseconds and isolates less.
What actually is a Kubernetes pod?
The scheduling unit: one or more containers sharing a network namespace, IP and volumes; scheduled together, always on one node.
Liveness vs readiness?
Liveness failure restarts the container; readiness failure removes it from the Service. Confusing them causes restart loops during a dependency outage.
Requests vs limits?
Requests drive scheduling and guarantees; limits cap usage. CPU limits throttle, memory limits kill.
Why does Terraform have a state file?
It maps configuration to real resource IDs — without it Terraform cannot tell "create" from "already exists". Remote, locked, encrypted.
Terraform vs Ansible?
Terraform provisions infrastructure declaratively; Ansible configures what is already there. Increasingly the second is replaced by images and containers.
SLI, SLO, SLA?
The measurement, the target you hold yourselves to, and the contract with money attached. Set the SLO tighter than the SLA.
What is an error budget for?
It converts reliability into a spendable quantity, which turns "should we ship" into arithmetic rather than opinion.
Where do secrets live?
In a secret manager, injected at runtime, short-lived where possible; never in git, images, or environment files in the repo.
Why OIDC in CI instead of keys?
Short-lived credentials scoped to a repository and branch — nothing long-lived to steal from the CI provider.
What is idempotency and why does it matter here?
Running twice equals running once — the property that makes retries, reruns and reconciliation loops safe.
What is the first thing you do in an incident?
Declare it and name an incident commander. Then mitigate. Diagnosis comes after users are served.

Production readiness checklist

Before a service takes real traffic Delivery □ pipeline builds, tests, scans, signs, deploys □ rollback is one command and has been executed once □ image pinned by digest; no :latest anywhere □ migrations are backward compatible (expand/contract) Runtime □ liveness, readiness and startup probes, distinct □ requests and limits set from measured usage □ graceful shutdown: SIGTERM handled, drain before exit □ PodDisruptionBudget; anti-affinity across zones □ runs as non-root, read-only root filesystem Data □ backups automated, off-account, immutable □ a restore has been performed and timed □ connection pool sized against the database limit Observability □ RED metrics; USE for resources □ structured logs with a trace id □ a dashboard someone can read at 3am □ alerts on symptoms, each linked to a runbook Resilience □ timeouts on every outbound call □ retries bounded, jittered, idempotent only □ behaviour when each dependency is down is known □ load tested to the knee; capacity number written down Ownership □ a named team, an on-call rotation, an escalation path □ SLO agreed, error budget policy agreed □ runbook exists and was used in a game day

The part that does not go out of date

Tooling in this field turns over every few years — the specific YAML you learned will be replaced. What persists is the reasoning underneath: version everything, make changes small and reversible, automate the repeated thing, measure before you change, keep blast radius small, and treat failure as expected rather than exceptional. Someone who holds those principles picks up the next tool in a week. Someone who memorised the last tool starts over.

Last interview follow-up

“What makes a good DevOps engineer?” The honest answer is not a tool list. It is someone who reduces the number of things that require a human, who leaves the system easier to operate than they found it, and who can be trusted at 3am — calm, structured, honest about what they do not yet know. Every technical chapter in this handbook is in service of that.

Commit to Deploy — Git, GitHub and the operational toolchain, in the order a change travels.

Commands are marked safe, care or destructive. Learn the recovery for the last two before you need it.

The tools in this book will be replaced within a decade. The failure modes will not.
